THE WISH SERIES
FOUR REALITIES
A collection of four stories about desire, choice, and reality

Author's Note
This work was created under the umbrella of ScriptaHub as part of two interconnected research programs, one dedicated to Artificial Intelligence and the other to Outfinitism, both led by Sînică Alboaie. To explore the details of how these two programs intersect and build upon each other, we invite readers to visit the Outfinity Venture Studio page at www.outfinity.ch .
While Artificial Intelligence language models were used to assist with text generation, the foundational philosophy, thematic structure, and analytical approach derive exclusively from the author's decades of dedicated study of social phenomena, social technologies, and genuine hard technologies resulting from various European and self funded research projects. Recently, within the ScriptaHub research project, we have been deeply studying AI generated literature and its automated verification using neuro symbolic approaches; all the free books made available to the public are part of the suite of works we use to test our latest technologies.

STORY 1
WISHFULL
TUESDAY, BEFORE THE MONEY
On the morning he became rich, Ethan Reed let a man die because he had a presentation at nine.
Later, he would tell himself that he had not let him die. Not in the strict sense of the word. There had been at least thirty people on the platform at Stadelhofen, perhaps forty, and two had already stopped beside the fallen man. A woman had taken out her phone. A young man in an orange jacket had knelt down. Ethan had seen all of this through the train doors, in the four seconds between the warning tone and their closing.
The rain was so fine that it did not seem to be falling, only to exist in the air. The man lay on his back, one arm bent beneath him, his face turned toward the train. His eyes were open. For a fraction of a second, Ethan had the impression that the man was looking directly at him.
The doors began to close.
He could have put his foot between them. He had done it a few times for an umbrella, for a coffee, once for a woman who was running and had smiled at him in gratitude. This time he felt his body decide before he did: one small step backward, his hand tightening around his briefcase, his chin lowered. The people beside the door were blocking his way anyway. The young man in orange was shouting something. The woman with the phone was looking around for help.
The train moved off.
Ethan checked his watch. 7:12.
— There are still people back there, someone said.
He did not turn around. Through the window, the platform slid backward, then disappeared behind an illuminated advertisement for an investment app. In the advertisement, a man his age was laughing with his head thrown back in front of a yacht. Beneath him were the words: DON’T WORK FOR MONEY. LET MONEY UNDERSTAND YOUR AMBITION.
Ethan ran a finger over the knot of his tie. It was too tight. He had made it that way deliberately, because a tight tie gave the impression that its wearer had no time to breathe.
The nine o’clock presentation would determine whether Helix Meridian continued selling the Atlas model to three cantonal banks. Ethan had spent six weeks demonstrating that the model should not be sold to anyone. Atlas underestimated contagion risk during periods of low liquidity, and it did so elegantly—that is, in a way sufficiently complicated to look like a discovery. If the markets remained calm, the model saved millions. If they did not, it turned a problem into a disaster and labeled it a rare event.
Ethan was forty-one and made his living explaining to rich people why they were not as intelligent as luck had made them appear. He did it well and was paid reasonably. Not enough for the apartment he wanted, the dinners to which he wanted to be invited, or the kind of peace he imagined came bundled with money. But enough to oblige him to pretend to be grateful.
On the train, no one was talking about the man on the platform anymore. A woman applied lipstick using the front-facing camera on her phone. A schoolboy whispered French vocabulary to himself. In front of Ethan, a couple watched the same screen and laughed at something he could not see.
He opened his presentation. The first slide read: ROBUSTNESS UNDER STRESS. Beneath it was a photograph of a bridge in a storm, chosen by the communications department.
For a moment he imagined what it would have been like to get off. He would have run to the man. He would have placed two fingers against his neck, although he had not done so since the mandatory first-aid course. Perhaps he would have saved his life. Perhaps the man would have died anyway. Ethan would have missed the presentation. Felix Brecht would have spoken in his place and omitted the very charts that made the conclusion inevitable. The board would have approved Atlas. Five years later, if what Ethan considered likely had happened, everyone would have forgotten the presentation and would be looking for someone to blame.
Arriving on time was a form of responsibility.
He repeated the thought until it sounded reasonable.
At the entrance to the Helix Meridian building, the facial scanner did not recognize him on the first attempt. Blue light passed over his eyes, then turned red.
— Look straight ahead, the voice said.
Ethan was looking straight ahead.
— Remove any objects covering your face.
There was nothing on his face. The guard behind the desk looked up only after the third alert.
— Rough morning? he asked.
— Apparently for the system.
The guard pressed a button. The turnstile opened.
— Systems need coffee on Tuesdays.
— Systems shouldn’t need anything.
The guard smiled without knowing whether it was a joke.
On the twenty-first floor, Felix was waiting beside the meeting room. He was seven years younger and had worn the same haircut since his promotion: short at the sides, long enough on top to look as though he had just run a hand through it after an important idea.
— Did you change the conclusion? Felix asked.
— No.
— Then change the tone.
— Tone does not alter the loss distribution.
— For the people in that room, tone alters almost everything.
Felix tipped his chin toward the frosted doors. Beyond them sat the regional director, two representatives of the banks, and Olivier Heller, the founder of Helix, a man who spoke rarely because he had learned that a billionaire’s silence was interpreted as depth.
— Say there are vulnerabilities, Felix continued. Say we need an additional calibration stage. Don’t say the product is dangerous.
— It is dangerous.
— A knife is dangerous. We still sell it without a hundred-and-eighty-page report.
— If a knife convinced the buyer that it cut only under normal conditions, we would write the report.
Felix watched him for a few moments. Behind him, the glass wall offered a clean view across the city. Wet rooftops, the gray lake, trams moving like impulses across a circuit board. Ethan saw his own reflection beside Felix’s figure: taller, thinner, his hair beginning to recede at the temples. Ten years ago, people had told him he had an interesting face. Five years ago, a serious face. Recently, no one had felt the need to describe it.
— You have a rare talent, Felix said. You can be right in a way that makes people want to see you proven wrong.
— You have a rare talent too.
— I know. I can sell other people’s work without looking as though I’m stealing it.
Felix opened the door for him.
— See? That is why I will lead the presentation.
The first twenty minutes went well. Felix spoke about opportunity, confidence, and first-mover advantage. Ethan showed the charts, explained the datasets, answered questions. The regional director nodded at regular intervals. The bank representatives wrote down words that did not seem related to what he was saying. Heller sat at the far end of the table drawing squares on a sheet of paper.
On slide forty-two, Ethan reached the contagion scenario.
— Here, he said, the assumption of local independence can no longer be sustained. The model nevertheless continues to use it. The result is an artificial reduction in required capital immediately before the period in which capital becomes impossible to obtain.
One of the bank representatives, a man with transparent frames and deeply tanned fingers, raised his hand.
— But what is the probability of your scenario?
— Between eight and thirteen percent over the next four years, depending on monetary policy.
— So there is almost a ninety percent chance that it will not happen.
— No. There is almost a ninety percent chance that it will not happen within the defined window and in the exact form of this scenario. That is not the same thing.
— For a board of directors, it is close enough.
Felix intervened before Ethan could answer.
— What Ethan is emphasizing, perhaps in a more sober manner than this hour requires, is the value of our continuous monitoring service.
Several people smiled. Ethan felt something tighten beneath his sternum.
— No, he said. What I am emphasizing is that the product should not be launched.
The silence that followed was not profound. It was merely uncomfortable.
Heller stopped drawing squares.
— Mr. Reed, he said, how many perfect models have you validated in your career?
— None.
— Then why should perfection be the criterion this time?
— It isn’t. The criterion is that we do not conceal a hole beneath a smoothing function.
— That kind of language is not helpful.
— The hole does not become smaller if we call it a calibration opportunity.
Felix placed his palm on the table near Ethan’s arm, as though calming an animal.
Heller looked at him without expression.
— We pay you to measure risk, Mr. Reed. Not to decide which risks are worth taking.
Ethan looked at the squares on his sheet. There were seven. Each had one side left open.
— Then my report is useless.
— Your report is one component of a decision.
— A component you will cite if things go badly and ignore if they go well.
The regional director closed her pen. Felix withdrew his hand.
Heller smiled for the first time.
— Now I understand what Felix meant about your tone.
The presentation had ended at 9:37. At 9:44, Ethan was sitting alone in a toilet stall with the lid down, trying not to smash his phone against the wall.
He was not angry because he was right. He was angry because being right had no market value.
The message from Nadia had appeared during the meeting.
“I found the box with your books. Can you collect it by Friday? After that I’m taking it to recycling.”
They had not spoken for four months. The breakup had happened a year and a half earlier, but they had continued sending each other articles, photographs of places they had visited, and, from time to time, sentences neutral enough that they could pretend they meant nothing. Their last conversation had ended when Nadia told him that he turned every regret into a project and every project into an argument for why she had to keep waiting.
Ethan typed:
“Friday at seven?”
He deleted it.
He typed:
“You can keep them.”
He deleted it.
He typed:
“I’ll come by Thursday.”
He sent it.
The phone vibrated immediately. It was not Nadia.
YOUR BANK: A significant change in your balance has been recorded. For security, please authenticate in the app.
Ethan looked at the message. Then at the time in the corner of the screen: 9:45.
Scammers had become better. They used the same short numbers as banks, imitated apps, bought certificates. His father would have tapped it. His father had spent his whole life tapping things that promised money and asked, before the money, only for a small proof of trust.
Ethan closed the message.
The phone vibrated again.
CALL: KANTONALBANK — WEALTH MANAGEMENT.
He declined the call.
At 9:46, someone knocked on the stall door.
— Occupied, Ethan said.
— Mr. Reed?
It was the regional director’s assistant.
— One minute.
— Ms. Lutz would like to see you.
Of course. Felix had needed seven minutes to turn a professional objection into a behavioral problem. Ethan stood, smoothed his trousers, and flushed, although he had not used the toilet.
Ms. Lutz received him in a small, windowless room. Felix was already there. Two bottles of water and a gray folder lay on the table.
— Ethan, she said, have a seat.
He disliked it when people who did not like him used his first name. It made them sound as though they regretted what they were about to do.
— I would rather stand.
— As you wish.
Lutz was in her fifties, with short silver hair and a voice that never rose high enough to be accused of aggression. She had recruited Ethan seven years earlier. At the time she had told him that Helix needed people who did not confuse consensus with truth.
— Today’s meeting was difficult, she continued.
— The model is more difficult.
— We are not discussing the model.
— Then the meeting was simple.
Felix ran his tongue over his teeth. Lutz opened the folder.
— You have received two informal warnings in the past six months regarding the way you communicate with the commercial teams.
— I received two requests to remove conclusions from reports.
— You were not asked to remove conclusions.
— The word “contextualize” was used.
— There is a difference between integrity and hostility.
— There is also a difference between context and cosmetic alteration.
Lutz looked at him. On the white wall behind her was a clock without numbers. The second hand moved smoothly, without ticking.
— We are proposing two weeks of administrative leave, she said. Paid. During that time, we will conduct an internal review of your role.
— A review of my role, or of the fact that I performed it?
— This is exactly the kind of response that makes the conversation impossible.
The phone vibrated again in his pocket.
Ethan did not take it out.
— What happens to the report? he asked.
— Felix will finalize it.
— Then put his name on it.
— It is a company document.
— Put the company’s name on the consequences.
Felix sighed.
— You do not have to turn every room into a courtroom.
— No. Some rooms are already markets.
Lutz pushed the folder toward the edge of the table.
— Your access card remains at reception. IT will collect the laptop. I recommend that you use the two weeks to consider whether this is the right environment for you.
Ethan looked at the folder. He did not touch it.
He had imagined leaving many times. In the most satisfying versions, he left after predicting a disaster, and the disaster occurred in time for the others to call him. In some, he bought the company. In others, Heller apologized in front of everyone. Only once had he imagined simply leaving and no longer caring. That version had been the hardest to sustain.
— It’s all right, he said.
Lutz blinked.
— Good.
— No, I mean it is all right. You can keep the laptop.
— That was our intention in any case.
— And the report.
— Ethan—
— I am not going to fight for the right to warn you.
He left before the sentence could be assessed as an additional incident.
At his desk, the screen was already locked. An IT technician whom Ethan had helped with a mortgage analysis two months earlier stood beside the chair and avoided looking at him.
— I only need to take my personal things, Ethan said.
— Sure.
His personal possessions fit into a paper bag: a black mug, two books, a charger, a photograph of his mother in front of an orchard, and a box of tea no one had drunk. In the bottom drawer he found a two-franc coin. He picked it up, rolled it between his fingers, and tossed it onto the desk.
The coin rolled, struck the monitor stand, and stopped on its edge.
The technician laughed.
— You don’t see that often.
Ethan stared at it. It stood perfectly upright, its ridged edge resting on the laminate.
— The desk is slightly tilted, he said.
— That is exactly why it should fall.
Ethan picked up the coin and put it in his pocket.
At reception, his card had been deactivated. The turnstile let him out after the guard pressed the same button as that morning.
— Coffee didn’t help? the guard asked.
— For the system, perhaps.
Outside, the rain had grown heavier. Ethan walked without an umbrella to a café beside the river. He chose a table at the back and ordered a double espresso. When the waitress left, he took out his phone.
He had seven missed calls. Four from the bank’s number, two from an unknown number in Geneva, one from his mother.
The banking app opened normally. Facial identification. Code. Biometric confirmation.
His current-account balance was 8,416.20 francs. His savings balance, 37,900. Nothing unusual.
Below them, however, a new section had appeared.
WEALTH MANAGEMENT — ACCOUNT UNDER REVIEW.
Ethan tapped it.
The screen took longer than usual to load. For a moment, there was only the white background and a thin pulsing line. Then the figures settled into place.
CHF 31,486,772.14.
He looked at them without understanding. Not because there were so many, but because they were too exact. Thirty-one million could be an error, a joke, a fantasy. Thirty-one million four hundred eighty-six thousand seven hundred seventy-two francs and fourteen centimes looked like the result of a history.
Beneath the amount it said: PROVISIONAL. AVAILABLE FOR TRANSACTIONS: CHF 5,000,000.
The waitress brought his coffee.
— Would you like anything else?
Ethan turned the phone face down.
— No.
— Are you sure?
The question was ordinary. The tone was not. She watched him too closely, as though waiting for a different answer.
— Not for now.
She smiled and walked away.
Ethan called the bank using the number on its website, not the one in the missed calls. He went through four automated menus and two verification checks. At last, a man named Bauer answered.
— Mr. Reed, yes, I can see the case. We have been trying to reach you.
— What case?
— The transfer of ownership of your stake in Nacre Systems and the distribution of funds resulting from the acquisition.
— I do not own a stake in Nacre Systems.
A pause. Keyboard sounds.
— According to the documents, you own 4.7 percent through a convertible note issued eleven years ago.
— I have never heard of the company.
— You served as a technical consultant during its initial phase.
— I did not.
— The documents include your signature and an appendix concerning an algorithm for optimizing distributed storage.
Ethan’s mouth went dry.
Eleven years earlier, he had worked for three months on a project called Nacre, but it had not been a company. It had been five people in a shared workspace in Winterthur, trying to reduce data duplication across unstable networks. The project had ended after the founder disappeared with the grant money and one of the programmers left for Singapore. Ethan had received two thousand francs and a vague promise of equity if anything ever came of it.
Nothing had.
— Nacre was never incorporated, he said.
— The entity that acquired the intellectual property retained the original obligations. There have been several mergers. The file is complex.
— Who bought it now?
— Orison Infrastructure.
He knew the name. Orison controlled data centers, submarine cables, backup energy networks. A company large enough that people used its services without ever seeing its name.
— Why was I not contacted before?
— According to the note, the rights were activated only by a transaction exceeding six hundred million. The acquisition closed yesterday.
— At what time?
— I do not have the exact time.
— How did you verify that I am who you say I am?
— Enhanced KYC, tax records, historical signatures, biometric data.
— Without speaking to me.
— Data already validated by the bank were used. The funds are partially restricted pending tomorrow’s meeting.
— Tomorrow?
— You have an appointment at ten at our Paradeplatz headquarters.
— I did not make an appointment.
Another pause.
— It appears as confirmed in the system.
Ethan looked at the rain running down the window. A woman crossed the pavement beneath a yellow umbrella. Behind her, a man ran without a coat. For a moment, the angle of his body reminded Ethan of the young man in the orange jacket on the platform.
— Who confirmed it?
— Authentication was completed through your app at 8:03.
At 8:03, Ethan had been in the lift, rehearsing the opening sentence of his presentation.
— I want everything frozen.
— The funds are already subject to a security hold.
— And the five million shown as available?
— They can be frozen at your request.
Ethan paused.
Five million. Available.
He did not intend to spend it. Obviously not. But freezing it would add no real security. The account was in his app, at his bank, linked to his identity. If it was an error, the bank could correct it regardless of the availability label. If it was fraud, the money did not exist. If it did exist, freezing it would be a symbolic gesture.
— Leave it, he said. I will not make any transactions before tomorrow.
— Of course.
— Send me every document.
— They have been uploaded to your digital vault.
Ethan ended the call. His espresso had gone cold.
He opened the vault. He found a 117-page file containing incorporation documents, assignments, capitalization tables, and signatures. He searched for his name. It appeared forty-three times.
Page nineteen contained a copy of his old passport. Page twenty-three, his signature. Page twenty-seven, a technical appendix written in a style he recognized. Short sentences. Variables named after rivers. One recurring mistake: he used “seperat” in English comments, an error he had not corrected until after turning thirty.
He did not remember the document.
He did, however, remember the room in which he might have signed it. White-painted brick walls, cables across the floor, the smell of warm dust. Alain, the founder, opening a bottle of beer against the edge of the table. Someone saying that, if they succeeded, servers would never forget anything again.
The digital vault also contained a twenty-six-second video recording.
Ethan pressed play.
The image was poor, probably filmed on an old phone. Ethan himself sat at a table, younger, with longer hair and a gray T-shirt. Alain held a sheet of paper in front of him.
— Say something for posterity, a voice behind the camera said.
The younger Ethan looked up and smiled.
— If this is ever worth anything, I’m buying myself time.
— How much time?
— All of it.
Laughter. The image shook. The younger Ethan signed.
The clip ended.
Ethan watched it again. Then once more.
The voice was right. The gestures were his. At the end, just before the image cut out, he lifted the pen and tapped it twice against the table. He still did that when he was thinking.
He did not remember saying the sentence.
His phone vibrated. Nadia had replied.
“I won’t be home Thursday. I’ll leave the box with the concierge.”
Ethan began to type, then stopped. What could he tell her? That he had just discovered he was rich? It would sound exactly like the kind of lie a man invented when a woman was removing him from her life. That he had been suspended? It would confirm all the things she had stopped bothering to tell him.
Instead of replying, he opened the account again.
31,486,772.14.
The number remained.
He set the two-franc coin on the table and flicked it with a fingernail. The coin spun, slowed, and fell heads up.
He picked it up and tossed it again. Heads.
A third time: heads.
A fourth: heads.
The waitress was watching him from the bar.
Ethan closed his fist around the coin. The probability of four identical outcomes was one in eight, if the coin was balanced. Nothing remarkable. People saw miracles in short sequences and ignored the millions of sequences in which nothing happened.
He tossed it a fifth time.
The coin landed on its edge.
It did not roll. It was not leaning against anything. It stood upright in the middle of the table.
Ethan stopped breathing for several seconds.
Then the table vibrated faintly as a tram passed in the street, and the coin fell.
The waitress laughed.
— Today is your lucky day, she said.
Ethan put the coin in his pocket.
— I don’t believe in lucky days.
— They don’t need you to believe in them.
The waitress turned back to the bar before he could decide whether the answer had been strange.
He remained in the café for almost three hours. He read every available document, searched the company names, checked registries, called a former colleague from the Nacre project. The number was no longer in service. He searched for Alain. Alain had died six years earlier in a diving accident in Croatia. Ethan found a photograph from the funeral and recognized two of the people from the workspace. Neither answered his messages.
At 13:26, the bank sent confirmation of the meeting. At 13:31, a law firm sent him a welcome letter. At 13:45, he received an invitation to a confidential discussion with an Orison vice president.
At 14:02, the available balance rose from five million to seven and a half million.
Ethan called the bank again.
— A provisional tax adjustment, a woman explained. It is normal.
— It is not normal to alter my available balance without my consent.
— The funds belong to you.
— That is what I am trying to verify.
— Of course. Everything will be explained to you tomorrow.
Everyone sounded calm. Their calm began to irritate him more than the amount.
At three in the afternoon, he left the café. The rain had stopped. The city had that temporary cleanliness it acquired after rain, when even the cigarette butts looked deliberate. Ethan walked toward his apartment in Wiedikon. He had nowhere else to go. The company laptop was gone, his access had been cut off, and the day’s schedule had emptied like a room after a move.
On the bridge, his phone rang again. His mother.
He answered.
— Hi, Mom.
— Are you at work?
— Not exactly.
— I called you this morning.
— I had a meeting.
— Have you eaten?
— It’s three o’clock.
— That is not what I asked.
Ethan smiled. Ilona’s voice sounded weaker than it had the week before, though that might have been the connection. The center where she lived stood on the edge of the city, in a building with large windows and plants that never died. Her diagnosis had a long name and a short progression. Some days were good. On others, she asked about his father as though he had merely gone to the shop.
— I ate, he lied.
— A girl came to see me today.
— Who?
— She said her name was Elina.
Ethan stopped on the pavement. Cyclists swerved around him.
— I don’t know anyone named Elina.
— She knows you.
— What did she say?
— That you were going to be very busy and I shouldn’t be upset.
— Mom, was it a nurse? The new nurse?
— She wasn’t dressed like the nurses.
— How was she dressed?
Ilona laughed softly.
— Like a woman who knows other people are looking at her.
— Can you get Mrs. Hart?
— She left.
— Elina?
— Two hours ago. She brought me apples.
Ethan felt a cold shiver, although the air had grown warmer.
— What kind of apples?
— Red. Too beautiful. The beautiful ones have no flavor.
— Did you eat one?
— I peeled one.
He saw his mother’s hand in his mind, the thin fingers holding a knife, the peel unwinding in one continuous spiral. When he was a child, he and his father used to bet on whether she could finish without breaking it. His father cheated, touching the peel with his fingernail. Ilona pretended not to notice.
— Don’t eat any more until I get there, Ethan said.
— Ethan, they aren’t poisoned. They’re just bad.
— I’m coming now.
— You sound like your father when he thought he had discovered something.
— I haven’t discovered anything.
— That is what he used to say before he lost money.
The connection cut out for a second. In that gap, Ethan heard a sound unlike interference. A murmur of several voices, very far away, then one clear syllable: “seven.”
— Mom?
— I’m here.
— Did you say something?
— That the apples are bad.
— Before that.
— No.
Ethan called a taxi.
The medical center was twenty-five minutes away. On the way, he tried to find an explanation that did not sound ridiculous. Perhaps Elina worked for the bank or the law firm. Perhaps they had tried to contact him, failed, and found his emergency contact. Visiting his mother would have been inappropriate, but rich people received inappropriate services packaged as care. Perhaps the name had been different. Alina. Hanna. Ilona sometimes confused names.
The taxi took less than fifteen minutes. Every traffic light was green.
— You were lucky, the driver said as he stopped.
Ethan paid him and went inside.
Mrs. Hart, the coordinator, was at reception. She checked the visitors’ register.
— No one visited your mother today, she said.
— She told me a woman came.
— Perhaps one of the therapists.
— Named Elina.
— There is no Elina on duty.
— What about the apples?
Mrs. Hart led him to Ilona’s room. His mother was asleep in the armchair, a blanket across her knees. On the small table, in a white bowl, lay six red apples.
Beside them, the peel from one had been arranged in a perfect spiral.
Ethan went closer. The apples had no labels. They were almost identical, glossy, unblemished. He picked one up and smelled it. Nothing.
Ilona opened her eyes.
— You came quickly.
— Who brought them?
— I told you.
— What did she look like?
— Beautiful.
— Hair? Eyes? Age?
— Why are you asking like the police?
— Because no one signed her in.
Ilona looked at the apples, then at him.
— Maybe she didn’t come for me.
— What does that mean?
— Maybe she only wanted to see whether you would come.
Ethan felt the room shrink slightly. Not physically. Like a photograph whose perspective had been corrected badly.
— Did she leave you anything? A note?
Ilona shrugged.
— She asked what you would take with you if the world stopped.
— What did you tell her?
— That the world had stopped before. People call it something else afterward.
— Mom.
— I told her I would take you, if you weren’t so busy saving yourself.
It was the sort of sentence Ilona could say. It was also the sort of sentence someone could have written for her.
Ethan sat on the edge of the bed. He took her hand. Her skin was cold and thin.
— How do you feel?
— Like yesterday, but with more apples.
— I’m going to ask for the security recordings.
— Ask. Perhaps you’ll also find out where the nights go when I no longer remember them.
Mrs. Hart brought him a tablet with the entrance footage. Ethan reviewed the period between eleven and one. Visitors, staff, a courier, a woman with a therapy dog. No Elina. At 11:47, the camera jumped from one frame to another, with two minutes and thirteen seconds missing.
— Does this happen? Ethan asked.
— Sometimes we lose footage when the system updates.
— Exactly two minutes and thirteen seconds?
— I don’t know what “exactly” means in this case.
— I want the original file.
— We have privacy rules. — My mother received an unregistered visitor. — Or she confused one person with another. Ethan looked at the apples. Mrs. Hart looked at them too. — Perhaps someone on staff brought them, she said. — Then someone on staff knows. — I’ll ask. Ilona had dozed off again. Ethan adjusted the blanket around her. Before leaving, he took one of the apples and slipped it into his coat pocket. At the exit, his phone displayed a new notification from the banking app.
TEST TRANSFER COMPLETED.
Amount: CHF 10.00. It was Tuesday, October 13. Ethan opened the details. The transfer had been credited to his current account. The balance was ten francs higher. He called the bank. The call ended before the first ring. He tried again. The screen went black for a second. In the reflection, he saw his face and, behind him, the corridor of the medical center. At the far end stood the man from the platform. The same dark coat. The same arm held oddly beside his body. The same pale face. This time he was standing. Ethan turned around. The corridor was empty. When he looked at the phone again, the banking app had closed. Only the time remained on the screen.
7:12.
Ethan blinked. The time returned to 16:38. In his coat pocket, the red apple was warm.
A SMALL ERROR IN YOUR FAVOR
Ethan slept for two hours and dreamed that every window in his apartment looked into the same room.
There was nothing in the room except a metal chair and a white light that cast no shadows. Each time he approached a window, the chair was a little closer. At the last one, someone sat on it with his back turned. Ethan raised his hand to knock on the glass, and the man turned his head before Ethan’s fingers touched it.
It was him, but older. Or perhaps merely tired in a way Ethan had not yet learned.
He woke at 4:19 with the red apple on the bedside table.
He did not remember bringing it into the bedroom. He had left his coat in the hall, and the last thing he had done before lying down was place the apple in a bowl beside the sink. Now it sat twenty centimeters from his face, gleaming in the dark.
Ethan switched on the lamp. A thin scratch crossed the peel, like a numeral. He turned it toward the light. It was not a numeral, only a mark probably made by his keys.
He carried it to the kitchen, set it on the cutting board, and cut it in half.
The apple had seeds, flesh, a brown patch beside the core. Nothing perfect. He smelled one half and, for the first time, caught its scent. It was not the neutral smell of fruit bred to survive transport. It smelled like the apples from his grandparents’ orchard, before the orchard had been sold and cleared for a warehouse. Cold earth, wet wood, bitter peel.
He took a bite.
The taste struck him with unpleasant precision. He saw the kitchen table of his childhood, the oilcloth patterned with blue flowers, his mother’s hand drawing the peel into a spiral, and his father saying there was a system for predicting rain from the number of seeds. Ethan had been eight and had believed him. It had rained. His father had won the bet and taken his chocolate coin.
Ethan spat the piece into the sink.
Not because it tasted bad. Because it tasted exact.
He put the halves in a bag and sealed them in the freezer. Then he photographed the apple, the transfer dated in the future, and every relevant page of the file. He sent copies to a storage account he had not used in years and another encrypted copy to an address configured to forward them automatically if he failed to confirm access each week.
It was absurd. An incorrect sum in an account did not justify a dead-man protocol. Nor did an apple justify frozen evidence. But preparation calmed him. Fear became manageable when it could be divided into files.
At 6:30 he went for a run. He did not usually run on Wednesdays, but there was no longer a nine o’clock meeting. The streets were almost empty. The cold air cut into his lungs. He passed yesterday’s café. The waitress was not there. A man sat at the back table behind an open newspaper, but when Ethan slowed, he lowered the pages and proved much younger than the man on the platform.
Ethan continued to Stadelhofen.
The platform had been washed clean by the night’s rain. People hurried toward trains, avoiding one another with the competence of those who had rehearsed the same choreography for years. There was no police tape, stain, candle, or sign that anyone had died there twenty-three hours earlier.
He asked at the information desk.
— A medical incident yesterday morning, around seven ten. Do you know whether the man survived?
The employee searched the system.
— Nothing appears here.
— He collapsed on platform two. Did an ambulance come?
— We have no report.
— There were dozens of witnesses.
The woman shrugged.
— Perhaps he left on his own.
— He did not look capable of it.
— Then someone helped him.
Ethan looked at the place where he had seen him. A boy in an orange jacket ran past. For a second, Ethan mistook him for the young man who had knelt down. The boy boarded a train without looking at him.
At 8:03, his phone vibrated.
TEST TRANSFER COMPLETED.
The date was now correct.
Ethan opened the history. The ten-franc transaction appeared only once, executed Wednesday at 8:03. The screenshot taken the previous evening was still in the archive and showed the same transaction dated Wednesday. Had he not remembered the moment, he might have believed he had misread it.
He checked the image metadata. It had been created Tuesday at 16:39.
He sat on a bench and watched people enter the trains. One part of him, the part he had used for years to find errors in models, began constructing hypotheses. An interface defect: the app had displayed the scheduled date rather than the current one. An automated test prepared for the following day. A time-zone problem, although the discrepancy was one day, not one hour. The screenshot proved nothing beyond the fact that an application could display incorrect information.
The other part remembered the phone showing 7:12 and the man in the reflection.
At 9:42, Ethan entered the bank’s Paradeplatz headquarters. The building had cream marble, living plants, and the silence of an institution that had spent heavily to make money seem unlike an emergency. A woman met him before he gave his name.
— Mr. Reed. I am Claudia Bauer. Thank you for coming.
She was around fifty and wore a navy suit without any visible mark of its brand. She shook his hand with precisely the warmth appropriate to thirty-one million: more than an ordinary client, less than a truly rich man.
— Did you speak with me yesterday? Ethan asked.
— Probably a colleague. We work as a team.
— A man named Bauer.
— There is no man by that name in wealth management.
Ethan stopped.
— I wrote down the name.
— Perhaps Mayer? With an a.
— Perhaps.
She led him into a room overlooking the square. Coffee, water, a leather folder, and a thin man with a short gray beard were waiting on the table.
— This is Dr. Samuel Vogel, the independent attorney appointed to clarify the transaction, Bauer said. His fee does not depend on the bank.
— On whom does it depend?
— On the transaction estate, the attorney replied. But it is capped.
— Then it depends a little on the bank and a great deal on my money.
Vogel smiled.
— I was told that you work in risk.
— I did yesterday.
The next two hours were disappointingly coherent. Vogel showed him the original documents, the history of assignments, and the arbitral award recognizing Ethan’s contribution to Nacre. An obscure clause had survived three restructurings. Orison had purchased a portfolio of storage technologies for 1.4 billion. After taxes, fees, and the discharge of certain obligations, Ethan’s share was exactly the figure shown in the app.
— Did you find an error? Ethan asked.
— Several, Vogel said. Fortunately, all in your favor.
— There are no errors in someone’s favor. There are errors that have not yet been charged back.
— You have a very moral view of accounting.
— I have a very accounting-based view of morality.
Vogel turned a sheet toward him. The signature on the original looked identical to the one in the video. The paper had been dated and certified. The ink, the attorney explained, had undergone forensic examination during arbitration.
— Why did the arbitration take place without me?
— It was between the company’s successors. You were a third-party beneficiary. The firm representing you tried to contact you at an old address.
— And no one tried an email?
— The file was administered largely automatically.
— Automation does not explain eleven years of silence.
— Distributed incompetence explains more than conspiracies, Mr. Reed.
It was a sentence Ethan himself had used many times. Hearing it from a stranger made it sound quoted.
Claudia Bauer then presented the account structure, tax obligations, and available services. She spoke about capital preservation, diversification, and reputational risk. On a discreet screen, thirty-one million had become colored slices in a circle.
Ethan watched the figures and waited to feel something proportional. Joy, fear, release. He felt only intense, almost clinical attention. Like the moment a car lost traction and the seconds widened.
— Can I transfer money now? he asked.
— Yes. The temporary limit is seven and a half million until completion of the face-to-face verification. You have just completed it.
— How long does an internal transfer take?
— Instantly.
Ethan opened the app. He entered his mother’s current account, which he had saved for the care center’s expenses. He typed CHF 250,000. He stopped before confirming.
— Does this trigger anything?
— An additional check, perhaps a call. But I am here.
— Not fiscally. In the world.
Bauer looked at him a fraction too long.
— It will change the balance of two accounts.
Ethan confirmed.
The phone requested facial recognition. This time it identified him instantly. The number disappeared from the available balance and appeared in his mother’s account.
No one entered the room to tell him it had been a mistake. The ceiling did not open. The time did not change.
— Congratulations, Bauer said.
— For what?
— Most new clients buy something for themselves first.
Ethan felt a small irritation.
— It was not generosity. I pay for the center anyway.
— Of course.
Her tone said she would allow him to keep that version.
After the meeting, the attorney stopped him in the hall.
— A piece of unpaid advice, Mr. Reed.
— The most expensive kind.
— Do not make major decisions during the first three months.
— What counts as major?
— Any decision that changes the people around you faster than it changes you.
— Money does not change people that quickly.
Vogel straightened his cuff.
— I assure you it can.
In the reception area, a woman stood with her back to him beside a metal sculpture. She wore a dark green dress beneath a pale coat, and her ash-blond hair was pinned up with apparent effortlessness. When Ethan approached, she turned.
Her beauty was not delicate. It was organized. Her eyes, mouth, height, and the way she held her shoulders seemed chosen so as to leave the observer no neutral place to rest.
— Mr. Reed, she said. Elina Rask.
Ethan did not take her hand.
— Were you at my mother’s center yesterday?
A small surprise crossed her face. It could have been performed. It could have been real.
— I did not know you had a mother in Zurich.
— Then how do you know my name?
— Orison. I coordinated beneficiary relations for Nacre.
— Are you an attorney?
— Do I look like an attorney?
— Attorneys look like the people they bill.
Elina smiled.
— Integration strategy. When we buy a technology, we need to understand the people who built it.
— I did not build the technology you bought.
— You built something without which it would not exist.
— A deduplication algorithm from 2015.
— Foundations do not always resemble buildings.
Around them, clients and bank employees moved without seeming to notice her. Ethan noticed precisely because it was impossible not to. A man turned to look after her only as she walked through the door.
— Did you send apples to the care center? Ethan asked.
— No.
— Someone using your name did.
— Then I hope they were good.
— They were not.
— The beautiful ones rarely are.
Ethan felt a brief hollowing in his stomach.
His mother’s exact words. It could be an expression. It was not one he had ever heard before.
— Why are you here?
— To invite you to lunch. The company would like you to remain involved with the project.
— For thirty-one million, I would assume you bought the right never to see me again.
— We bought code. People remain the unbilled complication.
— I am not interested.
— In the project or in lunch?
— In complications.
Elina studied him with a curiosity he disliked because he liked it.
— You have just received the freedom to refuse anything. It would be a pity to use it only to continue yesterday’s life.
She held out a business card. It bore nothing but her name, a number, and a symbol made of seven concentric lines.
— I did not give you my number, Ethan said.
— I did not ask you to.
— That is not what I said.
— I know.
Elina left.
Ethan turned over the card. On the back, someone had written by hand: “What would you buy if no one ever found out?”
He put the card into his pocket beside the two-franc coin.
At noon, his account was still there. At two, the attorney had sent legal confirmation. At three, the tax authority acknowledged receipt of the notice. Reality was doing its bureaucratic work of turning the impossible into a form.
Ethan went to Helix for the things that remained. The receptionist asked him to wait. Two minutes later, Felix came down.
— You could have told me, he said.
— What?
— About Nacre.
The news had traveled quickly. Or perhaps the bank had informed his employer as part of its verification. Felix shook his hand with an energy that had not existed the day before.
— I did not know.
— Of course.
— I did not know.
Felix examined him, then laughed.
— This is extraordinary. We have to celebrate.
— Yesterday I was supposed to take two weeks to consider whether I suited the environment.
— Yesterday I did not know you had founded half of Orison’s infrastructure.
— I founded nothing.
— It does not matter. The story is good.
Ethan looked through the glass doors toward the hall where he had worked for seven years. People who had rarely greeted him were now waving. Director Lutz stepped out of the elevator and came toward him.
— Ethan. I am glad I caught you.
She did not look glad. She looked prepared to be.
— I have spoken with Olivier, she continued. We believe yesterday morning was handled poorly by both sides.
— What was my side?
— This is not the time to revisit it.
— For me it is.
Lutz inhaled.
— The administrative leave can be canceled. More than that, Olivier would like to discuss your heading an independent validation unit. Reporting directly to the board.
It was almost identical to one of his fantasies. Not a recent one. An old one, repeated on nights when he came home after Felix had presented Ethan’s conclusions as an intuition of his own. In the fantasy, Ethan did not accept immediately. He let them humiliate themselves a little.
Now that the scene had arrived, the lines were weaker than he had imagined.
— Will the Atlas model be launched? he asked.
Lutz looked at Felix.
— The decision is under review.
— It will be launched.
— There are contractual obligations.
— Then we have nothing to discuss.
— You do not have to decide now.
— I was just advised not to make major decisions for three months.
Felix smiled.
— Clever.
— My leaving is no longer a major decision.
Lutz’s expression changed. For the first time, Ethan saw something in it resembling fear. Not fear of losing him as an employee, but of having him outside the building with enough money for his opinion to matter.
— What do you intend to do? she asked.
Ethan did not know. But he answered before deciding:
— What you should have done.
He took his box and left.
On the pavement, the sentence struck him as theatrical. He liked it nonetheless.
Over the next few hours, the world began offering him things without being asked. A real-estate agent he had contacted six months earlier sent him three properties
“before listing.”
A former colleague invited him into a new fund. A restaurant where he had never managed to get a table confirmed a reservation for that evening, although Ethan had not made one. A car company asked when he wanted to test-drive a model he had searched for online the previous night.
The explanation was simple: data circulated. The bank had reclassified him. His commercial profile had changed. Algorithms smelled money faster than people did.
At 17:10 he received a message from Nadia.
“The concierge leaves at six. If you want the books, I’ll be home until then.”
Ethan arrived at 17:42.
Nadia still lived in their old apartment near the university. Her name remained on the buzzer, not his. The door opened before he pressed a second time.
She wore jeans, a gray sweater, and her hair tied back carelessly. She was not a woman whose beauty entered a room before she did. You had to stay, hear her speak, see how she changed when she laughed. Ethan had loved that. Later it had bothered him that it could not be displayed quickly.
— You came, she said.
— You said you were taking the books for recycling.
— It worked.
The box stood beside the door. Ethan recognized the spines: Bayesian statistics, two novels he had pretended he would reread, an atlas of clouds his mother had given him.
Nadia looked at him more closely.
— What happened?
— Why do you assume something happened?
— You are wearing yesterday’s suit, you have not slept, and you arrived in a taxi that costs as much as my rent for a day.
— It does not cost that much.
— You still have the reflex of correcting the unimportant part.
Ethan lifted the box. It was heavier than he expected.
— I was suspended.
— Because of Atlas?
— Because of my tone.
— Your tone sometimes deserves a vacation.
— Thank you.
— Were you right?
— Yes.
— Then I am sure you were unbearable.
Nadia smiled, and for a second their separation became something that had happened to other people.
— There is something else, Ethan said.
— Did you come to tell me before or after collecting the box?
— I do not know.
— Come in. You look as though you have seen a ghost that owes you money.
In the kitchen, Nadia put water on to boil. Ethan noticed that his blue mug was gone and that a new photograph was attached to the refrigerator: Nadia on a mountain trail beside a bearded man and a woman Ethan did not know. The man had a hand on Nadia’s shoulder. The gesture could have been friendly. Ethan analyzed it until it no longer was.
— Who is he? he asked.
Nadia followed his gaze.
— Marc.
— I know a lot of Daniels.
— Then I do not need to explain.
— Is he someone?
— He is a person. We are still checking the rest.
Ethan took out his phone and opened the account.
— I received thirty-one million.
Nadia looked at the screen, then at him.
— Francs?
— Yes.
— Why?
He told her about Nacre, the documents, the meeting. Not about the man on the platform, the apples, or the transfer from the future. As he spoke, the story sounded more normal. A forgotten clause, an acquisition, legal luck. Nadia took the phone and enlarged the figures.
— Is it real?
— As real as a number in an app can be.
— Did you call the bank?
— Three times.
— An attorney?
— An independent one.
— Taxes?
— In progress.
— Did you transfer anything?
— Yes.
Nadia put the phone on the table.
— Then congratulations.
She did not throw herself into his arms. She did not look at him differently. Ethan did not know what he had expected, but he felt a small, shameful disappointment.
— That is all?
— What would you like me to do?
— Nothing.
— I can scream, if that helps.
— It does not.
— I can tell you that you deserved it.
— That would be false.
— Then perhaps there is hope.
The water boiled. Nadia poured the tea.
— What will you do with it?
— I do not know.
— You are lying.
Ethan looked at the photograph on the refrigerator.
— Perhaps I will start something of my own.
— You said that when you had two thousand in the bank.
— Now I can.
— Now you can buy the freedom never to find out whether you could have.
— Do you have a line for every amount?
— You brought the amount here.
They fell silent. Steam rose between them.
— Does Marc know you keep photographs of exes? Ethan asked. Nadia looked at him, then gave a short laugh. — There are no photographs of you on the refrigerator. — That is not what I said. — Exactly. Ethan stood. — I have to go. — Of course. — I have a reservation. — Did you make it before or after becoming rich? — I did not make it. Nadia folded her arms. — That sounds promising. At the door, she stopped him. — Ethan. He turned. — Do not spend the money proving that people underestimated you. It never ends. — That is not what I am doing. — Not yet. He lifted the box and went downstairs. The restaurant had confirmed a table for two. Ethan arrived alone and told the hostess there had been a mistake. — Your guest has already arrived, she replied. Elina sat at a table beside the window. She wore black this time. Two glasses stood before her, one with water and one with wine. When Ethan approached, she did not rise. — You said you were not interested in lunch, she said. I did not ask about dinner. — You made a reservation in my name. — The restaurant made it. I accepted the vacant place. — How did you know? — Orison has good relationships with people who keep tables free for improbable clients. Ethan should have left. Instead, he set the box of books beside his chair and sat down. — You were at my mother’s center.
— You said exactly what she said about the apples.
— What did I say?
— That the beautiful ones are rarely good.
Elina tilted her head.
— It is a commonplace observation.
— Not quite in that form.
— Perhaps your mother and I have known the same apples.
The waiter came. He did not offer them menus.
— The chef has prepared the tasting menu, he said.
— I did not order it, Ethan said.
— It is the preference noted for your table.
Elina looked at him over the rim of her glass.
— The world has already begun guessing what you want.
— The world collects data.
— Is there a difference?
Ethan did not answer.
Dinner was excellent in an unsurprising way. Every course seemed chosen according to preferences he had never articulated: fish without sweet sauces, meat almost raw, a nut dessert with very little sugar. Elina asked about Nacre, his father, and Nadia without naming her. Ethan answered more than he intended because her questions made him sound interesting.
— What would you buy? she asked at the end.
— I did not say I would buy anything.
— Everyone buys. Even those who keep the money buy the image of a man unchanged by it.
— A better apartment.
— How much better?
— Quiet. Light. A view. No neighbors above.
— Is that all?
Ethan thought about the yacht advertisement, Nadia and Marc, the meeting room, Heller drawing squares.
— A place where no one can tell me I have to leave.
Elina smiled, this time without irony.
— That costs more than an apartment.
After dinner, she showed him a property ten minutes away. The top two floors of a renovated building, a terrace, private elevator, views of the lake and the city. The agent was waiting, although it was nearly ten at night.
— The owner wants discretion, the agent said. It is not on the market.
Ethan moved from room to room. Pale wood, stone, high windows. It was too large for one person and exactly the kind of space in which one person could pretend he was not alone but free.
— How much? he asked.
The agent told him.
A day earlier, Ethan would have calculated the sum in years of salary. Now it represented less than one sixth of what had appeared in his account.
— The owner will accept a quick offer ten percent below asking, Elina said.
The agent looked at her in surprise.
— We have not discussed—
— Ask him.
The agent stepped onto the terrace to make a call.
Ethan approached a wall entirely covered in smoked mirror. Elina stood several meters behind him. In the reflection, the apartment already appeared inhabited by them.
— Why are you doing this? Ethan asked.
— Because I can.
— That is not an answer.
— It is the answer people give after they no longer need justifications.
— You had never met me before today?
— No.
— And my mother?
— No.
— Then tell me something you do not know about me.
Elina came closer. In the mirror, her movement seemed fluid and Ethan’s rigid.
— I do not know what you will do when all the things you want cease to cost you anything.
The agent returned.
— The owner accepts.
Ethan turned toward him.
— I did not make the offer.
— Ms. Rask said you intended to.
— Ms. Rask does not represent me.
— Of course. My apologies. I can withdraw it.
Ethan looked at the city beyond the glass. In his current apartment, the upstairs neighbor started the washing machine at midnight. The bedroom window faced a wall. The kitchen had two drawers that would not close. Nadia had once said the apartment bothered him because there was no one else he could blame for it.
— Make the offer, he said.
The agent smiled.
— Congratulations.
The preliminary documents reached his phone before he left the building.
Elina refused the taxi.
— I will walk.
— Where do you live?
— In places that do not belong to me.
— That sounds convenient.
— It is.
She came close and kissed his cheek, near the corner of his mouth. The gesture was intimate enough to unsettle him and ambiguous enough that it could not be claimed.
— Good night, Ethan.
He had not invited her to use his first name.
Elina walked away along the pavement. Ethan watched until she disappeared around the corner, then went back into the building. He told the agent he had forgotten a book and rode the private elevator up alone.
The apartment was dark. The city lit the rooms through the windows. Ethan walked to the smoked mirror.
He raised his right hand.
The reflection did the same.
He lowered it.
The reflection lowered it.
He felt ridiculous. Lack of sleep, stress, money. The brain looked for fractures when the story changed too quickly.
Ethan turned toward the door.
In the mirror, his reflection remained facing him for a fraction of a second longer.
Then it turned as well.
CHAPTER 3
NO LOSSES
During the first ten days, Ethan lost only an umbrella.
He left it in a taxi and remembered only after entering the new apartment. When he went down to ask the concierge, the umbrella was already in the lobby, leaning against the desk.
— The driver came back, the concierge said.
— In three minutes?
— Perhaps he found it immediately.
It was a cheap umbrella with one bent rib. Ethan took it, thanked him, and felt an almost comic disappointment. Had the driver kept it, something in the past few days would finally have been normal.
The apartment sale had closed in a period the attorney called “remarkable, but not impossible.” The owner had accepted every condition. The bank had approved the transfer. The notary’s office had found an opening after another appointment was canceled. The furniture shown in the photographs remained included because the owner was moving to Singapore and did not want to store anything.
Ethan had entered with a suitcase, the box of books, and the sense that he was occupying a hotel room in which someone had studied his tastes.
On the second night he placed a camera in front of the smoked mirror. He filmed himself raising his hands, turning suddenly, switching the light off and on. He reviewed the footage frame by frame. The reflection was correct. At normal speed, any delay could have been fatigue; at thirty frames per second, there was nothing.
On the third night he dismantled the camera.
He had told no one about the mirror. Nor about the transfer dated in the future or the man on the platform. He had told Vogel only about the unregistered visit to his mother. The attorney had recommended a formal complaint to the center and a security assessment. When Ethan requested the original recordings, the two missing minutes had been recovered from backup. The corridor was empty throughout them.
The apples had disappeared. Mrs. Hart said Ilona had asked for them to be thrown away because she did not like them. Ilona did not remember Elina.
— Perhaps you dreamed it, Ethan’s mother had told him.
— You told me.
— I say many things. You do not have to believe me when I become interesting.
The one remaining apple half had been sealed in a bag in the freezer of the old apartment. Ethan had forgotten it when he moved. When he returned for it, the freezer was empty. The cleaner denied having seen it.
Evidence did not disappear spectacularly. It turned into things a reasonable person was ashamed to call evidence.
The money, by contrast, remained.
After taxes and the purchase of the apartment, Ethan had more than twenty-four million. The bank proposed a prudent strategy. He accepted it for half and kept the rest liquid, although Claudia Bauer explained the real loss caused by inflation.
— I want to be able to see the money, he said.
— It is numbers no matter where you keep it.
— Some numbers are closer to the door.
Invitations began arriving. He did not know who had informed the world, but the right world had found out. Founders who had never answered his emails called him to lunch. A professor who had rejected one of his papers asked for his opinion on a project. A gallery invited him to a preview. A private club that had ignored his application years earlier sent him a membership card without an initiation fee.
Ethan declined almost everything in the first few days. Refusing gave him a new pleasure. It was not the same as being unwanted. From a distance they looked alike, but only one came with choice.
Elina sent him one message a day, never a simple question.
“Did you sleep in the place you bought, or merely guard the entrance?”
“The first person who flatters you after the money is lying. The second may simply have noticed the rules.”
“How much would you keep if you knew prices would no longer exist tomorrow?”
Ethan did not answer. He read each message twice.
Nadia did not write at all.
On the eleventh day, Felix called him.
— Do you have five minutes?
— It depends what they are worth.
— I deserved that.
— It was not a joke.
Felix fell silent.
— Atlas failed one of the banks’ liquidity tests, he said. Your secondary scenario exactly. — It should never have reached testing. It was in the report. — The final report did not include the appendix. — I know. — How do you know? — Because you know me well enough to call only when you need something you deleted. — I need the original model. — It is on the Helix servers. — Your local version. With the contagion simulator. — IT collected the laptop. — Ethan. Something appeared in Felix’s voice that Ethan had never heard before: not humiliation, not yet, but the space in which it might grow. — The contract is suspended unless we present a fix within forty-eight hours, Felix continued. Olivier has put it all on my team. — Then your team will work. — We do not have the code. — You have the report. — The technical appendix was removed. — By you. — At Legal’s request. — Then ask Legal to write code. Felix swore softly. Ethan went to the windows. The lake was flat and metallic. A small boat left a white wake that seemed drawn with a ruler. — What do you want? Felix asked. — Nothing. — Everyone wants something. — Yesterday I wanted you to listen to my conclusion. — And today? Ethan thought about his desk emptied into a paper bag, Felix’s hand on the table, Lutz using his first name. — Today I want to see what you do without it.
He ended the call.
At noon, the share price of a listed company that provided liquidity protection to the cantonal banks dropped sharply. Ethan had followed it for years. There was no public explanation for the decline, but if Atlas had failed its test, one of the banks might be reducing exposure. The market interpreted the move as negative information about the provider, although demand for its services was in fact about to increase.
Ethan bought options.
Not many. Two hundred thousand francs, an amount that would have seemed obscene two weeks earlier and now seemed a footnote. He built the position in three tranches and closed the app.
Two hours later, the bank announced it was abandoning Atlas and expanding its contract with the liquidity provider. The shares rose twenty-seven percent. Ethan’s options were worth a little over a million.
He looked at the profit.
It was not luck, he told himself. It was analysis. He had understood the system, possessed contextual information, assumed risk. The fact that the information came from the call of a desperate man did not change the mathematics.
Felix called again. Ethan did not answer.
That evening, he accepted a fund’s invitation to a private poker salon. He said he was going out of curiosity. In truth, he wanted to lose.
The salon occupied the top floor of a hotel beside the lake. There were no signs at the entrance. An employee took his coat and led him into a room of dark wood, low light, and six people who already knew who he was: three fund managers, an heir to a pharmaceutical company, a woman who had sold an advertising platform, and a former Formula E driver.
— The Nacre man, the heir said. You held the shares for eleven years. Discipline.
— I forgot them.
They all laughed. Honesty was amusing when it had cost thirty million.
Ethan was not a good player. He understood probabilities, which in poker meant less than people who understood probabilities imagined. He could not read hesitation, had no patience for theater, and grew rigid when he lied.
In the first hour, he lost twenty thousand.
He felt disproportionate relief.
In the second, he received exactly the card he needed on the river three hands in a row. The first had been plausible. The second, rare. The third silenced the table.
— Nacre, the driver said, do not tell me you forgot the cards in the deck as well.
On the next hand Ethan pushed his chips toward the center with a low pair. The woman from advertising followed. The board completed four of a kind for him.
— That is not possible, she said.
— It is, Ethan replied. It just happened.
— You know what I mean.
Yes. He did.
By the end of the evening he was up one hundred eighty-six thousand. Before leaving, he donated the sum to the hotel’s foundation. The gesture restored some of his control, although the foundation worked in children’s education and he had not checked whether it was effective.
In the elevator, the heir clapped him on the shoulder.
— When luck comes, do not argue with it.
— Luck has no arguments.
— That is why it wins.
Ethan arrived home after midnight. He placed the two-franc coin on the kitchen table and took a one-franc coin, a fifty-centime piece, and a commemorative coin from a drawer.
He tossed the one-franc coin a hundred times, recording the results.
Fifty-three heads. Forty-seven tails.
Normal.
He repeated the exercise with the fifty-centime coin.
Forty-eight to fifty-two.
Normal.
He picked up the two-franc coin from Helix.
The first seven throws came up heads. The eighth, tails. The ninth, heads. After a hundred throws, the ratio was sixty-one to thirty-nine. Improbable, but not extraordinary. A deviation millions of people produced every day without the universe changing.
Ethan wrote a small program that generated random bits from the noise in the phone camera. One million results: an almost perfect distribution.
He then wrote a rule before each series.
“I win if there are more tails.”
Result: fifty-one percent tails.
“I win if there are more heads.”
Fifty-two percent heads. The differences were too small for conclusions, but the direction disturbed him.
He tried a more precise rule.
“The next ten tosses will all be heads.”
He tossed the coin.
Heads.
The second: heads.
The third: heads.
At the fourth, he stopped.
For the first time, not continuing felt like a form of pure fear. He was not afraid the prediction would fail. He was afraid it would not.
He put the coin in a glass and poured water over it, as though that changed anything.
The next morning, the options profit had risen to 1.4 million. The provider had announced an additional contract Ethan had not anticipated. Another investment, made through the bank’s prudent strategy, had gained overnight after a takeover bid. The car company delivered a vehicle for testing in a color that had not been available on its site, precisely the shade he had admired years earlier in a photograph.
— I did not order it, he told the representative.
— It was entered as a preference.
— By whom?
— Our profiling system.
— The system knows what colors I liked eight years ago?
The representative smiled professionally.
— Systems sometimes know before we do.
Ethan refused the car.
An hour later, he received a message: the model’s price had been reduced by thirty percent in a private campaign. He refused again. Another message offered free leasing for the first year.
It was as though he were negotiating with a salesman who could not bear to hear no.
At noon, Elina appeared at the apartment door with two bags of food and without having announced herself to the concierge.
— How did you get up here?
— The elevator.
— You need access.
— The concierge recognized me.
— He has never seen you.
— People recognize me easily.
Ethan did not invite her in, but he stepped aside. Elina entered and surveyed the rooms as though they were the result of an experiment.
— You have changed nothing. — It was furnished. — Then you do not live here. You have taken over the life of someone who left. She put the bags on the table. Thai food from a place Ethan had frequented before it became too popular. The exact order he usually chose. — Are you doing this? he asked. — Bringing lunch? — Reservations, preferences, the car, the invitations. — I know nothing about the car. — Orison? — Orison does not need to buy your affection. It has already bought what it wanted. — Then why are you here? Elina opened the containers. — Because I like the way you try to turn luck into an interrogation. Ethan took the coin from the glass. The water had left a round mark on the table. — Choose, he said. Heads or tails. — Edge. — Seriously. — The edge is serious. Merely rare. — Choose. — Heads. Ethan tossed the coin. It landed heads up. — Again. Heads. The third: heads. Elina took the coin. — What are you proving? — Toss it. She tossed it. Tails. Ethan watched her. — Once more. Elina covered the coin with her palm. — Say what you want it to be.
— It does not matter. — That is precisely what matters. Ethan hesitated. — Heads. Elina lifted her palm. Heads. — You turned it over. — No. — You held it under your hand. — Then you do it. Ethan tossed the coin and covered it. He felt the metal strike the table, spin, and stop beneath his skin. — What do you want? Elina asked. — Tails. He lifted his hand. Tails. Elina smiled. — See? — One result means nothing. — For a man who has just won thirty million, that is a courageous belief. Ethan pushed the coin aside. — You were at my mother’s center. — Not yet. The words fell between them. — What does “not yet” mean? Elina seemed to realize what she had said. — It means I may meet her. — Why? — Because the people we desire come with people we did not choose. — Do you desire me? The question came out more brutally than he intended. Elina did not flinch. — I do not yet know whether I desire you or merely like that you need to find out. She came closer and kissed him.
Not on the cheek this time. The kiss was slow and certain, without the hesitation through which two people negotiate first contact. Elina seemed to know exactly when Ethan would pull his head back, how much pressure he liked, where he would place his hand. He caught her by the waist. Her body responded like a memory.
Ethan was the first to pull away.
— You have done that with me before.
Elina looked at him, breathing almost normally.
— I would have remembered.
— The way you— He stopped. He could not explain without sounding ridiculous. Nadia had kissed him like that at the beginning. Not identically—no one could measure the identity of a kiss—but with the same brief pressure of the fingers at the base of his skull, just before withdrawing. A gesture Ethan had associated with desire for years.
— The way I what? Elina asked.
— Nothing.
— Then do not turn it into something.
They ate. Elina spoke about an event she wanted to take him to the following evening, a meeting of investors and researchers. Ethan declined. She did not insist. When she left, she kissed him again, differently this time.
After the door closed, Ethan sent Nadia a message.
“Are you free tonight?”
The reply came twenty minutes later.
“No.”
He wrote:
“With Marc?”
Deleted it.
He wrote:
“I wanted your opinion.”
Nadia replied:
“About the money or the blond woman?”
Ethan reread the message.
“How do you know?”
Three dots appeared, vanished, and appeared again.
“There is a photograph of you with her in the paper.”
She attached a link. A financial article about the Nacre acquisition had been published an hour earlier. The photograph showed Ethan leaving the bank with Elina beside him. The headline: THE FORGOTTEN PROGRAMMER WHO MADE THIRTY MILLION OVERNIGHT.
The article described Ethan as
“the discreet architect of the infrastructure that made the acquisition possible.”
No journalist had contacted him. It cited sources close to him who said he intended to launch a fund for transformative technologies.
He intended nothing of the kind.
At the end was a sentence:
“According to people present, Reed is said to have remarked that there are no losses, only costs we do not yet understand.”
Ethan had never said that.
But it sounded like him.
Over the next few hours, six publications republished the article. His nearly inactive social accounts gained thousands of followers. An old clip of him explaining systemic risk was circulated as evidence of an ignored genius. The comments said he had been a visionary, that the system had held him down, that his fortune proved merit eventually found its way.
Ethan knew the story was false. He also knew he liked it.
That evening, he accepted Elina’s invitation.
The event took place in a villa above the lake. Researchers, investors, artists, politicians. People asked his opinion on subjects he had never studied. When he said he did not know, they interpreted caution as depth. When he made a weak joke, they laughed. When he remained silent, they moved closer.
Elina disappeared and reappeared beside him just before he began to feel alone. She wore a simple white dress. Three men tried to attract her attention; she treated them politely and turned back to Ethan. Their desire made her more desirable, and her choice made him visible.
At 22:16 Nadia sent:
“I hope the matter of the opinion has been resolved.”
Ethan did not reply.
After midnight, an organizer told him his car was waiting. Ethan had not ordered one, but he had been drinking and accepted.
The driver opened the door of a black sedan. He was about sixty, with thinning hair and a white scar on his chin. Ethan recognized him before he saw his whole face.
The man from the platform.
Ethan remained beside the car.
— Have we met?
The driver looked at him calmly.
— I do not believe so, Mr. Reed.
His voice was different from the one Ethan had imagined for the fallen man. Lower, with an accent he could not place.
— You were at Stadelhofen on Tuesday morning.
— I drive all over the city.
— You collapsed on the platform.
The man touched his scar, perhaps without realizing it.
— I have not fallen in a long time.
Ethan stepped closer.
— What is your name?
— Elias.
— Your surname?
— You do not need it for the journey.
Elina appeared behind Ethan.
— Is there a problem?
— Do you know him?
— He is the villa’s driver.
— Have you seen him before?
— No.
Elias held the door open. Inside, the light was dim and warm.
— I can call another car, Elina said.
Ethan should have refused. Instead, he got in. Elina sat beside him.
On the way, Ethan watched Elias in the rearview mirror. The scar appeared each time they passed beneath a streetlamp. The man drove without haste. Every traffic light turned green before they reached it.
— Have you worked for the villa long? Ethan asked.
— Long enough.
— How long is long enough?
— Long enough to take you home.
Elina touched Ethan’s knee.
— Let the man drive.
— You have never seen him, yet you trust him.
— I trust that the road wants us to arrive somewhere.
Ethan looked at her.
— Do you always talk like that?
— Only when you listen.
The car stopped in front of the building. Elina got out first and went inside, although Ethan had not explicitly invited her. Before he closed the door, Elias turned toward him.
— Mr. Reed.
— Yes?
— Have you decided what you will keep?
Ethan felt the noise of the street recede.
— What do you mean?
Elias looked toward Elina, who was waiting in the lobby.
— When you can no longer keep everything.
The door drew itself shut with the mechanism.
The car pulled away before Ethan could photograph the plate.
In the lobby, the concierge greeted them.
— Who sent the driver? Ethan asked.
— What driver?
— The car that just left.
The concierge looked through the glass.
— I did not see a car stop, Mr. Reed.
Elina laughed softly.
— He was on the phone when we came in.
The concierge lifted the landline receiver from his desk.
— No, ma’am. I was waiting for you.
Ethan looked at her.
— He was waiting for you?
— I called earlier.
— You said you did not know whether you were coming.
— I decided.
The elevator opened. In the mirror inside, Ethan saw himself between Elina and the concierge who remained in the lobby. For a moment, before the doors closed, he had the impression that the concierge was not looking at them but at their reflection.
Upstairs, Elina took off her shoes and walked toward the windows as though she had lived there.
— Are you afraid? she asked.
— Of a driver?
— Of the world beginning to agree with you.
— The world does not agree. People have learned that I have money.
— That is the most widespread form of agreement.
Ethan took out his phone and searched for the villa’s transport company. He called. The organizer answered after three rings and checked the list.
— Your car was canceled, he said. The system shows that you left on foot.
— With someone named Elias?
— We have no Elias on the team.
Ethan ended the call.
Elina watched him from the other side of the room.
— What do you want the result to be? she asked.
— The result of what?
— All of this.
Ethan thought of the man on the platform, the thirty-one million, the coin beneath his palm, Nadia looking at the number without loving him again, the eyes upon him at the villa.
The truth was that he did not want an explanation. Not yet. An explanation might take the things away.
— I want not to lose, he said.
Elina came toward him.
— Then stop counting what it costs.
CHAPTER 4
THREE WOMEN, ONE VERSION
Elina stayed three nights and brought nothing with her.
On the first morning, Ethan found a still-sealed toothbrush in the bathroom, the same brand she used. He had not bought it. Elina said the concierge kept items for guests.
— The concierge did not know you would be a guest.
— Did you know?
— And yet I was.
The toothbrush seemed a smaller problem than the answer. Ethan called reception. The concierge confirmed that, before the move, the apartment had been stocked with
“a complete guest set.”
The cupboard Ethan had not opened contained robes, slippers, toothbrushes, and toiletries for both sexes.
The explanation was correct. Almost every explanation was correct once he went looking for it.
Elina asked nothing of him. She did not want money, gifts, promises, or exclusivity. She liked the food he ordered, the films he chose, the low temperature in the bedroom. When Ethan worked, she read. When he wanted to talk, she put down the book before he asked. When he did not, she asked no questions.
It was, he told himself, what happened when two compatible adults did not turn every gesture into a negotiation.
It was also what he had imagined for years love would be if women did not need things at inconvenient moments.
On the fourth morning, Elina left before he woke. On the kitchen table she had left a business card bearing seven concentric lines and a message:
“Today you will meet someone who does not believe you. Try not to fall in love immediately.”
At 8:03, Orison called him.
A hurried-sounding man explained that the Zurich research team was examining an old component from the Nacre portfolio and needed clarification regarding a function Ethan had written. The meeting had been scheduled for eleven.
— I did not agree to work for Orison, Ethan said.
— It is a one-time consultation. The proposed fee is in the document.
— I do not need a fee.
— Then we can donate it.
— That is not what I said.
— Dr. Idris insisted that we contact you directly. She said that if you did not come, she would publish that your algorithm produces impossible results.
— She said
“impossible”?
