— I am quoting her.
The Orison laboratory occupied a former underground switching station near Hardbrücke. There was no company name above the entrance, only a black plaque and the seven-line symbol. The guard scanned Ethan’s retina and asked him to leave his phone in a shielded drawer.
— Why seven? Ethan asked, indicating the symbol.
— I do not know.
— You work here.
— I open the door.
An elevator descended without displaying floors. Ethan counted approximately nine seconds. When the doors opened, the air was colder and smelled of metal, new plastic, and ozone.
Dr. Salma Idris was waiting in a room full of equipment. Her black hair was tied back, she wore thin-framed glasses, and something gray stained the cuff of her white shirt. She did not look up at once. She was writing on a tablet with a stylus and speaking to a technician.
— If you compensated for the drift after measurement, you did not compensate for it, she said. You disguised it.
Ethan smiled involuntarily.
Salma looked up.
— What is amusing?
— Nothing. I had the same discussion yesterday about another kind of model.
— And did you win?
— I became rich.
— That is not what I asked.
The technician left. Salma held out her hand.
— Salma Idris. I do not care what your stake is worth, and I do not believe you built what the press says you built.
— Elina warned me.
— Who?
— Elina Rask. Integration strategy.
Salma looked at him without recognition.
— There is no Elina Rask in our research organization chart.
— I did not say she worked in research.
— Our organization chart includes the people who enter this building.
— Perhaps she works in another division.
— Perhaps.
Her tone told him she would waste no time on it.
On the central table was an assembly of boards, optical fibers, and black cylinders mounted in a frame. It did not look spectacular. Ethan had expected something cleaner from a company that built data centers for governments.
— What does it do? he asked.
— It answers before we ask.
— Then ask faster.
Salma did not smile.
— Your deduplication function used sequence prediction to avoid retransmitting probably redundant blocks. One of Nacre’s successors moved the predictor into the physical controller. Orison combined it with an optical substrate. At certain temperatures, the controller writes a block’s hash between eight and forty milliseconds before the block enters the system.
— Hidden cache.
— We isolated every source.
— Desynchronized clocks.
— Four independent standards.
— Electromagnetic leakage.
— Faraday room, random optical input, external thermal generator.
— Then you are measuring it incorrectly.
— I hope so.
Salma showed him the graphs. Ethan spent twenty minutes looking for the obvious error. It was not there. Another forty finding the less obvious one. That was not there either. The signal appeared before the input, correlated with future data well enough to exclude noise.
— How stable is it? he asked.
— Three months. It improves when we put a human observer in the loop.
— What do you mean by observer?
— Someone who knows what outcome would be useful.
Ethan looked up.
— Selection bias.
— I blinded the experiment.
— Then the experimenter knows.
— I automated the selection.
— Who wrote the automation?
— I did.
— You knew.
Salma pressed her lips together, not offended but interested.
— Exactly. That is why I called you. The original code has a branch no one understands. The variable is called orchard.
Ethan felt the back of his neck tighten.
— In English?
— `orchard`.
A fragment of old code appeared on the screen. Ethan recognized the style, the comments, the mistakes. The `orchard` variable gathered a value from an area of memory that had not been initialized in the preserved version. Yet the function used it to determine the order of blocks.
— That should produce errors, he said.
— In simulation, it does. On hardware, it produces the prediction.
— I did not write it that way.
— The history shows your name.
— The history can be falsified.
— By whom? In 2015, to make you rich eleven years later and ruin my sleep?
— I do not know.
Salma enlarged a comment above the function.
`keep what cannot be reconstructed` Keep what cannot be reconstructed.
Ethan leaned closer to the screen.
— I did not write comments like that.
— You wrote “seperated” with an e.
— That I did.
— Then you had a poetic day.
— Can I see the commit?
Salma opened the archive. Date: October 13, eleven years earlier. Time: 7:12.
— Where were you working then? she asked.
— In a space in Winterthur.
— The metadata contains a reference to “room7.”
— We did not have numbered rooms.
— Perhaps it was a server.
— We named them after rivers.
— Have you ever been in a room seven?
The question brought the dream to mind. The metal chair. The light without shadows.
— Everyone has been in a numbered room.
— That is not what I asked.
— I do not remember.
Salma looked at him longer than necessary.
— Your pupils are dilated.
— The laboratory is dark.
— Not dark enough.
Ethan took a step back.
— What do you want from me?
— Tell me what you thought you were doing when you wrote the function.
— I do not remember writing it.
— Then remember.
— It does not work that way.
— The press says you are a visionary. You should be able to see backward as well.
This time she smiled. It was the first sign that she was deliberately provoking him.
They spent the rest of the day retracing the code. Ethan forgot to eat. Salma did not flatter him and did not tolerate his vague formulations. Three times she proved his assumptions wrong. On the fourth, he found a correlation her team had missed: the temporal lead increased not with the complexity of the data, but with the value the observer assigned to the prediction.
When the stakes were described as zero, the signal preceded the input by eight milliseconds. When a correct result prevented the deletion of a unique file, it reached thirty-one. When Salma had told participants that the result could prevent a medical device from shutting down, it had reached forty.
— It does not predict the data, Ethan said. It predicts importance.
— Importance is not a physical quantity.
— It is to a system that optimizes actions.
— Then where does it measure it?
Ethan looked at the assembly of boards.
— From us.
Salma looked at him. Instead of dismissing the idea, she moved to the console.
— We will run a test.
She introduced two statistically identical streams. One controlled the temperature of an incubator containing biological samples. The other switched on a lamp in a closed box. Neither Ethan nor Salma knew which was which; the technician assigned the labels and left.
— Choose the important stream, she said. — We do not know. — Choose. Ethan put his hand on one of the consoles. He had no basis. He remembered the coin, Elina’s demand, the sensation of metal beneath his palm. — Right. The signal appeared twenty-nine milliseconds earlier on the right-hand stream. The technician returned and opened the key. The right-hand stream controlled the incubator. Salma remained motionless. — Did you know? — No. — Did you see the assignment? — No. — Who told you? — No one. — Do not tell me you were lucky. Ethan thought about the three heads and the fourth toss he had not made. — I was lucky. Salma shut down the system. — We touch nothing else until tomorrow. — Why? — Because people who get the result they want become stupid very quickly. — And those who do not? — They become religious. On the way out, Salma asked for his personal number. It was already in the file, but she made him dictate it. — I want to know I am not speaking to an Orison-approved version, she said. — You think they fabricated the code? — I think a company that buys submarine cables does not pay thirty-one million for deduplication out of sentimentality. — Then what did they buy? — Perhaps you. — I signed nothing.
— You signed eleven years ago.
Ethan thought of the video: If this is ever worth anything, I am buying myself time. All of it.
— Do you know a woman named Elina Rask? he asked again.
Salma removed her glasses and cleaned them with the edge of her shirt.
— Why does it matter?
— She works for Orison and knows things she should not know.
— Like every good employee.
— She went to see my mother before meeting me.
— Your mother told you that?
— Yes.
— Is your mother well?
The question held no irony. Ethan felt the impulse to defend himself.
— She has a neurological condition.
— And have you slept?
— Enough.
— How much?
— It is not relevant.
— It becomes relevant when you tell me unknown women appear in rooms and the code reads the future.
— You measured the code.
— Exactly. One impossibility is enough for me.
Salma touched his arm, briefly, almost professionally.
— Sleep. Tomorrow we try to break the machine.
Her touch was nothing like Elina’s. It had not been calculated to produce an effect. That was precisely why it did.
In the entrance hall, he found seven missed calls from Nadia on his phone.
He called her immediately.
— It is your mother, Nadia said. She fell.
Ilona had become dizzy in the bathroom. She had broken nothing, but the center had sent her to the hospital for tests. Nadia remained the secondary contact in the old file and had answered before he did.
Ethan arrived in twenty minutes. Nadia sat beside the bed holding a glass of water. Ilona was asleep.
— Where were you? Nadia asked.
— In a laboratory. The phone was shielded.
— Of course.
— What does that mean?
— That your life now contains shielded laboratories.
— What did the doctors say?
Nadia explained. Low blood pressure, a possible side effect, several hours of observation. Ethan checked the monitor, the IV, the doctor’s name. He did what he knew: turned fear into questions.
— She is stable, Nadia said. You can sit beside her without auditing the machine.
Ethan sat down.
Ilona opened her eyes and smiled when she saw him.
— You both came.
— How do you feel? Ethan asked.
— Like a woman who fell and was given a more expensive room.
— You do not have to joke.
— Nor do you have to frown. Yet we cling to our habits.
Nadia adjusted her pillow. Ilona caught her hand.
— Do not let him buy too many things, she told her.
— He is no longer my responsibility.
— Responsibility does not ask whether you are still together.
Ethan felt an old warmth, followed immediately by resentment that his mother chose Nadia as his interpreter.
The door opened.
Elina entered carrying a bouquet of white flowers.
Ethan stood so quickly that he struck the chair.
— What are you doing here?
— You told me at lunch where your mother was hospitalized.
— I did not tell you.
— You mentioned the center. The concierge told me the hospital when I asked for you.
Nadia watched her. Elina placed the flowers on the table and approached the bed.
— Mrs. Reed. I am pleased to meet you.
Ilona studied her for a long time.
— We have met before.
The room fell silent. Elina did not blink. — I do not believe so. — You brought me apples. Ethan looked at her. — You said you had not been there. — Because I had not. — Mother, are you sure? Nadia asked gently. — I am not sure of many things. I remember beautiful women so I know whom to fear. Elina smiled, but her face had gone pale. — Perhaps I resemble someone. — We all resemble someone when we do not want to explain, Ilona said. For several seconds, Ethan felt that his mother was more lucid than any of them. Nadia stood. — I am leaving. She needs rest. — You can stay, Ethan said. — You have company now. Elina turned toward her. — I am Elina. — I know. I saw you in the paper. — Nadia, Ethan said. The two women shook hands. The gesture was polite and without warmth. — Ethan talks about you often, Elina said. It was not true. Or he had not spoken to her. Nadia raised her eyebrows. — I hope he kept the version in which I am impossible. — He said you see through him. Ethan looked at Elina. He had said that about Nadia once, years earlier, in a message to Felix after an argument. He had never told Elina. — When did I say that? — At dinner.
— Perhaps you put it differently.
Nadia took her coat.
— Neither of you has to prove anything, she said.
She stopped, as though the sentence had surprised even her, then left.
Elina remained beside the door.
— Are you accusing me of something?
— How do you know what I said about Nadia?
— You told me.
— I did not.
— You had been drinking.
— Not that much.
— Then perhaps I guessed. It is not difficult. Every man says one woman truly sees him when he regrets that another sees him too easily.
Ilona began to laugh in the bed.
— You like her, she told Ethan.
— Mother.
— Not this one. The other one.
— Nadia?
— The one you have not brought yet.
Ethan thought of Salma, although he had never told his mother about her.
— Whom are you talking about?
Ilona closed her eyes.
— The one who does not believe you.
Elina looked at him. For the first time since he had met her, she seemed afraid.
Ilona fell asleep again.
In the corridor, Elina insisted she had not been at the center. Ethan asked for her phone. She handed it to him without protest. Her location history showed that she had been in Geneva on the day of the visit. Photographs, payments, a calendar meeting. Evidence sufficient for an attorney and insufficient for Ethan.
— Perhaps your mother saw my photograph, Elina said.
— The article appeared after the visit.
— Then perhaps she confused me with someone else.
— And the sentence about the apples?
— Coincidence.
— And Nadia?
— What about Nadia?
— You used information I never gave you.
Elina took back her phone.
— You like to believe you are difficult to read. You are not. You are merely well argued.
— Why did you say Ethan talks about her often?
— Because he does. Not in words. In every room he checks whether she would approve of him.
Ethan’s anger came so quickly that it confirmed her line.
— Leave.
Elina looked at him without moving.
— Do you truly want me to?
— Yes.
— Tell me without hoping I will not listen.
— Leave.
Elina left.
Ethan remained at the hospital until after midnight. When Ilona was discharged back to the center, he accompanied her. Nadia did not answer. Elina did not write.
At 1:40, Salma sent him the results of an additional test.
“No human observer: zero effect. When I said aloud that the choice mattered: 12 ms. When I thought about you: 31 ms. Do not interpret that romantically. You are the problem.”
Ethan smiled for the first time in hours.
“Tomorrow at eight,” he replied.
“Seven. Rich people can come early.”
At home, the apartment seemed emptier than it had before Elina. Ethan poured himself a glass of water and opened the recording app on his tablet. He had left it running in the bedroom the previous night. Not to spy on Elina, he had told himself, but to check whether she woke, spoke, or moved during hours he did not remember.
The recording was six hours and thirteen minutes long.
The first two contained whispers, laughter, the sounds of them. Ethan skipped ahead. At 2:08, their breathing became regular. At 2:46, Elina probably rose and went to the bathroom. At 3:12, silence.
At 3:17, her voice could be heard.
— You do not have to prove anything.
It was the same sentence Nadia had spoken in the hospital room. Perhaps a common one. Perhaps Elina had said it in her sleep and Nadia had somehow picked it up without knowing. Ethan rewound.
— You do not have to prove anything.
Five seconds later, another voice answered.
Not Ethan’s. Not Elina’s. It was low, without an accent, and so close to the microphone that it seemed to be inside the tablet.
— Not yet. He still needs to believe he chose.
Ethan froze.
He listened again. The voice existed. The waveform was clear.
After the line, there was a short sound like a click. Then Elina said:
— How much?
The voice answered, but the words were covered by white noise that rose abruptly. Ethan amplified it. Filtered it. Tried to separate the frequencies.
Fragments emerged beneath the noise.
“...not the object...”
“...from before...”
“...seven...”
At 3:18, Ethan himself spoke in his sleep.
— I will keep it.
Then nothing until morning.
Ethan looked at the bedroom door. The bed was unmade. A small hollow remained in Elina’s pillow. In the bathroom, her toothbrush was still in the glass.
The phone vibrated.
A message from Elina.
“Do not listen to what people say when they sleep. Sometimes it is not them.”
Ethan looked up toward the windows. The city was dark, but the smoked mirror reflected the entire room.
In the reflection, someone sat on the sofa.
Ethan turned.
The sofa was empty.
When he looked at the mirror again, no one was there.
THE MACHINE THAT ANSWERED FIRST
The next morning, the voice had disappeared from the recording.
Ethan opened the file at 5:50, after getting no sleep at all. The waveform looked the same. At 3:17, Elina could be heard saying,
“You do not have to prove anything.”
Five seconds of silence followed, then Ethan’s sleep-thickened voice:
“I will keep it.”
No fourth voice. No white noise. No
“how much.”
He checked the automatic cloud copy. Identical. The copy sent to the safety address. Identical. The app history showed that the file had not been modified. Yet Ethan remembered every syllable, including the way the accentless voice had pronounced
“chose,” with an overly exact pause between consonants.
Perhaps he had fallen asleep with the tablet in his hand. Perhaps he had dreamed while listening to a real sentence. Hypnagogia could produce voices, presences, the certainty that one had awakened. He knew the theory. The theory did not slow his pulse.
At 6:20, he downloaded a professional audio-analysis application and opened the raw file. In the spectrogram, exactly in the interval where he remembered the voice, there was a very faint band below twenty hertz. Ordinary speakers could not reproduce it. Perhaps the building’s vibration, the elevator, the ventilation system. Amplified and shifted into the audible range, the band became a broken rumble. For a moment, Ethan thought he could distinguish words. Then he could not.
At 6:47, Salma wrote:
“If you are late, I will assume the fortune changed your time zone.”
Ethan reached the laboratory at 6:58.
Salma looked at him when he entered.
— You did not sleep.
— I have a recording.
— That is what people say before showing me a ghost or a cat.
— It may be both.
He told her. Not everything at first. Only about the voice, Elina’s message, and its disappearance from the file. Salma listened to the copy, examined the metadata and spectrogram. She did not laugh.
— A subsonic band can produce sensations of presence, she said. Some ventilation systems do it accidentally.
— It does not produce sentences.
— The brain produces sentences. Especially when you keep it awake and give it a woman who talks in her sleep.
— Her message came afterward.
— What message?
Ethan showed her the screenshot. Elina: “Do not listen to what people say when they sleep. Sometimes it is not them.”
Salma read it twice.
— She is a dramatic woman.
— She knew I was listening.
— Perhaps you left the app open. Perhaps she saw it. Perhaps she was provoking you.
— She was not in the apartment when I listened.
— She was there before. People can see things before you see them. We do not need new physics for that.
Ethan looked toward the assembly on the central table.
— Do we need it for that?
— Unfortunately.
Salma handed him a coffee. It was black, without sugar, exactly as he drank it. Ethan looked at the cup.
— Did you ask how I take it?
— No. You have the face of a man who considers milk an epistemic weakness.
— Elina would have said the same thing.
— Then Elina is right once.
Salma started the system. During the night, the team had isolated every module and repeated the tests. The effect disappeared when the `orchard` predictor was removed. It returned when the function was restored, even when rewritten in another language. It did not depend on any particular physical defect. It seemed tied to the structure.
— The algorithm creates a selection loop, Ethan said after reading the logs. It does not predict directly. It generates many possible orderings and retains the one that minimizes information loss.
— Every optimizer does that.
— No. This one treats as loss things that have not entered yet.
— Meaning it uses the future in its cost function.
— Meaning the cost function is defined incorrectly.
— Incorrectly does not produce precognition.
— It should not.
Salma opened the original code.
— Look here. `keep what cannot be reconstructed`. What cannot be reconstructed?
— Unique data.
— But the predictor does not know which data are unique until it sees them.
— Exactly.
— Then who knows?
Ethan looked at her.
Salma raised one hand.
— Do not say “we” in a mysterious tone. I am too tired for metaphysics.
— The observer knows what it will hurt to lose.
— Sometimes even the observer does not.
— Perhaps the system measures the reaction before it appears.
— Through what channel?
— I do not know.
— Good. That is the first credible thing said today.
They designed a new experiment. On an external server, a program would randomly select one of a thousand files and delete it permanently. Nine hundred ninety-nine contained noise. One held the only digital copy of a volunteer’s childhood photograph. Neither Ethan, Salma, nor the volunteer knew its index. The predictor had to allocate redundancy before the selection.
— If it saves the photograph, either we have a leak we cannot find or a larger problem, Salma said.
— And if it does not save it?
— We have a sad man and a normal morning.
Salma pressed start. The system began redistributing the blocks. On the screen, one file received thirty-one times more protection than the others.
The external program made its selection.
The file chosen for deletion was the protected one.
The volunteer opened the saved copy. The photograph showed a little girl on a beach holding a green bucket. Behind her, a man waved toward the camera. The volunteer began to cry.
Ethan felt no triumph. He felt as though someone had entered the room without opening the door.
— Repeat it, Salma said.
They repeated the experiment with a legal document, a cryptographic key, a fragment of unpublished music, and an artificially generated photograph that the volunteer was told was the only image of his dead sister. The predictor protected the first three real items. It also protected the false photograph as long as the volunteer believed the story. When he was told the truth, the effect disappeared.
— It does not measure value, Ethan said. It measures belief in value.
— Worse.
— Why?
— Value can be verified. Belief does not have to be true.
Ethan thought of the article that called him a visionary architect. Of the people who had applauded him for a false story. Of how much he had liked it.
— We can use this for binary decisions, he said.
— No.
— If we define loss as an incorrect outcome—
— No.
— Why?
— Because we still do not know what we are paying.
— There is no sign of additional consumption.
— Exactly. In nature, when you receive information without cost, the cost is merely somewhere you have not measured.
Ethan went to the board and began writing. An error-correcting code, two branches, a mechanism forcing the system to protect a bit if the observer considered one of its values important. Salma challenged him. He changed the construction. For an hour, they forgot caution.
At 10:31, they had a simple box on the screen: YES or NO. The question would be entered after the predictor allocated redundancy. If it protected the YES branch, the answer was interpreted as yes. If it protected NO, as no.
— It is a fortune-telling machine, the technician said.
— It is a machine optimizing a badly defined function, Salma replied.
— Ask it whether it will rain tomorrow.
— We have the forecast.
— Ask it something we do not know.
Ethan thought of Elias’s question.
— Is Elina Rask an Orison employee? he said.
Salma looked at him.
— Seriously?
— It can be verified.
He entered the question after the system completed allocation. Answer: NO. — The organization chart is right, Salma said. — Perhaps she is a consultant. — The question was “employee.” Ethan entered another: Was Elina Rask at Ilona Reed’s care center on October 13? The system hesitated longer. The redundancy curve oscillated between branches, then stabilized. Answer: YES. — That proves nothing, Salma said. — Why? It proved the photograph. — No. With the photograph, we verified the result. Here you want the answer to be yes. — I want the truth. — No human being says that without having a preference. Ethan entered: Is Elina Rask a threat to Ethan Reed? The system stopped. An error appeared on the screen: LOSS FUNCTION UNDEFINED. Then, without anyone touching the keyboard, the text changed.
WHAT DO YOU WISH TO KEEP?
The technician laughed nervously. — Is that your interface? — No, Salma said. Ethan felt his hands grow cold. An empty field and a cursor appeared on the screen. — Disconnect it, Salma said. The technician removed the input fiber. The text remained. He cut power to the optical module. The monitors went dark, but one of the black cylinders continued to emit a faint light from within. — A battery? Ethan asked. — It does not have one. The light went out after seven seconds. No one spoke.
At 11:12, the laboratory director came down with three Orison representatives and two attorneys. News of the experiment had traveled through telemetry. Salma tried to stop them. The director demanded a demonstration.
— The system displayed unauthorized text after disconnection, she said. Until we know the source, we do not repeat it.
— That is precisely why we repeat it, the director replied. With witnesses and a recording.
Ethan recognized him from an annual report: Armin Koenig, head of advanced research. A small man with white hair and a pleasant voice.
— Mr. Reed, Koenig said, congratulations. You hid a beautiful surprise in the code.
— I hid nothing.
— Modesty is useful in public, but here it slows us down.
— We do not understand the system.
— That is what research means.
Salma positioned herself between Koenig and the console.
— It also means risk.
— You requested a budget to produce impossible results. Now that you have one, you want to hide it?
— I want to know whether the result comes from the machine or from something using the machine.
Koenig smiled.
— A philosophical distinction.
— A security distinction.
Ethan looked at the company representatives. One wore a seven-line badge. The other was a woman with a tablet and a face Ethan might have seen at the bank. Neither seemed surprised.
— You knew? he asked.
— Knew what? Koenig said.
— That the function produced this.
— We hoped.
— Why did you pay thirty-one million for Nacre?
— We paid 1.4 billion for a portfolio.
— Why that portfolio?
Koenig folded his hands.
— Because the infrastructure of the future will not merely store what happened. It will have to anticipate what people cannot afford to lose.
It was a prepared formulation. Too close to the comment in the code.
— Who is Elina Rask? Ethan asked.
Koenig’s expression did not change.
— I do not know the name.
Ethan looked at the woman with the tablet. She lowered her eyes.
— Then we do not perform the demonstration, he said.
Koenig turned toward him.
— The company owns the equipment and the code.
— I can tell the public what it does.
— And who will believe you?
— This week? Almost everyone.
Ethan had not intended the line as a threat, but it worked. Koenig reassessed him. The money did not give him control over the laboratory. The public story did.
— What do you want? the director asked.
The question had been repeated too often over the past few days. Ethan felt that every answer was already expected.
— Full access to the data. No testing without the consent of Dr. Idris and me. An external audit of the code and hardware. And the identity of everyone who has had access to the Nacre archive.
Salma looked at him in surprise.
— In exchange? Koenig asked.
— We perform a controlled demonstration.
— Today.
— After the audit.
— Today, Koenig repeated. Or we close the laboratory and move the system to a location where the contracts are clearer.
Salma swore.
Ethan understood the game. Orison could take the equipment. He could create a scandal, but without data he would look like a new millionaire claiming to predict the future. Salma would lose her laboratory. Koenig did not need to win the argument, only to control the infrastructure.
— One question, Ethan said. Chosen by us. Every stream recorded. Afterward, the system is shut down.
Koenig agreed.
A legal auditor chose the question and sealed it in a digital envelope: would the central bank cut its policy rate at the next meeting? The decision had not been announced. The market estimated a probability close to one half. The answer could be verified in three days.
Salma started the system. The predictor allocated redundancy to the YES branch before the question was entered. The auditor entered it. On the screen: YES.
— Three days, Salma said. Until then, we know nothing.
Koenig did not seem troubled.
— We need a name.
— We do not need publicity, she replied.
— Everything needs a name before it can be budgeted.
Ethan looked at the `orchard` variable on a side monitor.
— Orchard, he said.
Salma turned her head.
— Why?
— Because that is what it was called before we knew what it did.
Koenig tasted the word.
— Orchard. Good. It sounds productive and harmless.
That afternoon, Orison sent Ethan a seven-figure consulting contract, access to the laboratory, and temporary veto rights over public communications. Attorney Vogel advised him not to sign without negotiations. Ethan signed that evening.
— Why did you call me if you do not listen? Vogel asked.
— So I know which risk I am ignoring.
— Money has made you a more honest man.
— No. Merely harder to stop.
Salma remained at the laboratory. Ethan returned home and found a package on the lobby table. The concierge said a courier had brought it, but there was no entry in the log.
Inside was a black clothbound notebook.
The first pages contained equations. The handwriting was Ethan’s. Not merely similar: the same narrow letters, the same crossed seven, the same tendency to press harder at the end of a line. The equations described an extension of the predictor, a way to convert the temporal difference into mechanical work. Ethan did not fully understand the proof, but he saw its direction: if the system selected future states with lower loss, it could be forced to make that selection against a load. Information became energy.
Page twenty-one contained a drawing of the laboratory assembly, including a modification Salma had made that morning.
Page twenty-two said:
DO NOT GIVE THEM THE ORIGINAL.
Beneath the sentence:
ROOM 7 IS NOT A ROOM.
Ethan photographed every page. This time he used a separate camera with instant film bought from a shop. The photographs developed normally. The writing remained.
He sent Salma only an image of the first equation.
She called immediately.
— Where did you get this?
— I derived it.
— You do not lie well when you are tired.
— Does it matter if it works?
— It matters if someone is directing our steps.
— Come and see the notebook.
— Bring it to the laboratory. We do not test anything else at home.
Ethan dressed again. Before he left, the phone rang. Elina.
He did not answer.
Message:
“Do not take the notebook downstairs.”
Ethan stared at the words. He had told no one the notebook existed except Salma, and Salma did not know its color or location.
He wrote:
“How do you know?”
Elina replied:
“Because you took it every time.”
Ethan called. The number did not exist.
The room became very quiet. The ventilation system stopped. The lights flickered.
Ethan put the notebook in a bag and went downstairs.
The laboratory was almost empty at one in the morning. Salma waited at the entrance. She read Elina’s message without comment, then examined the notebook wearing gloves. The paper was modern, the ink fresh. No fingerprints but Ethan’s on the cover.
— What does “every time” mean? she asked.
— I do not know.
— Have you received the notebook before?
— No.
— Have you built Orchard before?
— I do not remember.
— Have you been here before?
— I was here yesterday.
— Before yesterday.
— No.
Salma opened the notebook to the drawing of her modification.
— This did not exist before nine twenty this morning. Whoever made the notebook knew.
— Perhaps Orison.
— Then Elina works for them.
— Koenig says he does not know her.
— Koenig lies in such a pleasant tone that people thank him.
They decided to test only the first part of the equation, without connecting the load. Ethan rewrote the formula on a board, careful not to copy it blindly. Salma checked every step. At 2:13, they started the module.
The temporal lead curve rose from thirty-one to ninety-seven milliseconds.
— Stop it, Salma said.
Before the night technician could touch the console, the lights went out.
The emergency lighting did not come on. Only the central cylinder emitted a white glow.
A single line appeared on the monitor powered by a separate source:
WHERE IS THE ORIGINAL?
Ethan could not see Salma’s face, only her outline.
— Don’t answer, she said.
— There isn’t even an answer field.
The text changed.
ETHAN.
Then:
ROOM 7.
A metallic sound came from behind them. The storeroom door in the opposite wall opened.
Beyond it was not the storeroom.
Ethan had seen it when he came in: shelves of cables, boxes, two folding ladders. Now a white corridor stretched beyond the threshold, far longer than the building’s geometry allowed. The walls had no seams. Light came from everywhere and cast no shadows.
At the far end stood a metal chair.
Salma seized his arm.
— Don’t go in.
Ethan heard a sound from the corridor. Someone was breathing. Slowly, heavily, as after a run.
— It’s a projection, he said.
— Then let it project a good reason for you to stay here.
A figure appeared in the chair, sitting with its back to them.
Ethan took a step.
Salma pulled him back.
— Ethan.
The figure turned its head.
The face was not his. It was his mother’s, but young, as she appeared in the photograph from the orchard. She held a red apple and peeled its skin into a long spiral. Her lips moved.
Ethan did not hear the words. He read them.
Don’t let him in.
— Whom? he whispered.
The figure looked past Ethan toward something behind him.
Salma shouted.
A powerful blow struck the base of his skull. At first he felt no pain, only white light swallowing the laboratory.
When he opened his eyes, he was on the floor. The emergency lighting was working. The storeroom door was closed. Salma was beside him, blood on her sleeve.
— What happened? Ethan asked.
— You fell.
— Someone hit me.
— There was no one there.
— The corridor.
Salma looked at him.
— What corridor?
Ethan pointed toward the storeroom.
— The door opened.
— The door never moved.
— You told me not to go in.
— You walked toward the cylinder. I told you not to touch it.
— My mother was there.
Salma checked his pupils.
— You lost consciousness for seven seconds.
There was no text left on the monitor. The logs showed a voltage drop and a system shutdown. The surveillance camera showed Ethan taking three steps toward the cylinder, then collapsing. Salma caught him too late. The storeroom door remained closed.
The black notebook was gone.
They searched until morning. In the bag, on the tables, beneath the equipment. Nothing.
The only evidence consisted of the instant photographs from the apartment and the image sent to Salma. At 6:41, when she opened it again, the equation in the photograph had changed. In place of the formula, the notebook page read:
THE ATTEMPT FAILED.
Beneath it, in handwriting that no longer resembled Ethan’s:
INCREASE THE REWARD.
CHAPTER 6
THE MAN THE WORLD HAD BEEN WAITING FOR
The central bank cut the rate by a quarter point.
The announcement came at ten, three days after the demonstration. At 10:01, Armin Koenig sent a message to the laboratory team:
“Useful confirmation. Prepare communications.”
At 10:03, a financial journalist called Ethan and asked him to comment on
“the Orison machine that anticipated the decision.”
At 10:05, Salma entered Ethan’s temporary office and threw her phone onto the desk.
— Who talked?
— Not me.
— Koenig says the information came from an external source.
— Koenig is the external source of his own lies.
Salma closed the door.
In those three days, the laboratory had been transformed. Two legal teams, a security team, communications specialists, and executives who had never come downstairs before now moved among the equipment. The Orchard system had been transferred to a room with transparent walls, like a dangerous animal displayed in a museum. Every cable bore a seal. Every test required six signatures. Yet the missing notebook and the text on the monitor appeared in no official report.
Salma had written a separate report. Ethan had refused to sign it.
— You saw the corridor, she had told him on the first day.
— You didn’t see it.
— You saw something.
— After seventy hours with almost no sleep and a blow to the head.
— The blow came afterward.
— That’s what I remember.
— Memory is not chronological order. It is advocacy.
— Exactly. That is why we don’t put hallucinations in a technical report.
Salma had looked at him with the disappointment reserved for people who use good arguments to support convenient conclusions.
Now, after the central bank’s confirmation, the problem could no longer be contained in the laboratory.
— If they have announced it, Ethan said, denying it makes us look frightened.
— We are frightened.
— You are.
— So are you. You just like what you’re getting in return more than I do.
Ethan looked through the transparent wall at Orchard. Technicians were checking its temperatures. The central cylinder had not glowed again without power. The system had answered twenty-three verifiable questions correctly and four incorrectly. The rate exceeded chance, but not by enough to qualify as miraculous. The effect grew, however, when the question mattered to Ethan.
In one test, Salma had hidden one of the apple samples she had received from Ilona after the incident. The question was whether the sample contained a dangerous bacterial strain. Orchard had answered NO before the laboratory confirmed it. The lead had been one hundred and nine milliseconds, the largest measured.
In another test, an investor had asked whether a company would beat its quarterly estimates. The system had been wrong.
— Perhaps it doesn’t care about money, Salma had said.
— Or the investor didn’t care enough.
— He lost four million.
— He had two hundred.
Ethan had not mentioned what disturbed him: when he asked the same question, Orchard answered correctly.
At 10:12, Koenig called a meeting. The public statement had already been prepared. Orison would announce a “preliminary demonstration of anticipatory storage,” without using the word prediction. Ethan was presented as co-author of the concept. Salma appeared as head of the experimental team.
— We have no mechanism, she said.
— The public does not buy mechanisms, the communications director replied. It buys results and metaphors.
— Then say we have an anomaly.
— Anomalies frighten the markets.
— They should.
Koenig turned to Ethan.
— What do you propose?
A month earlier, no one would have asked him to decide the tone of a global announcement. Now seven people waited for his answer.
— We state the verifiable truth, he said. The system allocated information before input and anticipated a public decision. We do not know how. We publish the protocol, not the code.
— The code belongs to us, a lawyer said.
— That is precisely why we don’t publish it.
— The protocol permits replication.
— If the effect is real, they will replicate it anyway. If it isn’t, we need them to find out.
Salma studied him. She did not agree completely, but she was on his side.
Koenig folded his hands.
— And who presents it?
Ethan knew the answer before anyone gave it.
— Mr. Reed, the communications director said. The story is already associated with him. — Dr. Idris led the experiment, Ethan said. — The public needs a face. — She has one. — That is not what it means. Salma stood. — Let him do it. He became rich by accident and everyone thinks he is a genius. It is exactly the level of rigor this moment demands. She walked out. Ethan followed her into the corridor. — Was that necessary? — It was true. — Does it bother you that I’ll be speaking? — It bothers me how quickly you like it. — I haven’t agreed yet. — You agreed when you asked who was writing the speech. Ethan did not remember asking, but a document entitled “Key Messages for Ethan Reed” lay on the table. Perhaps he had opened it without noticing. — Publicity protects us, he said. If Orison wants to hide something, it no longer can. — Publicity protects them from us. Once the world believes in the miracle, every doubt becomes sabotage. — Then stand onstage and doubt it. Salma stopped. — You don’t want me onstage to doubt. You want me there to make you credible. She was right again in a way Ethan considered unhelpful. — I don’t want them to be able to say I’m the only one who saw it. — You aren’t the only one. — I was the only one who saw the corridor. Salma looked at him, surprised that he had named it. — And yet you don’t want it in the report. — Because I don’t want the only impossible thing we can measure buried beneath a story about my mental health. — Perhaps that story is relevant.
— To whom?
— To you.
Before he could answer, Ethan’s phone vibrated. Nadia.
“Your mother keeps asking about Room Seven. I don’t know what it means. Call me.”
Ethan showed the message to Salma.
— Did you tell Nadia? she asked.
— No.
— Your mother?
— No.
Salma bit the inside of her cheek, a gesture Ethan was beginning to recognize when she refused a conclusion.
— We’re going to see her, she said.
— We have the conference at four.
— You just said publicity matters less than truth.
— I didn’t say that.
— I know.
At the care center, Ilona sat in the common room sorting buttons by color, an activity designed to assess dexterity without looking like a test. Nadia was beside her. When Ethan and Salma entered, Ilona looked up.
— That’s her, she said.
Salma stopped.
— Who?
— The one who doesn’t believe you.
Nadia looked at Ethan.
— She said the same thing to me. All morning she has been repeating that you have to return to Room Seven before it closes.
— Mother, what is Room Seven? Ethan asked.
Ilona selected a red button and placed it among the blue ones.
— The place where you said you don’t remember.
— When did I say that?
— When you were little.
— There was no Room Seven.
— Then why were you afraid of it?
Ethan felt Salma’s gaze.
— How old was I?
Ilona shrugged.
— The same age you are now. Children grow older faster in dreams.
Nadia leaned toward Ethan.
— She didn’t sleep well. The doctor changed her medication.
— Who told her the number?
— Perhaps she heard it on television. The article about the laboratory uses the seven-line symbol.
— The article appeared an hour ago.
— The television is on.
On the screen in the corner, a news channel displayed Ethan’s photograph. Beneath it: THE MACHINE THAT SEES AHEAD? ORISON PREPARES AN ANNOUNCEMENT.
Ilona pointed toward the image.
— Look, you’ve become the man the world was waiting for.
— The world wasn’t waiting for me.
— Then why does it look so relieved?
Salma sat beside her.
— Mrs. Reed, do you know me?
Ilona touched her hand.
— No. But he had a harder time inventing you.
Nadia pulled her chair back.
Ethan felt a flicker of anger, although he did not know at whom.
— That’s enough, Mother.
Ilona looked at him. For a moment her eyes were completely clear.
— Don’t be angry with me. I’m one of the parts that hurt.
Then she returned her attention to the buttons.
In the corridor, Nadia stopped him.
— What is happening?
— I don’t know.
— Don’t tell me only that.
— There is an anomaly in the laboratory. The old code predicts some outcomes.
— I know what the press says.
— The press knows nothing.
— And you?
— I know more than I can prove.
Nadia looked toward Salma, who had remained in the room with Ilona.
— Does she know too?
— Yes.
— Elina?
Ethan hesitated.
Nadia smiled without pleasure.
— Of course.
— It isn’t what you think.
— I don’t know what I think. Every week a new woman appears who knows something about you before you do. It is difficult to choose the most insulting interpretation.
— Salma is the lead researcher.
— And Elina?
— I don’t know what Elina is.
— That is what attracts you, isn’t it?
Ethan did not answer.
Nadia lowered her voice.
— You only look well when everyone else is a little frightened. Now everyone is. Be careful not to confuse that with love.
At the conference, the hall was full. In less than six hours, Orison had invited international press, investors, and government representatives. Behind the stage, an animation showed luminous branches growing before the trunk appeared. The name ORCHARD had been translated into English and registered as a trademark.
Ethan wore a new suit delivered directly to the laboratory. The cut made him look taller and thinner. A stylist had applied powder to his forehead. When he looked in the mirror, he no longer saw the auditor who had left carrying a paper bag. He saw the man from the articles.
Elina sat in the front row.
She had not answered his calls. Now she wore a red dress and watched him with a calm that resembled ownership. The seat beside her remained empty. Salma had refused until the last moment to attend, but appeared two minutes before the beginning and sat in precisely that place.
The two women greeted each other. Elina whispered something. Salma turned sharply toward her. Ethan could not hear.
Nadia was probably watching the broadcast from the care center. Or perhaps not. The thought that she might see him changed his posture more than the cameras did.
Koenig introduced him as “the mind that, a decade ahead of the industry, understood that the future of data begins with what we refuse to lose.” The applause came before Ethan had fully stepped onto the stage.
He began with the prepared speech. After three sentences, he abandoned it.
— I did not build a machine that sees the future, he said. We found a system that behaves as though loss can be felt before it occurs. The distinction matters. A prediction tells us what will happen. Our system appears to ask us what would hurt if it happened.
The hall fell silent.
— We do not know the mechanism. We do not know the limit. We do not yet know whether the effect can be separated from the observer’s belief. These unknowns are not flaws in the announcement. They are the announcement.
He spoke without the teleprompter. The words came easily, better than the rehearsed ones. In the front row, Salma watched him intently. Elina smiled.
The demonstration used an ordinary question selected by a journalist: did a sealed box contain a red object or a blue one? Orchard answered RED before the question was entered. The box contained a red cube.
Applause.
The second question had been prepared by Salma. Two identical containers, one holding a living cell culture, the other sterile. The system correctly protected the data associated with the living culture. The applause was weaker, perhaps because people did not understand why it mattered.
During questions, one reporter asked for stock-market predictions. Another asked about elections. A woman asked whether the system could tell her how long she had left to live. Ethan repeated the limitations. With each refusal, interest grew.
Then a man in the fourth row stood.
He had a white scar on his chin.
Elias.
— Mr. Reed, he said. Who asks the questions when the machine answers without them?
Ethan stopped.
— I don’t understand.
— You are not using it. It is using your sense of importance to find something. What do you give it when you show it what you do not want to lose?
A security agent approached.
— Leave him, Ethan said.
— Have you decided what you are keeping? Elias asked.
The microphone was taken from him. Two agents lifted him by the arms.
— Who are you? Ethan shouted.
Elias did not answer. As they removed him, he looked at Ethan and touched his scar.
Koenig intervened with a joke about philosophical questions. The hall laughed. The conference continued.
Afterward, Ethan asked for the man’s identity. Security had no record of the incident. In the official recording, the fourth row was empty. At the moment of the question, Ethan appeared to stare at the hall for several seconds, then answer a female journalist about data protection.
— Everyone saw him, Ethan said.
— Saw whom? Koenig asked.
Salma pulled him aside.
— I saw a man stand up.
— Elina?
— She says she didn’t.
Elina was waiting for them backstage.
— You didn’t see him? Ethan asked.
— I saw you stop.
— He spoke to me.
— Perhaps only to you.
Salma stepped toward her.
— Who are you?
Elina regarded her with interest.
— Exactly the question Ethan hoped you would ask.
— You don’t work for Orison.
— Neither did Ethan until last week.
— You went to the care center.
— Perhaps.
Ethan seized her arm.
— You said you hadn’t.
Elina looked at his hand. Ethan released her.
— I said what you could accept at the time, she replied. — Why did you go? — To see what remembers you before you do. — What does that mean? — Ask Orchard. Salma took a step toward Elina. — Stay away from the laboratory. — I don’t have to go near it. The laboratory is coming to you. Elina kissed Ethan on the cheek and walked away through the crowd. Salma looked at him as if the gesture had insulted her personally. — Are you sleeping with her? — That isn’t relevant. — It is to observer bias. — Are you asking as a researcher? — No. The answer remained between them. Salma turned to leave. Ethan caught her hand. — Then what are you asking? — Whether you are stupid enough to believe that a woman who knows your memories is merely interesting. — I don’t believe that. — Then why do you let her in? — Because when she is there, things happen. Salma looked at him with anger and with something more dangerous than anger. — That is not a scientist’s answer. — I am not a scientist. — No. You are the man the world was waiting for. She kissed him. The kiss was brief and violent, without Elina’s precision. Ethan felt her surprise as clearly as his own. Salma pulled away immediately. — That was an error, she said. — In whose favor? Salma almost smiled, then remembered not to. — Don’t turn everything into a statistic.
In the weeks that followed, Ethan’s life accelerated. Orchard became a global subject. Orison shares rose. Governments requested access. Hospitals proposed diagnostic uses, militaries the anticipation of attacks, funds the market. Religious leaders declared that the machine confirmed providence; skeptics called it a fraud; both camps invited Ethan to prove them right.
He appeared on television, at conferences, in meetings inside buildings where he would once have passed through three security checks merely to deliver a report. People quoted his own sentences to him. A speech about the danger of believing predictions was shared as evidence that he himself could see the future.
His money grew without his touching it. Companies in which he invested accidentally received contracts. A digital currency purchased years earlier reappeared in an old wallet and multiplied in value a hundredfold. Claudia Bauer congratulated him on his
“instinct.”
Ethan told her it had not been instinct. She replied that the result was the form through which instinct retrospectively acquired its name.
The women had become part of the story as well. Photographs of Elina circulated in the press. A tabloid called her
“the algorithm’s muse.”
Salma appeared in serious articles, described as
“the real experimental mind,” wording that irritated her because it implied the existence of a false mind. Nadia refused to speak, which made her more interesting to journalists. A photographer followed her to the care center. Ethan offered her security; she refused.
— I don’t want your people in my life, she told him.
— They aren’t my people.
— That makes them worse.
Ethan visited Ilona almost every day, although sometimes for only ten minutes. His mother grew more confused. She spoke about an orchard that must not be opened at night and a boy who asked her for seedless apples. The doctors said the illness was progressing.
In the laboratory, Salma and Ethan reconstructed part of the notebook’s equations from his memory. He did not recognize the proof, but whenever they reached an impasse, the next step appeared with the clarity of a memory. They connected the predictor to a small actuator. On the first attempt, it lifted a one-gram weight with more energy than had entered the system. On the second, ten grams. On the third, one hundred.
— It isn’t free energy, Salma said.
— We are measuring every input.
— Then we aren’t measuring all the places it can come from.
— Perhaps it selects a rare thermal state.
— Out of how many states?
— Enough.
— And what happens to the others?
Ethan did not answer.
The small device produced one continuous watt for seven hours with no visible power source. Then two. Then thirty-one. With each increase, Ethan felt a cold euphoria. It was not merely discovery. It was final revenge against every limit that had told him reality did not negotiate.
Orison prepared a demonstration reactor. Ethan demanded control of the patents and an independent foundation to distribute the technology. Koenig accepted almost everything. Governments promised accelerated approvals. Prizes and honorary doctorates were being discussed before the first device powered a building.
One night, after the laboratory reactor sustained one hundred kilowatts without any detected input, Ethan stepped outside. It was nearly three. Journalists still waited at the laboratory barrier. When they saw him, they shouted questions. Security agents formed a corridor. One man asked him to save the factory in his town. A woman asked whether the energy would be free. Someone shouted that he was the Antichrist. Someone else that he was the only honest man left.
Ethan got into the car. His phone held messages from ministers, Elina, Salma, Nadia, Felix, and people he had never met. Each wanted a share of the night.
The car became trapped in traffic. Although it was three in the morning, hundreds of people had gathered around the laboratory. Horns, sirens, shouting, helicopters. The city that had ignored him all his life would no longer let him leave.
Ethan pressed his palms to his temples.
— I would give anything for them to be quiet, he said.
The driver did not answer.
The noise stopped.
It did not diminish. It did not recede. It stopped.
The horns remained lit. People’s mouths moved without sound. The helicopter hovered above them like an image without an audio track. Ethan could not hear the car’s engine, the air conditioning, or his own breathing.
He placed his fingers against his throat.
He felt no pulse.
The dashboard clock read 7:12.
The driver turned around. He was not the man who had collected Ethan from the laboratory. He was Elias.
— You still haven’t given anything, he said, the only voice in the world.
— What do you want?
— Not what you have.
— Then what?
Elias raised a hand and touched the rearview mirror. It did not reflect the car or the street. It showed the white corridor and the metal chair.
— What remains when you receive everything.
The noise returned all at once. Ethan flinched. Horns erupted, people shouted, the engine vibrated. The driver in front was a young man named Pavel, hired two weeks earlier.
— Did you say something? he asked.
Ethan still had his fingers against his throat. His pulse was beating rapidly.
— How long did it last?
— What?
— The silence.
Pavel looked at him in the mirror.
— There was no silence, Mr. Reed.
On his phone, every message received during the previous few minutes bore the same time.
7:12.
CHAPTER 7
THE TRIAL APOCALYPSE
At first, the end of the world resembled a price reduction.
In the first three months after the demonstration, Orchard lowered the cost of energy in the test regions by ninety-four percent. The reactor burned nothing, produced no smoke, and occupied less space than a gymnasium. Officially, it extracted mechanical work from thermal fluctuations selected by the algorithm. Unofficially, no one could explain why the selected fluctuations occurred often enough to power entire cities.
Ethan had learned to answer without lying completely.
— We are not violating conservation of energy, he said in interviews. We are expanding the system within which we measure it.
When asked how large that system was, he smiled and spoke about ongoing research.
In Geneva, the demonstration reactor powered a hospital and seven districts for two weeks. Bills were suspended. A fertilizer factory preparing to close restarted. Seawater was desalinated at negligible cost. Videos circulated online of elderly people thanking Ethan because they could heat their homes without choosing between warmth and food.
He watched them at night, alone, and felt something stronger than joy. He felt vindicated.
For years he had been told that problems were complicated, that change required compromise, that resources were limited and distribution was political. Orchard made all those sentences sound like excuses written by people without imagination. For the first time, Ethan did not have to explain why something could not be done. He could simply ask who was stopping him.
He founded the Orchard Foundation and announced free licenses for hospitals, water systems, and agriculture. Orison retained control of the optical components, but Ethan had secured veto power over military and financial uses. The press called him an idealist. Investors called him intelligent, because the free licenses obliged governments to purchase Orison infrastructure.
Salma called him dangerous.
— Not because you are giving people energy, she told him. Because you are making them believe the gift has no cost.
They stood in the Geneva test reactor, looking at a wall of monitors. Output had risen above two hundred megawatts. Detected input remained below ten.
— The cost may lie in the entropy of a larger region, Ethan said.
— How large?
— We don’t know.
— Then when you say “free,” you are hiding a creditor.
— I don’t say free. The press does.
— You smile beside the word.
Ethan pointed to the graphs.
— Four million people have cheaper energy.
— And the effect increases when the operators believe failure would cause suffering.
— It is a control property.
— It is dependence on stakes. Yesterday we ran the reactor on an artificial load. Output fell to twenty percent. When we connected the hospital, it came back.
— The system prioritizes real loss.
— Or it needs frightened people.
— The difference is semantic.
— Not to the people we connect to it.
Salma had sent him reports. Reactor output rose during medical alerts, storms, and evacuation drills. It fell at night, when the city slept, even if the load remained constant. Engineers compensated by introducing simulated failure scenarios. After several weeks, the simulated scenarios had stopped working. The system appeared to distinguish between real fear and theater.
— We don’t know that it “appears” to do anything, Ethan said. You are using intentional language.
— You named it Orchard.
— It was a variable.
— And now it has a logo, a foundation, and children drawing apple trees at school.
Ethan turned toward her.
— What do you propose? That we shut down hospitals until we have an acceptable ontology?
— I propose that we not connect national grids yet.
— If we don’t do it, someone else will.
— The favorite argument of people who want to do something quickly and remain moral.
Ethan moved closer. Their relationship had acquired no name. They had kissed four times and slept together twice. The first time after a night of work, on a sofa in his office, half dressed while sensors sounded in the next room. The second in a Geneva hotel, after both had promised it would not happen again. Salma did not want to be seen with him in public. Elina had not left his life, and Nadia remained a point of reference each woman sensed without knowing her.
Ethan did not consider himself to be lying. He had promised exclusivity to no one. Even so, he kept his phone face down when Salma entered.
— Why are you still here? he asked her.
— To stop you when you become convinced that an objection is merely envy.
— You can’t stop everything.
— I know. That is why I sleep badly.
That evening, the first commercial reactor in Rotterdam shut down for seven minutes and twelve seconds.
The port lost power. Cranes remained suspended, pumps stopped, navigation systems switched to backup. When power returned, every clock in the facility showed the previous day. The dates corrected themselves after the restart. Orison called the incident a synchronization problem.
Two days later, the Geneva reactor produced a surge sufficient to burn out three transformers. During the preceding minute, the hospital emergency system had mistakenly issued a disaster code. Orchard had responded to a danger that did not exist as though trying to prevent it.
Then the danger appeared.
A geomagnetic storm, smaller than those the grids had been designed to withstand, disabled a group of communications satellites. Positioning signals developed variable deviations. Aircraft were grounded. Ships slowed. Financial networks switched to emergency procedures.
That same day, a cyberattack erased property registers in three countries. Backups existed, but some contained transactions dated after the attack. People discovered houses sold before they had been placed on the market, accounts closed before their holders died, and marriages registered between people who had never met.
Governments asked Orchard to select the “minimum-loss version” of the registers.
Ethan agreed.
Within seventy-two hours, the system reconstructed most of the data. Not always according to verifiable truth, but according to the version the affected people defended most intensely. In an Italian village, the same vineyard came to belong simultaneously to two families, each with valid documents and coherent memories. When a judge issued a ruling, half the documents changed to support it.
— Shut down the system, Salma said.
— If we shut it down, we lose the entire register.
— Perhaps the register is already lost.
— People need a version.
— Not the version that wants most badly to win.
Ethan chaired a meeting of ministers, bankers, and infrastructure directors. They all spoke at once until he raised his hand. Silence came immediately. He heard his calm voice saying that the chaotic distribution of Orchard must be suspended, that every reactor must be brought under a common protocol, that the Foundation would coordinate allocation.
No one asked for his mandate.
A month earlier, Ethan would have called this an intolerable concentration of power. Now it seemed the only responsible solution. The people in the room were competent, but each defended an institution. He defended the system as a whole. It was, he told himself, the difference between ambition and necessity.
Elina was waiting aboard the private plane taking him back to Zürich.
— You aren’t on the passenger list, Ethan said.
— Lists have been having problems lately. — Where have you been? — Where you weren’t looking for me. — That doesn’t narrow it down much. Elina poured him a glass of water. Not alcohol, although he would have liked some. She knew when he needed to remain lucid. — You have obtained control, she said. — Temporarily. — All power says that before ordering its furniture. — I didn’t ask for it. — You built a world in which it was irresponsible not to give it to you. Ethan looked at her. — You know what is happening with Orchard. — I know what is happening with you. — Then tell me. — You are receiving larger and larger answers to smaller and smaller desires. — I did not wish for a satellite crisis. — You wished to be the only man who understood it. — That is magazine psychology. — Magazines understand you very well now. Ethan stood, but the plane entered turbulence and forced him to sit again. — Who are you? — A question you use so that you do not have to answer others. — You visited my mother before you met me. — Yes. The confirmation struck him less forcefully than the earlier lies. — Why? — Because she remembered the orchard. — What orchard? — Not the one with apple trees. — Room Seven? Elina looked toward the window. Beyond it, the clouds were lit by moonlight. — Sometimes a room is merely the way a mind draws a boundary, she said.
— Who do you work for?
— I don’t know.
— I don’t believe you.
— Neither do I. That is the problem.
Ethan studied her. Elina did not look seductive at that moment. She looked tired. Beneath her makeup were dark circles, and the nails on two of her fingers had been bitten. Details he had either failed to notice before or that had not existed.
— Do you remember something else? he asked.
— Fragments. Other conversations. You saying the same things in different rooms. Me answering differently and ending up in the same place.
— A simulation?
The word was spoken between them for the first time.
Elina did not smile.
— Perhaps. Or memory. Or illness. Or a system teaching us to use words it can measure.
— Who is “us”?
— I don’t know whether there is an us.
The plane descended through the clouds. Zürich appeared below, unevenly lit. Part of the city was dark.
Upon landing, they learned that the European grid had been separated into islands to halt a frequency cascade. Orchard reactors sustained some regions but produced fluctuations when transferred between loads. Orison had lost contact with two centers. Koenig was not answering.
Over the following week, the crises overlapped until people stopped naming them separately.
A fungal disease struck wheat crops in Eastern Europe. A submarine cable broke simultaneously in three places. Payment systems worked intermittently. In the markets, algorithms anticipated orders that had not yet been placed and canceled them before traders decided. Exchanges closed. Transport slowed. Gasoline became scarce precisely because no one knew whether it would still have a price a month later.
In some cities, energy was almost free. In others, there was none.
Ethan appeared on television every day. He said the situation was temporary, that infrastructure had to be protected, that panic produced more losses than shortages. While he spoke, people bought water, flour, batteries, and weapons.
He received security reports. Some groups called him a savior. Others accused him of causing everything in order to seize the grids. Both sent threats if he did not do exactly what they wanted.
One night, Felix appeared at his apartment.
Ethan had not seen him since Atlas fell. Helix had lost its contracts, Heller had resigned, Lutz had been transferred. Felix wore a battered jacket and had let his beard grow.
— I need a job, he said.
Ethan let him wait for several seconds in the hall. Not because it was necessary.
— With me?
— At the Foundation. Anywhere.
— What happened to your talent for selling other people’s work?
Felix accepted the blow.
— The market closed.
— And I am the last market still open.
— Yes.
The honesty pleased Ethan.
— Why should I hire you?
— Because I know how people behave when they are told their model doesn’t work. Now the model is the world.
Ethan hired him as operations director for Asterion.
On paper, Asterion had been a pilot campus for energy resilience. In reality, it was a complex built in a side valley of the Alps, with greenhouses, reservoirs, data centers, a clinic, shelters, and an independent Orchard reactor. The project had begun before the crises, after Elina showed him land Orison had purchased years earlier.
— A place where no one can tell you that you have to leave, she had said.
Ethan had ordered the expansion without acknowledging the name of the fantasy.
Governments now called it a continuity center. Admission lists contained engineers, doctors, agronomists, security specialists, their families, and a small number of officials. Ethan had retained fifty discretionary places.
Ilona had one. Nadia had received another.
— I’m not going, Nadia said when he presented the plan.
— The city may lose power.
— Then help the city.
— That is what I’m trying to do.
— From a private bunker.
— Asterion can coordinate the reactors even if the grids fail.
— And it has a swimming pool, a wine cellar, and apartments with views.
— The building already existed.
— Of course. Your apocalypse came furnished.
Ethan kept his voice calm.
— Mother has to be moved. The center’s generator cannot last more than two days.
Nadia looked at Ilona, who was sleeping.
— Will she go if I do?
— She feels safer with you.
— And you?
— I feel safer when systems work.
— That isn’t what I asked.
Nadia agreed for Ilona’s sake.
Salma came for the reactor. Elina appeared on the list without Ethan having added her. Felix swore that Ethan had approved the name. Ethan did not remember, but the digital signature was his.
The evacuation began on a gray morning. The convoy left Zürich with six vehicles, an escort, and medical equipment. The streets held lines outside shops, motionless trams, and groups watching the black cars. Someone threw a bottle. Another person held a sign: ETHAN, DON’T LEAVE US IN THE WRONG VERSION.
Ilona slept in an ambulance. Nadia sat beside her. Salma traveled with the technical team. Elina took a seat in Ethan’s car without asking.
At the edge of the city, people blocked the road. Police tried to create a corridor. A woman with a child broke away from the crowd and ran toward the convoy. Guards stopped her.
Ethan saw her through the window. The woman was thin, wearing an oversized coat, dried blood on her forehead. The boy was perhaps nine and carried a green backpack. He was not crying.
— Keep moving, the head of security said over the radio.
The woman struck the car window with her palm.
— Mr. Reed! My child! Please!
Ethan pressed the button and lowered the window several centimeters.
— Where do you want to go?
— Asterion. I know you have a clinic. My husband worked on cables for Orison. We have a code.
She raised a broken access card. It might have been genuine or taken from anywhere.
— The list is closed, the guard said.
The woman looked at Ethan, not the guard.
— On television you said no child should have to pay for panic.
Ethan remembered the sentence. He had said it. It had sounded good.
— What is his name?
— Anik.
The boy moved closer to the window. His eyes were very dark, and he had a scratch on his cheek.
— I know you, he said.
— You saw me on television.
— No. From before.
Elina turned her head toward him.
— Before what? Ethan asked.
The boy lifted his backpack. A toy shaped like a red apple hung from the zipper.
— From the place where you keep asking, he said.
The guard pulled him back.
— We have to go.
The crowd was tightening around them. Someone struck the hood. In the distance came the sound of an explosion or a transformer blowing.
Ethan had fifty discretionary places. He had used thirty-eight for donors, advisers, two ministers’ families, and people he considered useful. He could add two. At this scale, capacity was not a real limit. But if he opened the door in front of the crowd, the convoy could be trapped. One child became a hundred.
— Put them in the third car, he said.
The head of security hesitated.
— Both of them?
Ethan looked at the woman. She gripped her son by the shoulder.
— Both.
The guards pushed them toward the vehicle. The crowd saw and began to shout. Other people surged forward. Police raised their shields. The convoy moved. In the side mirror, Ethan saw a woman fall and a man struck with a baton.
— You saved two, Elina said.
— Yes.
— Now you can forget the others with a better story.
— Would you rather I had left them?
— No. I would merely prefer you to know what you bought.
— I didn’t buy anything.
— You bought the right to think of yourself as the man who stopped the car.
Ethan turned toward her.
— Why are you here?
— Because Asterion is the place where all your favorite things will have to live together.
The journey to the mountains took three hours. The signal disappeared several times along the way. In one small town, people stood outside an empty gas station. In another, every light was on at noon, powered by a local reactor that could no longer be shut down. Tractors stood motionless in the fields.
Asterion appeared after a long tunnel: low buildings of stone and glass, greenhouses, communications towers, entrances carved into the mountain. Snow still lay on the valley’s shaded slopes. The reactor kept the lights and heating on. People waited for them at the gate.
When Ethan stepped out, applause began spontaneously.
It was ugly to applaud a man’s arrival while entire cities were shutting down. It was also impossible not to feel relieved.
Felix came to report. All systems operational. Reserves for eighteen months. Two hundred seventy-nine people admitted, thirty-one en route. Global communications intermittent. Reactor stable.
— You did well, Ethan told him.
Felix smiled. Their roles had been completely reversed. Ethan had expected to feel triumph. He felt only that the correct order had been restored.
Ilona was taken to the clinic. Nadia walked beside the stretcher. Salma unloaded with the equipment and disappeared toward the reactor. The woman from the roadblock, whose name was Iris, held Anik’s hand. The boy looked at Ethan without gratitude.
— Where do you know me from? Ethan asked him.
— I don’t remember anymore, Anik said.
— You said from the place where we ask.
— Mother says I invent things when I’m afraid.
Iris tugged him gently.
— Thank Mr. Reed.
— Thank you, the boy said.
The words contained nothing of what Ethan had seen in the videos of rescued children.
That evening, Asterion lost contact with Zürich. The final video feed showed smoke above the station and people entering a shop through its window. The European grid had separated completely.
Two Orchard reactors no longer responded to commands. A third produced power with no load and heated the soil around it.
Ethan convened the crisis team. Maps, lists, priorities. People listened to him. When two proposals reached an impasse, he decided. No one asked whether he had the right. Each choice closed a door for thousands of people he could not see.
At two in the morning, he stepped onto the terrace of the main building. The air was cold. The valley was silent. Above the mountains, the sky was clear.
Elina found him there.
— Are you happy? she asked.
— The world is falling apart.
— That isn’t what I asked.
Ethan leaned against the railing.
— I’m useful.
— More than you have ever been.
— That doesn’t mean I wanted what is happening.
— No. No one wishes for the apocalypse. People wish only for everything they think it would give them: debts erased, hierarchies reset, rules gone, enemies punished, a community that finally recognizes their value.
— And deaths.
— The dead are the part they call the price when fantasy becomes political.
Ethan looked at her.
— Did you cause this?
Elina laughed without humor.
— You overestimate me because you need someone to have been powerful enough to choose you.
In the sky, the moon rose above the ridge.
Ethan looked at it. Then, a little farther to the left, a second moon appeared.
It was smaller and pale, with the same craters in a different orientation. The two bodies remained in the sky for seven seconds.
— Do you see it? Ethan whispered.
— Yes, Elina said.
The second moon went dark like a screen.
The reactor alarm sounded from inside the building.
Elina seized his hand.
— Now begins the part you wanted.
— I didn’t want this.
— Then prove it.
CHAPTER 8
THE NECESSARY ONES
The reactor alarm sounded like a child trying to breathe through a closed door.
Ethan and Elina ran toward the technical building. In the corridor, the lights shifted from white to red but did not go out. People emerged from their rooms half shod, asking what was happening. Ethan did not answer. He did not know.
In the control room, Salma stood before the main panel. Felix was beside her with a headset over one ear. Two female technicians were manually closing secondary circuits.
— Power is rising without a load, Salma said. Three hundred megawatts and climbing.
— Shut down the module.
— The command isn’t reaching it.
— Disconnect the output.
— If we disconnect it at this level, we burn out the bus.
Ethan watched the curves. Output was rising in regular steps: 312, 343, 374. Thirty-one megawatts every seven seconds.
— What changed before the alarm?
— We lost contact with Zürich, Felix said. Then every external channel began reporting that the reactors were failing.
Salma looked at Ethan.
— The stakes increased.
— The reactor doesn’t read the news.
— The people around it do.
— Then evacuate the room. Reduce the number of observers.
Salma had no time to answer. A sharp crack came from the module room. One camera image went white. One of the technicians screamed:
— Mateo is inside.
Mateo Ruiz, the shift engineer, had gone in to inspect a mechanical breaker. On the monitor, his silhouette appeared for an instant in the excessive light, arms raised. Then the video feed died.
— Open the emergency door, Ethan said.
— The field is active, Salma replied. It will kill anyone who goes in.
— He may still be alive.
— If we open it, we lose containment and the reactor discharges into the building.
Ethan looked at the clock on the panel. 2:17.
— How long until overload?
— Two minutes, perhaps less.
— Does Mateo have a suit?
— Not for this.
Felix gripped Ethan’s shoulder.
— We have to seal the room.
It was one of those sentences that hide a death inside a technical verb.
Ethan opened the internal channel.
— Mateo, can you hear me?
No answer.
— Mateo.
Static. Then a very faint voice:
— Don’t seal it.
The technician beside Salma began to cry.
— We can divert the power to the thermal reservoirs, Ethan said.
— They are at capacity.
— To the greenhouses.
— We would boil them.
— To the grid.
— The external grid is unstable. We could kill people in the valley.
On the monitor, output had reached four hundred ninety-eight.
— Ethan, Salma said. If we seal the room and eliminate the human signal, it may fall.
— Mateo is the human signal.
— Exactly.
He understood. Mateo’s fear, the stakes of not dying, could be feeding the reactor. A loop: the greater the danger, the more energy Orchard selected; the more energy it selected, the greater the danger.
— We can’t leave him in there as a source, Ethan said.
— We aren’t leaving him. We’re trying to stop the loop.
— Say what that means.
Salma looked directly at him.
— It means isolating the room and accepting that we can no longer save him.
Mateo’s voice returned through the static.
— Mr. Reed, please.
Ethan saw the full-isolation control on the panel. Procedurally, the decision was not his. But everyone was looking at him.
Several months earlier, he had wanted a world in which no one could ignore his conclusion. Now every second asked whether he was prepared for its consequence.
— Do it, he said.
Salma pressed the control.
The safety doors descended. The audio channel cut off in the middle of a word. Output continued rising for another fourteen seconds, then collapsed to twenty megawatts. The alarm stopped.
The silence afterward was worse.
They waited thirty minutes before opening the room. Mateo lay on his side beside the manual panel, the back of his suit burned. He had not died from the heat, the doctor said later. His heart had stopped. Perhaps an electromagnetic field, perhaps shock, perhaps something else.
