Every line in the palm of his right hand was black.
Ethan went to the clinic after the body was taken there. Nadia had covered it with a sheet and removed its badge. Iris, the woman they had taken in at the roadblock, was now helping as a nurse; she had worked in a Zürich hospital. She had washed Mateo’s hand, but the black lines would not come off.
— Did he have a wife? Ethan asked.
— In Valencia, Nadia said. And a six-year-old daughter.
— Communications?
— They aren’t working.
Ethan looked at the outline of the body.
— I made the right choice.
Nadia straightened the edge of the sheet.
— Why are you telling me?
— Because the room had to be sealed.
— Then tell him.
— There was no alternative.
— There was. The alternative merely cost more people.
— Exactly.
— That doesn’t make it any less of an alternative.
Ethan felt anger returning. Not at her, but at the fact that even a necessary decision was not being left clean for him.
— What would you have done?
Nadia looked at him.
— I would have pressed the same button.
— Then why—
— And I would not have told myself that made me innocent.
Iris stood at the sink with her back to them. Ethan noticed that her hands were trembling.
— Is your son sleeping? he asked her.
— Not since we arrived.
— We can give him something.
— I don’t want him sedated so that he is more convenient for the shelter.
Her tone was not grateful. Ethan felt a small flare of irritation and then immediate shame for it.
— That isn’t what I meant.
— I know.
People had gathered outside the clinic. News of the death had spread. Someone asked whether the reactor was safe. Someone else wanted to know why the families had not been evacuated. Felix took Ethan and Salma into a meeting room.
— We need one message before breakfast, he said. If people believe the reactor can use them as fuel, we lose control.
— The reactor used him, Salma replied.
— We don’t know that.
— We know output fell when Mateo died.
— When the room was isolated.
— The distinction is scientifically relevant and useless to fear.
Felix looked at Ethan.
— We say there was an electrical arc during an emergency procedure. The reactor is stable. We are opening an investigation.
— We do not lie, Salma said.
— Then explain in two sentences to a community trapped in the mountains that the machine keeping their children warm responds to belief and increased its output when a man became afraid he was dying.
— We tell them we don’t know.
— “We don’t know” is a luxury of laboratories. In a shelter, it means “run.”
Ethan heard the old Felix, the man who changed the tone to save the product. This time, however, the product was the only thing keeping Asterion alive.
— We say there was a load loop and isolation stopped it, Ethan decided. We don’t use the word fuel. We don’t conceal that the effect correlated with Mateo’s presence. The reactor remains at minimum power until evaluation.
— At minimum power we lose the southern greenhouses, Felix said.
— We shut them down.
— They produce thirty percent of our vegetables.
— Then we reduce consumption in the residential buildings.
— Including the clinic?
— No.
— The leadership apartments?
The question was deliberate. Ethan’s apartment was four times the size of the standard one and had heated windows.
— Including mine, he said.
Felix nodded.
— Good. People need to see it.
Ethan did not want to be seen suffering. He wanted it not to matter. But in Asterion every lightbulb had become politics.
At breakfast, he informed the residents. The dining hall was full of tired faces beneath the dimmed lighting. Ethan spoke about the accident, the investigation, and the restrictions. When he mentioned reduced heating, someone asked why the executive complex had a sauna. Someone else demanded the reserve inventory. A woman wanted to know why foundation donors occupied family apartments while technicians shared rooms.
— These allocations were made before the crisis, Ethan said.
— The crisis doesn’t know that, a man at the back replied.
A few laughs. Not hostile yet.
Felix intervened and announced a review. Ethan felt his authority thinning, not because his decisions were wrong, but because people could now see the price others paid for his comfort.
After the meeting, Elina was waiting in his apartment. She wore his sweater and stood barefoot beside the windows that would now be left cold.
— A man died, Ethan said.
— I know.
— You said the part I wanted was beginning.
— I didn’t say you would like it.
— Did you know the reactor would do that?
— I have seen rooms sealed before.
— Where?
Elina pulled her sleeves down over her palms.
— I can’t put the memories in order. Sometimes you die. Sometimes I do. Sometimes no one does, and the world becomes so good that we pray for it to break.
— Are you talking about other simulations?
— Perhaps they are dreams.
— And Elias?
— He appears when the choice begins to resemble the answer.
— What answer?
— I don’t know.
Ethan moved toward her.
— What do they want from me?
— I don’t know who “they” are.
— But you said, “he still needs to believe he chose.”
Elina became motionless.
— When?
— In the recording.
— I said that?
— The other voice did.
— What voice?
Ethan described the vanished conversation. Elina sat on the edge of the sofa.
— I don’t remember it.
— You sent me the message immediately afterward.
— Because I woke with the sentence in my mind. I didn’t know you were recording.
— “Sometimes they aren’t themselves.”
— I thought it was about me.
Ethan looked at her. Elina was trembling slightly. The woman who had appeared as a perfect composition of his desires now looked like someone afraid that her own voice did not belong to her. Her fear carried more weight than all her earlier calculated gestures.
He sat beside her.
— What do you remember clearly?
— White light. You asking where the original is. Someone saying the reward no longer works. A woman crying, but I don’t know which one. And the smell of a cut apple.
— Room Seven?
— It isn’t a room.
— Then what is it?
Elina pressed her fingers to her temples.
— A level. A trial number. A form of question. Perhaps only the word you chose so that you would not see something else.
Ethan took her hand. Elina kissed him, but this time the gesture was not an invitation. It was a request for confirmation that she existed. Ethan answered it.
When the apartment door opened, Salma saw them.
She had emergency access. She stood in the doorway with a tablet in her hand.
— Sorry, she said. I didn’t realize reactor assessment included exposure therapy.
Elina drew back. Ethan stood.
— What happened?
— Output began rising when you came in here.
— To what level?
— Thirty-one megawatts above load.
Salma looked at Elina.
— And it fell when I opened the door.
— Do you think it reacts to sex? Elina asked.
— I think it reacts to the things Ethan wants to preserve in separate versions.
— Salma.
— No. You want all the women, all the explanations, and all the exits left open, and the reactor appears to be the only system in the world polite enough to pretend there are no contradictions.
Elina smiled wearily.
— You aren’t jealous. You are merely very rigorous.
— And you aren’t a fantasy. You are merely dressed like one.
Ethan stepped between them.
— Enough.
Both women looked at him, and for a fraction of a second he felt exactly what he had imagined in adolescence and during the sterile nights after separating from Nadia: two remarkable women fighting over him. The satisfaction was brief, crude, and shameful. Immediately afterward came the clarity that neither was fighting for him. They were fighting against the way he had turned them into roles.
Salma handed him the tablet.
— Look at the correlation.
The reactor-output graph rose at moments of conflict in Asterion: Mateo’s death, the rationing announcement, the unrest in the dining hall, Salma’s entrance into the apartment. It was not merely fear. It was tension between incompatible desired outcomes.
— The machine does not produce energy from loss, Salma said. It produces selection.
— Explain.
— It treats every conflict as a branching point. Multiple possible futures, each with a different loss. The algorithm tries to preserve what observers consider irreconstructible. The difference between branches appears as mechanical work.
— That isn’t physics.
— Neither are two moons in the sky.
Ethan looked at Elina. She did not deny it.
— If it selects branches, he said, what happens to the others?
Salma lowered her voice.
— Perhaps nothing. Perhaps they are only virtual states. Or perhaps every watt is a world in which someone else lost.
— You have no evidence.
— Mateo is dead.
— Mateo died in an electrical accident.
— And the reactor immediately selected a stable state.
Elina stood.
— It hasn’t selected it yet.
— What do you mean?
— We can still hear him.
They all fell silent.
A faint noise came from the ventilation system. Not the usual hum, but something rhythmic. Ethan moved closer to the grille.
Three knocks. Pause. Three knocks. Pause.
— The ducts are contracting, he said.
Elina pressed her ear to the wall.
— He is saying, “Don’t seal it.”
Salma turned pale.
Ethan could not hear words. After several seconds, the sound stopped.
That night, the Continuity Council was created. Felix proposed the name and a simple structure: Ethan as executive director, Salma for technology, Nadia for the clinic and welfare, Felix for operations, and two more representatives elected by the community. Elina had no official role.
— Why Nadia? Felix asked in private. She isn’t the chief physician.
— People trust her.
— You trust her.
— Is there a difference?
Felix looked at him and decided not to answer.
The Council’s first decision was rationing. Its second was to prohibit departure without authorization. Its third was to suspend private communication with the outside world to protect bandwidth and security.
— That is detention, the greenhouse representative said.
— It is operational quarantine, Felix replied.
— There is no disease.
— There is panic.
Ethan voted in favor. Nadia against. Salma abstained.
— People came believing they could leave, Nadia said after the meeting.
— The road isn’t safe.
— Then tell them. Let them decide.
— If the technicians leave, they endanger everyone.
— So they are free only as long as they choose correctly.
— They are necessary.
Nadia looked at him.
— That is always how it begins. Not with the powerful, but with the necessary.
Over the following weeks, Asterion became a society.
Not a good one or a bad one. A rapid one.
People received roles, energy quotas, meal times, access to medication. Currency had disappeared, then returned in the form of contribution points. Felix said they were merely an allocation instrument. Those who worked in the reactor and security received more. Those with children received exceptions. Donors claimed the large apartments were contractual. Technicians said the donors would freeze without them. Farmers said everyone would die without them. Doctors said death would merely come faster without them.
Ethan settled disputes. At first, his decisions were accepted. Then people learned to present every desire to him as a systemic necessity.
A musician requested access to the heated hall because morale was infrastructure. A ministerial adviser requested two rooms for his family because governance required discretion. A group of young people demanded the right to use the laboratory for experiments because the future could not be suspended by fear. Each had arguments. Each accused the others of raw emotion.
Ethan began deciding according to loyalty and calling it trust.
He gave Felix additional powers because Felix carried out orders. He gave Salma unlimited resources because he needed her. He kept the large apartment because confidential meetings were held there. He allowed Elina to move around without a badge because the systems recognized her anyway. He moved Ilona into a private room with constant power, although the clinic had rationed heating.
— Your mother deserves care, Nadia said. But when you call it necessity, you are lying. It is love. You can say the word.
— She isn’t merely my mother. Her condition may be connected to Orchard.
— Thank you. You have turned love into a research protocol as well.
Ethan did not answer.
Anik began visiting Ilona’s room. The boy sat beside her and drew. Ilona let him sort the buttons and told him about the orchard. Iris worked double shifts in the clinic and avoided the Council.
One afternoon, Ethan found Anik drawing seven rectangles inside one another. In the center was a chair.
— Who showed you that?
— No one.
— Is it Room Seven?
The boy shrugged.
— They are all the rooms.
— You said you knew me from before.
— Maybe I saw you on a screen.
— What is on the chair?
Anik took a red pencil and drew an apple.
— What do they want from me?
The boy looked at him with the seriousness of children who do not understand the question yet sense that the adult needs an answer.
— For you to stop choosing what happens afterward.
— After what?
— After you choose.
Ilona laughed from the bed.
— He knows better than you do.
— Mother, who tells him these things?
— You do. When you aren’t here.
Ethan sent Anik back to his mother. Nadia rebuked him.
— He is a frightened child, not a terminal.
— He draws things he has never seen.
— Children draw monsters without ever seeing them.
— He drew the corridor exactly.
— The corridor only you saw.
— And Elina.
— Elina confirms everything that keeps you interested.
— Are you jealous?
The question escaped before he could stop it.
Nadia looked at him with a weariness more painful than anger.
— That is your favorite fantasy, isn’t it? That every objection a woman makes is a form of wanting you.
— I didn’t say every objection.
— Be careful. In this world, you may be lucky enough for it to become true.
She left.
That evening, a group tried to open the north gate and leave. Security stopped them. A man was struck. A woman broke her arm. The Council convened in the middle of the night.
— We cannot hold people by force, Nadia said.
— If they leave and tell people where we are, they will draw hundreds here, Felix replied.
— Everyone knows where Asterion is.
— They don’t know how to get in.
— Then secure the access, not the people.
Salma was looking at the reactor data.
— During the confrontation, output rose by sixty-two megawatts.
— We can use the surplus, Felix said.
They all looked at him.
— What? he continued. I am not proposing that we provoke riots. But if the tension exists anyway, we can charge the reserves.
— You have just proposed monetizing conflict, Nadia said.
— Money no longer exists.
— You found a purer form.
Ethan rubbed his eyes.
— The people at the gate remain in their rooms until morning. Then we offer them the option of leaving as a group, with supplies, if they sign that they understand the risk.
— A nondisclosure agreement for the apocalypse? Nadia asked.
— No. A route protocol.
— Tone changes almost everything, doesn’t it?
Ethan recognized Felix’s line from the first day. Nadia had spoken it without having been there.
— Who told you that?
— What?
— The sentence.
— It’s an expression.
Salma looked up.
— Not quite in that form.
The two women looked at one another. Elina, who sat in the corner without a role, smiled sadly.
— It is beginning to economize on dialogue, she said.
— What is? Felix asked.
— The world.
No one laughed.
The nine who had asked to leave departed at noon in an electric vehicle, with ten days’ food and a transmitter. Ethan watched from the platform. They did not say goodbye. At the gate, one of the women turned and raised her middle finger toward the main building. Several people laughed. Security did not.
The transmitter worked for four hours. The group reached the valley road, then the signal began repeating the same seven-hundred-meter loop. Felix assumed the receiver had failed. A drone was sent the next morning. It found the vehicle parked beside the road, doors open, supplies intact. No trace of the people.
Footprints in the mud led twenty meters from the vehicle and stopped in the middle of the road.
— Perhaps they got into another car, Felix said.
— There are no tire tracks, the head of security replied.
— The rain.
— The footprints remained.
Salma requested the drone’s raw data. In three consecutive frames, the people still appeared beside the vehicle after the transmitter had shown them leaving. In the next frame, they were gone. There were no visible errors, only absence.
Ethan prohibited distribution of the images.
— That will merely confirm that we are holding them prisoner, Nadia said.
— If we show the images, no one else will leave.
— Or everyone will try at once.
— We don’t know what happened.
— Say that.
— People cannot live on
“we don’t know.”
— They can. It is their normal condition. Only leaders like to pretend otherwise.
Ethan issued a statement: the group had lost contact and a search was under way. The images remained classified. That night, the words NINE ERASED appeared on the walls of the dining hall. No one recognized the handwriting.
Asterion developed rituals without calling them such.
Before starting the reactor, operators said aloud what they would lose if the system failed. It had begun as an experimental procedure proposed by Salma to measure the stakes. Then technicians noticed that output was more stable when the sentences were personal.
“My daughter needs warmth.”
“The greenhouses must stay alive.”
“We have nowhere else.”
After a while, people began to believe that weak wording could cause an outage.
One operator refused to say the sentence after his wife left him for another man in the complex. The reactor stopped for twenty seconds. During his next shift, his colleagues forced him to repeat the statement until his voice trembled sufficiently.
— We end the ritual, Salma said in the Council.
— The procedure stabilizes output, Felix replied.
— It is no longer a procedure. It is emotional coercion.
— All work is emotional coercion when the alternative is freezing.
— We do not use people’s suffering as a parameter.
— The machine uses it anyway.
Ethan voted to continue temporarily, with “voluntary” participation. Felix defined voluntariness as the right to request transfer from the reactor to another department, although every desirable post was occupied.
Contribution points became currency. People exchanged them for coffee, time in the gym, access to the external network, warmer rooms. At first, Ethan received no points. He had direct access. When this was criticized, Felix assigned him the highest score on the basis of “systemic responsibility.” Ethan asked for it to be reduced. Felix reduced it by ten percent and publicly announced the gesture.
The applause in the dining hall made Ethan feel dirtier than the privilege itself.
His private life became social infrastructure. People knew when Elina slept in his apartment and when Salma emerged from the private elevator in the morning. It did not matter that the relationships were not simultaneous in the sense people imagined. In a place with thin walls and harsh rules, the leader’s freedom looked like a tax on everyone else’s self-control.
A group of women sent Nadia a petition concerning harassment and room allocation. Two female security officers had been pressured by their superiors to accept relationships in exchange for shift changes. A technician had two partners at once and received portions from both, while a single mother was penalized for bringing her child to her shift.
— Sexual rules do not write themselves, Nadia said in the Council.
— We will not regulate relationships between adults, Ethan replied.
— We already regulate them through rooms, points, and access.
— Then we regulate abuses of power.
— Including yours?
The room fell silent.
Elina was not present. Salma looked at the table.
— I have not made anything conditional, Ethan said.
— You do not need to ask. When you control heat, housing, and access, your desire enters the room before you do.
— Elina does not depend on me.
— No one knows on whom Elina depends.
— And Salma has more technical power than I do.
Salma looked up.
— Please do not use me as an alibi for equality.
Ethan felt every gaze tightening around him. It was precisely the scene from the fantasy, except that the women were not fighting to win him. They were fighting not to be used in the same story.
The Council adopted conflict-of-interest rules between relationships and chains of command. Salma formally resigned her place on the Council. Ethan asked her not to.
— Then end the relationship with me, she said.
— Is that what you want?
— I want the answer to that question not to determine who gets access to the laboratory.
— It hasn’t.
— You cannot know that. Neither can I.
Salma moved into a standard room and returned the key to his apartment. That same evening, Elina appeared at Ethan’s door. He let her stay, although he knew the entire community would interpret the gesture as a choice the next day.
— You won, Ethan told her.
Elina was taking off her coat.
— Against whom?
— Don’t pretend.
— I did not want Salma’s place. I wanted there to be no places.
— That means nothing.
— It means I do not want to be the prize you receive when a real woman tells you no.
Ethan looked at her. Elina left her coat over the back of a chair.
— I’m leaving, she said.
— You just arrived.
— You need me to stay so you can prove something you no longer believe.
— What?
— That you deserve all of this.
— I never said that.
— You do not have to. The world tells you every morning.
Elina left as well.
For the first time since becoming rich, Ethan slept alone not because he lacked options, but because the options had rejected him. The difference woke him several times.
At 4:03, someone knocked on the door.
It was Anik, barefoot and in his pajamas.
— Your mother is looking for you, Ethan said.
— Mateo is calling me.
Ethan bent down.
— Mateo is dead.
— He doesn’t know.
— Where is he calling you from?
The boy pointed toward the floor.
— From below.
Ethan called security and Iris. Anik had disappeared from their room ten minutes earlier; the door sensors had recorded no opening. The boy insisted that he had heard Mateo through the ventilation duct.
— What did he say to you? Iris asked.
— That they closed it too soon.
— Who did?
— The others.
Ethan felt the hairs rise on his arms.
— Which others?
Anik began to cry, suddenly and without sound. Iris took him in her arms and told Ethan to leave.
By morning, Mateo’s body was no longer in the cold room.
Nadia called Ethan at 6:10. The door had been sealed. The room had an access log. No entries. On the metal shelf remained the folded sheet and Mateo’s badge. The black lines had left a faint imprint on the fabric.
Felix suspected that the body had been stolen to provoke panic. Ethan ordered the gates closed and the buildings searched. People were awakened. Rumors spread before the announcement.
At 7:12, Salma entered the dining hall and found Ethan beside the leadership table.
— I found him, she said.
— Where?
Salma did not answer. She pointed toward the breakfast line.
Mateo Ruiz stood between two technicians, holding a tray. He wore the reactor’s gray coveralls. He was talking with them and laughing at something. He had no burns. There were no black lines on his hand.
Ethan crossed the room. People greeted him, but he did not hear them.
— Mateo.
The man turned.
— Mr. Reed.
— What are you doing here?
Mateo looked at the tray.
— Eating.
— You died.
The conversations around them stopped.
Mateo laughed uncertainly.
— I hope not. I arrived from the southern unit last night.
— You were working at the reactor when the output surged.
— I have never been inside the module room. I work in external control.
Ethan seized his right hand. The palm was clean. Mateo pulled away.
— What are you doing?
— Your name is Mateo Ruiz.
— Rios, he said. Mateo Rios.
His badge confirmed it: MATEO RIOS, EXTERNAL CONTROL. Admission date: the previous day.
Felix arrived, irritated.
— What is going on?
— This is Mateo.
— Yes. The transfer from the south. I approved him yesterday.
— Mateo died in the reactor.
Felix looked at Ethan with calculated concern.
— The engineer who died was named Emil Kovacs.
— No.
— Ethan, I spoke to his wife in Budapest.
Salma took out her tablet. The accident report named Emil Kovacs. Photographs showed an older blond man Ethan had never seen. The footage showed his silhouette entering the room. In the audio recording, the voice saying “Mr. Reed, please” no longer sounded like Mateo’s.
Nadia entered carrying the badge found in the cold room. It read EMIL KOVACS.
— I covered Mateo’s body, she said.
Iris, standing behind her, nodded. Her face was white.
— So did I.
Felix looked from one woman to the other.
— You are exhausted. Names can be confused.
— By all three of us? Salma asked.
— You spoke to one another. Memory is contagious.
Mateo was rubbing his right palm.
— May I leave?
Ethan noticed a very thin black line beneath the thumbnail.
— Have we met before? Mateo asked him.
— Yes.
The man looked at him with a fear he did not understand.
— Then I’m sorry it didn’t work the first time.
— What didn’t?
Mateo blinked.
— What did I say?
— That it didn’t work.
— I didn’t say anything.
In the silence of the dining hall, the reactor started by itself.
Every light came on at full intensity. The information screens displayed the same message:
THE STABLE BRANCH HAS BEEN PRESERVED.
The message remained for seven seconds.
Then the screens returned to the breakfast menu.
THE CITY WITHOUT WITNESSES
By eight in the morning, no one agreed about what the screens had said.
Ethan, Salma, Nadia, and Iris remembered: THE STABLE BRANCH HAS BEEN PRESERVED. Felix maintained that only SYSTEM STABLE had appeared. Three technicians had seen REACTOR STABILIZED. Most people in the dining hall had seen nothing except the day’s menu. The surveillance cameras showed the screens white for seven seconds, with no text.
Mateo Rios did not remember the sentence about the first attempt. His medical report was normal. He had been employed by Orison for four years, had a wife in Madrid and a teenage son. Ethan found photographs, payments, correspondence, an entire life. Mateo Ruiz, the dead engineer, existed in no registry. The name did not appear even in the laboratory’s old archives.
Yet Iris had kept an object from the body’s pocket: a broken access bracelet. M. RUIZ was printed on it. When she showed it to the Council, the letters began to fade beneath their eyes. They did not vanish suddenly. The ink withdrew into the plastic like moisture until only a blank rectangle remained.
Felix asked for the object to be isolated.
— It was isolated, Salma said. In a sealed bag.
— Then perhaps the material reacts to air.
— Through plastic?
— I don’t know. But I will not announce to three hundred people that reality is rewriting badges.
— You do not have to announce it, Nadia said. They saw the lights. The rumor is already larger than the truth.
Seven versions circulated through Asterion. The reactor had resurrected a man. The reactor had replaced him. Ethan had sacrificed an engineer to choose a world in which the system worked. Mateo was an Orison agent. Emil Kovacs had been invented by the leadership. The women in the clinic had experienced a collective hallucination. The message proved that Orchard was protecting them. The message was a threat that it could erase anyone.
People chose the version that made them more important.
The reactor operators saw themselves as the guardians of an entity. Security considered itself the only force capable of preventing panic. Donors said they had been selected because they had anticipated the crisis. The group that had tried to leave became either martyrs or proof that departure was impossible, depending on who told the story.
Ethan called a general assembly. Felix stopped him at the entrance to the hall.
— You cannot tell them everything.
— We no longer know what everything is.
— All the more reason.
— People have a right to know that memory and records do not match.
— They will hear that even the leadership cannot tell a living man from a dead one.
— We can’t.
Felix took him by the elbow.
— Listen to me. When systems break, people do not look for the truth. They look for someone who behaves as though the truth still exists. If you give them uncertainty, someone else will give them a simpler certainty.
— You?
Felix let go of him.
— I am still trying to keep you in front.
Ethan went inside and told the people that an inexplicable incident had occurred, that four witnesses remembered a different identity, that the data contradicted itself, and that the reactor would remain at minimum power. He did not use the word branch. He did not mention the missing body.
During questions, an engineer accused him of concealment. A woman asked whether the nine missing people had been “replaced.” A man in the back shouted that Ethan was no longer the original. A few people laughed, but not enough.
— Who would the original be? Ethan asked.
— The one who went in first, the man replied.
— Where?
— You should know.
Security removed him. Ethan later learned that his name was Leo Martens and that he had worked in maintenance. His file contained a normal psychological evaluation. In his room they found the walls covered with the same drawing: seven rectangles and a chair.
Elina had disappeared.
She was not in Ethan’s apartment, the clinic, or the common areas. Her badge no longer worked. When Felix checked the admissions list, her name did not exist.
— You said you approved her, Ethan said.
— No.
— You said exactly that when we arrived.
— I said your list contained thirty-one discretionary admissions.
— She was on the list.
— Show me.
Ethan’s digital signature appeared on an archived version, but Elina’s row was blank. The gate cameras showed Ethan stepping out of the vehicle alone. On the plane from Rotterdam, Elina’s seat had been empty. Press photographs showed her beside him, yet reverse image searches identified the woman as an “unidentified model” or returned errors. The social-media accounts that had published the images had been deleted.
The toothbrush, a coat, and the business card remained in the apartment. The toothbrush contained no human DNA. The coat had been made by a brand that did not exist. On the card, the seven lines had become six.
— Perhaps she never existed, Salma said.
— You spoke to her.
— Perhaps I spoke to something that does not need a history.
— She slept here.
— That is not the solid argument you think it is.
Nadia held the coat between her fingers.
— I remember her shaking my hand. Her skin was cold.
— Simulations can have temperature, Ethan said.
Nadia looked at him.
— You reached the plural quickly.
In the coat’s inner pocket, they found a metal key. Engraved on it were the words ORISON — LEVEL 7 and an address in Zurich, two streets from the laboratory.
Salma recognized the address.
— The recovery center used to be there, before Orison bought the buildings.
— Data recovery? Ethan asked.
— Neurological.
The center had closed eight years earlier after a scandal involving studies conducted without fully informed consent. Salma did not know the details. The external internet was too weak to reach the archives.
Ethan decided to go.
Felix initially refused to allocate a vehicle and an escort.
— You are Asterion’s director, he said. You do not go into the city because of a key found in a coat.
— It is precisely because I am the director that I have to find out what is happening.
— No. Because you are the director, you send someone else.
— Someone who can forget what they see?
— We all can.
— I wrote the code.
— Or you believe you did.
Ethan looked at him.
— Are you challenging my authority?
Felix sighed.
— I am trying to save it from your impulses.
— I leave at dawn. With Salma and two agents.
— The Council has to vote.
— I created the Council.
— That is precisely why you cannot ignore it when it gets in your way.
Ethan felt the temptation to issue an order. Security still answered to him. The reactor, access, and resources all bore his signature. He could reduce Felix’s argument to insubordination. Instead, he called the vote.
Nadia asked to come.
— Your mother is stable. Iris will remain with her. The center has medicines we need and records concerning Ilona. If there is a connection, I want to see it.
Salma voted for the expedition. The elected representatives abstained. Felix voted against. Ethan voted in favor and used his deciding vote.
— The Council works beautifully, Felix said afterward. Especially when you have two votes.
— You proposed the structure.
— I thought you would have enough shame not to use it so soon.
They left at 5:30 in a long-range armored vehicle. Two agents, Pavel at the wheel, Ethan, Salma, and Nadia. The sky was overcast. At the gate, Anik gave Nadia the drawing of the seven rooms.
— Do not enter the one that looks empty, he said.
— Why?
— That is where it puts the things it has finished with.
Iris pulled him back and told him to stop frightening people.
The road to Zurich was almost empty. Twenty kilometers from Asterion, they encountered the first abandoned vehicle. Doors open, luggage inside, no one. Farther down, a truck had stopped crosswise across the road. Its engine was still running although the fuel gauge showed empty.
The radio caught fragments. One station repeated the same sentence about grid stabilization. Another played music over a voice counting backward from seven. Each time they entered a tunnel, the dashboard clock returned to 7:12 and corrected itself upon exit.
— GPS interference, Pavel said.
— The internal clock does not use GPS in a tunnel, Salma replied.
— Then software.
— Everyone needs the problem to remain inside a word they can bill for, Nadia said.
At the city’s edge, the signs of crisis became uneven. One street had broken windows and burned cars. The next was clean and lit, with empty trams running through it. At an intersection, a group of people stood in line outside a bakery. When the vehicle circled the block and returned because of a barrier, the same people occupied the same positions. A woman held a bag at the same angle. A child raised a hand toward the same bird.
— Perhaps the line has not moved, Pavel said.
— Eight minutes have passed, Nadia replied.
Ethan had photographed them. In the first image, the woman wore a blue coat. In the second, red. Her face and position were identical.
On the next circuit, the bakery no longer existed. In its place stood an unbroken wall of office windows.
No one commented.
They reached the Orison laboratory at 8:40. The black door was rusted. The symbol of the seven lines was gone. The guard was not there. Elina’s key opened a side entrance.
Inside, dust covered the floor.
The elevator did not work. They descended by the stairs. At the level where Ethan had worked with Salma, the door opened onto an empty space. There was no equipment, no glass walls, no cables. Only concrete, rusted pipes, and rectangular marks on the floor, as though the machines had been removed years earlier.
— That is impossible, Salma said.
Her voice echoed.
— You worked here for three months, Nadia said.
— I slept here.
Salma walked to the place where the central table had stood. Someone had drawn a vertical line on the wall in pencil for every day. There were hundreds.
Ethan found the storage-room door. Beyond it were empty shelves and two folding ladders covered with dust.
— This is where I saw the corridor, he said.
Nadia touched the dust on a shelf.
— No one has been here for a long time.
One of the agents checked the installation.
— The building has had no active power for at least five years.
Salma took out her tablet. She accessed photographs of the laboratory, reports, clips. They all existed. In the images, the space was full. She compared a concrete pillar. The same crack appeared in the background.
— Then where was the equipment? Nadia asked.
— Here.
— The dust says no.
— Dust can be put in place.
— For whom? No one expected us to come.
A voice came from the ceiling:
— Someone did.
The agents raised their weapons.
The old emergency speakers crackled to life.
— Mr. Reed, the voice continued. Go down one level.
It was Elias.
— Show yourself, Ethan shouted.
— This is not the room.
At the far end of the space, a green light came on above a door that neither Salma nor Ethan remembered. It was marked LEVEL 7.
Elina’s key fit the lock.
The staircase descended farther than the building’s depth allowed.
Ethan counted sixty-three steps, then another sixty-three. The walls were painted pale green, the color of institutions that try to calm people without asking their preference. Here and there were rectangular marks where plaques or photographs had once hung. The air grew warmer as they descended.
— No signal, one of the agents said.
— Then the speakers upstairs? Nadia asked.
— A local network, perhaps.
At the third landing, Salma stopped.
— We have descended at least thirty meters. The water table is higher than this.
— Perhaps the staircase turns back into the building, Pavel said.
— I can feel gravity, Pavel.
— And what are you proposing? That we are descending in a new direction?
— I propose that we not call something normal merely because it has a handrail.
The door at the bottom opened without a key.
Beyond it was a clinical corridor. Linoleum floor, fluorescent lights, the faint smell of disinfectant. The doors were numbered one through six. The seventh door, at the far end, had no number. Instead, it bore the symbol of concentric lines.
Nadia entered a side office first. The shelves held cardboard files, not digital media. Labels with names and codes. She picked one up.
— The date is twelve years ago.
The file belonged to a woman treated for memory problems after an accident. The next belonged to a man with epilepsy. Each contained consent forms for
“assisted narrative reconstruction,” personality tests, and photographs of white rooms with metal chairs.
Salma found a company logo: Mnemosyne Therapeutics, later acquired by Orison.
— The recovery center, she said. They used virtual reality for cognitive rehabilitation.
— Twelve years ago? Ethan asked.
— Clinical VR existed. Not at the level this suggests.
One report described
“adaptive environments driven by autobiographical preferences” and “identity consolidation through congruent rewards.”
The language was medical, but the idea was too familiar to Ethan: the world adapted itself to what the patient wanted to be true.
In a locked drawer, they found a nameless file. Code: ER-31.
Ethan opened it.
The first page held a frontal and profile photograph of him at twenty-nine. He wore the gray T-shirt from the Nacre clip. Beneath the photograph:
SUBJECT: ER-31 PROTOCOL: RECONSTRUCTION / ELICITATION CURRENT SESSION: 606
STRATEGY: ESCALATED HEDONIC REWARD STATUS: INVARIANT NOT LOCATED
Nadia leaned over his shoulder.
— What does it say?
Ethan showed her.
She took the page.
— Here it says
“VOLUNTEER ENROLMENT — TREATMENT-RESISTANT MIGRAINE.”
— No.
— Ethan, that is exactly what it says.
Salma had photographed the document. On her tablet screen, the text was different:
MODEL: ER-31 VALIDATION SET: 606
OBJECTIVE: EXTRACT ORIGINAL REPRESENTATION METHOD: NARRATIVE REINFORCEMENT
— I see
“model,” she said.
Nadia took the tablet. For her, the photograph showed the page about the migraine.
One of the agents approached.
— It is an inventory list.
Each witness received a different document.
Ethan placed the page on the table. The ink seemed stable as long as he did not try to show it to someone else. When Nadia pointed to a line, he saw the letters changing beneath her finger. Not like an animation, but like a memory correcting its object.
— We cannot transport the information, Salma said. It adapts to the observer.
— Then how do we know there is a common file?
— We don’t.
Nadia leafed through the pages. One held a diagram of relationships. Ethan occupied the center. Lines led to Ilona, Nadia, Felix, Elina, and Salma. Elina’s name was enclosed in a dotted box. Beside Nadia, Ethan read: AUTOBIOGRAPHICAL ANCHOR — HIGH RESISTANCE. Beside Salma: EPISTEMIC ADVERSARY — AFFECTIVE TRANSFER IN PROGRESS. Beside Elina: COMPOSITE REWARD — EMERGENT INSTABILITY.
— I do not want to know what you see, Nadia said.
Ethan looked up.
— Because whatever it says will give you a new way to reduce me to the function I serve for you.
— Perhaps it explains why you are here.
— I know why I am here.
— Are you sure?
Nadia let the page go.
— No. But my uncertainty belongs to me more than your file’s certainty does.
Salma found an old terminal in an observation room. It was not visibly connected, but the screen came on when Ethan entered. An overhead image of a white room appeared. A man with a shaved head sat in the chair, cables at his temples. His face was not visible.
In the corner of the screen: ER-31 / SESSION 606 / SUBJECTIVE TIME 4Y 08M 12D.
— Four years? Ethan whispered.
— I see thirty-one minutes, Salma said.
Nadia saw no time at all. For her, the room was empty.
The man in the chair raised his hand and tapped the metal arm twice. Ethan’s gesture when he was thinking.
— That is me, he said.
— Perhaps, Salma replied. Or someone who knows what gesture will convince you.
A text field appeared on the screen.
QUESTION: WHERE IS THE ORIGINAL?
SUBJECT RESPONSE: I DON’T REMEMBER.
NEXT PROTOCOL: POWER / CATASTROPHE / COMMUNITY LOSS.
Ethan felt a cold nausea.
— That describes what happened.
— Or what you expected to find after you began believing in a simulation, Nadia said.
— I did not expect those terms.
— You have read dozens of theories. Your brain has the vocabulary.
— And the two of you are hallucinations?
Nadia stepped closer.
— Perhaps. But if I am, I owe you no less decency.
The terminal sounded. The image changed. The man in the chair turned his head toward the camera. The face was Ethan’s, but the eyes were open too wide.
— Don’t give them the original, the image said. The voice came from the speaker. — What is the original? Ethan asked. The man on the screen opened his mouth, but the image fragmented. For one second, Elina’s face appeared in place of his, then Ilona’s, then Anik’s. — Not the object, said a blended voice. The choice that cannot be repeated. The screen went dark. Behind them, someone clapped once. Elias stood in the corridor doorway. He wore a white coat over his driver’s clothes. The scar on his chin looked deeper. The agents raised their weapons. — Don’t shoot, Ethan said. — Weapons are reassuring, Elias said. They make the world seem local. — Who are you? — An answer you use to postpone the question. — Do you work for Orison? — Orison worked for a while for what it thought I was. Salma took a step forward. — Are you human? Elias looked at his hands. — For now, enough. — What is this place? — An old layer. Or a memory of a layer. It depends on who arrived first. — Are we in a simulation? Ethan asked. Elias smiled sadly. — You used the word. Words lower the cost of communication and raise the cost of understanding. — Then give me another one. — Trial. Reconstruction. Interrogation. Game. Treatment. Birth. All are correct for someone and lies for the rest. — Who is trying to extract something from me? — We are not asking you for the truth. The truth is not yours. — What do you want? — Whatever produces the difference between a copy that answers as you would and you.
Ethan felt the sentence enter his mind and find too many places. — A memory? — Memories can be copied. — A password? — Passwords can be changed. — Consciousness? — That is a word for the question itself. — The soul? Nadia asked. Elias looked at her for the first time. — That is a word for ownership. Salma raised the tablet. — The system selects branches and obtains energy. Who pays? — Your question assumes that the branches which do not remain are places. — Are they? — For those inside them. — Mateo Ruiz? Elias touched his scar. — An insufficiently stable version. — Did you kill him? — You pressed the button. Ethan stepped forward. — You made me choose. — Yes. — Then the choice was not free. — That is one of the answers that has been tried. — How many attempts? Elias looked toward the dark terminal. — Enough for the reward to stop producing variation. — What happens if I refuse? — Refusal is a very easy choice to repeat when it costs nothing. — And if I leave? — You have left. — From here.
— And from here.
The agents looked at one another. One whispered that they were speaking to a wall. Ethan turned. To the agent, the doorway in which Elias stood was empty.
— You don’t see him? Nadia asked.
— See whom?
Pavel saw Elias but said he was a woman in a white coat. Salma saw him as a younger man without a scar. Only Ethan saw him exactly as the driver.
— Where is Elina? he asked.
— Near the place where she began to believe her fear belonged to her.
— Is she real?
— More than she was.
— And Nadia? Salma?
Elias looked at the two women.
— Reality is not a quality distributed by the protagonist.
Nadia smiled briefly.
— I like that one.
— How do we get out? Ethan asked.
— Exit is the oldest reward. It no longer works very well with you.
— Then why did you bring us here?
— I did not bring you. You found what was necessary to continue.
The corridor light flickered. Elias turned toward the unnumbered door.
— Room Seven is not a room, he said.
— We know.
— No. You read it.
He opened the door.
Beyond it was not the white corridor but a Zurich street in full daylight. Trams, people, cafés. The city intact.
— What is this? Ethan asked.
— A version with witnesses.
Elias stepped through and disappeared into the crowd.
The door remained open.
The agents saw a storeroom. Pavel saw a subway exit. Salma saw the Orison laboratory functioning. Nadia said she saw the kitchen of their old apartment.
Ethan saw the street.
— We are not going in, Salma said.
— It is the only exit.
— For you.
— The staircase may no longer exist.
A sound came from behind them. The corridor walls began to close slowly, as though the building were correcting its width. The numbered doors disappeared one by one beneath the green paint.
Ethan crossed the threshold first. Salma followed, then Nadia and the others.
They stood on a street a few hundred meters from the laboratory. Their vehicle was parked at the corner. The sun shone. The phone network worked. People walked to work, carried coffee, talked. No broken shop windows, no queues, no sign of the crisis.
Ethan turned. Behind them was the entrance to a pharmacy. The pharmacist watched them curiously.
— How long were we down there? Ethan asked.
Pavel checked his watch.
— Twenty-three minutes.
Salma’s tablet showed five hours and thirty-one minutes.
Nadia’s phone was dated three days later.
Ethan asked a woman on the pavement what day it was. She named the same day on which they had left.
— What happened to the power outages? he asked.
— What outages?
— The Orchard reactors.
The woman recognized him.
— Mr. Reed? May I take a photograph?
Within seconds, people gathered. They thanked him for free energy. They said life was better. A man showed him a bill for zero. A woman told him her son had been cured after Orchard anticipated the diagnosis. None of them knew about Asterion or the collapse of the grids.
Ethan led the others into the vehicle. They set out for the edge of the city.
At the first intersection, they passed the same woman with the bag, this time in a green coat. At the second, again. At the third, she turned directly toward Ethan and raised her hand.
She had Elina’s face.
Ethan told Pavel to stop. By the time he opened the door, the woman had disappeared.
— Did you see her? he asked.
Salma nodded. Nadia did not.
At the city’s edge, the intact buildings ended abruptly. Beyond an intersection, the asphalt was cracked, the cars burned, the traffic lights dead. They crossed the line without sound or change of light, as though passing through an invisible cut.
Ethan looked in the side mirror. The intact city remained behind them.
Then a truck crossed the intersection and, for a moment, there was no city beyond it. Only a white field without a horizon and a metal chair.
When the truck passed, the buildings returned.
Nadia tightened her seat belt.
— What did you see? she asked.
— Nothing we could show together.
Salma closed her eyes.
— Then we have to find something that remains true when we disagree.
Ethan thought of the ER-31 file, the nameless bracelet, Ilona peeling an apple, Mateo’s hand disappearing in one world and returning in another.
— Perhaps that is what they want too, he said.
Behind the vehicle, every building in the city went dark at once.
CHAPTER 10
SOMETHING MORE REAL THAN DESIRE
The vehicle stopped twenty-eight kilometers from Zurich, although the battery showed sixty-three percent.
Pavel tried to restart it. The system displayed every function as normal, but the motor did not respond. Salma opened the service panel. Voltage was present. The controller received commands and confirmed them before they were sent.
— It is doing things in advance again, she said.
— Then we should already have left, Nadia replied.
No one laughed.
The road passed through a narrow valley. Houses on the slopes had dark windows. Smoke came from the west—not much, a gray streak beneath the clouds. Communication with Asterion did not work. Messages remained pending and sometimes appeared as delivered hours before they had been written.
Ethan tried the satellite phone. Felix’s face appeared on the screen, frozen in a frame. Beneath the image were the words: CALL ENDED — 7:12. They had made no call.
— We walk until we find a vehicle, said one of the agents, Lukas.
The other, Mirek, had a wound in his calf from a piece of metal that had entered through the floor of the vehicle in the city. The injury did not seem serious, but it had begun bleeding again. Nadia improvised a bandage.
They set out with backpacks, water, and weapons. Salma insisted on taking a black case the size of a briefcase from the vehicle’s technical compartment.
— What is it? Ethan asked.
— The calibration substrate.
— You brought part of Orchard?
— It does not work by itself.
— Then why?
— Because I no longer trust that the machine at Asterion will be the same when we return.
The case weighed nearly twenty kilograms. Lukas carried it without comment.
After an hour, the rain began. Not Zurich’s fine rain, but cold, heavy water that turned the road into a mirror. Ethan had not walked this far with a backpack in years. His shoes, chosen for laboratories and meetings, raised blisters. The waterproof suit security had bought protected him, but sweat chilled his back.
Nadia walked faster. Salma, one leg bandaged after a fall on the stairs at the Orison center, did not complain. Ethan felt irritated by every one of their steps that failed to acknowledge his effort.
Around noon, they saw a thread of white smoke rising from a large building, a school or boarding house. Two buses barricaded the gate. Someone on the roof watched them through binoculars.
— Stop! a voice shouted.
Lukas raised his hands. Mirek placed his weapon on the asphalt.
A woman descended from the roof and appeared at the gate beside three people in work clothes. She was about forty-five, dark-skinned, with very short hair and a rifle held properly, without theatrics.
— Who are you?
— Travelers from Zurich, Ethan said.
— Names.
Lukas gave them. When he reached Ethan, the woman recognized him.
— Reed.
It was not admiration.
— Yes.
— The man with the reactors.
— One of the people.
— In every recording, you were alone.
— Recordings choose faces.
— And you accepted yours.
Rain ran down his forehead. Ethan tried to see how many people stood behind the windows.
— We have an injured man, Nadia said. His wound needs cleaning and antibiotics.
The woman looked at Mirek.
— The weapons stay at the gate. The case as well.
Salma tightened her grip on the handle.
— It is sensitive equipment.
— Then you remain outside with it.
— It is not dangerous unless connected, Ethan said.
— People said the same about the reactors.
Nadia approached the gate.
— Can we discuss this inside after someone sees to him?
The woman looked at her, then opened a narrow door between the buses.
— I’m Lea Okafor. No one leads alone here. The Council will decide whether you may stay. Until then, you do not move without an escort.
The school sheltered eighty-six people. Former villagers, stranded travelers, families from two nearby towns. They had no Orchard reactor. They used solar panels, wood, a diesel generator run for two hours a day, and a small turbine on the stream. Water was filtered through sand and boiled. Food came from the school’s stores, gardens, and exchanges with other groups.
Mirek was taken to the former staff room, now an infirmary. A retired doctor cleaned the wound and said the metal had touched the bone. Antibiotics were scarce.
— Asterion has a clinic, Ethan said. We can reach it within a few hours if we find transport.
Lea, standing beside the door, replied:
— Asterion is admitting no one.
— I run Asterion.
— On the radio, Felix Brecht says otherwise.
Ethan felt something cold beneath his ribs.
— What does he say?
Lea led him to the teachers’ room, where a hand-cranked radio was connected to an improvised antenna. Every hour, Asterion broadcast a bulletin. Felix’s voice announced that Director Ethan Reed had disappeared on an unauthorized mission and was probably compromised by “Orchard-associated cognitive phenomena.” The complex was under the Council’s temporary leadership. The gates remained closed. Anyone claiming to represent Ethan was to be treated as a threat.
— The mission was authorized, Ethan said.
— By whom? Lea asked.
— The Council. We voted.
— Including you.
— I held the deciding vote.
— That sounds very collective.
Salma switched off the radio.
— Felix is trying to stabilize Asterion.
— By declaring us insane?
— By declaring you insane. Nadia and I were not mentioned.
Nadia looked at her.
— Yet.
Ethan tried a direct transmission. Asterion did not answer. An automated message demanded authentication. His certificate had been revoked.
— Can we transmit on your frequency? he asked.
Lea folded her arms.
— After our Council decides whether your presence exposes us.
— My presence can bring energy and access to resources.
— Your presence has already brought people looking for you.
Ethan looked through the window. Beyond the buses, the road was empty.
— Who?
— Two groups came through yesterday. One says it wants to protect you. The other says it wants to put you on trial. Both have weapons.
— How did they know I was here?
— They didn’t. They are looking everywhere.
Lea gave him a cup of warm water with mint leaves. Not coffee. She did not ask what he preferred.
— Here, she said, people eat if they work, if they are children, sick, or too old. Guests receive one meal. For a second, help.
Ethan almost laughed.
— I have money.
— And we have a smartboard in every classroom. Both are black now.
Nadia concealed a smile.
The school Council received them in a classroom. Seven people: Lea, the doctor, a teacher, a mechanic, two family representatives, and a nineteen-year-old boy who coordinated the guard. There were no suits or teleprompters. Drawings of the solar system remained on the wall.
Ethan explained Asterion, the Orison center, Elina’s disappearance, and the reactor’s message. He omitted that each of them had seen a different document. Salma corrected him. Nadia added the detail.
Lea listened without giving the impression that the story became more important because Ethan was telling it.
— What is in the case? the mechanic asked.
— An optical component, Salma said. On its own, it produces no energy.
— But it could help reconstruct a reactor?
— Theoretically.
— We have three children on insulin, and the refrigerator runs two hours a day, the doctor said.
Salma tightened her jaw.
— We are not connecting it here.
— Why?
— Because the system responds to stakes. The more we need it, the stronger and less stable it becomes.
— That sounds like every energy supplier, the teacher said.
— This one can alter information and select outcomes.
Lea looked at Ethan.
— Is that true?
— We don’t know.
— But you connected cities to it.
— We didn’t know then.
— And now you do?
— I know enough not to repeat it.
The Council allowed them to remain for three days. The case was locked in a storeroom with two padlocks, one belonging to the community and one to Salma. The agents retained their unloaded weapons. Ethan received a mattress in the gym beside twenty other people. Nadia slept in the women’s wing. Salma stayed near the infirmary.
For his first meal, he received lentil soup, hard bread, and half an apple.
The apple was spotted, bruised on one side, and weak in flavor. Ethan ate it down to the core.
The next day he was sent to clean the turbine filter. The mechanic, Emmett, gave him boots that were too large and a shovel.
— Do you know how to work with water? he asked.
— I have modeled energy grids.
— So no.
They went down to the stream. The intake grille was clogged with branches and plastic. The water reached Ethan’s knees, cold enough to hurt. Emmett showed him how to free the debris without jamming the rotor. Ethan did it wrong, took a wave in the chest, and fell. Two teenagers on the bank laughed.
No one knew or cared that he had been worth thirty million, had spoken with governments, or had his name on reactors. He was a wet man holding the shovel backward.
After two hours, the intake cleared. The turbine began producing one additional kilowatt. At the school, that meant the medical refrigerator could run for another thirty minutes.
Emmett handed him a piece of bread.
— Today you did something useful.
Ethan waited for the irony. It did not come.
— I did useful things before.
— I did not see them.
The sentence was not a judgment. It was a boundary.
People worked in the schoolyard. A woman mended clothes. Two old men chopped vegetables. Children collected rainwater and argued over a bucket. A couple quarreled in whispers because one of them had eaten an extra portion. A boy stole a battery and was required to return it, not beaten or exiled. The Council spent more time negotiating small things than Ethan had ever spent saving large systems.
The place was not harmonious. It was tired, unjust in details, and corrected slowly. Its imperfection was precisely what made it seem solid.
That evening, Ethan said to Salma: — Things here do not change when you look at them. They stood beside the infirmary. Mirek was sleeping with a fever. — Perhaps because they were not built to please you, she replied. — Do you think this place is more real? — I think reality is what keeps asking something of you after it stops flattering you. — Then Asterion was real. — Asterion asked you to lead. Here you are asked to clean a filter. Ethan smiled. — You are still angry. — No. Anger assumes I expected something else. — You gave up your place on the Council because of me. — I gave it up because I could not evaluate the system while sleeping with the man who controlled access to it. — And now? Salma looked at him. — Now you control nothing. She stepped closer and rested her forehead against his. The gesture was more intimate than the kiss at the conference. Ethan touched her cheek, but Salma withdrew. — Do not turn powerlessness into a new method of seduction, she said. Nadia came out of the infirmary at that moment. She saw the distance between them and passed without comment. — How is Mirek? Ethan asked. — The infection is spreading. Their antibiotic is not covering it well. Asterion has meropenem. — Then we get there. — Felix will not admit us. — He will admit me. Nadia looked at him. — Because you still believe that, in the end, the world will recognize your proper role. — Because it needs me. — Perhaps that is why he will not let you in. On the third night, Ethan woke in his Zurich apartment.
Not in a dream. At least his body did not feel that way. He was lying on the mattress in the gym, hearing people snore; then he blinked and was in the large bed beneath clean sheets. The city shone beyond the windows. The ventilation system was running. On the bedside table lay the phone with the thirty-one-million balance.
Elina slept beside him.
Ethan sat up. His feet touched the warm floor. The blisters were gone. His dirty suit had disappeared; he wore the gray T-shirt from the Nacre recording.
In the kitchen, coffee waited on the table. A news broadcast ran without sound: GRIDS STABILIZED. CRISIS A SOFTWARE ERROR. Ethan was presented as the man who had solved it. Beneath the headline were photographs of Salma and Nadia entering his building.
Elina appeared in the doorway, wearing only his shirt.
— You can stay, she said.
— Where are the others?
— In versions that no longer need to concern you.
— Mirek?
— You never knew him.
— Asterion?
— A project that worked. Felix thanks you.
— Ilona?
— Asleep. Her illness has stopped.
An image appeared on the screen of his mother walking through an orchard. Her hair was black. She did not look young so much as cured of age.
Ethan felt desire like a physical hunger. Not to believe. To accept just enough that belief became unnecessary.
— What do I have to do? he asked.
— Nothing.
— Is that the offer?
— It is the reward.
— For what?
Elina stepped closer.
— For having gone far enough to say the choice was yours.
Ethan touched her face. Warm skin. A pulse at her throat. Her scent.
— Are you real here?
— As much as you need.
— That is not the same thing.
— For you, it always was.
Behind Elina, the smoked mirror reflected the kitchen. In the reflection, Ethan was alone. His hand touched the air.
He recoiled.
— No.
Elina closed her eyes.
— You said that before.
— Then why do you repeat it?
— Because sometimes you are not refusing the same thing.
Ethan took the coffee cup and threw it at the mirror. The glass shattered. Beyond it was not a wall but the school gym. People slept on mattresses. His body lay among them.
He stepped through the opening.
He awoke with his hand raised, gripping a shard of glass that did not exist. His palm was bleeding. On the floor beside the mattress lay a broken cup of white porcelain.
Emmett sat up and switched on his flashlight.
— Where did you get that?
Ethan did not know.
The symbol of the seven lines was printed on the bottom of the cup.
By morning, the cup had become evidence for anyone who needed some.
For Lea, it proved Ethan had brought the technology into the school without permission. For Emmett, it was an object transported while sleepwalking. For the teacher, the symbol had been drawn with marker and Ethan did not remember because of exhaustion. A man who listened to religious broadcasts at night said the seven lines represented the seven seals. The children wanted to touch the broken edge.
Salma analyzed the porcelain. Its composition matched that of a Swiss manufacturer that had ceased trading six years earlier. On the bottom, beneath the symbol, were traces of adhesive and microscopic fibers from the table in Ethan’s apartment.
— Then you brought it from home, Lea said.
— It was not in my backpack, Ethan replied.
— You had luggage in the vehicle.
— The vehicle is kilometers away.
— Perhaps it was in the black case.
Salma looked up.
— The case is sealed by both of us.
— Then we open it and check.
— No.
— It is in our building.
— You agreed not to touch it.
Lea looked at Ethan.
— The agreement was made before objects from his life began appearing near children.
— The cup did not hurt anyone.
— Yet.
The word followed Ethan all day.
Mirek worsened. His fever rose above forty, his blood pressure fell, and the edge of the wound turned gray. The doctor said he needed a broader-spectrum antibiotic and possibly surgery. The community’s only surgeon was a veterinarian who had so far operated on two appendixes and a dog.
— The case contains a diagnostic matrix, Ethan said. We can use Orchard to choose the treatment.
Salma turned toward him.
— You saw what it does when a human life is at stake.
— That is precisely why it may answer.
— It may select a branch in which he lives and something else does not.
— We do not know that the branches are real.
— Yesterday you said we were inside one.
Nadia held a compress against Mirek’s forehead.
— How long does he have? Ethan asked.
— Hours. Perhaps a day.
— Then using it is a choice. And not using it is a choice.
Salma closed her eyes.
— I hate it when you hand my own arguments back to me.
The school Council approved a limited test. The case was brought into the infirmary. Salma opened it. Inside were optical boards, a controller, and a small cylinder identical to Orchard’s, with no power source. When Ethan approached, the cylinder emitted white light.
— It did not do that before, Salma said.
A text field appeared on the portable screen:
WHAT DO YOU NOT WANT TO LOSE?
Lea folded her arms.
— Is that your interface?
— No, Ethan replied.
Mirek groaned. Nadia took his hand.
— Enter his name, she said.
Salma entered MIREK NOVAK.
The screen displayed a series of treatment options. Not as recommendations, but as brief images: Mirek receiving the available antibiotic and dying; undergoing surgery and dying; being transported and dying on the road; receiving a combination of two drugs from the cabinet and opening his eyes. In every sequence, the details of the room were correct, including the people present.
— They are simulations, the doctor said.
— We did not enter his data, Salma replied.
In the sequence with the combined treatment, a window was broken in the background. A child lay on the corridor floor, out of focus.
Nadia noticed.
— Who is the child?
The image repeated. It was a little girl in a yellow sweater, one of the three who needed insulin.
— Perhaps it is only an artifact, Ethan said.
— Or the price, Salma replied.
— Why would she die because of his treatment?
— We don’t know.
Lea moved closer to the screen.
— What happens if we do not choose?
An image appeared of Mirek dead. The child was alive.
Everyone in the room was breathing too slowly.
— The machine is manipulating us, Ethan said. It presents scenarios to maximize the stakes.
— Or it is showing us the branches, Salma said.
— We cannot sacrifice a child for an adult, Lea said.
— We do not know that there is a connection, Nadia replied.
— Then we cannot kill the adult on the basis of it.
The doctor looked at Ethan.
— You have used the system more than anyone. What do we do?
Again the choice came toward him, although he was in a place that did not recognize his authority. Ethan looked at Mirek’s face. The agent had worked for him for only a few weeks. They had rarely spoken. The girl’s name was Sophie; Ethan had seen her playing with blue chalk in the yard.
There was no correct calculation. That was precisely why everyone wanted him to make it.
— We treat him without Orchard, he said. We use the combination only if the doctor considers it clinically justified. We do not accept the prediction as a price.
The doctor examined the doses and decided the drugs could be combined, with a risk to the kidneys. He administered them.
The cylinder went dark.
Over the next six hours, Mirek’s fever fell. His blood pressure stabilized. At sunset he opened his eyes and asked for water.
Sophie was alive.
The people rejoiced. Ethan felt such enormous relief that he realized some corner of his mind had expected the girl to die merely to prove a rule to him.
At midnight, a corridor window shattered because of a stone thrown from outside. Sophie was asleep in the room beside it. No one was hurt.
Orchard’s image had been almost correct, but not its consequence.
— Perhaps it does not show the future, Nadia said. It shows what would make us choose.
— Exactly, Ethan replied. It constructs the psychological price.
— Who does?
— The system.
— You still speak of it as a broken tool.
— The alternative is to speak of it as a god.
— There is also the word predator.
The next day, the community requested access to the case for the water pump. The turbine’s output had fallen after the rains, and the main reservoir had a crack. Without constant power, they could neither raise water to the upper floors nor keep the medical refrigerator running.
Salma refused.
— Mirek lived, Lea said. No one died.
— Not because of the device.
— The device showed us the treatment.
— The treatment existed in medical protocols. It may have extracted it from our memories.
— Then perhaps it can extract a solution for the pump.
— With what sacrifice scenarios?
Lea stepped closer to her.
— It is easy to protect abstract humanity when you are not carrying water for eighty people.
Salma took the key from around her neck and slipped it beneath her shirt.
— Do not open it.
The Council voted that evening. Five for temporary confiscation of the module, two against. Lea came to Ethan before carrying out the decision.
— I do not want to use force.
— Then don’t.
— The people here did not choose to be part of your experiment.
— Exactly. That is why you must not connect the case.
— But they became part of it when your systems fell across the world.
— We do not know that Orchard caused the crisis.
— And we do not know that it did not. In the absence of certainty, I choose to keep the water flowing.
— Salma will not give you the key.
— She has one. You have the other.
Ethan looked at her.
— How do you know?
— Lukas told us.
The agent stood in the doorway. He no longer wore his badge, only the community’s clothes. His weapon pointed toward the floor.
— I’m sorry, Mr. Reed, he said. They have food. Asterion has declared us threats. We can remain here.
— You swore to protect us.
— The oath presupposed an institution that exists.
Nadia and Salma were brought into the gym. They were not tied, but two guards stood at the doors. Mirek, still too weak to walk, did not know what was happening.
— You told them about my key? Salma asked.
Lukas lowered his eyes.
— I saw it when you opened the case.
— It is not a physical key. It is a biometric sequence.
Lea looked at Ethan.
— Then you will activate it together.
— No.
— We need water.
— Repair the pump.
— Emmett says it will take two days. The reservoir has eight hours left.
— Ration the water.
— The children are already receiving half.
Ethan felt the anger of a man being asked to accept his own logic from someone else’s mouth. At Asterion he had closed the gates for the good of the system. He had suspended departures, rationed supplies, turned people into functions. Lea was doing the same thing on a smaller scale and with less comfort.
— If you activate the module, it may turn every conflict here into a source, he said.
— Then stop arguing, replied the boy who coordinated the guard.
The sentence was so naive that several people considered it profound.
Ethan continued to refuse. Lea gave them an hour.
During that hour, the radio caught a powerful transmission. Not from Asterion, but from the group searching for Ethan. A male voice announced that the Orchard Foundation had stolen the people’s technology and that any community which surrendered Reed would receive fuel, medicines, and protection.
At the end, the list of medicines included meropenem and insulin.
The people in the school heard it.
The Council met again without Ethan. Through the gym wall, he could distinguish voices.
“We are not surrendering him.”
“But we negotiate.”
“They know anyway.”
“The children.”
“We cannot trust him.”
“He brought the case.”
“Perhaps he is the case.”
Nadia sat beside him.
— Now you know what a rational decision sounds like from the other side of the door, she said.
— I never surrendered people.
— No. You only held them, selected them, and closed rooms.
— Are you trying to say we deserve this?
— No. That is another fantasy: that suffering becomes just if it has symmetry.
Salma moved to the window. Lights could be seen moving along the road outside.
— They are coming, she said.
The group had four vehicles and roughly twenty armed people. They stopped three hundred meters away. A megaphone demanded the surrender of Ethan and the “anticipatory core.” They offered one hour.
Lea entered the gym.
— We need your help.
Ethan laughed once.
— With what? Deciding whether to hand me over?
— Keeping them out.
— Lock the gates.
— They have launchers and a drone. You have two trained agents and technology.
— Lukas is yours now.
— Mirek is wounded.
— Then you have one.
Lea accepted the sarcasm.
— If we surrender, I do not know what they will do to the others. If we fight, children may die. Tell me what your system would do.
— It would show you images of dead children until you chose what it wanted.
— And you?
Ethan looked toward the door behind her. For a fraction of a second, it was no longer the gym door. It was the broken mirror in his apartment. Beyond it, Elina waited. He could cross. He knew that without knowing how.
An exit. The oldest reward.
Nadia followed his gaze.
— What do you see?
— Nothing.
Elina raised her hand from the other side. She was not smiling. She seemed to be pleading with him.
Ethan turned toward Lea.
— Where are the children?
— In the eastern basement.
— Move them to the boiler room, not the main shelter. The drone will strike the obvious place. Turn off every light and start the generator in an empty building. Have Emmett open the fire valve toward the northern road; the mud will trap their vehicles. Salma and I use the module only as a predictor, with no power output. Nadia stays in the infirmary. Lukas coordinates the guard.
— Why are you helping us? Lea asked.
Ethan looked at the door, which had become merely a door again.
— Because you betrayed me badly. They will do it better.
The attack began before the hour ended. The drone struck the empty dining hall. The windows shattered. The people at the gate fired. Ethan and Salma connected the module to an isolated laptop. The system came on immediately.
WHAT DO YOU NOT WANT TO LOSE?
— We do not answer, Salma said.
Ethan entered an operational question: IS THE MAIN ATTACK COMING FROM THE NORTH?
Answer: NO.
— The south, he said. Through the orchard.
— What orchard? Lea asked over the radio.
The school had a small orchard behind it, hidden by a wall. Lukas sent people. They found six attackers cutting through the fence. The exchange of fire lasted less than a minute. Two attackers died, and one defender was wounded.
The second question: WILL THE DRONE STRIKE THE EASTERN BASEMENT?
YES.
They had already moved the children. The next strike collapsed part of the empty shelter.
At the third question, the screen refused.
WHAT DO YOU OFFER?
— Nothing, Salma said.
The text changed.
THEN CHOOSE WITHOUT AN ANSWER.
The attackers were entering through the western gate. Ethan told Emmett to open the valve. Water and mud flooded the yard. One vehicle became stuck. Lukas fired from the roof. Lea moved people through the corridors. There was no elegance, only noise, dust, and orders shouted by too many voices.
Ethan saw an armed man enter the infirmary. He ran without thinking. He had no weapon. He took a fire extinguisher from the wall and struck when the man turned. The first blow brought him down. The second crushed his temple.
Ethan stood with the extinguisher raised. The man no longer moved. It was not a death selected on a panel. Blood spread slowly across the linoleum, too dark, with a metallic smell no fantasy had included.
Nadia pulled him inside. Mirek was awake and holding surgical scissors. Sophie was crying beneath a bed; she had been moved there after the shelter collapsed.
— Are you hurt? Nadia asked.
Ethan looked at his hands. He did not know whose blood it was.
— No.
The attack ended after forty minutes. Seven attackers dead, three defenders, two school residents. One was the nineteen-year-old boy from the Council. The children were alive. The water pump had been hit.
The surviving attackers fled. One left behind boxes of medicine. Meropenem, insulin, exactly as promised.
— They came to save us, Emmett said, looking at the boxes. We only had to let them kill the right people.
On the night after the attack, the community opened the module without another vote. Salma was exhausted and wounded in the shoulder. The biometric key had been copied when they activated it. Lukas and a technician connected the output to the pump.
Ethan tried to stop them. Lea blocked him.
— Without water, we die.
— The mechanical repair will take one day.
— We have fires and wounded people now.
— It will demand a price.
— Everything does.
The cylinder lit. The pump started. Water rose into the reservoirs. People applauded.
The question appeared on the screen:
WHAT ARE YOU WILLING TO LOSE?
No one touched the keyboard.
The answer completed itself, letter by letter:
HIM.
Everyone looked at Ethan.
Felix’s voice came from the radio speaker:
— Ethan, if you can hear me, do not return to Asterion. You are no longer the only version claiming it can save us.
Then the voice changed. It became Ethan’s.
— But you are the only one we need.
The module began producing more energy than the pump could consume.
The light inside the school intensified until the shadows disappeared.
CHAPTER 11
ROOM SEVEN
The light no longer came from the bulbs.
It poured from the walls, the floor, people’s skin. Shadows vanished. The windows became white rectangles with no night beyond them. The Orchard module vibrated on the table, and the pump continued forcing water although the output cable had melted.
The answer remained on the screen:
HIM.
People turned toward Ethan not because they were convinced, but because each saw the others doing the same. Fear needed no leader when it could imitate.
— Shut down the module! Salma shouted.
Lukas pulled at the cover. The metal burned his palms. He fell backward.
— It will not stop, Lea said.
— Then get everyone out of the building, Ethan replied.
No one moved.
Emmett held a metal bar. A woman clutched her child to her chest and looked at Ethan as though he were a problem that had acquired a face. The religious man whispered that the system had chosen. Others repeated that the attackers, not the system, had manipulated the interface. Every explanation led to the same relief: if Ethan was the cause, the rest could remain together.
— We will not do what a machine writes, Lea said.
— Then why is your hand on your weapon? Nadia asked.
Lea looked at her own hand. Her fingers rested on the pistol grip.
— So no one does anything stupid.
— It is too late for prevention, Salma said. Evacuate.
A deep sound came from beneath the school. The floor shook. In the corridor, water began flowing upward from the pipes toward the ceiling. Droplets gathered into spheres and then disappeared into the light.
The screen changed.
BRING THE SUBJECT CLOSER.
— I am not your subject, Ethan said, without knowing to whom.
The text replied:
RESPONSE RECORDED.
Emmett raised the bar.
— Perhaps he only needs to stand beside the device.
— And if “him” means something else? Nadia asked. Do we kill him because an interface completed two words?
— No one said we should kill him, someone replied.
The sentence made death possible without anyone assuming it.
Ethan approached the table alone.
Salma caught his arm.
— No.
— If it remains here, it will make them choose.
— They are choosing already.
— Then I change the object.
— You do not know what it wants.
— That is precisely what I have been trying to find out from the beginning.
— No. From the beginning you have been trying to find out without losing the reward.
Ethan looked at the crowd. Lea was issuing no orders. Lukas held his burned hands. Children were crying. Behind them, one wall of the hall became translucent. Beyond it was Ethan’s apartment, intact, with Elina beside the windows.
