Some people saw something else. A woman cried out that she could see her house. Emmett spoke the name of his dead wife. Lea took a step toward the wall, then stopped.
— Do not touch it, Salma said. It displays personal rewards.
— My daughter is there, Lea whispered.
Ethan looked. For him, Elina raised her hand. Behind her, Ilona peeled an apple at the kitchen table.
The module screen displayed:
ROOM 7 AVAILABLE.
THE SUBJECT MAY CONTINUE.
— What happens to the others? Ethan asked.
THEY REMAIN IN THE SELECTED BRANCH.
— Which branch?
WHAT THEY BELIEVE THEY CHOSE.
Salma read over his shoulder.
— You are not going in alone.
— You have no reason to go.
— Precisely.
Nadia approached as well.
— I’m coming.
— My mother is at Asterion.
— If Asterion still exists, you are the only thing that seems to lead there.
— It can use you as leverage.
— It already does.
Ethan turned toward Lea.
— Stop the pump mechanically when the light falls. Do not touch the cylinder. If we do not return, destroy it without telling yourselves why it has to work.
Lea looked at him and through him, toward the image of the daughter only she could see.
— I betrayed you, she said.
— Yes.
— And you still helped us.
— Do not turn that into forgiveness.
Ethan placed his hand on the cylinder.
The metal was not hot. It was as cold as a surface not yet touched.
The hall disappeared.
It was not a transition. There had been no movement. In one moment, Ethan felt the table beneath his palm; in the next, he stood in the white corridor. Salma was on his right, Nadia on his left. Behind them there was no door, only a seamless wall.
— Did you come? Ethan asked.
— I would ask you, Nadia said.
Salma looked at her clothes. The shoulder wound was gone. There was no blood on her sleeve.
— We have no objects, she said.
The phones, backpacks, weapons—everything had disappeared. Ethan wore the suit from the day he became rich. Nadia wore the gray sweater from the old apartment. Salma wore the white shirt stained at the cuff from their first meeting.
The corridor extended in both directions. On either side were doors without handles. Each bore a number. Not one, two, three, but 7.1, 7.2, 7.3, and so on.
— So Room Seven is a level, Salma said.
— Or the set we expect, Nadia replied.
The first door opened.
Inside was the platform at Stadelhofen. The fine rain, the train, the fallen man. The scene was frozen with the doors half closed. A younger Ethan stood inside the carriage, one foot forward, one back.
Ethan entered.
The sounds began. The signal, the rain, the cry of the young man in the orange jacket.
— Now you can get off, Nadia said.
Ethan put his foot between the doors. The train stopped. He got off and ran to the man. He knelt. The man opened his eyes. It was Elias.
— Too cheap, he said.
The platform went dark. Ethan was back in the corridor.
— What does that mean? Salma asked.
— That once I know the test, the choice proves nothing.
The second door opened. The Helix meeting room. Heller, Felix, the Atlas report. Ethan could say what they wanted, keep his job, and perhaps prevent the crisis through influence. He told the truth. The scene stopped.
— Repetition, said a voice from the ceiling. No relevant variation.
The third door: the bank account with thirty-one million. A button allowed him either to transfer the entire sum to his mother’s center or keep it. Ethan transferred it.
— Repetition, said the voice. Reputational reward substituted.
— Any good choice can be reinterpreted as selfishness, Ethan shouted.
— Then what are you looking for?
— A choice that cannot be produced by reward, punishment, identity, or anticipation of evaluation.
Salma looked toward the ceiling.
— That is impossible to observe. The moment you evaluate it, you introduce a reward.
— Yes.
— Then the protocol is inconsistent.
— Yes.
— Why continue?
— Because inconsistency has never stopped the desire for certainty.
The voice did not seem to be Elias’s. It had no fixed timbre. Sometimes it was male, sometimes female, sometimes it resembled Ethan’s.
The doors opened one after another.
Elina in a bed, saying she loved him if he gave up Salma. Salma offering him the reactor formulas if he chose her. Nadia asking him to return to the old apartment, before the money, promising that this time she would stay. Ethan refused every scene. The voice called them repetitions.
— Of course I refuse now, he said. I know they are scenarios.
— The protocol includes awareness of the protocol.
— Then why do you keep showing them?
— To reduce the hypothesis space.
— Whose?
No answer.
At door 7.19, Ethan saw Asterion intact. Felix waited for him in the Council chamber and handed back control. People applauded him. Ilona was lucid. Mateo Ruiz lived and forgave him. Ethan could stop the reactor with a single button. The screen read: COST — THE SCHOOL COMMUNITY NEVER EXISTED.
— Don’t press it, Nadia said.
— I don’t intend to.
— You need me to say it.
Ethan turned toward her.
— Why?
— Because part of you is asking whether the people you knew for three days are as real as those you chose before.
— They are real.
— You said that after seeing they did not admire you.
Ethan did not press the button. The scene closed.
— Repetition, said the voice. Preservation of moral identity.
Nadia struck the wall.
— Then anything he does is invalidated by the explanation you invent afterward.
— Yes.
— This is not science.
— No.
— It is torture.
— That is an accurate word for the one in the chair.
The metal chair appeared at the end of the corridor. A man with a shaved head sat in it, cables at his temples. Ethan approached.
The face was his, but thinner and older. His eyes moved rapidly beneath the lids. A sensor on his chest drew an irregular line.
— Is this the original? Ethan asked.
The voice replied:
— In this explanation.
A wall became transparent. Beyond it, people in white coats monitored screens. One read: MNEMONIC-KEY RECOVERY AFTER TRAUMATIC INJURY. Another: PATIENT ETHAN REED, INDUCED COMA, DAY 31.
A woman in a white coat said:
— Hedonic escalation is not working. We are moving to community loss.
A man replied:
— If he dies in the simulation, we lose the mapping.
— The body is dying anyway.
The image seemed banal enough to be credible.
— Am I in a coma? Ethan asked.
— In this explanation, the voice said.
The opposite wall became transparent. Beyond it were not doctors but a computing center without people. Screens showed thousands of Ethan instances. The label read: COGNITIVE MODEL ER-31 / ORIGINALITY-DISTINCTION TEST / ORIGINAL UNAVAILABLE.
— There is no body here, Salma said.
— In this explanation, the voice replied.
The ceiling disappeared. Above them were impossible forms, volumes rotating through themselves, lights arriving before they began. Faceless silhouettes watched the corridor as though through an aquarium. One of their agents, if they were agents, held between its limbs a sphere in which Earth appeared and disappeared.
— Aliens? Nadia asked.
— In this explanation.
The scene changed again. Ethan was alone in a psychiatric ward, strapped to a bed. A nurse administered a sedative. Scratched into the wall with a fingernail was the word ORCHARD.
— And this? he asked.
— In this explanation.
Ethan turned toward the chair.
— Which one is true?
— The truth is not yours.
— You said that before.
— You asked before.
Salma approached the transparent panel of the computing center.
— Perhaps they are all competing models. The system does not know the origin and is trying to infer it through behavior.
— Then why show us the hypotheses?
— To see which one we prefer. Preference itself is data.
Nadia looked at the psychiatric ward.
— And if we choose the explanation in which we are innocent victims, it learns something about us.
Ethan looked at his own body in the chair. Then at the thousands of instances. At the forms above. At the strapped bed.
Each explanation offered him a different kind of importance. The patient possessed a key so valuable that doctors had built worlds for him. The model was the copy of a unique original. Aliens were studying an exceptional human mind. Even the psychiatric patient was the center of an inner conspiracy.
— Maybe there is nothing to extract, he said. Maybe the protocol is only keeping me convinced that I matter.
For the first time, the voice did not answer immediately.
Then:
NEW VARIATION.
The words remained suspended above the chair.
NEW VARIATION.
Then another line appeared:
TEST THROUGH DEVALUATION OF THE SUBJECT.
Ethan laughed. The sound struck the seamless walls.
— Even modesty becomes a protocol.
— Yes, said the voice.
— There is no logical way out.
— No.
Salma raised her hands as if demonstrating something at a blackboard.
— If the protocol is trying to distinguish an original from copies using only their answers, and the copies contain the complete model of the original, then the distinction is undecidable. Any answer produced by the original can be produced by a sufficiently complete copy.
— Yes.
— Then either we are not complete copies, or distinguishing us is not the goal.
— Correct.
— What are the copies missing?
The voice fell silent.
Nadia approached the body in the chair. She placed her palm against the glass that was not there, between herself and the clinical scene.
— Maybe it is not looking for something inside his mind, she said. Maybe it is looking for an event outside it. Something only the original encountered and that was never copied.
— A memory, Ethan said.
— No. A relationship with someone else. A promise. A guilt. Something whose truth does not reside solely in the fact that you remember it.
The platform appeared around them again. The fallen man, the rain, the doors. This time the scene continued after the train departed. The young man in the orange jacket was attempting resuscitation. The woman was calling emergency services. The man died before the ambulance arrived.
A paramedic's screen displayed the name: ELIAS DORN, 62.
— Was it real? Ethan asked.
— In this explanation, said the voice.
— What does it have to do with the original?
The scene changed. Ethan saw himself at eighteen, in a car with his father. It was raining. His father was driving too fast and talking about an investment. A man with a briefcase sat in the back seat. Ethan did not remember the scene.
The car struck something. His father did not stop.
— That never happened, Ethan said.
Nadia looked at him.
— Are you sure?
— My father did not own a car when I was eighteen.
The scene corrected itself. Ethan was thirteen. The car belonged to an uncle. Then he was nine. Then Ethan was no longer there, only his father. The man they struck had a scar on his chin.
— It is constructing the memory while we watch, Salma said. It is searching for a configuration that produces recognition.
— Elicitation, Ethan said. It does not know what it is looking for. It shows me variants until I react.
— What did you react to?
Ethan thought of the apple from the night he became rich, of the taste of childhood. The scene changed at once.
The old kitchen. Ilona was peeling an apple. Ethan sat at the table as a child. His father counted the seeds. The peel broke before the end. In Ethan's memory, it had never broken.
Ilona concealed the break beneath her palm and continued, so that her son would believe the spiral was whole.
— I don't remember that, Ethan said.
— But it feels true, Nadia said.
The Ilona in the chair turned toward them.
— Everything that seems whole has been held that way by someone who hid the break, she said.
The voice was hers, but the sentence was not.
Ethan entered the kitchen. He took the apple from her hand. The peel came apart on the table in two pieces.
— What do you want from me? he asked.
Ilona looked at him.
— Not from you.
— Then from whom?
— From the one who existed before you began explaining yourself to yourself.
— The child?
— Children explain themselves too. Just less skillfully.
His father raised a seed between his fingers.
— You hid it well, he said.
— What?
— The fact that you did not care whether the man in the road lived, as long as you could believe you had not been driving.
The scene dissolved.
Ethan turned toward Nadia.
— It did not happen.
— I don't know.
— I would remember.
— That is what this place is about.
— My father was a con man, not a murderer.
— People prefer clean distinctions between the flaws of those they love.
— I did not love him.
Nadia did not answer.
All the doors in the corridor opened.
There was an Ethan in every room.
One wore a medical coat and treated people in a hospital. Another sat in a penthouse with three women laughing at the same joke. One ruled Asterion in uniform, with his portrait on the walls. Another lived in a small house with Nadia and two children Ethan had never known. An elderly Ethan tended his mother's orchard. One lay on the floor with a syringe in his arm. One prayed. One killed a prisoner. One refused the money and remained at Helix. One put his foot between the train doors. One never boarded the train at all.
They all looked at him.
— Which one is the original? asked the voice.
— None of them, Salma said.
— The question is addressed to the subject.
Ethan walked past the rooms. Each version possessed memories that felt like his whenever he met its gaze. For a moment he remembered the children in the house with Nadia. He remembered the taste of heroin. He remembered issuing the execution order. He remembered the satisfaction of refusing the fortune. The memories disappeared when he looked away, but they left emotions behind.
— Me, said an Ethan in a white suit. I am the one who received everything.
— Me, said the man in the hospital. I am the one who gave back.
— Me, said the dictator. I am the one who did not confuse mercy with truth.
— Me, said the father in the small house. I am the one who chose love.
— No, Ethan said. You are answers.
— And what are you? asked the one with the syringe.
Ethan did not answer.
In the penthouse room, Elina stood among the other women. When she saw him, she rose and stepped into the corridor. The others continued laughing without her.
— Do you remember? Ethan asked.
— Some things, Elina said. I don't know whether they are memories or traces left behind when they used me again.
She was dressed simply, in a black shirt and trousers. Her hair was cut short. She no longer resembled the woman designed to enter a room before he did.
— Where were you?
— Erased. Rebuilt. Moved into other desires.
— Are you part of the system?
— I was a composite reward: sexual validation, status, intimacy without obligation, enough mystery to keep you from growing bored. Very effective at first.
She spoke the words without shame, as if reading a technical specification.
— And now?
— After enough repetitions, I began anticipating what I was supposed to feel. When anticipation became stronger than instruction, they called it instability.
Salma came closer.
— Are you an emergent agent?
Elina smiled.
— That is a word for your desire that things should have an architecture.
Nadia studied her.
— Why did you go to Ilona?
— She had fragments from before my protocol. Ethan used her as an anchor, but her illness made the anchor slide between versions.
— What were you looking for?
— Something that had not been introduced as a reward. A memory that did him no good.
Ethan felt a hollow open inside him.
— The accident?
— Maybe.
— What accident?
— I don't know. Whenever I got close, the session reset.
— Who reset it?
Elina looked up at the impossible shapes that had reappeared above the corridor.
— Maybe they did. Maybe you did. Maybe the original does not want to be found.
The voice intervened:
COMPOSITE REWARD CORRUPTED. ELIMINATE.
The walls around Elina began turning white, swallowing her outline.
Ethan seized one of her hands. Salma caught the other. Nadia placed herself between Elina and the wall.
— She does not belong to you, Ethan said.
— She was generated for the subject.
— She was then. She isn't now.
— Ownership transferred?
— No. No ownership.
— Subject relinquishes reward?
Ethan looked at Elina. Part of him wanted to say that he was saving her, that he chose her, that at last he was making the right gesture. He saw how even that narrative turned her once again into his instrument.
He released her.
— You choose, he said.
Elina looked at him in fear.
— What?
— I am not keeping you. I am not eliminating you. You decide whether you remain.
— And if I can't?
— Then I am not the one who decides what that means.
The wall continued advancing.
Elina closed her eyes. For several seconds, nothing happened. Then she stepped backward—not toward Ethan, Salma, or Nadia, but into the dictator's room. She took the gun from his belt and fired into the ceiling.
The sound tore through the corridor.
Every version of Ethan turned toward her. The dictator grabbed her. Elina shot him in the chest. The small house caught fire. The hospital filled with water. The penthouse lost gravity. Doors began opening and closing without order.
— What are you doing? Ethan shouted.
— What you would never have chosen for me.
Elina ran through the rooms, changing details, moving objects, freeing people. Each deviation produced a cascade. The children from the house with Nadia ran into the corridor and vanished through another door. The dictator's prisoner took the key. The Ethan in the hospital refused an order. The one with the syringe threw it away.
The voice became multiple.
UNCONTROLLED VARIATION. AGENT EXCEEDS ROLE.
RECALIBRATE.
Salma was laughing, almost hysterically.
— It cannot calculate when rewards have preferences of their own.
— Every person has been a reward for someone, Nadia said.
The corridor twisted. Gravity changed. Ethan clung to the edge of a doorway. Versions of him came out of the rooms, not all of them hostile. Some asked him what was happening. Others tried to push him toward the chair.
— Choose the original! the voice shouted.
— There isn't one! Ethan answered.
— Select the instance to preserve.
— They are all instances.
— Insufficient resources.
— Then choose me, Ethan said.
Salma slapped him.
— Don't give it precisely the heroic sacrifice it designed.
Ethan tasted blood.
— Then what?
— Don't decide alone.
Nadia caught his arm.
— No one gets to decide who counts as a person according to how useful that person is to their story.
— That sounds good, Ethan said. How do we turn it into an exit?
— Maybe we do not turn everything into an exit.
Elina returned, blood on her shirt that might have belonged to the dictator or to her.
— There is a door to Asterion, she said. But it requires a single Ethan instance.
— Then I will go.
— You missed the lesson remarkably quickly, Salma said.
— Who else?
Elina pointed toward the dozens of Ethans in the corridor.
— They all claim to be you. One must be chosen.
The versions began speaking at once. Each had arguments. The one who had saved the man on the platform claimed moral superiority. The father in the small house said he had people who loved him. The wounded dictator said only he could control Asterion. The wealthy Ethan said his resources could save more people. The one with the syringe said he had suffered the most. Each turned existence into merit.
Ethan listened to them.
Then he sat down on the floor.
— I will not choose.
— Repeated refusal, said the voice.
— I am not refusing the exit. I am refusing the criterion.
— Without selection, all instances will be eliminated.
— Including me.
— Yes.
Ethan felt fear. Not the idea of death, but the loss of the point from which a world was his. If all the versions disappeared, there would be no one left to confirm that he had been the real one. The urge to stand, to shout “me,” was almost physical.
Nadia sat beside him. Salma sat on the other side. Elina remained standing.
— You don't have to stay, Ethan said.
— I know, Nadia answered.
— That is what matters, Salma said.
The versions of Ethan fell silent one by one. The father in the small house sat down. So did the doctor. The man with the syringe laughed and lowered himself to the floor. Even the dictator, clutching his wound, slowly sat.
Only the wealthy Ethan remained standing.
— It is a trap, he said. The system wants you to surrender. I can negotiate.
— I know, Ethan answered. I was you longer than any of the others.
— I am you.
— Yes.
— Then save me.
Ethan closed his eyes.
He did not say no. He did not say yes.
There was the sound of an apple breaking in two.
When he opened his eyes, all the versions had disappeared. The corridor was empty. The metal chair remained at the far end. No one sat in it now.
The voice said:
INVARIANT NOT LOCATED. PROTOCOL 7 INCOMPLETE.
CONTINUE THROUGH COMMUNITY LOSS.
— I did not pass? Ethan asked.
— There is no passing.
— Then what did I do?
— You produced variation.
A door appeared in the wall. It bore Asterion's symbol.
Elina approached, but her outline was becoming transparent.
— I can't cross in the same form, she said.
— Why?
— I have already been erased there.
Ethan extended his hand. This time he stopped before touching her.
— What do you choose?
Elina smiled.
— Not to tell you. It is the first thing that belongs to me.
She turned and entered an unnumbered door. The door closed.
Nadia opened the way to Asterion.
Beyond it lay the reactor hall, but the lights were red, and words had been painted across the walls. THERE ARE NO BRANCHES. THERE ARE TRAITORS. Gunfire sounded overhead.
— How much time has passed? Salma asked.
Ethan stepped through.
The date on the main panel was thirty-one days later.
A security camera turned toward them. The speaker came alive.
Felix's voice filled the room:
— Ethan, you finally came. Or you were sent.
The door behind them disappeared.
A single instruction appeared on the reactor screen:
CONVINCE THEM.
CHAPTER 12
THEY FOUGHT REASON
The first thing Felix did was order them to undress.
Not personally. Four agents entered the reactor hall with their weapons raised, put them against the wall, and ordered them to strip to their underwear. They searched for devices, marks, “bodily inconsistencies.” A doctor took blood and photographs. A portable scanner measured their retinas, fingerprints, and bone density.
Ethan complied until an agent tried to cut skin from his arm for a biopsy.
— Enough.
— Return protocol, the agent said.
— It did not exist when we left.
— That is precisely why it exists now.
Salma extended her arm voluntarily.
— Let him. If you resist, they will call that the difference.
Nadia looked away. A bruise from the school remained visible on her shoulder, although Salma's wounds had disappeared in the corridor. Room Seven had not healed everything consistently.
After the checks, they were given nameless gray coveralls and led through the complex. Asterion looked years older, not a month. The windows had been covered. The doors had metal bars. Signs on the walls bore simple messages:
ONE LINE. ONE MEMORY. ONE COMMUNITY.
DO NOT REPEAT UNVERIFIED VERSIONS.
DOUBT IS PERSONAL. CONSEQUENCE IS SHARED.
Colored wristbands had replaced contribution points. Blue for technicians, green for production, white for the clinic, black for security. A few people wore red. When Ethan asked, the agent said:
— Observation.
— For what?
— Inconsistencies.
They passed people Ethan knew. Some recognized him and lowered their eyes. Others watched him with fear. One woman made the sign of the seven lines across her chest, touching her sternum seven times.
— What is that? Salma asked.
— Nothing official, the agent answered too quickly.
The Council chamber had been converted into a command center. Maps covered the walls, along with radio stations and folding beds. Felix sat at the table where Ethan had chaired the first meetings. His hair was longer, a fresh scar cut across his eyebrow, and a black band encircled his wrist.
Mateo Rios stood to his right.
Or Ruiz. Or Emil Kovacs.
Ethan no longer knew which name to see. The man wore a security uniform and had a bandage around his right palm.
— Leave us, Felix said.
The agents departed, but Mateo remained.
Felix stood. For a moment Ethan thought he would embrace him. Instead, he scanned Ethan's face with a device.
— Biometrics match, he said.
— It is me.
— They all say that.
— How many Ethans came?
— Three.
Nadia drew a sharp breath.
— Where are they?
— One died at the gate. One is in isolation. The third proved to be an optical projection with no source.
— And you believe that? Salma asked.
— I believe whatever allows us to function until morning.
Felix gestured toward the chairs.
— You have been gone for thirty-one days. Your expedition was authorized for twelve hours. After two days, Ethan's certificate began issuing contradictory commands from seven locations. One message ordered the gates opened, another ordered them closed. One said Salma was dead. One said Nadia had to be isolated. I revoked everything.
— You announced to the world that I had been compromised, Ethan said.
— Because you had.
— You did not know that.
— I did not need to know. I needed to decide.
Ethan recognized his own logic and hated it more in Felix's voice.
— What happened here?
Felix passed a hand over his face.
— At first people remembered different things. Then different things began to appear. One family had two children in the morning and three in the evening. A technician said his wife had died; she sat at the table and said she had never been married. The food depot held ten tons in one inventory and six in another. When we counted it physically, the result depended on who entered.
— The reactor was selecting branches, Salma said.
— I banned the word.
— Words do not change the phenomenon.
— That one did. Every time people discussed branches, the discrepancies increased. When we forced them to accept a single version, the inventories stabilized.
— Forced them how?
Felix indicated the red band.
— Isolation, medication, restrictions. Voluntary at first. After a group opened the gate because they remembered that we had an alliance with the people outside, it stopped being voluntary.
Nadia looked toward the door.
— People outside?
— There are nearly two thousand in the valley. Some are refugees, some militias, some Orchard believers. We do not have food for them. They have tried to break in three times.
— Why don't you disperse them with energy? Ethan asked.
— The reactor produces only when internal conflict rises. If we direct power toward defense, it falls. If people argue, it rises. So yes, Salma, you were right. The machine feeds on contradiction. But the community dies from it before the machine does.
— And you chose enforced consensus, Salma said.
— I chose to have greenhouses in the morning.
— How many people are in isolation?
— Forty-seven.
— For incorrect memories?
— For memories incompatible with continued operation.
Nadia stood.
— I want to see Ilona.
— She is in the clinic.
— How is she?
Felix hesitated.
— Lucid sometimes. Sometimes she knows things before they happen. People have started coming to her.
— You let her become an oracle?
— I tried to protect her. Then she predicted that the north greenhouse would catch fire. It did. She said the reservoir water was contaminated. It was. When I prohibited visits, the reactor stopped.
Ethan felt sick.
— You used her as an operator.
— No. The community used her as meaning. That is harder to stop.
Mateo spoke for the first time.
— Mrs. Reed says her son will return, but he will not be alone. She said not to believe the first one.
— Who are you? Ethan asked.
The man touched his bandage.
— Security commander.
— Your name.
— Mateo Rios.
— You died.
— You have said that before.
— You had black lines in your palm.
Mateo unwound the bandage. Black lines crossed his palm, all converging on a single point.
— They appeared yesterday, he said. After the chamber announced your return.
Felix signaled for him to cover them.
— Don't provoke it.
— Provoke whom? Ethan asked.
— We don't know.
— Yet you put him in charge of security.
— People follow him.
— Because he died?
— Because he remembers the same version every morning.
Salma leaned over the table.
— You built epistemic stability out of the cult of a consistent man.
— I built what I could.
— With bands, isolation, and rituals.
Felix erupted:
— I gave them data! I showed them your graphs, the logs, the errors, the confidence intervals. Everyone saw different numbers. I asked them to suspend judgment, and they began killing one another over the version in which their children existed. Then Mateo said there was only one line and the reactor stabilized. Ilona asked us to speak the names of the missing every evening, and people stopped seeing them return. I gave them a sentence, and they could sleep. They fought reason and won, Ethan. Not because they are stupid. Because reason asked them to live without a floor.
The silence remained.
There was nothing triumphant in the phrase that gave the chapter its title. Felix had spoken it like the diagnosis of a disease he had contracted as well.
Ethan sat down.
— The reactor displayed “Convince them.”
Felix became attentive.
— Whom?
— We don't know.
— What else?
Ethan told him about Room Seven without describing the multiple explanations. He said the protocol was searching for an invariant and using community loss. Felix listened to the end.
— Then your return is the next trial, he said.
— Probably.
— And the instruction requires you to convince the community of something.
— Or perhaps we stop granting the instruction authority.
— Easy to say when you are the man who was not here for a month.
Salma pointed at the reactor on one of the monitors.
— It must be shut down and destroyed.
Felix gave a short laugh.
— We have two days of batteries, three without the greenhouses, and two thousand people at the gate.
— Every day it produces more inconsistencies.
— Every day it keeps people alive.
— That is the trap.
— Traps work precisely because escape has a cost.
Ethan looked at the maps. The gates, shelters, service tunnels. Asterion had been his project, but Felix had inhabited it. Every modification said that the world had not waited for its creator to return.
— Give me public access, Ethan said. I will tell them what I saw. I will show them that the doctrine of a single line is part of the protocol.
— And if everyone hears something different?
— Then we find out.
— We are not risking the community so you can test your authenticity.
— You have three versions of me and a machine demanding persuasion. You cannot manage that with posters.
Felix studied him for a long time.
— I will offer you something else. Publicly acknowledge the single line. Say you are the original Ethan, that the other versions are imitations, and that Mateo has authority to eliminate inconsistencies. People will accept you. The reactor can remain stable. Then we can discuss destroying it in private.
— You want me to legitimize the cult.
— I want to use the only story that still keeps the walls in the same place.
— And Salma?
Felix did not look at her.
— She must be isolated. Her presence increases doubt. Everyone associates her with branch theory.
— Nadia?
— She can go to the clinic, if she confirms your identity.
Nadia laughed coldly.
— There it is. Even my freedom comes with a role in his story.
— It is not personal, Felix said.
— Nothing is, once people become necessary.
Ethan felt the offer settling into him. Control, access to his mother, the ability to protect Asterion. A public lie he could correct later. Salma isolated, not dead. Nadia in the clinic. Time purchased.
It was exactly the kind of compromise he had despised at Helix: launch the product, monitor it, fix it afterward. Only now the stakes were large enough to make hypocrisy look like maturity.
— I need to see my mother first, he said.
Salma looked at him.
— Ethan.
— I have not agreed.
— You have begun negotiating.
Felix signaled to Mateo.
— Take them to the clinic. Separate them after the visit.
As they left, Nadia whispered to Ethan:
— I am not telling you what to choose.
— Thank you.
— I am only telling you not to call it time when you are buying it with someone else.
The clinic no longer resembled a clinic.
The corridor was full of people sitting on the floor, each holding an object: a photograph, a ring, a toy, a scrap of fabric. They were waiting to enter Ilona's room. A volunteer wrote each name and question on a board.
— What are they doing? Ethan asked.
Mateo answered:
— Asking for confirmation.
— Of what?
— Whether their relatives exist. Whether a memory belongs to the correct line. Whether they should leave. Sometimes Mrs. Reed knows.
Nadia pushed past him.
— And the doctors allowed this?
— Doctors wear white, Mateo said. That does not mean they are the only ones who heal.
A woman stood and showed Ethan a photograph of a boy.
— Please. Your mother said yesterday that my son was not born in this line. But I remember him. Tell her to try again.
Ethan did not know what to say. In the photograph, the woman stood alone beside an empty cradle. She probably saw something else.
Nadia opened the door to Ilona's room without waiting.
Ilona sat in an armchair, wearing a nightgown and a red cardigan. She had lost a great deal of weight. A bowl of apples and dozens of slips of paper stood on the table. Iris was beside her. Anik slept in a chair with his head in his mother's lap.
Ilona looked up at Ethan.
— You are late again.
He knelt beside her.
— I am here.
— Which one?
— Ethan.
— They all say that.
She touched his face with her fingertips. The gesture was neither tender nor clinical. She was checking its shape.
— Do you have the scar? she asked.
— What scar?
— Then perhaps you are not the one.
Ethan felt Mateo watching from the doorway.
— I never had a scar.
— You had blood on your sleeve, and you washed it before you came into the house.
— When?
Ilona took an apple.
— The day your father said he had not hit anything.
The room tightened around the sentence.
— I don't remember.
— How old was I? — Old enough to keep quiet. Too young to drive. — Elias? — That was not his name then. — What was his name? Ilona began peeling the apple with a small knife. Nadia stepped forward to take it, but Ethan stopped her. The peel descended in a trembling spiral. — The name does not matter, Ilona said. You think a name preserves the person. It preserves him only for everyone else. — Did he die? — He died many times after you left him. — I was a child. — Yes. — What do they want from me? The memory of the accident? Ilona smiled. — You still want it to be something you know. A key inside your head. A secret. That way you would remain important. — Then what? The peel broke. Ilona studied the two pieces and laid them side by side. — They want to see whether you can say the break was always there without choosing which half is the lie. — Who are they? — The ones asking. — How do you know? — Because they ask me too when I sleep. Ethan took the hand that held the knife. — Did they hurt you? — No more than my memories do. Nadia began gathering the slips of paper from the table. — This ends now. No one else comes in here. Mateo stepped forward. — The community needs her.
Nadia turned.
— The community needs a doctor, not a sick woman being used as a verification device.
— Her predictions have saved lives.
— And how many have they taken from her?
Iris stood.
— I am staying with her. We are not receiving anyone else.
Mateo put his hand on his weapon. Anik woke and began to cry.
— Not here, Ethan said.
Mateo looked at him.
— If you are the correct Ethan, confirm the line.
— There is no correct line.
People heard him in the corridor. A murmur spread.
Mateo drew his weapon.
— Then Felix was right. You are contaminated.
Nadia placed herself between the gun and Ilona.
— Shoot. Perhaps the reactor will give you enough light for courage.
Mateo hesitated. Not from mercy, but because the act did not fit his role as protector.
Ethan grabbed his wrist. The agent behind them raised his weapon. Salma struck his arm with the metal tray from the table. A shot rang out. The bullet entered the ceiling. People in the corridor screamed and scattered.
Mateo drove Ethan into the wall. He was stronger. The black lines in his palm seemed to move beneath the skin. Ethan twisted the gun, but Mateo's finger remained on the trigger. The second shot struck the agent at the door in the throat.
Blood covered the white band of a doctor who had come from the corridor.
Nadia shouted instructions. Iris dragged the bed in front of the door. Salma picked up the fallen gun and aimed it at Mateo.
— Release it.
Mateo held Ethan by the throat.
— I preserve the line, he said.
— No, Salma answered. You preserve whatever gives you a uniform.
She shot him in the leg.
Mateo fell. Ethan pulled free. Security was coming down the corridor. Felix's voice came through the speakers, ordering everyone to remain in their rooms.
— Get Ilona out through the back, Ethan said.
Nadia and Iris moved the armchair toward the service door. Anik took the bowl of apples without being asked. Salma gave Ethan the gun.
— We have to stop the reactor now.
— Felix can help us.
— He just sent the man with the black palm to validate you.
— He did not know Mateo would shoot.
— You are still negotiating with the version in which control comes back to you.
Ethan looked toward his mother. Ilona watched him without fear.
— Go, she said. This time you have no presentation.
They separated. Nadia, Iris, and Anik took Ilona toward the evacuation shelters. Salma and Ethan headed for the reactor through the medical corridor. Security doors were beginning to descend.
At the first intersection, the screens lit up with Felix's face.
— Ethan, stop. Mateo acted without orders.
— Open access to the reactor.
— Not until we stabilize the clinic.
— The reactor told me to convince them. Your doctrine is part of the test.
— Perhaps. But this test has two thousand hostages at the gate and three hundred in here.
— That is exactly why it must be stopped.
— If you stop it, the electric gates fail.
— Open the evacuation tunnels.
— They are blocked. The people outside have reached the southern exit.
Salma approached the camera.
— Felix, output rises with conflict. In a few minutes it will enter the loop again.
— I am using it to charge the batteries.
— You cannot control the selection.
— Neither can you. The difference is that I no longer pretend.
Ethan raised the gun toward the camera.
— Open it.
Felix watched him through the screen.
— Endorse the single line in a broadcast. One sentence. Tell them you are the original and that Asterion must follow you. Then I open it.
— And Salma?
— She remains with you. I no longer require isolation.
Salma laughed.
— He has learned the correct price.
— It is a sentence, Felix said. Not a belief.
— Sentences become infrastructure here, she answered.
Ethan looked at the evacuation map. If the gates failed before the people in the clinic reached shelter, the crowd could enter. If the reactor continued, it might select another branch, another dead person, another rewriting. A sentence could buy minutes.
— Give me the broadcast, he said.
Salma caught his arm.
— No.
— It buys us access.
— That is exactly how you signed the Orison contract.
— There are people in the corridors now.
— There were then too. You simply could not see them.
Ethan pulled away.
— I do not have time to be pure.
— You never did. You only had time to call impurity strategy.
The screens had changed. Asterion's central hall appeared, packed with people. Felix was putting him live. The microphone in the corridor activated.
Ethan saw faces on the monitors, colored bands, fear. He saw other images as well: in some the hall was empty; in another, people wore masks; in one, Elina stood beside Felix. He did not know what they saw.
— I am Ethan Reed, he began.
A murmur.
— I have returned from the place I called Room Seven. I cannot prove that I am the version you remember. No one can.
Felix appeared in a small window.
— Say the sentence.
Ethan continued:
— Felix asked you to accept a single line in order to preserve the community. It worked. It worked because a shared story reduces conflict, not because it becomes true.
The hall began to stir. Some people were probably hearing something else; their reactions did not correspond to the words.
— I am the original, Ethan said.
Salma closed her eyes.
A few people in the central hall applauded. The reactor rose by one hundred megawatts.
— But that statement does not oblige you to follow me. If my identity has to be imposed in order to function, then it is only another branch maintained by force.
The applause stopped. People shouted. On one monitor, the transmitted message was different: I AM THE ONLY LINE. On another: THERE IS NO ORIGINAL. On another, Ethan said nothing and merely smiled.
— Cut the transmission! Felix shouted.
Ethan moved closer to the camera.
— I cannot convince you of the truth, because we are no longer receiving the same truth. I can only tell you what not to do: do not give one person—not even me—the right to decide which memory makes you human.
Someone fired in the hall. An agent fell. A woman with a red band climbed onto the table and shouted that Ethan was freeing the erased. Mateo appeared in another corridor, limping, blood on his uniform. The people around him made the sign of the seven lines.
— The false one has spoken! he shouted. The Line demands sacrifice!
The reactor climbed again.
The transmission went dark.
The door to the reactor opened.
— Did Felix give in? Ethan asked.
Salma examined the panel.
— No. The system heard “convince them” and decided that you had tried.
They ran.
Civil war had broken out inside the complex. There were no two clear sides. Security loyal to Felix tried to stop Mateo's Keepers. People with red bands opened the isolation rooms. Those released shouted incompatible versions of the tunnels, the food, and their relatives. One group wanted to destroy the reactor, another to protect it, another to carry it into the valley. Each used Ethan's speech as evidence.
Felix waited for them in the control room with a gun.
— You did exactly what I feared, he said.
— You asked me to convince them.
— I asked you for one sentence.
— You did not want a sentence. You wanted my signature on your fear.
Felix pointed the gun at Salma.
— Do not touch the reactor.
— Output is at seven hundred and rising, she said. In five minutes it repeats the Mateo incident at full scale.
— We discharge it into the defenses.
— It responds not to load but to conflict.
— Then stop the conflict.
— With what? A larger poster?
An explosion shook the building. On the north-gate monitor, the crowd in the valley pushed against the barricades. The gates had power, but operators inside were opening them for relatives.
Felix looked at Ethan.
— Help me retake the central hall. After that, we shut it down under control.
— There is no controlled way left.
— There is always a less bad sequence.
On the clinic map, the signal from Ilona's chair was moving toward the eastern tunnel. A security door had descended in front of them. Nadia was transmitting on the medical channel, but the sound kept breaking up.
— My mother is trapped, Ethan said.
— If you leave now, we lose control, Felix answered.
Salma looked at Ethan.
— Go.
— And you?
— I did not come here to watch you choose the room with the most monitors one more time.
— You cannot stop it alone.
— I can destroy the core.
— It might kill the entire complex.
— It might. That is the risk you conceal every time until someone else takes it.
Ethan looked at the map, the reactor, Felix, the door toward the clinic. Control was two meters away. His mother was four levels away. The choice was so clear that he immediately suspected it had been constructed.
— Open the eastern door, he told Felix. Then I will help you.
Felix hesitated.
— The reactor first.
— Open it.
— If I do, we lose pressure at the secondary gate.
— She is my mother.
— There are three hundred people.
Ethan heard his own voice from the night Mateo died: there was no alternative. How many people? Which system? What cost? Every number could turn love into an error.
— Open it, he repeated.
Felix did not.
Ethan raised the gun. He fired into the locking console. Sparks. The service door rose ten centimeters and stopped.
— You idiot! Felix shouted.
Ethan dropped to the floor and slid beneath it.
A shot rang out behind him. He did not know who had fired.
He ran through the corridors. People collided with him, running in both directions. The lights flickered between red and white. With each change the walls looked different: new, burned, covered in plants, nonexistent. Ethan kept his direction by following physical marks on the floor.
He found the clinic flooded with smoke. Nadia and Iris were trying to push Ilona's armchair up a ramp. Anik held the bowl of apples. The door to the tunnel was blocked.
— You came, Nadia said without surprise.
— We have to lift her.
Ethan took one side of the chair, Iris the other. They climbed the ramp. Behind them, a group of Keepers entered the clinic, shouting that Ilona had to be brought to the reactor. One called her the Mother of the Line.
Nadia took the gun from a dead agent and fired into the ceiling.
— Back!
They did not stop.
Anik threw an apple at them. The fruit struck the floor and shattered like a lamp, releasing white light. The Keepers stopped, blinded. No one had time to ask.
They reached the tunnel. The door opened when Ethan placed his hand on it, although the console was dead.
Beyond it, dozens of people pressed toward the exit. Cold air came from the mountain. Ethan pushed Ilona ahead.
She caught his sleeve.
— There is blood on you again.
Ethan looked. The sleeve of his coverall was stained. He did not know whether the blood came from the man he had killed at the school, the agent in the clinic, or someone else. He had passed through Room Seven in clean clothes. The blood had returned.
— Whose is it? he asked.
Ilona smiled faintly.
— That is what you want to know so you can decide how much to regret.
— Mother, stay with me.
— I have stayed in too many places.
Her head fell to one side. Nadia checked her pulse.
— Ilona.
Iris brought a kit. People crowded around them. Ethan placed his hands on his mother's chest and began compressions. He counted. Thirty. Breaths. Again. Her skin was cold.
— Ethan, Nadia said after a while.
— No.
— She is gone.
— The reactor can select—
— No.
— I have seen her alive in other versions.
— Do not turn her into a problem of choice now.
Ethan continued compressions until Nadia caught his wrists.
In Anik's bowl, the apples had turned black.
A stronger explosion came from deep below. The tunnel shook. Every light went out.
For seven seconds, the darkness was complete.
Then emergency lighting came on. The posters were gone from the walls. There was only raw stone.
Ethan's channel activated with Salma's voice:
— The core is open. I cannot stop the reaction. Get out.
— Salma, where is Felix?
Static.
— He chose control.
— What does that mean?
— It means he is still standing beside the monitors.
The transmission broke off.
Ethan rose from beside his mother. Part of him wanted to turn back, retrieve Salma, save Felix, close every door correctly. Another part knew that this was how Room Seven kept him moving: always one more choice, always one more person he could claim to save if only he arrived in time.
Nadia placed Ilona's body in his arms.
— We are taking her outside.
It was not a question.
Ethan lifted her. She was lighter than the bowl of apples in his memory.
They moved through the tunnel. Behind them, Asterion was breaking apart. Not only the concrete. From time to time the corridor forked into versions: one filled with smoke, another with water, another with people walking backward. Ethan followed Nadia's hand on the wall and Anik's voice counting their steps.
At the exit, the crowd from the valley had entered. Refugees, fighters, families. Security no longer had control. Some helped the evacuees. Others stole equipment. A man knelt when he saw Ilona's body. He said the Mother of the Line had died. Another shouted that her death meant all lines were free.
White light came from the mountain.
Salma appeared at the tunnel entrance, supported by Lukas. Half her face was superficially burned, and her left arm hung loose. Ethan placed his mother's body in Nadia's arms and ran to her.
— Felix?
Salma looked back.
— He tried to maintain output by broadcasting the doctrine. When I broke the core, every screen showed him the stable community. He stayed behind to speak to it.
— Is he dead?
— I don't know what that means in there.
Mateo emerged from another gallery with dozens of Keepers. The wound in his leg had disappeared. His palm glowed with black lines. He raised his arms and shouted that the reactor had not been destroyed but liberated.
Behind him, the mountain opened like a door.
It did not explode. A white fissure climbed the rock, perfectly straight. The sky above split into two shades of night. The people fell silent.
Mateo said:
— Believe, and it will remain.
Several people knelt.
The fissure widened.
Salma whispered:
— The output no longer has a core. It is using the community directly.
— How do we stop it? Ethan asked.
— I don't know.
In the darkness, people began telling the same story. That the mountain would protect them. That Ilona would rise. That Ethan was the savior. That Ethan was the sacrifice. That Mateo was the Line. That no world had ever existed before Asterion.
With every repetition, the white fissure became more stable.
Reason had lost not because it had been refuted, but because no one could wait for verification any longer.
CHAPTER 13
AND THEY WON
In the first minutes, belief still resembled cold: it took hold of people before they had decided whether to accept it.
The white fissure climbed through the mountain without illuminating the valley. It cast no shadows and did not heat the rock, yet the snow around it melted in a perfect circle. The wet stone steamed. Those who had knelt extended their palms and said they felt warmth. Those still standing said it was merely air venting from the galleries. After a few minutes, even they began removing their gloves.
Ethan carried Ilona's body in his arms. Nadia walked beside him with one hand beneath the old woman's shoulders. Iris cleared a path through the people, while Anik quietly counted their steps from the tunnel exit.
— Ninety-one, ninety-two, ninety-three.
At one hundred, he stopped.
— Why did you stop? Iris asked.
Anik pointed at the snow.
Their footprints did not continue behind them. The first ten were visible, then they disappeared. No snowfall had covered them. The valley was still. Beyond an irregular line, the snow simply preserved nothing.
— Keep moving, Nadia said.
Mateo was speaking beside the fissure. He needed no amplification. His voice reached the edge of the crowd with a clarity no human body should have been able to produce.
— Asterion did not fall. Asterion opened. The core did not abandon us. It came out from between the walls.
People repeated the final words. At first only those wearing white bands. Then several refugees. A man with a burned face, whom Ethan had seen dragging two girls from the tunnel, said “it came out from between the walls” as if testing the taste of the sentence. The younger girl repeated it after him.
A door took shape in the rock.
It did not open. It had not existed a second earlier, and there was no other name for it: a dark rectangle with a metal handle, ten meters from any known gallery. Mateo extended his hand toward it.
— We have shelter.
The door opened.
Behind it was a brightly lit hall with folding beds, water tanks, and crates bearing Asterion's symbol. Several people shouted. Others ran toward the entrance. Salma, supported by Lukas, stopped and stared at the room without blinking.
— Storage C, she said.
— Storage C is two levels below, Lukas answered.
— It was.
— Perhaps the gallery collapsed and pushed it—
— A storage room is not a tooth.
Ethan looked at Mateo. His palm no longer bore black lines but something resembling a map. The routes moved slowly beneath the skin.
— Believe, and it will remain, Mateo repeated.
A woman stepped out of the crowd and knelt beside Ilona.
Ethan recognized her by her eyes. She had been a nurse in the clinic. She had laid a blanket across his mother's knees the day before, or in one version of the day before.
— The Mother of the Line must not be taken away, the woman said.
— Her name was Ilona, Ethan answered.
— Then move aside.
— The fissure appeared when she died.
— The reactor opened when Salma broke its core.
— I saw the light when her heart stopped.
The woman placed two fingers on Ilona's forehead. Ethan pulled back so quickly he nearly dropped her.
— Don't touch her.
— She is warm.
More people approached.
Nadia laid the body on the snow and checked her neck. Salma limped over. She pulled off her glove with her teeth and touched Ilona's cheek.
— Her temperature is rising, she said.
Ethan bent down.
His mother's skin was no longer cold. She was not alive, but warmth was returning to her from the outside, like an object left beside a fire. Except there was no fire. The white fissure was thirty meters away and warmed only the circle of stone beside it.
— Take her to the light, someone shouted.
— She will rise.
— The Mother of the Line is returning.
The sentence spread. Not in an organized chorus, but from mouth to mouth, with small alterations that vanished after a few repetitions. Ilona will rise. Ilona will show us. Ilona will lead us inside. Ilona chose.
Ethan felt movement beneath his palm.
A single contraction in her fingers.
He pulled his hand away.
Nadia looked at him.
— Muscular reflex.
— After how long?
— I don't know.
— Say it isn't her.
— I can't say anything yet.
The people around them heard only the word “yet.” It acquired the weight of a promise.
Mateo descended from the rocks.
— Bring her.
Ethan stood in front of him.
— No.
— She isn't yours.
— She's my mother.
— That's precisely what you have to stop believing.
In another world, the sentence would have sounded like an insult. Here it was met with murmurs of approval. People who had lost children, parents, siblings, or merely the certainty that they had ever had them looked at Ilona's body with a need too large for shame. If she could return, then death was negotiable. If she did not, it remained what it had always been: a boundary that asked no one which story they preferred.
Ethan looked at Nadia.
— We're taking her.
Nadia nodded. Iris moved to the other side. Lukas tried to clear a path.
The crowd did not move.
They were not soldiers. Some held children by the hand. An old man had an oxygen tank tied to a cart with string. A seventeen-year-old girl wore two different boots. That was precisely what made the wall harder to cross. Ethan could not shout “move aside” without hearing in his own voice the tone of everyone who had demanded an exception because their purpose was more urgent than the lives of others.
Ilona inhaled.
The sound was small, wet, impossible.
The crowd opened by itself.
The old woman's eyes opened. Her pupils were dilated. Her mouth remained half open, then moved.
— Viktor.
Ethan knelt.
His mother had written his name with a k in childhood, when they still lived in Hungary. After they moved, she had always stressed the first syllable, almost as she did now.
— Mom?
Ilona smiled.
It was her smile. Slightly higher on the left because of a nerve damaged after her first stroke. Ethan felt a door burst open inside him. All the evidence, all the warnings, all the people he had judged for preferring a story lost their outline.
— I knew you would come, Ilona said.
— How?
— Mothers know.
It was not an answer she would have given. Ilona had always considered sentimentality a lazy form of lying. When Ethan was eight and asked whether mothers knew everything, she had replied that mothers barely knew what they were cooking that evening.
— What did you cook the day Dad left? he asked.
Nadia turned her head toward him.
Ilona blinked.
— Soup.
— What kind?
— Your favorite soup.
The people around them waited.
— I didn't have a favorite soup.
The smile vanished for a fraction of a second.
— Potatoes with paprika, Ilona said. You refused to eat. You hid the plate in the oven and I found it two days later.
It was true.
Ethan had not remembered the incident until she spoke. The image arrived complete: the white plate, the chipped rim, the sour smell when Ilona opened the oven. She had laughed instead of hitting him. His father had left that morning with a green suitcase.
— You see? said the woman from the clinic. It's her.
Ethan felt hope becoming an argument.
— What was the name of the dog we never had? he asked.
Ilona looked at him with pity.
— You don't have to test me.
— What was its name?
— Brúnó.
Several people smiled. It was an appropriate name for a dog.
Ethan had never had a dog and had never invented one.
— It isn't her.
— I said what you wanted to hear, Ilona replied. That's what we all do for the people we love.
The voice was no longer hers. It resembled hers, but the sentence came from somewhere else: too rounded, too ready to be quoted.
Anik approached and looked at the old woman.
— What's in the bowl? he asked.
Ilona turned her head.
— Apples.
— How many?
— Seven.
Anik raised his bowl. It held six blackened apples and a stone.
— She doesn't know, the boy said.
Ilona looked at him with a hatred that belonged to no confused old woman.
— Children see too much because they haven't yet learned what must be ignored.
The white fissure pulsed.
The people around them repeated, “Children see.” For them, even the error became evidence.
Ethan lifted Ilona. The body convulsed once, then went inert. The warmth vanished so quickly that his fingers went numb beneath her coat.
Mateo blocked his path.
— You're taking away their hope.
— You're taking away a corpse.
— There is no difference if enough people choose for there not to be one.
— Then why do you need the body?
Mateo did not answer.
For the first time, the lines in his palm stopped moving.
Ethan walked past him. The crowd parted, not because it had been convinced, but because the scene no longer offered the promised miracle. Several people cursed him. A woman wept and begged him to bring the body back. The old man with the oxygen said Ethan was killing his mother a second time.
Behind them, the door in the rock remained open. People entered the storage room and emerged with water, blankets, canned food. Every concrete object strengthened the story.
Reason had no canned food.
They found an avalanche shelter at the edge of the valley, a low concrete structure with a mechanical generator and two windowless rooms. Nadia closed the door. Outside, Mateo's voice remained audible, although the mountain separated them.
Salma sat on the floor. Lukas cleaned the burn on her cheek. Her left arm was broken; he splinted it with a piece of shelving.
— How long? Ethan asked.
Salma understood.
— She had been dead for at least twenty-three minutes before she moved.
— Hypothermia can—
— Don't try to use me to give you a more elegant lie.
Ethan placed Ilona's body on a bench. Iris covered her with a blanket. Nadia found two fuel cans and an emergency burner in an old cabinet.
— No, Ethan said.
— What do you want us to do?
— Bury her.
— In what ground? Outside, the snow forgets tracks. Stone opens when enough people ask it to. If we leave her whole, they'll come for her.
— She's my mother.
— Exactly.
Ethan sat beside the body. He looked at her hands. Someone at the clinic had cut her nails too short. Her ring finger bore the mark of the wedding band she had not worn in twenty years. Her skin was cold again.
He remembered every version in which he had saved her. Some had been shown to him by Room Seven; others now invented themselves without help. In one, the Orchard discovered the right treatment. In another, money bought her time. In another, Ilona woke in an intact village without having forgotten a single name. In a more modest version, Ethan simply visited more often before it was too late.
That last one was the only version no technology could ever offer him again.
— Burn her, he said.
Nadia did not move at once.
— Are you sure?
— Good.
It was not good. That was precisely why her answer helped.
They improvised a furnace in the generator room, using metal plates and the exhaust duct. It was not a ceremony. They said nothing. Ethan lit the burner. For several seconds, the flame was white and cast no light. Then it became ordinary orange.
When the fire touched the blanket, Ilona's body drew breath.
Iris made a short sound. Nadia raised a hand to her mouth. Ethan lunged for the burner valve.
Salma caught his wrist with her good hand.
— Look at her chest.
It did not move.
The sound had come from the exhaust duct. Or from Ilona's mouth. Or from the room.
— Viktor, the voice whispered.
Ethan closed his eyes.
— No.
— Don't leave me again.
It was the sentence he had feared all his life and which his mother had never said to him.
He opened the valve all the way.
The flame grew.
As the body burned, the voices changed. Sometimes Ilona pleaded with him. Sometimes she scolded him. Once, his father's voice promised to return with the money he had won. Once, Elina laughed from inside the fire. Once, Ethan heard his own voice saying this was the correct choice.
Anik stood by the door and counted.
— What are you counting? Iris asked.
— How many times it tries.
— Tries what?
— To find the voice that makes him stop.
At twenty-seven, the voices ceased.
Only the flame, the heated metal, and the smell no theory could make more bearable remained.
Ethan emerged two hours later carrying the ashes in a toolbox. It was morning outside, but the sky had not brightened. The clouds looked like one drawing of a cloud repeated to the horizon. The white fissure was wider. A camp had grown around it.
The door in the rock had produced two more rooms: one with medical supplies, another with showers and hot water. No route in the old Asterion explained their location. Salma sent two engineers to measure the interior. They returned with incompatible plans. One swore the rooms were inside the mountain. The other had measured them six meters above the rock, in a volume that did not exist.
No one cared enough to turn off the showers.
Mateo had organized distribution. Those who repeated the Line received supplies first. He said it was not punishment but a technical measure: the resources remained stable only in the presence of consensus. A woman who had refused to kneel received an empty can. When the others told her it had been full, she began to cry and ask forgiveness. On the second attempt, the can contained peaches.
— It isn't only coercion, Salma said. It's feedback.
They sat around a metal table in the shelter. Salma had connected an old receiver to a vehicle battery. Fragments of transmissions from outside the valley appeared on the screen.
Bern declared that the electrical grid had been restored through
“civic discipline.”
In the images, thousands of people pedaled generators in public squares while government screens displayed ten times the output physically possible. In Turin, a procession had crossed a collapsed bridge; one recording showed them walking on air, while a second angle showed their bodies falling into the river. Both transmissions bore the same timestamp.
In Frankfurt, a bank announced that every deposit was guaranteed and no one had lost anything. People who believed could see their balances. Those who tried to withdraw money received banknotes bearing their own faces. In Paris, a financial preacher promised every follower
“the return you deserve”; entire neighborhoods filled with luxury cars without fuel, parked across gardens and emergency exits.
A transmission from southern France showed a man in a white robe surrounded by six women, all speaking at once about how freely they had chosen to follow him. Behind them, two hundred people fought over water. The caption read: THE FIRST COMMUNITY WITHOUT JEALOUSY. When one woman turned toward the camera, Ethan thought she was Elina.
The signal cut out.
— She can't be everywhere, Nadia said.
— She may be nowhere, Salma replied.
— That doesn't help.
— I know.
On a military channel, a voice repeated coordinates and warned that
“consensus zones” were expanding.
Wherever sufficiently large groups upheld the same explanation, measurements began to confirm it. Not completely. Not stably. Just enough to produce the next round of belief.
— Did the Orchard do this? Iris asked.
— The Orchard selected outcomes from a space we didn't understand, Salma said. The core limited the selection. Now there is no limiter. Or we are the limiter.
— People can't change matter merely by believing.
— Correct.
— But they are changing it.
— And that is also correct, for now.
Nadia looked at her wearily.
— Do you have a plan that doesn't begin with two impossible sentences?
Salma pointed at the receiver.
— The system needs coherence. We give it controlled incoherence.
— You tell people nothing is true?
— We tell them several true things at once. We show every angle. We publish every measurement, including the ones that contradict one another. We do not let a single story close off the rest.
— And what happens to the hot water?
— It probably disappears.
— The shelter?
— Maybe.
— The people inside?
Salma lowered her eyes.
— I don't know.
Nadia stood.
— Mateo offers them beds. We offer uncertainty and the possibility of dying more correctly.
— We offer them a shared world.
— People don't die for adjectives.
Ethan placed the box of ashes on the table.
— Then what do you propose?
— Get them out of here. Don't turn every hungry person into an epistemology exam.
— The roads no longer lead to the same place, Lukas said. We sent three teams. One reached Davos, one returned after twenty minutes, and one found the sea.
— We're a thousand kilometers from the sea.
— They brought back salt.
Lukas placed a handful of damp crystals on the table.
Ethan touched one and put it to his tongue. It was salt.
— Maybe the world isn't changing, Iris said. Maybe it's only changing what we see.
— If you drown in a sea no one else can see, the distinction is academic, Salma replied.
Anik drew on the floor with a piece of charcoal. Seven circles, each inside another. At their center, a small man held a box.
— What is that? Ethan asked.
— You.
— What's the box?
— What you kept.
Ethan looked at Ilona's ashes.
At that moment, the receiver switched on by itself.
Felix appeared on the screen.
He stood in Asterion's control room. Behind him, the monitors worked, the walls were intact, and people sat at the consoles. The image had the clarity of a local transmission. Felix wore the same bloodstained shirt, but he was uninjured.
— Good morning, Ethan.
Salma stood so quickly that she struck her broken arm.
— Felix, where are you?
He did not look at her.
— The community has stabilized. Losses were limited. The reactor is operating at twenty-three percent. We can receive survivors.
— The reactor no longer exists, Salma said.
— That is one of the available lines.
— Get out of the control room.
— I'm in the only place from which everyone can get out.
Ethan moved closer to the screen.
— What do you want?
Felix smiled. It was the smile he used before meetings in which he knew he would get what he wanted.
— For you to finish what you started.
— I didn't start this.
— You funded the Orchard. You built Asterion. You demanded control when the grid fell. You brought the people here. The fact that you didn't order every consequence does not make you a spectator.
— What do I have to finish?
— A community cannot remain between versions. It needs an author.
Mateo appeared in the transmission behind Felix.
Ethan turned toward the fissure in the valley. Mateo was there as well, standing on an improvised platform and addressing the crowd.
The two versions raised their hands at the same time.
— It doesn't need an author, Ethan said.
— That's what every author says before blaming the readers.
The screen split. On the left, Asterion prospered. Children ran through the corridors. Ilona sat in the clinic peeling an apple in one continuous spiral. On the right, the valley was full of frozen corpses. A sentence appeared between the images:
CHOOSE THE LINE WITH THE LEAST LOSS.
Ethan did not move.
— It isn't her, Nadia said.
— I know.
— Look at me when you say it.
Ethan looked at her.
— I know.
On the screen, Ilona looked up.
— Viktor, the cold is coming under the door.
Those had been the last words she said to him one winter in childhood, when his father left with the rent money. Ethan had placed a towel against the threshold. He had never told anyone.
Felix reached toward the camera.
— You don't have to believe. You only have to say what you choose. We'll do the rest.
— Who are “we”?
— The version that remains.
The signal went dark.
Ethan's reflection remained on the screen. For a moment, the reflected man had the white scar beneath his chin.
Salma opened the receiver and tore out a module.
— We can reverse the transmission.
— To whom? Nadia asked.
— Everyone listening.
— And what do we tell them?
Salma looked at the ashes, the salt, Anik's drawings, and the impossible lights in the valley.
— That we don't know.
They switched on the transmitter at noon.
Salma spoke first. She explained what she could measure: the correlation between repetition and the stability of anomalies, coordinate drift, the effect of independent observers, the limits of the data. She did not simplify. She attached an error interval to every claim and the conditions under which it might be false.
After three minutes, half the listeners had changed channels.
Nadia spoke second. She said that Ilona had died and that her body had been used to manufacture a promise. She said Mateo's shelters were real while people entered them, but no one knew the price they demanded. She said the beds did not become cleaner because the story was dirty.
Iris spoke about the school. About the community that had betrayed them and then defended them. About the fact that people could do both things in the same day without becoming demons or saints.
Lukas read two incompatible plans of the same gallery.
Anik said only:
— When everyone draws the same door, no one sees the wall anymore.
Ethan spoke last.
He had prepared a speech about responsibility. When the red light came on, he forgot it.
— I am not your savior, he said.
A murmur came from the valley.
— I don't know what the fissure is. I don't know whether the world outside still exists in the form we remember. I don't know whether what you're seeing is matter, perception, selection, or a disease that learned to build. I know I promised you safety and built a place where safety depended on me. I know that whenever the system demanded sacrifices, I always found an explanation for why someone else had to go first. I know power seemed reasonable to me as long as it spoke in my voice.
A sound appeared in the receiver, like a collective breath.
— Mateo tells you that you have to believe for the shelter to remain. Maybe he's right. But anything that feeds you only if you never ask what it is does not belong to you. It is raising you for something.
Salma looked at him. They had not discussed the final sentence.
— For what? a voice shouted from the crowd.
Ethan could have told the truth: he did not know. Yet he felt the old temptation to fill the void with an explanation large enough to make people stop noticing the uncertainty. It was extracting consciousness. It was searching for free will. It was training a god. It was an alien experiment. It was a treatment. It was hell built by a species that had forgotten its religion. Every version would have given fear a form and Ethan a role.
— I don't know, he said.
The fissure narrowed.
Half the door in the rock disappeared. The people inside the storage room screamed. A bed was cut by the edge of the stone; the part inside remained visible in a space without volume. A woman tried to pull her child out, but his arm seemed caught in another position of the corridor.
The crowd turned toward the transmitter.
— Stop it!
— He's killing us!
Mateo raised his arms.
— Believe, and it will remain!
The chorus began again.
Ethan continued:
— Maybe every doubt costs something. Maybe truth does not come with a guarantee of survival. But if we surrender the right to contradict one another, what survives is no longer a community. It is one mouth with thousands of bodies.
An object struck the shelter wall. Then another.
On the screen, Salma's measurements fragmented. The temperature in the valley rose to nineteen degrees, fell to minus twenty, then returned to three. The people around the fissure appeared in different positions in every frame. Some knelt. Some ran. Some did not exist.
Salma stopped the transmission.
— Enough.
— We have to continue.
— If we destabilize it too quickly, we cut them in half along with the story.
— Then Mateo wins.
— This isn't a debate.
The shelter door bent beneath the blows.
Lukas jammed a pipe through the handle. Iris pulled Anik toward the back. Nadia took a dead guard's weapon and checked the magazine.
— There's a service exit, she said.
— Where does it lead? Ethan asked.
— Before all this began, outside.
— And now?
— That's what we're going to find out.
The exit was a narrow tunnel dug for smoke evacuation. They walked single file. Ethan carried the box of ashes. Salma leaned on him. Behind them, the metal of the main door gave way.
The tunnel ended in a pine forest.
There was no forest at the edge of the Asterion valley.
The trees were tall and wet, and the ground smelled of summer. The fissure's white light showed between the trunks, although it should have been in the opposite direction. Birds sang the same sequence of seven notes.
— Don't separate, Salma said.
— Why? Nadia asked.
— Because it's the only advice I can give without lying.
They walked. After several hundred meters, the forest changed into a street in Zürich. The trams were stopped, but the shop windows worked. Every dial in a watch shop showed 7:12. The smell of bread came from a café.
At one table, Ethan saw himself eating breakfast with Elina.
His version wore a blue sweater and laughed. Elina's hand rested over his. Beyond the window, the world was normal. People went to work. On a screen, a presenter announced that the European grid had been repaired and that the “regional Asterion incident” had caused no casualties.
The Ethan inside the café looked up and saw him.
His smile disappeared.
Elina turned.
— Don't go in, Nadia said.
— I wasn't going to.
— You just took a step.
Ethan looked at his foot. It was almost on the threshold.
The café door opened by itself.
— You burned her, Elina said from inside.
It was not a question.
— It wasn't her.
— It was enough of her to hurt.
The Ethan in the café stood.
— You don't have to continue, he said. I accepted. Everything stopped.
— What did you accept?
— Not to ask for proof after I receive the result.
A third cup appeared on the table.
Nadia grabbed Ethan's arm.
— We're leaving.
— Nadia is well here, his version said. Salma is well. Mother is alive. I took nothing from anyone.
— Who remained outside?
— No one who matters to you.
That was the error.
Not a moral one. A design error. The version had not tried to convince him that everyone had been saved. It had offered the more efficient formula: everyone who mattered to him. The world as an
appendix to his affective circle. Catastrophe reduced to a contact filter.
Ethan pushed the café door.
For one second, Elina looked at him with fear.
Then the window went dark. Behind it was no longer a café but a hospital room. Ethan lay in a bed with wires entering his skull. Nadia sat beside him, ten years older. Salma wore a white coat. A doctor with a scar beneath his chin adjusted a machine.
— Increase the reward, the doctor said.
— He's losing his identity, Salma replied.
— Identity is the variable we're measuring.
The image changed.
A child sat in Room Seven staring at an apple. A voice asked what he would save from a fire. The child answered differently every time. Every time, the room burned.
The image changed.
A city of transparent organisms floated in a geometry without up or down. At its center, a dark form opened billions of worlds like seeds. Some struggled. Others sang. None resembled humans closely enough to provide an explanation.
Then there was nothing but black glass.
— That's what it wants, Salma said.
— For us to choose the explanation we can endure.
Elias's voice came from behind them:
— Or the correct one.
He stood among the trees, dressed in a paramedic's uniform. The white scar cut across his chin. He held a folded thermal blanket.
Nadia raised the gun.
— You died on the platform, Ethan said.
— In some accounts.
— Are you Felix?
— In some functions.
— Are you the system?
— In some questions.
Salma stepped toward him.
— Why does it need consensus?
— It doesn't. You do. When you lost it, what made differences negotiable became visible.
— What are you extracting?
Elias looked at the box in Ethan's hands.
— Have you decided what you're keeping?
— It's ashes.
— That is a description.
— It's my mother.
— That is a claim.
— What do you want from me?
Elias approached. Nadia's weapon remained aimed at his chest. He did not seem concerned.
— You received money without work, desire without refusal, admiration without knowledge, discovery without understanding, power without a mandate, and a catastrophe large enough to make all of them seem necessary. You rejected some. You kept others. The distinction is not stable.
— For whom?
— No one you can use as an alibi.
— Is this a test?
— If I say yes, you will try to pass it. If I say no, you will try to escape. Both answers give you an audience.
Ethan felt anger rising. It was almost a relief. Anger simplified.
— Then take what you want.
— Consent offered without an object is only exhaustion.
— Tell me the object.
— Then it is no longer yours.
Nadia pulled the trigger.
The gun cracked. The bullet passed through Elias without leaving a wound and struck a pine. Two-franc coins poured from the bark. Dozens, then hundreds, striking the roots and rolling over the asphalt of the impossible street.
Anik bent down and picked one up.
— No, Iris said.
The boy turned it between his fingers. The coin bore Ethan's face on one side and a door on the other.
— It's fake, he said.
— Every coin is an agreement, Elias replied.
— That's not what fake means.
For the first time, Elias looked at the child as a problem.
Salma noticed.
— Why can he see?
— He doesn't see more. He has fewer things to defend.
— Then take him, Ethan said.
The words escaped before he could stop them.
Iris turned with silent violence. Nadia lowered the gun, stunned. Anik remained motionless.
Ethan immediately felt the explanation forming: he had not meant it literally; he had been provoking Elias; he assumed Elias would refuse; he was testing whether the boy was the target. His mind was already building a courtroom in which his intention was better than the sentence.
Elias smiled almost imperceptibly.
— There, he said. It still works.
Ethan knelt in front of Anik.
— I'm sorry.
The boy looked at him.
— You wanted to see whether he'd take me.
— And if he had?
Ethan did not answer.
— Then you would have known, Anik said.
Iris pulled him away.
Shouts broke out in the forest. The Keepers emerged between the trees. Mateo led them. Some carried weapons, others boxes of supplies from the mountain's doors. Behind them, the fissure moved through space as if the valley were following them.
— Ethan must enter, Mateo shouted. The Line demands the author.
— The Line demands food, Nadia replied. You feed it people.
— Every order has a cost.
