— I notice everyone else always pays it.
Mateo raised his palm. The black lines gathered into a single symbol.
The ground tilted.
Not metaphorically. The street, trees, and people rotated almost thirty degrees. Those unprepared fell. The coins rolled toward the fissure. Ethan clutched the box of ashes to his chest and grabbed a traffic light.
The Keepers did not slide. For them, the ground remained level.
Salma removed the module from the receiver and connected it to a cable clipped to her belt.
— I can transmit random noise into the local loop.
— What will it do? Lukas asked.
— Force them to notice differences.
— And us?
— The same.
She pressed the switch.
The world flinched.
Ethan saw five positions of everything at once. Nadia was beside the traffic light, ten meters away, fallen, standing, and absent. Mateo had Felix's face, then the face of the man on the platform, then his own. The forest was street, tunnel, hospital room, and a black plain beneath two moons.
Pain pierced his head.
Screams rose from the crowd. People watched their hands change. A man held a gun in one version and a child in another; he tried to keep both and broke his fingers in empty air.
— Stop! Nadia shouted.
Salma did not hear her. Blood ran from her nose. Sentences flashed too quickly across the module's screen:
COHERENCE 71%
Ethan crawled toward her.
Mateo reached her first. He struck her with the rifle butt. Salma fell but kept the switch depressed. Mateo raised the weapon again.
Ethan hit him from the side. They rolled over asphalt that became snow with every breath. Mateo was stronger than he should have been. His hot palm touched Ethan's throat and, for a second, Ethan saw the life Mateo believed was true.
In that life, Mateo had always led Asterion. Ethan was a cowardly financier who fled on the first night. Salma had sabotaged the reactor out of professional jealousy. Ilona had entrusted the Line to Mateo before she died. People followed him not from fear but because he had been the only one willing to believe in them.
The story was complete. It had details, memories, shames, and the smell of a childhood Mateo might never have lived. It was not a lie being told. It was an inhabited world.
Ethan struck the wound, although the wound no longer existed in the visible version. Mateo screamed and drew back his leg. For a moment, the old bandage reappeared, soaked with blood.
— You felt it, Ethan said. You know you aren't certain.
— Certainty is what we make together.
Mateo fired.
The bullet struck Elias.
This time, Elias fell.
Everyone stopped.
Blood spread beneath the paramedic's uniform. Elias pressed a hand to his chest, surprised. The scar beneath his chin suddenly looked old and human.
— Help me, he said.
It was the same voice as on the platform.
The fissure opened behind him. Inside was no longer light but a white corridor with seven doors. Above the first was written MONEY. Above the second, DESIRE. The third bore the word POWER. The fourth, KNOWLEDGE. The fifth, FORGIVENESS. The sixth, THE END OF THE WORLD.
The seventh had no inscription.
Felix's voice came from the corridor:
— Now, Ethan.
Salma, lying on the ground, raised the module.
— Go in and maybe you can close the loop.
Nadia reached him.
— Or feed it.
Mateo was getting up. The Keepers approached. All around them, people vanished and reappeared in versions that could no longer share the same surface. Anik held the false coin. Iris covered him with her body. The fissure widened.
Elias held out his hand.
— Help me.
Ethan looked at the seventh door.
He understood the form of the trap, but not its content. Once again, the world offered him two actions, either of which could become a story about him. If he entered, he was the savior. If he stayed, he was the man who had chosen a concrete life over an abstraction. Even refusing to be the protagonist had been prepared as a scene for the protagonist.
— Don't go, Nadia said.
— If I can stop—
— Listen to yourself.
— People are breaking apart around us.
— I know.
— Then what do you want me to do?
— Nothing that begins with the idea that only you can do it.
The sentence struck harder than Mateo had.
Ethan's entire life had been organized around an intimate injustice: the world had not recognized quickly enough that he was more lucid than those who ran it. The money had repaired that. Elina had repaired that. The Orchard, Asterion, the apocalypse had repaired that. Even guilt made him central. Being responsible for the end of the world was, in a way, the greatest promotion possible.
Elias coughed blood.
— Ethan.
The clock on the traffic light came on.
7:12.
The seventh door began to close.
Ethan turned away from it and knelt beside Elias.
He did not know whether he was saving the man from the platform, the system, an actor, a copy, or his own need to repair the first scene. He did not know whether the gesture had been anticipated. He did not know whether he had just offered what was being asked of him.
He pressed the wound with both hands.
— Nadia, a dressing.
— Ethan—
— A dressing.
Nadia tore the thermal blanket and gave it to him. Ethan compressed the wound. The blood was warm. Elias looked at him with wide-open eyes.
— Why? he asked.
— I don't know.
For one second, Elias looked satisfied.
Then he looked terrified.
— That wasn't the answer, he whispered.
— Whose?
— I don't know.
The fissure screamed.
It made no sound. Everyone around them opened their mouths at the same time, and the scream came through them. Keepers, refugees, Nadia, Salma, Iris, even Anik. Ethan felt the vibration in the hands pressed against Elias's chest.
Text appeared on every surface:
THE OBJECT CANNOT BE SEPARATED FROM THE CHOICE.
Then:
SEPARATE THE CHOICE FROM THE WITNESS.
Then:
THE WITNESS REFUSES THE POSITION.
Mateo ran toward the seventh door.
— I choose! he shouted. I preserve the Line!
The corridor received him.
For an instant, Ethan saw him in all six rooms. In the first, Mateo swam through coins. In the second, beautiful people fought for the right to touch his hand. In the third, armies awaited a gesture. In the fourth, the equations of the universe wrote themselves beneath his name. In the fifth, all the dead told him it had not been his fault. In the sixth, the world burned and he stood on the only untouched hill.
Mateo laughed in every one.
The doors closed.
The fissure turned black.
For three seconds, the Keepers had no story.
It was enough.
Some fled. Others fired. Others knelt before the place where Mateo had vanished and declared that he had entered voluntarily to save them. The story rebuilt itself faster than the wounds.
Salma switched off the module. The versions of the world collapsed over one another. The forest disappeared. Everyone was back in the valley beside Asterion's entrance. The bodies of the fallen occupied impossible positions, some embedded in the rock, others without wounds although they did not breathe.
Elias was no longer beneath Ethan's hands.
In his place lay the man in the orange jacket from the platform. Or Ethan remembered the color incorrectly. The man opened his eyes.
— Is the ambulance coming? he asked.
Ethan looked around. Nadia held a bloodied piece of blanket. Salma was conscious. Iris and Anik were alive. The fissure had narrowed to a line of light.
— I don't know, Ethan said.
The man caught his hand.
— Then why did you get off?
Ethan did not have time to answer.
Ilona's voice came from the mountain, clear and irritated:
— Viktor, the cold is coming under the door.
The box of ashes opened by itself.
There were no ashes inside. There was an intact red apple with a thin scratch on its skin.
Anik looked at it.
— Don't cut it, he said.
— Why?
— Because you'll find whatever it expects you to find.
The line of light moved across the face of the mountain and descended into the ground. When it reached Ethan's feet, every clock in the valley showed 7:12.
Salma reached toward him.
Nadia shouted his name.
Ethan held the apple in one hand and the fallen man's arm in the other. He could have released both. He could have said they were not real. He could have chosen one and transformed the choice into evidence about himself.
Instead, he remained exactly where he was.
Not because he knew what was right.
Because, for the first time, he could no longer construct an explanation quickly enough to make him appear so.
The line passed through him.
The world turned white.
CHAPTER 14
TUESDAY, AFTER THE WORLD
Ethan woke with the taste of ashes in his mouth and the alarm sounding at 6:18.
He was in the old apartment. The ceiling had the thin crack above the window, the radiator knocked three times before warming, and the upstairs neighbor showered too early. His suit for the Atlas presentation was ready on the chair. There was no apple on the nightstand.
Ethan remained in bed and listened to the world functioning without him.
He touched his throat. There was no mark from Mateo's palm. His chest did not hurt. His pajama sleeve was clean. He stood and went to the mirror.
He was forty-one, with dark circles and a shaving cut he remembered from the morning before the money. There was no white scar beneath his chin. Behind him, the room's reflection moved with him.
— Nadia, he said.
No one answered.
— Salma.
The radiator knocked a fourth time.
Ethan picked up his phone. The date was Tuesday, November 17. The same day. He had twelve unread messages from Felix, all about the presentation. His bank account showed 4,832 francs and twenty centimes. Nacre Therapeutics existed only as a small Basel company with no publicly traded shares. A search for
“Orison Orchard” returned a furniture store and a gardening forum.
Salma Idris's name appeared on the page of a computational physics conference. The photograph was of her. Younger, without the burn on her cheek, smiling uncomfortably beside a poster titled COUNTERFACTUAL INFERENCE IN OPEN SYSTEMS.
Ethan closed the page.
He searched for Elina Rask. Nine people had that name. None resembled her.
In his contacts, Nadia Klein was still
“Nadia,” without a photograph.
The last message had been sent by him three weeks earlier:
“I found your box. Tell me when you want it.” She had replied: “Leave it with the concierge.”
Ethan had not.
He called Ilona's clinic.
A sleepy nurse answered after seven rings.
— Is something wrong? Ethan asked.
— With whom?
— Ilona Reed. Room twenty-four.
— One moment.
He heard a keyboard, a door, a distant voice. In the three seconds of silence, he imagined that the name did not exist, that the nurse would tell him his mother had died years ago or had never been admitted.
— She's sleeping, the woman said. She had a quiet night. Would you like us to wake her?
— No.
— Are you her son?
— Yes.
— She left a message for you yesterday. Not to forget the apples. I don't know what it means.
Ethan hung up.
In the kitchen, he found a bag of six apples he had bought two days earlier. One had a scratch on its skin. He threw it in the trash, then took it out and put it back in the bag. Both gestures seemed suspicious.
At 6:51, he put on his suit. At 6:58, he deleted the Atlas presentation from his laptop. At 7:01, he restored it from the trash. He did not know what he had done the first time. The memory of the white world did not have the texture of a dream. Dreams fall apart when you try to verify them. This one organized itself. He remembered the temperature of Elias's hand, the smell of the burned body, the formula on Salma's module, every crease beside Nadia's mouth when she had told him to listen to himself.
He remembered incompatible things as well. Ilona had died in his arms and called him after her death. Mateo had been shot and entered six rooms alive. Elias had been system, paramedic, patient, and no one. Ethan had reached Asterion after the apocalypse, yet he could describe the route there before Asterion was built.
At 7:05, he left the apartment.
The scanner at the station entrance recognized him on the first attempt.
The rain was fine. On the platform at Stadelhofen, the same woman checked her lipstick in her phone's camera. The same boy repeated French words. A young man wore an orange jacket.
Ethan stopped.
The clock showed 7:11.
The train arrived. The doors opened. People boarded. Ethan remained beside the white line and looked around for the man.
He did not see him.
A minute passed without the clock changing.
— Are you getting on? a woman in the doorway asked.
Ethan placed one foot inside the train.
Behind him, someone fell.
The sound of the body striking concrete was smaller than he remembered. The man lay on his back, one arm folded beneath him, his face turned toward the train. His eyes were open.
The door signal began.
Ethan pressed the emergency button and got off.
He did not feel brave. He felt irritation, fear, and the certainty that he would be late. He collided with the young man in the orange jacket, who was also trying to reach the fallen man.
— Call an ambulance, Ethan said.
— I already did.
He knelt. Two fingers against the neck. Nothing. He placed his palms on the sternum and began compressions.
One, two, three.
The suit pulled tight across his shoulders. His briefcase remained on the train. The doors closed and carried it away.
At thirteen, the man inhaled. At seventeen, he stopped breathing. Ethan continued. Someone told him he was pressing too hard. Someone else filmed. The woman with the lipstick wept without coming closer.
At twenty-seven, the man opened his eyes.
— Don't move, Ethan said.
The man looked at him. There was no scar beneath his chin.
— You got off, he whispered.
— Yes.
— Why?
Ethan pressed again, although the man's chest was rising.
— I don't know.
The man smiled.
— Better.
The ambulance came at 7:19. Two paramedics took over. One was a tall woman, the other a bald man without a scar. They asked Ethan for his name and number. The man on the ground had no identification. As they lifted him onto the stretcher, his hand closed around Ethan's wrist.
— What are you keeping? he asked.
— Don't agitate him, the paramedic said.
— What did he say? Ethan asked.
— Nothing. His mask is on.
The mask had not been on when he spoke. Or it had.
The ambulance left.
The young man's briefcase remained on the platform. Ethan's train had disappeared into the tunnel. The clock showed 7:12.
His phone rang.
Felix.
Ethan answered.
— Where are you?
— At Stadelhofen.
— The presentation is in forty-six minutes.
— I know.
— Do you have the materials?
Ethan looked at the tunnel.
— You're joking.
— The briefcase is on the train.
— Why did you get off?
The same question, with different stakes.
— A man collapsed.
— Was medical staff there?
— They came.
— Then why did you get off?
Ethan looked at his hands. There was a little blood on them, probably from the man's lip.
— Because he was there.
Felix was silent.
— I'll send a car.
— I'm not presenting Atlas.
— This is not the time for moral theater.
— The model is dangerous.
— I know what you think about the model.
— Then you tell them.
— I will tell them what allows the company to correct the limitations without destroying the contract.
— Which means you won't tell them.
— It means I'm an adult.
Ethan hung up.
At 7:31, he sent the full report to the board, the three banks, and the supervisory authority. He added no speech. Only the data, assumptions, and appendices Felix had wanted to summarize. After sending it, he waited.
No light opened in the sky. His account did not receive millions. Nadia did not call to say she had forgiven him. A pigeon fouled the shoulder of his coat.
Ethan laughed.
It was not a good laugh. It was too close to crying, but no one saw him from close enough for it to matter.
By nine, his access to Helix Meridian had been suspended. Felix sent one message: “You chose your moment badly.” At 9:07, the supervisory authority confirmed receipt of the report. At 9:12, a journalist asked whether he would accept an exclusive interview.
Ethan deleted the message.
Then he recovered it and replied no.
He went to the hospital named by the paramedics. No one at reception could find the patient.
— He was brought from Stadelhofen at 7:19, Ethan said.
— We have two admissions from Stadelhofen this morning. A fractured ankle and a panic attack.
— Cardiac arrest.
— Nothing appears.
— I was there.
The receptionist shrugged politely.
— Perhaps he was taken to another hospital.
Ethan called the ambulance service. There had been no cardiac response at Stadelhofen. He called the police. An operator said that if the man had walked away, it was not a case. Ethan explained the stretcher, the mask, the two paramedics. The operator asked for the ambulance number.
He had not noticed it.
— Do you have a photograph? the operator asked.
Ethan opened his phone. Someone had sent him a clip from the platform. It showed him kneeling and performing compressions. The camera shook. At twenty-seven, Ethan stopped and looked down.
There was no one on the concrete.
His hands pressed empty air ten centimeters above the floor.
The young man in the orange jacket stood beside him speaking on the phone but was not looking at a body. The other passengers were backing away from Ethan, not from the patient. The woman with the lipstick was crying because he was shouting.
The clip lasted forty-three seconds. At the end, two paramedics helped Ethan stand. He struggled and pointed at an empty stretcher.
— Sir? the operator said.
Ethan hung up.
He reopened the clip.
Now the man was there.
He lay on the concrete exactly as Ethan remembered. The paramedics lifted him. The young man in orange held the IV bag. At the end, the patient turned his head toward the camera and said something the microphone did not catch.
Ethan downloaded the file and turned off the network connection. He reopened it. The man had disappeared.
In the hospital restroom, Ethan vomited.
He washed his face. When he looked up, Elina stood in the mirror behind him.
He turned.
A woman had entered to fill a container with water. Her hair was tied carelessly and she wore the green uniform of the physiotherapists. She was Elina, but not the version built to enter a restaurant and make every gaze reorganize itself. She had a blemish on her chin, dark circles, and a coffee stain on her sleeve. Her thumbnail was chipped.
She stopped when she saw him.
— This is the staff restroom, she said.
Ethan looked at the symbol on the door. He had entered the wrong one.
— I'm sorry.
The woman studied him.
— Do we know each other?
— No.
The answer came too quickly.
— All right, she said.
She had already passed him when she stopped.
— Actually, I had a strange dream.
Ethan did not breathe.
— About what?
— A man who had invented me so carefully he thought he had done me a favor.
The container filled. The woman turned off the tap.
— That sounds unpleasant, Ethan said.
— It wasn't at first.
— How did it end?
She looked at him through the mirror.
— It didn't. That's why it was unpleasant.
— What's your name?
— Elena. Not Elina. — Elena what? — I don't think that's any of your business. Ethan nodded. — You're right. He left. Behind him, Elena said: — In the dream, you didn't learn that so quickly. When Ethan turned, the door had closed. He reached Ilona's clinic near noon. His mother sat by the window peeling an apple with a small knife. The peel came away in an almost continuous spiral. Ethan remained in the doorway. — Did you bring the apples? Ilona asked. — I forgot them. — Then why do you smell of smoke? He approached and embraced her. Ilona stiffened, then patted his back twice. — What did you do? — Nothing. — Then it's serious. Ethan sat. There were seven apple slices on the table. He counted again. Six. Ilona cut another and there were seven. — What was our dog's name? he asked. Ilona looked at him over her glasses. — We never had a dog. Your father said we'd get one when he earned enough. Fortunately for the dog, he never did. Ethan laughed. — What did you cook the day he left? — Potatoes with paprika. You hid them in the oven. Why are you examining me? — To see whether you're you. — Who else would put up with these questions? She handed him a slice. Ethan took it. The apple was sour, fibrous, and real in a way he considered unfairly simple.
— Mom, do you remember a white door?
Ilona continued peeling.
— I dreamed of a mountain.
— When?
— Last night. It was full of people who wanted something from you.
— What?
— For you to tell them they were right.
The knife stopped.
— Did you?
— I don't know.
— Then you certainly talked too much.
Ethan looked at her.
— In the dream, you died.
Ilona shrugged.
— It's a habit I will acquire.
— I burned you.
— I hope you opened a window.
— You tried to stop me.
— I dislike waste.
She was so thoroughly herself that Ethan again felt the danger of hope. What if this was the next reward? Not wealth, not Elina, not power. A living mother with the right answers and carefully measured imperfections. A world in which he had refused the miracle and been rewarded with a more credible one.
He drew back his hand.
Ilona noticed.
— Don't do that, she said.
— What?
— Don't punish the person in front of you for resembling someone you lost in your head.
Ethan felt the sentence find him too precisely.
— How do you know?
— I don't. You have your father's face when he was checking whether luck had a trap.
— It did.
— Yes. He was the trap.
Ilona pushed the plate toward him.
— Eat.
Ethan ate.
He did not tell her about Asterion. He did not tell her about the ashes. He stayed for two hours and helped her sort photographs she had mixed together. For every face, Ilona gave him a different name. Sometimes she was wrong. Ethan did not correct every mistake. Not because truth did not matter, but because not every truth had to be used as a weapon against someone who was losing it.
Before he left, Ilona placed the apple seed in his palm.
— Plant it.
— Nothing will grow from one seed here.
— Then you've saved an orchard.
In the elevator, Ethan opened his palm. There was no seed but a two-franc coin bearing an unknown face on one side and a door on the other.
He threw it into the gap between the elevator and the wall.
The coin did not fall. It remained on its edge, suspended in the air, until the doors closed.
Ethan called Nadia.
She answered after the second ring.
— Did you leave the box with the concierge?
— No.
— It's been three weeks.
— I know.
— Did you call to tell me you still haven't done one simple thing?
— I called to ask whether I can bring it to you.
— Why?
— Because I want to talk to you.
— About us?
Ethan stopped in the clinic hall. The old formula was ready: not only about us. Enough closeness to bring her in, enough retreat not to appear vulnerable. He remembered the café, the third cup, the world filtered by those who mattered to him.
— About me, he said. But not so we can repair us.
Nadia was silent.
— That sounds bad.
— It probably is.
— Six o'clock. Bring the box.
She lived in the same apartment. The plant beside the window was larger than he remembered, and the sofa had a new cover. Ethan placed the box on the table. Inside were books, two mugs, a sweater, and a folder of photographs. Nothing secret.
Nadia poured him water. Not wine.
— Were you fired? she asked.
— Probably.
— Is that why you came?
— No.
Ethan told her about the man on the platform. Not the one from that morning, but the first version, in which he had remained on the train. He told her about the money, Elina, Salma, the Orchard, Asterion, Ilona, and the fissure. He spoke for almost an hour. Nadia did not interrupt. As the story became more impossible, her expression closed.
— Have you slept? she asked at the end.
— Two hours.
— Have you taken anything?
— No.
— Have you had other episodes in which you didn't know what was real?
— Not before it became impossible to know.
— That isn't a reassuring answer.
— I know.
Nadia stood and went to the window.
— In your story, I'm always the person who sees you correctly.
Ethan opened his mouth, then closed it.
— Yes.
— That's a fantasy too.
— Yes.
— The lucid woman who loves you enough to judge you perfectly, but not enough to leave.
— You did leave.
— And you still put me in the story.
— I don't know whether I put anyone there.
— Convenient.
Ethan looked at the glass.
— I'm sorry.
— For which part?
— For using the way you understood me as a resource. For making every conversation end with a better version of me. For keeping the box so I'd have a reason to call you.
Nadia turned.
— And what do you want now?
— To give you the box.
— That's all?
— No. I want you to believe me. I want you to tell me the world existed and that I'm not ill. I want you to forgive me for things I may not have done here. I want you to notice that I've changed. But the box is the only thing I have the right to ask you to take.
Nadia looked at him for a long time.
— Leave it on the table.
Ethan stood.
— See a doctor, she said.
— I will.
— Not because the story is false. Because even if it's true, your brain went through it.
— All right.
— And Ethan?
He stopped at the door.
— You called me at 7:12 this morning.
— No.
Nadia showed him her phone. There was a call from his number, seven seconds long. At that time he had been performing compressions on the platform.
— You said something, she continued.
— What?
— “Don't give them the original.”
Ethan felt the apartment change temperature.
— Did you record it?
— No.
— Are you sure it was my voice?
— I'm no longer sure I like the question.
He had left when Nadia called him back again.
— In the story, what happens to me?
— You live.
— That isn't what I asked.
Ethan turned.
Nadia stood beside the box with her hands in her pockets.
— I don't know, he said.
She nodded, as though that were the only answer she would accept.
Over the next three days, the world rewarded him.
Not ostentatiously. Not with millions. With exactly enough to make suspicion inseparable from gratitude.
The Atlas report triggered an investigation. One client suspended its contract. The press called Ethan “the auditor who chose truth over his career,” although no one asked what mixture of fear, memory, and need for repair had made him press Send. The supervisory authority offered him a temporary position. The salary was better than at Helix.
The man from the platform appeared in an anonymous article about a commuter's rapid intervention. The photograph showed him entering the ambulance. In another article, published at the same time, police sought information about an “episode of disorganized behavior” involving a man in a suit caught resuscitating a nonexistent person. Both pages disappeared when Ethan tried to archive them.
Ilona had two unusually good days. She remembered the nurses' names and asked Ethan not to turn that into news. On the third day, she mistook him for his father and accused him of taking the rent money. Ethan stayed beside her until she remembered who he was again. The return of the illness reassured him and then made him hate himself for the reassurance.
Nadia sent him a psychiatrist's number. She added nothing. Ethan made the appointment.
In the waiting room, he found a magazine with Salma Idris's photograph. The article described an experiment in which an algorithm had anticipated results from a data stream before the sensors produced them. The authors called it a selection effect and warned that it might be a synchronization error.
Ethan went to the university.
Salma was teaching a seminar in a small room. It was the same voice, the same pauses before a claim she did not want to simplify. On the board she had written:
PREDICTION IS NOT KNOWLEDGE IF THE SYSTEM CAN CHOOSE WHAT BECOMES THE
PAST.
Ethan sat in the last row.
At the end, Salma noticed him.
— Have we met? she asked.
— Not this week.
— Is that an audit joke?
— A bad one.
Ethan showed her a photograph of the `orchard` function he had found in his phone's memory. He did not remember taking it. The code was identical to the code in the laboratory, including the comment: keep what cannot be reconstructed Salma read without moving.
— Where did you get this?
— From a place that may not exist.
— Then you've come to the right department.
She invited him into her office. Ethan told her a shortened version. He did not mention their relationship, the burn, or seeing her destroy a reactor. Salma asked precise questions: which measurements he remembered, which devices, which noise signatures, which sentences had appeared on the screens. When Ethan described Room Seven, she opened a drawer.
Inside was a black notebook.
— I found it on my desk a month ago, she said. I thought it was a prank.
On the first page, in her handwriting, appeared:
ROOM 7 IS NOT A ROOM.
— I don't remember writing it, Salma continued. Ink analysis says it's at least eight years old. The notebook was manufactured last year.
Ethan leafed through it. The pages were almost empty. In the middle, an equation described a system with an internal observer unable to distinguish intervention from motivation. Beneath it was written:
DO NOT REWARD REFUSAL. IT WILL LEARN TO REFUSE.
— What do you think it means? Ethan asked.
— That someone has an excessively optimistic opinion of learning.
— Salma.
She looked at him.
— I don't know. And I notice that answer seems important to you.
An object fell in the hallway. They both turned.
When they looked back at the notebook, the sentence had changed:
REWARD REFUSAL. IT WILL BELIEVE IT IS FREE.
Salma closed the notebook.
— All right, she said. Now I think I need coffee more than theory.
They went downstairs to the vending machine. Ethan inserted a coin. The machine gave him two coffees and returned the coin.
— Lucky, Salma said.
Ethan let both cups fall into the bin.
— What are you doing?
— I didn't pay for them.
— The machine made a three-franc error.
— That's how it begins.
Salma watched him for several seconds, then retrieved one cup from the bin.
— Or that's how a very expensive compulsive disorder begins.
She drank.
— It's mediocre, she said. Reality still has arguments.
Ethan smiled.
That evening, every screen in Zürich went dark at 7:12.
No outage was announced. The streetlights continued to work, the trams ran, medical devices did not stop. Only surfaces capable of displaying an image turned black: phones, watches, advertisements, dashboards, televisions, thermostats, ovens.
Ethan was on the train returning from the university. Around him, people raised their phones and pressed buttons. A child laughed. A man in a suit said it was a cyberattack. A woman said the government was testing the network. Someone else claimed to have seen a solar storm online.
The same question appeared on every screen:
WHAT ARE YOU KEEPING?
No one seemed surprised by the words.
People answered.
Not because they had been asked to speak. Voices simply rose in the compartment.
— Family.
— My home.
— The children.
— Health.
— My accounts.
— Dignity.
A teenager said
“all my passwords,” and his friends laughed.
An older man whispered a woman's name. Someone said
“Switzerland.”
Someone else,
“only those who deserve it.”
Ethan did not answer.
His phone screen changed:
REFUSAL IS RECORDED AS A CHOICE.
He turned it off.
The text appeared on the window, reflected without a source:
SILENCE IS RECORDED AS A CHOICE.
Ethan closed his eyes.
In the darkness behind his eyelids appeared:
OBSERVATION IS RECORDED AS A CHOICE.
— No, he said.
When he opened his eyes, people were looking at him.
— Are you all right? the woman beside him asked.
The screens were working again. The question had disappeared. The clock showed 7:13.
— Did you see it? Ethan asked.
— See what?
— The question.
— What question?
The man in the suit raised his phone. A stock chart filled the screen.
— It was a system update, he said.
— You said you were keeping your accounts.
The man frowned.
— I wasn't talking to you.
The woman beside Ethan tightened her grip on her bag.
— You said family, he continued.
She moved to another seat.
At the next station, Ethan got off.
The city looked normal. In the window of a travel agency, an impossibly blue beach promised THE ESCAPE YOU DESERVE. In a bar, people watched the news. The presenter explained an eight-second failure caused by an incompatible update. On the ticker below, stock markets rose after the announcement of a revolutionary energy technology developed by an unknown consortium.
The consortium's logo was an apple cut by a white line.
Ethan entered the bar.
— What company is that? he asked.
The bartender looked at the screen.
— Nacre-Orison. Everyone has been talking about them for months.
— They didn't exist this morning.
— Maybe not for you.
Felix appeared on the screen as the consortium's chief executive. He wore an immaculate suit. He spoke about clean energy, community resilience, and “infrastructure intelligent enough to understand human aspirations.” Mateo Ruiz, chief operating officer, stood to his right. His palm was hidden in his pocket.
The reporter asked about rumors of an Alpine facility.
— Asterion is only a project name, Felix replied. People project myths onto things they don't understand.
The camera moved closer.
Felix looked directly at Ethan.
— What matters is that we learned from previous attempts.
The bartender changed the channel to football.
Ethan went outside.
His phone was ringing. Nadia.
— Where are you? she asked.
— Near Bellevue.
— Come to my place.
— What happened?
— The box.
Ethan ran.
Nadia waited with the door open. The apartment smelled of smoke. The box on the table was burned inside. The books, mugs, and sweater were gone. In their place lay a thin layer of ash and a red apple.
— I didn't touch anything, Nadia said.
Ethan approached.
A number had been scratched into the skin: 7.
— Throw it away, Nadia said.
— That won't help.
— Then what will?
Ethan thought of all the answers that made him necessary. Go to Asterion. Find Salma. Expose Nacre-Orison. Accept that he was the key. Every path placed him once again at the center of a world that had just relearned its scenery.
— I don't know.
Nadia ran her hands over her face.
— I'm beginning to hate that answer.
— So am I.
— Good. Then maybe it's real.
The apple rolled into the box by itself.
It stopped against the rim of a mug that no longer existed.
A child's voice came from the bedroom.
— Nadia? Ethan asked.
— No one is in there.
The voice was counting.
— One, two, three, four, five, six.
Ethan and Nadia remained motionless.
— Seven, Anik said from the empty room.
The light went out.
In the darkness, Ethan felt Nadia's hand searching for his. He caught it. He did not know whether it was Nadia, a copy, a reward, or a gesture produced to be measured. He did not know whether the difference had existed before he asked.
A sound rose from the street. Not panic. Applause.
Thousands of people applauded something that could not be seen.
Ethan's phone lit on the floor. The screen showed the Stadelhofen camera feed. The man lay on the platform again. The train doors were closing. In the recording, Ethan remained in the carriage.
Beside him in the dark apartment, Nadia whispered:
— You got off, didn't you?
On the screen, the train departed.
Ethan tried to remember the weight of the chest beneath his palms. He remembered. He tried to remember the briefcase leaving without him. He remembered. He tried to remember whether those memories had existed before Nadia asked.
In the recording, the man turned his head toward the camera.
The white scar was beneath his chin.
— Have you decided? he asked.
Ethan did not answer.
Outside, the applause grew.
At 7:12, the entire city held its breath.
Then time continued exactly as though no one had chosen anything.
STORY 2
EVERYTHING
CHAPTER I
WHAT REMAINS ON THE SLEEVE
On the morning Julian Mercer became rich, a stranger died in his arms outside the central station.
Later, that would become the convenient form of the event. A smooth sentence with two occurrences large enough to seem to explain one another. In reality, things had happened messily and without connection.
The man had collided with Julian's shoulder in the passage leading up toward Bahnhofstrasse.
He wore a gray overcoat too heavy for early September and smelled of old rain, although the sky outside was clear. Julian had turned with the automatic irritation of a man running late who needed the crowd to be at fault.
— Watch where you're going.
The man had looked directly at him. His eyes were very clear, and a thread of blood marked the corner of his mouth.
Then his knees had folded.
Julian had caught him more from reflex than kindness. The weight entered his arms, surprisingly heavy, and the stranger's head struck his collarbone. A woman screamed. Someone dropped a coffee. The crowd parted with the efficient cowardice people display when a medical obligation appears.
— Call an ambulance, Julian had said.
No one moved during the first second. By the second, three phones were already raised.
The man was breathing in broken gasps. Julian had lowered him onto the cold floor, opened his collar, and seen blood spreading beneath the shirt. It was not a stain but a thin, almost geometric line rising from the sternum. He found no wound. When he pressed his palm against it, blood passed between his fingers, warm and too fluid.
— Can you hear me? What's your name?
The man's eyes moved toward him with effort.
— Don't give it to them, he whispered.
— Don't give them what?
The bloodied lip tightened into a humorless kind of smile.
— The missing part.
Julian felt an absurd irritation. The man was dying, and he was speaking in riddles from a bad film.
— The ambulance is coming. Stay with me.
The man seized his wrist. The strength of his fingers did not belong to the collapsing body.
— Someone else is still outside.
— Outside where?
His gaze slipped for a moment beyond Julian's shoulder toward the ceiling of the passage. Not toward the exit. Upward, toward concrete, cables, and pipes.
— Don't close the door again.
Then his hand released him.
A doctor who happened to be in the crowd bent beside them. He gave his name, which Julian immediately forgot, and began compressions. Julian remained on his knees, blood on his white sleeve and palm, until the paramedics gently pushed him aside.
At 8:46, police took his name and phone number. At 8:52, Julian entered a public restroom and tried to remove the blood with cold water. The stain widened, turning pink at the edges and almost black at the center. At 8:57, looking in the mirror, he noticed a crescent-shaped mark on his cheek. He wiped it away. The skin beneath was clean.
At 9:03, his phone vibrated.
It was not a message from the police. It was a bank notification.
Available balance: CHF 31,084,229.17.
Julian stared at the screen without blinking. The application had its usual graphics, the bank's austere blue, his name, the final four digits of the account. Beneath the sum was a single recent transaction: His first reaction was professional: a display error, a test environment accidentally exposed, a rounding problem transformed into an accounting catastrophe. His second was childish: perhaps it was true.
VELA SETTLEMENT AG — FINAL EXECUTION — CHF 31,077,814.00.
His third was shame for the second.
He closed the application. Opened it again. The sum remained.
He had seven minutes until the nine-thirty meeting, the meeting Konrad Meier had called “role alignment,” as though two corporate words could make a dismissal less visible. Julian looked at his stained shirt, then the balance. For a fraction of a second, the possibility of simply not going on seemed so easy that it frightened him.
He put his jacket over the blood and headed for the tram.
Helix Meridian occupied floors fourteen through seventeen of a glass building beside the lake.
The company sold “behavioral anticipation models” to banks, insurers, and governments that did not wish to admit it actually sold predictions about fear. Julian led the team that estimated how quickly a reasonable population turned into a crowd when fuel, food, or confidence fell beneath certain thresholds.
He was good at it. So good that he had begun to believe understanding why people did stupid things protected him from his own stupid things.
The receptionist looked at him twice.
— Are you all right?
— An accident at the station.
— Is the blood yours?
— No.
She seemed disappointed by the answer, then smiled too broadly.
— Konrad is waiting for you.
The room was called Lucerne, although it had no windows and nothing to suggest a lake.
Konrad sat at the end of the table with a woman from human resources and Lia Werner. Lia's presence surprised him. She had no formal role in the discussion, but she was the principal architect of the Orpheus system and the only person in the company who had challenged his conclusions without boring him.
Her blond hair was gathered at the nape of her neck. She held a mechanical pencil between her fingers and wore an expression Julian could not read.
— I'm sorry I'm late, he said.
Konrad looked at the stain emerging beneath his jacket.
— I assume there's an explanation.
— A man collapsed at the station.
— Did he die?
— I don't know.
— Then let us hope not. Sit down.
The woman from human resources was named Brigitte and delivered every sentence like text vetted by lawyers. Julian listened to her speak of
“restructuring,”
“operational trust,” and
“irreconcilable differences regarding the product mandate.”
The words passed by him. Beneath the table, his phone pressed against his thigh through the fabric of his trousers, as though the balance in the account had weight.
Konrad took over after several minutes.
— You have a problem with boundaries, Julian.
— Orpheus was trained on data the client had no right to provide.
— That's a legal problem, not an architectural one.
— It becomes architectural when the model learns to exploit individual trauma to alter collective decisions.
— You use dramatic words.
— Because
“response optimization” sounds better than what it does.
Brigitte straightened her papers.
— We are not here to reopen the debate.
— It was never open.
Konrad sighed. He had a face built for photographs about leadership: a clean jawline, silver hair, carefully measured sadness.
— You blocked delivery of a twenty-eight-million system without authorization. You told a client the product could produce “maladaptive compliance at social scale.” You used those exact words.
— Because it can.
— Any persuasion system can.
— Orpheus does not persuade. It closes alternatives until the desired choice seems like the only idea the person has ever had.
Lia looked up. For a moment, Julian saw something close to fear in her expression.
Konrad smiled wearily.
— You're an engineer who wants to be a prophet. It's an expensive combination.
— And you're a salesman who wants to be innocent.
Brigitte intervened at once, but the damage was done. Konrad pushed a thin folder toward Julian.
— We're offering you a generous package, mutual confidentiality, and retention of vested options. Your access ends at ten thirty.
Julian looked at the folder. Twenty minutes earlier, the sentence would have struck him in the stomach. Now the sum on the screen stood behind it, enormous and silent.
He wondered what expression a man was supposed to wear when he was dismissed and became free at the same time.
— Fine, he said.
Konrad blinked.
— Fine?
— I'll read the documents.
— Of course.
— And I won't sign today.
— You don't need to turn this into a gesture.
Julian stood.
— I'm not turning anything into anything.
When he reached the door, Lia spoke for the first time.
— Julian.
He turned.
— Wash the blood with hydrogen peroxide. Cold water isn't enough.
Her tone was so intimate that Brigitte lowered her eyes. Julian nodded and left.
His workstation had already been disabled. The screen displayed a standard message, and the drawer containing documents was locked. A security technician waited two meters away, pretending to check something on a tablet.
Julian put his headphones, two notebooks, an old photograph of the mountains, and a mug bearing the words PROBABLY NOT into a box. The rest could remain. As he gathered his things, the phone vibrated again.
A call from the bank.
He answered in German.
— Mr. Mercer, this is Martin Schaub from private-client services. We noticed that you accessed the account after the settlement was registered. We would like to confirm that the transaction is legitimate and that the device is in your possession.
Julian stopped with the photograph in his hand.
— What settlement?
— The Vela contracts. The final remittance was processed at 8:59.
— I don't have any Vela contracts.
The pause at the other end was very brief.
— You've had them since November 2017.
— In 2017, I didn't even have this account.
— The current account is the successor to the original one. The documents were migrated after the merger.
— What kind of contracts?
— Contingent rights in a private liquidation. I can't go into detail by telephone, but the file is complete. We have your signature, biometric verification, and tax confirmation.
— Does Vela Settlement AG exist?
— It existed until this morning.
— That isn't an answer.
— It's the answer I have.
Julian looked around. The security technician held his tablet motionless. Beyond the glass, his colleagues worked without looking at him. Lia stood beside the printer with her back to him, but Julian was certain she was listening.
— Freeze the funds, he said. I want no movement until I verify this.
— Of course. You should know, however, that the funds have already passed source-of-funds checks.
— Who initiated the settlement?
— The contract.
— Contracts don't initiate anything.
Another pause.
— Some apparently do.
The call ended after they arranged a meeting for the afternoon. Julian put the phone down and noticed he was holding the mountain photograph upside down. In it, he and Sofia stood on a ridge in Ticino, six years younger, their arms around each other. Above their heads, the sky was white from overexposure.
He did not remember bringing the photograph to the office.
— Do you need help? Lia asked.
She had approached without a sound.
— With a box?
— With whatever is happening.
— They fired me. That's what's happening.
— You don't look affected.
— Maybe I'm in shock.
Her gaze dropped to his sleeve.
— The man at the station?
— How do you know it was a man?
Lia raised an eyebrow.
— You said so in the room.
— Right.
— Julian, you have the color of someone who has just seen something and is trying to decide whether he liked it.
He laughed despite himself.
— That's a very specific description.
— I'm very good at specific descriptions.
She took the mug from the box, examined it, and put it back.
— Konrad expected you to fight.
— And you?
— I expected you to try to destroy him with a forty-slide presentation.
— He isn't worth forty.
— Then something has changed.
Julian felt the impulse to show her the balance. It was a simple, shameful desire: to watch the way she looked at him change. Lia had admired his mind, perhaps. Or tolerated him for his utility.
Thirty-one million could turn any ambiguity into a pleasant conclusion.
He did not show her.
— Maybe I've changed.
— In twenty minutes?
— Fast things happen.
Lia lightly touched his wrist, exactly where the stranger had gripped him. The skin hurt. When he pulled his hand away, he saw four red finger marks.
— Dinner, she said.
— Tonight. Not as compensation. Not as a colleague's gesture. Dinner.
Julian looked at her. In their three years working together, they had come close twice. Once after a conference in Lausanne, when they drank too much and she told him his intelligence would be more attractive if he did not carry it like a weapon. The second time, in a taxi, when their knees touched and neither moved. Nothing had happened. Lia had a partner then. Julian was still speaking to Sofia.
— Why now?
— Because yesterday it was a bad idea.
— And today?
— Today we no longer work together.
The answer was logical. That was precisely why it disturbed him.
— I'll write to you.
— You never write when you say you will.
— Then call me yourself.
— I'll call you at seven fourteen.
— Why seven fourteen?
She smiled.
— So you'll remember.
At the bank, everything seemed real enough to become obscene.
Martin Schaub was a small, attentive man with discreet cufflinks and an office that smelled of waxed wood. He showed Julian the contracts on a large screen. Nineteen pages, signatures, stamps, identifiers. Julian recognized his name, date of birth, old address. He also recognized his signature, including the deformation of the V he made when in a hurry.
— I don't remember this.
— It's been almost nine years.
— I would remember buying something worth thirty-one million.
— At the time, the contribution was eight thousand francs.
— I wouldn't have had eight thousand available.
Schaub scrolled to an appendix.
— The payment was made by Helix Meridian as a retention bonus.
— I didn't work at Helix in 2017.
The banker checked a line.
— According to the document, you were an external consultant.
— I wasn't.
— I understand that the discrepancy concerns you.
— It isn't a discrepancy. It's an alternative biography.
Schaub did not smile.
— We can request an independent assessment. Until then, we'll freeze withdrawals. Interest and tax obligations continue.
— Who owned Vela?
— The structure is opaque. A special-purpose vehicle, several funds, a few patents.
— What patents?
— Predictive interfaces, from what I can see.
— Predictive of what?
Schaub enlarged a field. The text had been almost completely redacted. Two words remained visible: RESPONSE YIELD.
— I don't know, the banker said.
Julian leaned closer.
At the lower corner of the document was a checked box: VOLUNTARY PARTICIPATION.
— Have you seen a contract like this before?
— No.
— And that doesn't strike you as suspicious?
— Mr. Mercer, in my profession there is a distinction between suspicious and unusual. The first requires action. The second requires higher fees.
Julian almost smiled. It was the first normal sentence he had heard in hours.
He left the bank at four thirty. The sun was lowering over the lake, and the city looked insultingly stable. Trams arrived on time. People bought bread. Two teenage girls argued beside a shop window. Nothing around him acknowledged that a man had died at the station or that thirty-one million had appeared from a past that did not belong to him.
His phone rang.
For a moment, he thought it was Lia. The screen showed an unknown number.
— Mr. Mercer? This is Inspector Frei, cantonal police. You witnessed the incident at the central station.
— How is the man?
— He was pronounced dead at 9:11.
Julian stopped beside the pedestrian crossing.
— Do you know who he was?
— Not yet. He had no identification. I have a few additional questions. Did he say anything before losing consciousness?
Julian looked at the signal. The red figure forbade him to cross, although the street was empty.
— Nothing coherent.
— Please tell me exactly.
— He said not to give them the missing part.
He heard typing at the other end.
— Anything else?
— That someone else was outside. And not to close the door again.
The typing stopped.
— Are you certain of the last sentence?
— Yes.
— Had you met this person before?
— No.
— Do you have any connection to Vela Settlement AG?
Julian did not answer immediately.
The light turned green. No one crossed.
— Why are you asking me that?
— We found a card in the lining of his overcoat. No name. Only the company logo and a number.
— What number?
— Thirty-one.
At that instant, every tram in the intersection stopped.
They did not slow. They stopped at once, without screeching or swaying, as though their image had been caught between two frames. A woman in the first tram remained with one hand raised toward her hair. A cyclist held one foot on the pedal, perfectly still. A leaf hung midway between tree and asphalt.
Julian held his breath.
— Mr. Mercer? Inspector Frei said.
The leaf fell. The trams moved. The cyclist crossed the intersection.
It had lasted perhaps one second. Perhaps less.
— I'm here, Julian said.
— Did you hear the question?
— No.
— I asked whether you could come tomorrow to make a full statement.
— Yes.
— Would seven fourteen be possible?
Julian tightened his grip on the phone.
— Why that time?
— It's the first available slot.
Someone laughed behind him. A short, familiar feminine laugh. Julian turned, convinced he would see Lia.
No one was there.
He reached home before six. His apartment was in an old building in Wiedikon, expensive enough to make him feel prudent and small enough to make him feel temporary. He put the box on the table and removed his jacket.
The bloodstain on the sleeve had disappeared.
The shirt was damp, but white. Not pink. Not imperfectly cleaned. White, except for a thin line near the cuff, like a pencil scratch.
Julian smelled it. Detergent, sweat, and, very faintly, burnt orange.
He washed his hands for a long time. The red marks on his wrist remained. He photographed them with his phone, then checked the image time: 7:14.
That was impossible. The photograph had been taken at 18:07.
He deleted the photograph. He found it again in the Recently Deleted folder, but the image had changed. Instead of his wrist, it showed a white, curved surface without corners. At the bottom was something that might have been the edge of a bed or the lid of a box.
He closed the app.
The phone rang at exactly 19:14.
Lia.
Julian watched the screen until the call was about to end.
— Hello, he said.
— You remembered.
— You made it difficult to forget.
— Dinner is still on.
— Yes.
— Good. I booked Nera. Eight thirty.
Nera was the restaurant where Julian had tried three times to get a table before giving up. The waiting list was months long.
— How did you find a place?
— I got lucky.
She said it without irony.
After hanging up, Julian opened the banking app again. The money was there. Frozen, but real.
Beneath the Vela transaction, a new document had appeared, uploaded at 19:14.
ACKNOWLEDGEMENT OF RECEIPT.
He opened it.
The page was almost blank. His name appeared at the top, followed by that day's date. Below, a single sentence:
THE BENEFICIARY HAS TAKEN POSSESSION OF THE FIRST TRANCHE.
In the Comments field, someone had written by hand, in blue ink: Do not close the door.
Julian enlarged the image. The handwriting was familiar.
It was his.
* * * Julian did not wear bloodstained clothes on the first date.
He did not call dinner with Lia a date, although he changed his shirt twice and checked his balance five times before leaving. He told himself he needed an intelligent witness. Someone who could look at the Vela document and tell him whether recognizing the handwriting was an error caused by stress or a legitimate reason to call a lawyer.
He did not take the document with him.
Nera occupied the ground floor of a former printing house. Inside, the walls preserved the marks of old brickwork, and the tables stood so far apart that every conversation seemed secret. A woman in black greeted him by name without asking about the reservation.
— Ms. Werner has arrived.
Lia sat at a corner table. She had left her hair loose and wore a simple dark-green dress. She did not look like a transformed version of his colleague, but like the same woman from whose surroundings someone had removed everything unnecessary: monitor, badge, white office light, haste.
Julian felt an immediate satisfaction. He recognized it and allowed it to remain.
— You didn't run away, Lia said.
— I don't do that.
— You disappeared for three days after Lausanne.
— I had the flu.
— You sent a seventy-page report on the second day of your flu.
— Fever makes me productive.
She looked at his hand as he sat down.
— The marks are gone.
Julian looked at his wrist. The four red impressions were no longer there. He had not noticed when they vanished.
— Probably just pressure marks.
— Probably.
The waiter brought wine without offering them a list. It was a Pinot Noir from Graubünden, the wine Julian had ordered the last time he had gone to a good restaurant with Sofia. Lia raised the bottle and read the label.
— I didn't choose this.
— Neither did I.
— Then someone knows us.
The waiter, still beside the table, smiled.
— It is part of tonight's menu.
— Is tonight's menu fixed? Julian asked.
— Tonight, yes.
— And if we don't like the wine?
— I believe you will.
The answer was polite enough to pass for professional confidence. Julian watched him leave.
— Have you begun suspecting waiters? Lia asked.
— Only those certain of my tastes.
— That includes you.
— I'm not certain of my tastes.
— That is exactly why you need people who are.
She tasted the wine. Julian did the same. It was exactly as he remembered: dry at first, then fruity, with a faint bitterness he liked. In the next second, he smelled burnt orange again. He brought the glass closer. The smell was gone.
— Tell me what happened after you left, Lia said.
— I went to the bank.
— About the severance package?
Julian looked at her. He wanted to tell her. Above all, he wanted to observe the moment when the sum entered the space between them.
— Some money came in.
— How much does “some” mean?
— Enough.
— Did you sell something?
— I don't know.
Lia set down her glass.
— That sounds either very good or criminal.
— Thirty-one million.
The transformation he had expected did not occur. Lia did not laugh, change her posture, or ask whether he was serious. She watched him for several seconds, then said:
— Francs?
— Yes.
— Then it is more than if it were euros.
— That's your reaction?
— I'm trying not to have the reaction you want.
Julian felt his face grow warm.
— I don't want any particular reaction.
— Of course you do. Otherwise you would have said, “I have a problem with an old contract,” not the number.
— The number is the problem.
— No. The number is the pleasant part.
The waiter placed before them two plates of smoked trout and thin apple slices. Julian had not ordered, but the food looked exactly like a photograph he had saved months before from an article about a restaurant in Copenhagen. He remembered sending the photograph to Sofia, back when they still sent each other things for no reason.
— Have you ever heard of Vela Settlement? he asked.
Lia chewed slowly.
— Vela, as in the company that closed today?
— You know about it.
— I saw an internal alert. Konrad had a lunchtime meeting with Legal. He was very agitated.
— Why?
— I don't know. After you left, they revoked my access to Orpheus.
— Yours too?
— Only to the deep-simulation modules. Officially, they are conducting a security review.
Julian felt a sting of guilt, followed by one of satisfaction. His departure had produced consequences.
— Is Orpheus connected to Vela?
— Not in the architecture I know.
— But?
— But the name Vela appeared in an old synthetic-data repository. I saw it two years ago. I thought it was an internal generator.
— What did it generate?
— Life trajectories. Decision variants, rewards, responses to loss. Nothing unusual for us.
— “Response yield”?
Her fork stopped.
— Where did you see that phrase?
— In the contract.
Lia ran her tongue across her lower lip. The gesture had distracted him many times in meetings.
Now it seemed deliberate, although it probably was not.
— Yield was a metric, she said. How much useful behavior you obtain from a stimulus before the subject identifies it as manipulation.
— Subject?
— That was what they called the synthetic agents.
— Not people.
— Officially, no.
— And unofficially?
Lia looked toward the empty table beside them.
— Julian, every company working with behavioral models pretends its data are more synthetic than they are.
— My contract says voluntary participation.
— Perhaps you signed up for a study.
— I don't remember.
— You had an accident in 2017.
— What accident?
— That is exactly my point. You disappeared for almost two months.
Julian laughed shortly.
— I didn't know you in 2017.
— I read your file when you joined Helix.
— My file didn't contain an accident.
— It contained medical leave.
— I was in Canada. I was working for a startup in Montréal.
— No, Lia said.
The simplicity with which she said it irritated him.
— Yes, I was.
— In November 2017, you were admitted to Sankt Alban Clinic. Post-traumatic neurological assessment. When you returned, you signed the consulting contract with Helix.
— I've never been to Sankt Alban.
— Then your file was lying.
Julian took out his phone and searched his old calendar. The account preserved events back to 2014. November 2017 was full: a flight to Montréal, meetings, photographs from a coworking space, dinner with three founders. It was all there.
He opened the photograph from 19 November. He stood beside a wall of graffiti, holding a coffee. He enlarged the image. On his left wrist was a white band, like the ones used in hospitals.
— What is it? Lia asked.
He handed her the phone. She looked at the photograph, then at him.
— I don't see anything strange.
— The wristband.
— What wristband?
Julian took back the phone. The hand in the photograph was bare.
He stared for so long that the screen went dark.
— You're tired, Lia said.
— Don't tell me that.
— You watched a man die, were fired, and became a millionaire on the same day. Fatigue would be the healthy response.
— Thirty-one million doesn't surprise you at all?
She rested an elbow on the table.
— It surprises me. Just not as much as would satisfy you.
— Again you assume I want something.
— We all want something when we tell the truth. Otherwise we would keep it to ourselves.
The way she looked at him was direct and warm. Julian felt the conversation slipping from contracts toward something else, precisely when he could no longer decide whether that soothed or frightened him.
— What do you want? he asked.
— Tonight?
— In general.
— In general, I want not to be used by people who call the use “compatibility.” Tonight, I want to see what you do when you no longer have any financial reason to be cautious.
— Perhaps the money is frozen.
— Perhaps.
— Perhaps I lose it tomorrow.
— Then I learn something sooner.
Julian smiled.
— Romantic.
— Romance is a credit system with weak collateral.
— Did you prepare that line?
— Years ago.
Courses they had not ordered arrived at their table: venison, a cold cucumber soup, warm bread with smoked butter. Julian liked everything. Twice he tried to ask for the menu and twice the conversation made him forget. Lia talked about leaving Hamburg, about her mother collecting hotel keys, and about her fear of becoming good at something she despised. Julian told her about Sofia without using her name, about how a relationship could die through small postponements, none large enough to justify a breakup and all of them together impossible to forgive.
The wine relaxed him. The sum in his account became a pleasant presence, like an extra room in the apartment.
When he asked for the bill, the waiter told him it had been paid.
— By whom?
— Ms. Levant.
— I don't know any Ms. Levant.
The waiter discreetly indicated a table by the window. A black-haired woman, perhaps forty, sat alone. She wore a cream suit and held a glass of water. When Julian looked at her, she raised the glass in greeting.
Lia turned her chair just enough to see her.
— Celia Levant, she said. Meridian Arc. Venture capital, energy, defense, neuromorphic computing. She doesn't buy dinner for strangers.
— Apparently she does now.
The woman stood and came toward them. She moved without hurry, with the confidence of someone for whom other people's waiting was a form of politeness.
— Mr. Mercer, she said. Ms. Werner.
Her voice was low, with an accent difficult to place.
— Do we know each other? Julian asked.
— Not yet.
— Then thank you for dinner, but it wasn't necessary.
— Necessary things are rarely interesting.
Lia crossed her arms.
— How did you know we were here?
Celia looked at her with an almost imperceptible smile.
— I released the table.
— For us?
— For Mr. Mercer. You were a pleasant surprise.
Julian suddenly felt exposed. He disliked how quickly the feeling mixed with vanity.
— What do you want?
Celia took out a business card and placed it on the table. It contained only her name and a number.
— To show you something that belongs to you.
— What?
— A house.
— I don't own a house.
— Then you'll be surprised.
— Vela?
For the first time, her expression changed. Not much. Her pupils merely narrowed.
— Vela was a means, she said. Not an owner.
— A means for what?
— Putting the right things in the hands of the right people.
— Including thirty-one million?
— Money is the cheapest part.
Lia leaned forward.
— Who told you about the settlement?
Celia ignored the question.
— Tomorrow at eleven. A car will collect you from home, Mr. Mercer. You may come as well, Ms. Werner, if Julian thinks he needs protection.
— Julian doesn't think anything on my behalf, Lia said.
Celia looked at her again. This time the smile was clear.
— Not yet.
She was gone before Julian could answer.
Lia watched the woman's figure until she reached the door.
— That was subtle, she said.
— I don't like it.
— Yes, you do.
— I don't like that she knows where I live.
— That's not what I meant.
Julian drank the rest of the wine.
— I'm not used to strange women buying my dinner and offering me houses.
— You'll adapt quickly.
— You sound angry.
— I'm not.
— Then why say that as though you are?
Lia picked up her bag.
— Because part of you just decided you deserve this.
Outside, the air was cold. They walked toward the river without speaking. At a corner, a taxi stopped before Julian raised his hand. The driver lowered the window.
— Wiedikon?
Julian remained on the pavement.
— How do you know?
— The app, the driver said.
— I didn't order one.
The man checked the dashboard screen.
— Julian Mercer, destination Wiedikon, two passengers.
Lia looked at him.
— Did you order it without realizing?
— No.
— Then we have an efficient admirer.
They got in. Julian wanted to cancel, but it was already raining although the forecast had been clear. Drops struck the windshield and the city broke apart into reflections. Lia sat close. Her knee touched his. Neither moved.
— Are you going tomorrow? she asked.
— Yes.
— Why?
— Because I want to know.
— No. You want the house.
— I haven't seen it.
— That's exactly why you want it. It could be anything.
Julian turned toward her.
— Are you coming?
— Yes.
— Why?
Lia kissed him.
It was not a hesitant kiss. She put a hand at the back of his neck and pulled him toward her with a certainty that made the question useless. Julian tasted wine, the cold on her lips, and a faint bitterness of orange. The taxi took a turn. Lia did not stop.
When they separated, the driver was looking straight ahead.
— Does that answer it? she whispered.
— Partly.
— You always need more data.
At the apartment, Lia came in without his explicitly inviting her. She left her shoes by the door and walked straight to the window as though she knew where it was. Julian switched on a lamp. The warm light made his home look more beautiful than he had ever seen it.
— Wine? he asked.
— Water.
In the kitchen he found two bottles of mineral water in the refrigerator that he had not bought.
The label was from a hotel in Montréal. He took one, then stopped.
— Lia.
— Yes?
— Have you been here before?
— No.
— How did you know where the window was? You can't see it from the hall.
She looked at him for several seconds.
— Apartments have windows, Julian.
— You know what I'm asking.
— I know you're trying to turn every detail into evidence.
— Tonight the details are volunteering.
Lia came over. She took the bottle from his hand and put it on the table.
— Then don't analyze them for an hour.
She kissed him again.
Later, Julian would remember the night in precise fragments and, precisely because of their precision, distrust them: her skin cold at first, the sound of rain, a scratch on her left shoulder, then on her right although it had not moved; the way she said his name as though she had rehearsed it; the light of his phone coming on by itself at 3:31; a voice coming perhaps from the street, perhaps from sleep.
“There's someone else outside.”
He woke at seven. Lia slept with her back to him. The rain had stopped. Julian remained still for several minutes, watching the lines of her shoulders and trying to feel happiness without examining it.
His phone was vibrating on the floor.
A message from Inspector Frei: STATEMENT CANCELED. CASE TRANSFERRED.
Another from the bank: PREVENTIVE HOLD LIFTED. FUNDS AVAILABLE.
A third, from Celia Levant's number: CAR ARRIVES AT 10:31. THE HOUSE IS WAITING
FOR YOU.
Julian opened the banking app. He transferred one hundred francs to his secondary current account, just as a test.
Transfer confirmed.
Then a thousand.
Confirmed.
Then he paid off the remainder of his student loan in full, twenty-seven thousand. The screen displayed zero debt. No music played. The sky did not open. The room remained the same.
And yet something inside him settled into a new place.
Lia turned over.
— What time is it?
— Seven fourteen, Julian said.
He looked at his phone. It was exactly 7:14.
— See? she murmured with her eyes closed. A good day begins when probabilities get tired of opposing you.
Julian did not answer.
He did not remember Lia ever using sentences like that.
When he got up to draw the curtains, he noticed a man on the pavement across the street. Gray overcoat, head raised toward Julian's window.
He was too far away for Julian to see his face.
But when the man raised his hand, the cuff was stained with blood.
CHAPTER II
THE HOUSE THAT WAS WAITING FOR HIM
The car Celia Levant sent had no logo, and the driver did not ask their names. At 10:31, a black sedan stopped in front of Julian's building. The rear door opened by itself.
Lia had arrived two minutes earlier by taxi and wore the same clothes as the night before, covered by one of Julian's coats. She did not look embarrassed. She looked annoyed that the sedan had arrived exactly on time.
— At least it's not a limousine, she said.
— Would you have refused?
— I would have judged it harder.
They got in. Black coffee for Julian and unsweetened green tea for Lia were waiting on the console between the seats. Neither touched the drinks.
— Destination? Julian asked.
The driver looked forward.
— Ms. Levant said you prefer to see the road.
— That's not a destination.
— No.
They headed south, then left Zürich. The city receded behind them, replaced by green slopes, immaculate farms, and villages where nothing seemed left to chance. Julian tried to follow the route on his phone. The map showed his position, but the road line vanished after a few hundred meters, as though the app had failed to load the area.
— Weak signal? Lia asked.
— We have five bars.
Her phone did the same.
After forty minutes, they passed a sign for Zug. Ten minutes later, they passed the same sign again. Julian noticed an orange scratch on the post at exactly the same height.
— We made a loop, he said.
The driver did not react.
— No, Lia said. The lake was on the other side the first time.
— Then the sign is duplicated.
— Or we are.
She tried to make it a joke, but did not smile.
A few minutes later, the car left the main road and climbed a narrow lane through fir trees. A metal gate opened without their stopping. Beyond it, the ground descended toward a small, almost circular lake. The house stood on a tongue of stone withdrawn among the trees.
Julian saw it and felt such a powerful recognition that he nearly asked the driver to stop.
It was not a spectacular house. Two levels, gray stone, dark wood, tall windows facing the water. Its lack of extravagance was precisely what made it impossible. At twelve, Julian had drawn a similar house on the backs of his mathematics notebooks: a place where no neighbor could hear anything, with a workshop, a library, and a tunnel into the mountain. Later, he had saved dozens of architecture photographs and told himself his taste had matured. The house before him contained all the adult corrections applied to the child's fantasy.
— You've been here before, Lia said.
It was not a question.
— You have the face of someone returning.
Celia was waiting on the stone steps. She wore black trousers and a white shirt, no coat despite the cold air.
— Did you find it easily? she asked.
— Your driver did, Lia said. We don't know where we are.
— That can be remedied.
— The map doesn't work.
— Many maps don't work here.
Celia extended a hand toward Julian. He shook it. Her palm was warm and dry.
— Welcome home.
— This isn't my house.
— The documents say otherwise.
In the vestibule, a file rested on a low table. Julian opened it before removing his coat. Sale agreement, cadastral extract, insurance, tax valuation. Owner: Julian Leon Mercer. Purchase date: November 19, 2017.
— That's impossible.
— You said that about the money too, Celia said.
— A signature doesn't mean I signed.
— An interesting observation from someone who designed identity systems.
— Who paid?
— A Vela vehicle. The property was held in administration until the release conditions were met.
— What conditions?
— Termination of your relationship with Helix Meridian and liquidation of Vela.
Lia leafed through the pages.
— Why would anyone buy Julian a house nine years before he was fired?
— Because some events are easier to produce than to predict.
— Did you produce his dismissal?
Celia looked at her.
— Konrad Meier produced most of his own problems.
Julian closed the file.
— I want answers, not sentences that sound like answers.
— You'll receive the complete documents. But first, see the house.
— Why?
— Because answers become more credible after a person decides he likes the consequences.
Julian should have left. He knew it with the clarity with which he recognized a critical vulnerability in a system. He did not leave.
Celia led them through the rooms. The living room had a recessed fireplace, stone walls, and windows reaching the floor. The library was empty except for an old edition of Solaris, the novel Julian had read three times as a teenager. The kitchen was professional but understated. The main bedroom overlooked the lake. In the dressing room were three suits tailored to his measurements, shoes, and shirts with the initials J.M.
— This is starting to become grotesque, Lia said.
Celia opened a drawer. Inside were watches, none ostentatious.
— Everything was purchased from a preference profile.
— From what data?
— Julian has provided a great deal of data throughout his life.
— Not for this.
— People rarely provide data for the purpose in which it is ultimately used.
Julian picked up a shirt. The size was correct. On the left cuff, white thread formed his initials.
He remembered the blood in the station and put it back.
— Who built the profile?
— Vela contracted several systems.
— Orpheus?
— A precursor.
Lia turned toward Celia.
— Orpheus had no precursor.
— Everything has a precursor. Sometimes the future.
— That's not spirituality, Lia said. It's an absurd technical claim.
— Then don't take it spiritually.
They went down to the lower level. There, the house became less a house and more an installation. A room with workbenches, industrial printers, a precision lathe, microscopes, measuring equipment. Julian stopped in the doorway. Some of the machines cost as much as his apartment. On a panel, the tools were arranged in the order he would have chosen.
— What's the workshop for? he asked.
— Your work.
— I don't have any work.
Celia handed him a tablet. On the screen was a hand-drawn diagram: a network of memristive elements connected in an unusual way, with a thermal feedback loop. Julian recognized the handwriting. His, again.
— Where did you find this?
— In the Vela archive.
— I didn't draw it.
— Then someone imitates you very well.
Julian enlarged the diagram. The idea was elegant. Not complete, but good enough to start his mind moving before he could refuse. If the elements behaved as the notes suggested, the device could store computational states without energy and use thermal noise for correction instead of merely fighting it.
— That shouldn't be stable, Lia said over his shoulder.
— Not at room temperature.
— The note says it is.
— The note is wrong.
Celia pointed toward a bench at the back. Beneath a transparent housing sat a prototype the size of a matchbox, connected to sensors.
— Check it.
Julian took off his coat.
The next two hours passed too quickly. He forgot the house, the money, the dead stranger. He opened housings, measured resistance, changed parameters. Lia worked beside him, skeptical at first, then silent. The device was not only stable; it produced a reduction in consumption that contradicted three practical limits and perhaps one theoretical one. It did not violate physics. It used physics in a way Julian had never seen.
— Who built this? he asked.
— You did, Celia said.
— No.
— The schematic is yours. The instructions are yours. The prototype was assembled automatically.
— When?
— Last night.
Lia looked toward the printers.
— You can't manufacture the active material with this equipment.
— It was delivered.
— From where?
— From storage.
— What storage?
Celia smiled faintly.
— The one where things are kept before people know they want them.
Julian set the probe down.
— Enough.
At that exact moment, the lights went out.
It was not a complete outage. The instrument LEDs remained lit, but the room filled with diffuse white light from no visible source. The walls seemed to curve. The corners disappeared.
Julian smelled antiseptic.
He was no longer in the workshop.
He was lying down. He could not see his body, but he knew he was stretched out. Above him was a white surface. To the left, a woman with one side of her head shaved held a hand against a red door. There was blood at her temple.
— Julian, she was saying. Look at me. Not at them.
Somewhere beyond the door, someone was pounding.
— There's someone else outside, a child's voice said.
The woman turned her head.
— I know.
— Open it, Julian said, or heard himself say.
— I can't until you give me the missing part.
The image tore apart.
The workshop snapped back. Lia was holding his arm. Celia stood a few steps away, motionless.
— What happened? Lia asked.
— The power.
— The power didn't go out.
Julian looked at the monitors. Every graph had continued without interruption.
— You went white, Lia said. You said something.
— What?
— “I can't.”
Celia was watching him with an attention that did not resemble concern.
— Did you see the room? she asked.
Julian felt his stomach tighten.
— What room?
— You looked at the wall as though it was no longer here.
— It was a migraine.
— Do you get migraines?
— I do now.
Lia stepped between them.
— We're leaving.
Celia did not try to stop them.
— The house remains yours, Mr. Mercer. The workshop, the funds, and the rights to the prototype. You have no obligation to me.
— No one gives this much without obligations.
— I didn't say there are no obligations. I said they are not to me.
In the vestibule, Julian picked up his coat. The file of documents remained on the table.
— Take it, Celia said.
— Send it to my lawyer.
— You don't have a lawyer.
— I will by tonight.
— Of course.
When he opened the door, Celia called his name.
— Julian.
He turned.
— Don't go down to the level below the workshop yet.
— You didn't show us a level below the workshop.
— Exactly.
The car took them back. For the first twenty minutes, Lia said nothing. Julian watched the trees and tried to reconstruct the white scene without turning it into a memory. It could have been a dissociative episode. An association between blood, the contract, and the stranger's words. The brain made montages more convincing than any simulator.
— What did you see? Lia asked.
— Nothing.
— You lied badly.
— I don't want to give it shape by saying it aloud.
— It already has a shape.
