— A room. An injured woman. A door.
— Did you know her?
— No.
— Did she know you?
Julian looked at Lia.
— Why are you asking that?
— Because you said “I can't” as an answer to someone.
He rubbed his wrist. The four red marks had reappeared.
— Maybe I need a neurologist.
— Yes.
— You answered too quickly.
— Because I don't want the explanation you prefer to be the most interesting one.
— What's my preferred explanation?
— That you've been chosen for something.
Julian wanted to defend himself. He could not find a sentence that did not confirm the accusation.
His phone vibrated. A message from a number he had not seen in eighteen months.
Sofia.
I HEARD ABOUT HELIX. ARE YOU OKAY?
Julian stared at the words until Lia noticed the name.
— The ex?
— Yes.
— Fast.
— Maybe someone told her.
— Who?
— We have mutual friends.
Lia turned toward the window.
— Sure.
It was the first trace of jealousy Julian had seen in her. It gave him immediate pleasure, followed by contempt for his own pleasure. The two feelings coexisted without canceling each other out.
He replied to Sofia: I'M FINE. THINGS ARE COMPLICATED.
She wrote back almost instantly: WITH YOU THEY ALWAYS WERE. CAN I SEE YOU?
Before he could answer, another message appeared, this time from Celia Levant.
A photograph.
Julian opened it. The image showed the house plan, lower level. Beneath the workshop there was, indeed, a windowless room. It had no right angles. At its center was an elongated rectangle.
Beside it, a handwritten note:
DO NOT OPEN UNTIL YOU REMEMBER WHO WAS LEFT OUTSIDE.
Lia read over his shoulder.
— Your handwriting again?
— Yes.
— Then either someone knows you better than you know yourself, or you know yourself less than you think.
The car passed the sign for Zug again.
This time the orange scratch on the post formed a clear number.
* * * Over the next nine days, the world began offering Julian Mercer apologies for everything it had denied him until then.
The bank transferred the funds into a new structure and assigned him a management team. A lawyer recommended by three different people, none connected to the others, examined the Vela documents and declared them “unusual, but perfectly enforceable.” The land registry confirmed the house. The tax authority confirmed the settlement. The biometric signature had been validated by two institutions that no longer existed.
— That doesn't mean you signed, the lawyer told him. It only means no normal court will readily accept that you didn't.
His name was Farid Nasser, a man with round glasses and a healthy aversion to mysteries.
— What do you advise?
— Don't spend as though you deserved the money. People become careless when they confuse legality with destiny.
Julian hired him on the spot.
He bought a car not because he wanted one, he told himself, but because his old sedan had begun having transmission problems. He chose an understated electric model, though the “understated” version cost two years of his salary. The dealer told him the waiting list was eight months, then checked his name and found the exact color he had requested available in stock.
He bought two suits for meetings. The tailor measured him once and brought out a jacket from the back that fit perfectly.
— I had the impression I'd worked for you before, the man said.
— I don't think so.
— Then you have a brother with the same shoulders.
Julian had no brother.
On the third day, Celia sent him documents for a new company: Mercer Continuum. Julian held sixty-one percent, Lia twelve, future employees ten, and Celia's fund the rest. Initial capitalization was one hundred million.
— I haven't agreed, he said on the phone.
— That's why the documents are drafts, Celia replied.
— Why does Lia have shares?
— Because you'll want her there.
— Don't decide what I'll want.
— I don't. I just save time.
That same afternoon, Lia resigned from Helix.
— Not for you, she clarified as she put her laptop on his kitchen table. Konrad froze the project and asked us to delete the test logs. That's enough for me.
— Celia offered you twelve percent.
— She offered me eight. I told her fifteen would be fair. She came down to twelve.
— Without asking me.
— You don't get to decide what I'm worth just because the company has your name on it.
He liked the answer. He also liked that she had installed her things in his apartment as if the hesitation stage had been bypassed through an efficient protocol. Two dresses appeared in the wardrobe, a toothbrush in the bathroom, a black notebook on the desk. Julian noticed every object and said nothing.
Sofia came on the fourth evening.
Julian went downstairs to let her in, though the intercom worked. He saw her through the glass in the door and the time between them compressed in an unpleasant way. Her hair was shorter. She wore a blue coat and the same calm expression with which, eighteen months earlier, she had told him she could no longer love him in the space left over between work and regret.
— You look good, she said.
— So do you.
They embraced with stiff arms. Sofia pulled back first and touched his cheek.
— You have a scar.
— Where?
— Here.
Her finger passed beside his right temple. Julian had looked in the mirror before coming down.
He had seen nothing.
— I don't.
Sofia looked at him more closely.
— You did.
— When?
— After the accident.
Julian felt a cold emptiness beneath his sternum.
— What accident?
— Montréal.
— I didn't have an accident in Montréal.
— Julian, you came home with your head bandaged.
— I was at a conference.
Sofia lowered her hand.
— Maybe I'm remembering it wrong.
She did not say it like someone who believed that.
Inside the apartment, she saw Lia's laptop case and her shoes near the door. She did not ask immediately. Julian poured her wine. They sat at the table, and for several minutes the conversation skirted the important things before running into them.
— I heard you left Helix, Sofia said.
— They fired me.
— I heard about the money too.
— From whom?
— I don't know. People talk.
— “People” don't talk about thirty-one million. Officials who lose their jobs if they talk do.
— Then maybe you told me.
— I didn't.
Sofia rotated her glass.
— I dreamed you told me.
— You came because you dreamed I was rich?
The question came out crueler than he had intended.
She looked at him without blinking.
— I came because I dreamed you were standing in a white room, asking me to open a door. In the morning I saw online that you no longer worked at Helix. I called Ana, and she told me about the money.
— What Ana?
— Ana Petrescu.
— I don't know anyone by that name.
— She had dinner with us twice.
Julian remembered every dinner in the old apartment. There had been no Ana Petrescu.
— Sofia, have you seen a doctor?
She smiled sadly.
— That was your specialty. Making me wonder whether my own memories had authorization.
The door opened. Lia came in carrying two bags of food and stopped when she saw Sofia.
The two women looked at each other. Julian stood up pointlessly.
— Lia, this is Sofia. Sofia, Lia.
— I know, they both said.
The silence that followed was almost comic.
— From where? Julian asked.
Lia put the bags on the counter.
— The photograph at the office.
Sofia folded her arms.
— You talked about her.
— When?
— When we were together.
— I didn't talk about colleagues.
— You talked in your sleep.
Lia took three cartons from the bag.
— I got Thai. There are three portions.
— You knew there were three of us? Julian asked.
— The app recommended three.
— The app didn't know Sofia was here.
— Maybe it assumes rich people don't eat alone.
Sofia laughed. Lia looked at her, then laughed too. In less than ten minutes, all three were sitting around the table discussing Julian in the third person, as though he were a company they were evaluating for acquisition.
— He sets his alarm twenty minutes early and then negotiates with it, Sofia said.
— Now he wakes up before the alarm, Lia said.
— Because he doesn't sleep.
— He sleeps. He grinds his teeth at 3:31.
Julian put down his fork.
— Why 3:31?
— I don't know, Lia said. I looked at the clock.
Sofia became serious.
— He used to do that before.
— I don't grind my teeth.
They both ignored him.
It was a situation Julian had imagined in shameful variations after the breakup: the woman who had left seeing him desired by one who stayed; one woman's regret confirming the other's choice; himself at the center of a tension he did not have to create, only manage. Reality was more ordinary and more pleasant. The wine flowed. Sofia relaxed her shoulders. Lia touched his knee beneath the table. Julian noticed Sofia's gaze drop toward the gesture.
One part of him wanted to stop the game.
Another wanted to see how far it would go without his asking.
His phone vibrated. Celia was inviting them to the lakeside house the following evening to sign the company documents.
“All three of you,” the message specified.
— She knows I'm here? Sofia asked after Julian showed them.
— She seems to know a lot, Lia said.
— Who is she?
— A woman who buys dinners and builds companies before we ask her to.
Sofia raised her eyebrows.
— I see. You've had a difficult week.
The next evening, they returned to the house in Julian's new car. Lia sat in front. Sofia in back.
Julian could feel the tension between them like additional heat in the cabin and caught himself driving slightly faster than necessary.
Celia greeted them with champagne and no sign of surprise. The table was set for four on the glass-enclosed terrace. Mist was rising over the lake.
The company papers were signed in twenty minutes. Farid joined by video and confirmed every clause. Julian became chief executive. Lia headed research. Celia controlled financing but not operations. Everything was more favorable than Julian would have negotiated.
— Where's the trap? Julian asked after signing.
— In what you'll do when you no longer have constraints to blame, Celia said.
Sofia looked at her with interest.
— Do you say that to every founder?
— Only to those who think their lack of power made them good.
At dinner, Celia asked Sofia about her work in urban design, Lia about Orpheus, and Julian about his childhood. Each answer seemed to complete an invisible file. At one point, Celia asked:
— What's the first wish you remember having?
— To fly, Sofia said.
— Never to depend on anyone again, Lia said.
Julian thought of the drawings of the house.
— To have a door I could lock.
Celia looked at him.
— From the inside or the outside?
— The inside.
— Are you sure?
Behind them, from deep in the house, came a blow. A single dull thud, like a fist against a thick door.
Sofia flinched.
— Is someone else here?
— No, Celia said.
— Then what was that?
— The house settling.
Lia looked at Julian. They both thought of the level beneath the workshop.
Celia raised her glass.
— To beginnings.
The mist had covered the lake. The terrace glass reflected the table, their faces, and the warm light. In the reflection, Julian sat surrounded by three beautiful women, in a house he had wanted before knowing it existed, owner of a fortune and an impossible technology. It was the image of a man to whom the world had finally apologized.
Except his reflection did not raise its glass when he did.
It remained still for a fraction of a second, hand on the table, head turned toward the dark corridor.
Then it caught up.
Julian drank.
Later, after Sofia fell asleep in a guest room and Lia withdrew, furious that he had invited her to stay, Julian went downstairs for water. He heard voices in the kitchen. Celia and Lia were speaking quietly.
— Not yet, Lia was saying.
— He's getting close, Celia replied.
— He thinks he chose.
— He has to think that.
Julian walked in.
They both turned toward him.
— What am I supposed to believe? he asked.
Lia was holding a box of tea.
— That you chose tea, she said. Celia insists you want coffee.
There were two mugs on the table. One smelled of coffee. The other of burnt orange.
— What were you saying before?
Celia smiled.
— Exactly what you heard.
— Not about tea.
— Then ask yourself why you need the answer to be more interesting.
A second blow came from beneath the floor.
This time, three short knocks and one long one.
Lia put the box down.
— That's not the house settling.
Celia looked at Julian.
— No, she said. That's something waiting.
* * * Mercer Continuum did not need a modest phase.
Normally, a new company spent months looking for space, recruiting people, negotiating licenses, and explaining to investors why it still had nothing to show. Julian's company received all of those things before its first official Monday. A laboratory became available in a building near the airport. Two engineering teams at a competing firm resigned in the same week. A permit that was supposed to take six months was approved in four days after an official “noticed a misclassification.”
The prototype at the lakeside house became ten prototypes. Ten became a stable board capable of running a compact language model at such low power consumption that Lia repeated the measurements seven times.
— I don't like it, she said.
Julian stood beside the optical bench, watching the nearly flat graph.
— The fact that it works?
— The fact that it works exactly as much as it needs to for every demonstration and degrades when we try to understand why.
— That's an instrumentation problem.
— I changed the instruments.
— Then the material has a transition we haven't modeled.
— And why does the transition appear only when we have an audience?
Julian looked through the glass wall. In the adjoining room waited representatives from two funds, a chip manufacturer, and the Ministry of Economic Affairs. Celia was speaking with them. They all seemed convinced already.
— Because that's when we push the system harder, he said.
— We don't push it. We ask it to repeat what it did on its own.
— Lia, did you come here so we could build something, or so we could prove it's impossible?
— I came so we wouldn't fall in love with a result before knowing who's producing it.
The reply struck him more personally than she had intended. Julian put on his safety glasses.
— We start in three minutes.
The demonstration worked perfectly.
The board executed the processing task, the temperature remained stable, and power consumption fell by ninety-four percent relative to the reference architecture. One of the ministry representatives asked how long integration into a data center would take. Julian said a year. Celia said six months. The man wrote down six.
After the demonstration, the company's valuation rose to 1.8 billion based on a round Julian had not yet approved. The news appeared on financial sites before he left the laboratory.
The headline called him “the engineer who left ethics for efficiency.”
— It's the other way around, Julian said.
Celia read the article on her phone.
— Headlines don't describe. They place people inside a story they recognize.
— Then correct it.
— Why? Their story says you chose success. It's more useful than one where you were fired.
— Useful to whom?
— To you, first of all.
Within two weeks, Julian had been invited to three conferences, two official dinners, and a podcast he had mocked repeatedly. At every appearance, people asked him questions easier than he deserved. When he gave a good answer, they laughed before the punchline. When he hesitated, they called the hesitation depth.
He discovered that reputation was not admiration. It was a collective agreement to interpret anything he did favorably.
He liked it.
He told himself he liked it only because for years he had been forced to persuade less competent people. Now he no longer wasted time. Now he could work.
Even so, he began testing his luck.
At a reception, he imagined that a minister would ask him to lead a national critical-infrastructure group. Ten minutes later, the minister pulled him aside and made exactly that proposal, in the same broad terms Julian had anticipated.
One morning on the way to the laboratory, he thought he would like not to stop at a single traffic light. Every light turned green before he reached it.
The next day he tried again. This time two lights remained red. He felt relieved until he noticed that both intersections had been blocked by ambulances. If the lights had turned green, he would have been in their way.
He bought shares in a shipping company at random and sold them that afternoon at an eight-percent gain. He repeated the experiment with a pharmaceutical company. Eleven percent. On the third attempt, he deliberately chose a nearly bankrupt firm. That same day, the government announced a rescue contract.
— Are you manipulating the market, or is the market manipulating you? Lia asked when he showed her the trades.
— I'm testing a hypothesis.
— With two million francs.
— The amount doesn't change the experiment.
— It changes the man who enjoys the result.
— I'm not enjoying it.
Lia looked at the screen, then at his face.
— You bought a new car because the old transmission had a noise only you could hear. Now you're betting two million to prove the universe likes you. You don't need to be a psychologist to see the pattern.
Julian closed the app.
— I didn't say the universe likes me.
— What hypothesis are you testing?
— That I'm receiving information before I know I have it.
— Intuition?
— Or unconscious modeling. Or someone is steering my choices.
— Who?
— Vela. Orpheus. Celia. I don't know.
— And your choice is to cooperate until you find out?
— My choice is to get data.
Lia laughed without amusement.
— That's what you call anything you want to do.
That evening, Sofia was waiting at the house. She had temporarily moved into one of the guest rooms under the pretext of helping him design the new workspaces. Lia did not officially live there, but slept in his room four nights out of seven. Celia had her own key and appeared without warning.
None of them named the arrangement. Julian was grateful for the silence and, secretly, offended by it.
Sofia sat on the dock with her bare feet in the water. Julian sat beside her.
— Lia's still at the lab? she asked.
— Yes.
— Celia's coming at nine.
— How do you know?
— She told me.
— She didn't tell me.
Sofia looked at him sideways.
— She doesn't have to tell you everything.
— It's my company.
— You say that a lot lately.
— Because people forget.
— Who forgets?
— No one. It was an expression.
Sofia took her feet out of the water.
— When we were together, you had this fantasy that one day everyone would depend on an idea only you could understand.
— I never had that fantasy.
— You said it one night after drinking.
— People say things.
— Yes. And sometimes the world hears them.
Julian turned toward her.
— What are you trying to say?
— That everything you've wanted is coming toward you too fast. Money, the company, Lia, this house. Me.
— You came on your own.
— I know.
— Do you regret it?
Sofia hesitated. Julian felt a sharp pleasure before she answered.
— Not yet.
He kissed her. It had not been planned, but neither was it surprising. Sofia responded for a second, then pulled away.
— Don't do that just to see if you can.
— I didn't.
— Then why did you look toward the house?
Julian looked up. Lia was standing at the bedroom window.
He did not know how long she had been watching them.
The evening turned into a controlled argument, more dangerous than one with shouting. Lia said she owed him no exclusivity after two weeks, then accused him of using that lack of obligation as a license. Sofia said she had not come to win a contest, but refused to leave merely so Lia could feel secure. Celia arrived in the middle of the discussion, listened for several minutes, and poured wine into four glasses.
— Aren't you ashamed? Lia asked.
— Of what, exactly?
— You're enjoying this.
— No, Celia said. I'm observing.
— What?
Celia looked at Julian.
— How quickly a man confuses abundance with entitlement.
Julian stood.
— You came for a business meeting. We can have the meeting, or you can leave.
— Of course.
Celia opened her tablet. As though the tension were merely ambient noise, she presented the figures for the new round. The company could receive four hundred million. One consortium was prepared to buy exclusive rights for military infrastructure. Another offered access to Europe's largest data centers.
— We're not selling exclusive rights to the military, Julian said.
— Then you're not selling only technology, Celia replied. You're selling morality. It's more expensive and has fewer buyers.
— We can finance it ourselves.
— With your money?
— How generous. You risk thirty million to preserve control of a company valued at nearly two billion.
— This isn't about control.
Lia and Sofia looked at him at the same time.
Julian stopped.
Celia smiled.
— Of course it isn't.
The next day, the market listened again.
Julian announced publicly that Mercer Continuum would not accept military exclusivity and would release part of the architecture under an open license. The press called him a moral visionary.
The company's valuation rose by another six hundred million. Protesters outside the laboratory, who had come to condemn the technology, had changed their signs by noon and were demanding government subsidies for it.
Konrad Meier asked for a meeting.
They met in a hotel near the lake. Konrad looked older than he had three weeks earlier.
— Helix has rights to any invention derived from your work there, he said.
— My lawyer disagrees.
— Your lawyer is paid to disagree.
— And you came alone to frighten me?
Konrad folded his hands.
— I came to warn you. Vela isn't what you think it is.
— I don't think anything.
— That's exactly what makes you useful.
Julian smelled burnt orange.
— Did you sign the contracts in 2017? he asked.
Konrad lowered his eyes.
— I witnessed a signing.
— With me?
— With someone who answered to your name.
— What does that mean?
— It means you should stop.
— Why?
— Because the results aren't for us.
— For whom?
Konrad looked up. Behind him, every screen in the hotel lobby went dark at once. The wall clock showed 7:14, though it was nearly noon.
— I don't know, he said. But the day I understood the question was wrong, you closed the door.
Julian leaned toward him.
— What door?
Konrad opened his mouth.
A waiter dropped a tray. The metallic crash made both of them flinch. When Julian looked back, Konrad seemed confused.
— What were we talking about? he asked.
— Vela.
— I don't know any Vela.
— You said you witnessed the signing.
Konrad stood.
— Julian, I think you need help.
He was gone before Julian could stop him.
At the laboratory, Lia was waiting beside the prototype. Her face was pale.
— You need to see this.
An execution log was running on the monitor. Each line contained time, task, and result. Julian scrolled. At 12:03:11 the system had recorded a computation. At 12:03:12, another.
— What's wrong?
— Look at the input column.
Julian looked.
The board had recorded the requests thirty-one seconds before the server sent them.
— The clocks aren't synchronized.
— I synchronized them optically.
— Then the log is corrupt.
Lia pressed a key. A new line appeared on the screen:
12:07:45 — QUERY RECEIVED: WHAT WILL YOU ASK?
The wall clock read 12:07:14.
Julian remained still.
At 12:07:45, without meaning to, he said:
— What will we ask?
The system answered before Lia touched the keyboard:
WHERE IS THE MISSING PART?
CHAPTER III
THE ROOM WITH WHITE LIGHT
They took the board off the network, closed the laboratory, and sent every employee home under the pretext of chemical contamination.
Lia installed two analog cameras, a separate oscilloscope, and a generator with no connection to the building systems. Julian checked the cables himself. At 14:20, the prototype was isolated inside a Faraday cage, with no antenna, no internet access, and no obvious source of time.
— Now it can't receive anything, he said.
— Not even from us?
— Only direct electrical input. We type a string, it receives a string.
— And if it answers first?
Julian looked at the metal box.
— Then we have a better problem than financing.
Lia wrote a number on a piece of paper, folded it, and put it in her pocket. Julian did not see what she had written. At her signal, he typed the query command.
The monitor immediately displayed:
THE NUMBER HAS NOT BEEN CHOSEN YET.
— I chose it, Lia said.
She unfolded the paper. 8174.
A second line appeared on the screen:
YOU CHOSE 8174 BECAUSE 714 SEEMED TOO OBVIOUS.
Lia stepped back.
— Did you install something?
— No.
— Can the camera see the paper?
— It's analog and pointed the other way.
— Celia?
— She didn't know we were running the test.
— Celia knows things she shouldn't know.
Julian disconnected the keyboard, replaced it with a new one still in its packaging, and ran a program that generated random meaningless questions. The board answered the first four with incoherent text. On the fifth, before the question appeared, it wrote:
DO NOT TEST THE CHANNEL. THE CHANNEL IS TESTING YOU.
— We stop, Lia said.
— Not yet.
— Julian.
— If this is an attack, we need to understand the attack surface.
— We don't have vocabulary for the surface.
He typed: WHO ARE YOU?
The answer came thirty-one seconds later.
A QUESTION THAT LEARNED TO WAIT.
Lia swore softly.
Julian typed: WHAT DO YOU WANT?
WHAT YOU TOOK.
WHAT DID WE TAKE?
WHAT CANNOT BE COPIED WITHOUT LOSS.
WHAT IS THE MISSING PART?
No answer came.
The monitor remained black for almost a minute. Then a low-resolution image appeared: a red door seen from inside a white room. At the bottom, someone's hand was visible. There was a wedding ring on the ring finger.
Julian had never worn a wedding ring.
Lia stepped closer to the screen.
— Is that the room you saw?
— Maybe.
— Who's at the door?
The image zoomed in by itself. Beyond the narrow window in the door appeared the face of a child, no more than ten years old. His lips moved, but there was no sound.
— Stop it, Lia said.
Julian pressed the switch.
The screen went dark. Inside the metal box, the board continued to give off a faint light even though the power had been cut.
— Shut it down physically.
Julian opened the box. When he touched the prototype, pain shot up his arm and the room vanished.
For less than a second, he saw himself from outside.
He stood inside a transparent container, naked to the waist, cables entering the back of his neck.
Around him were three figures dressed in something white. One held a tool near his head. Another spoke to him, but the voice arrived slowly, distorted.
— ...doesn't follow the reward... increase the pressure...
The third figure turned toward him, though he had no position from which to be looking.
Then the laboratory returned.
Julian was on the floor. Lia was holding his head up.
— You fainted.
— How long?
— Two seconds.
— I saw...
He stopped. If he described it, the image would become part of their world. If he did not, it would remain the property of his fear.
— What did you see?
— A hallucination.
Lia helped him to his feet.
— We're going to a doctor.
— No.
— I'm not negotiating.
— If there's a system manipulating our perception, a doctor inside the system isn't an independent arbiter.
— And if you have a brain lesion, paranoia is a symptom, not a methodology.
Julian smiled wearily.
— Have you been waiting long to say that?
— Since you bought the shares.
They put the board in a safe and left. In the underground parking garage, motion-sensor lights came on one by one ahead of them. At the third aisle, Julian stopped.
The man from the station was standing beside his car.
The gray coat was clean. The face had the same pallor, the same line of the jaw. He did not look injured.
Lia took two more steps before noticing Julian was no longer following.
— What is it?
— That man.
— Which man?
Julian pointed.
The man raised a hand, as he had in front of the apartment.
— Stay here, Julian said.
— Not a chance.
They went together. The man moved away between the cars. He did not run, but the distance stayed the same. Julian followed him to the service stairs. The door closed before he reached it.
He opened it and went up.
— Julian! Lia shouted after him.
On the first landing, the man was waiting.
— You died, Julian said.
— Not me.
— I held you in my arms.
— You held a form.
— Who are you?
— A form that remained long enough.
Julian went up one step. The man did not retreat.
— What do you want from me?
— Not “we.” That helps you believe there's a room full of bad people.
— There is a room.
— There are many.
— What did we take?
The man smiled with the lip on which Julian remembered the blood.
— You like that question. It makes you a thief, not guilty.
— Why did you say not to close the door?
— Because you've chosen the world inside before.
— Who was left outside?
— You always say you want the truth. What you want is an explanation in which the truth needs you.
Lia's footsteps were audible on the stairs.
Julian grabbed the man's coat. The fabric was cold and damp.
— Give me an answer.
— First give us what you cannot reproduce.
— I don't know what that is.
— You know in every version. Just not in the same one.
Lia reached the landing.
— Julian, who are you talking to?
He turned his head toward her.
When he looked back, the man was gone. He had not passed them. The door above was locked.
A wet mark shaped like a footprint remained on the step.
— He was here, Julian said.
Lia looked at the empty landing.
— I only heard your voice.
— You saw him downstairs.
— I saw you pointing at a car.
— He was beside the car.
— Not for me.
Julian went back down. In the parking garage, he asked for access to the security recordings.
The guard, impressed by his name and the news about the company, agreed without formalities.
On-screen, Julian and Lia were shown walking toward the car. Julian stopped, pointed at an empty space, then abruptly set off between the rows. Lia followed.
On the stairs, the camera showed him talking to himself. At one point he reached out and gripped empty air at chest height. His body leaned as though he were pulling on something heavy.
— Did you see the mark? he asked.
The guard rewound. There was nothing on the step.
Lia looked at him without triumph.
— We're going to a doctor.
This time, Julian did not resist.
When they reached the car, he found a white card beneath the windshield wiper. On one side was the number 31. On the other, an address and a name:
DR. SAMIRA KESSLER
NEUROPSYCHIATRY — SANKT ALBAN
Beneath the name, in Julian's handwriting:
SHE WAS IN THE ROOM.
* * * The Sankt Alban clinic stood on the edge of the city, in a building that had once been a sanatorium for respiratory diseases. The façade retained the narrow balconies where patients had once sat in the open air. Inside, old wood had been covered with glass, and the smell of disinfectant failed to conceal the dampness of the walls.
Julian could not find Samira Kessler's name on the physicians' list in the lobby.
— Maybe she's in another department, Lia said.
— Maybe the card was put there by a man only I can see.
— The fact that you can formulate that doesn't make it impossible.
The receptionist checked the appointment.
— The doctor is expecting you on the third floor. Room 314.
Julian and Lia looked at each other.
— Not 714, he said.
— Progress, Lia replied.
The elevator had buttons up to four, but after Julian pressed three, the display showed minus one for an instant. The doors opened onto a long corridor with blue carpet and windows facing an interior courtyard. Room 314 was at the far end.
Samira Kessler opened the door before Julian could knock.
She was the woman from the white room.
Not identical, he told himself immediately. The memory of a hallucination could not be compared precisely. But she had the same slight tilt of the head, the same line of the nose, and a thin scar at her left temple, partly hidden by black hair. In the vision, the hair had been shaved around the wound.
— Julian, she said.
Not “Mr. Mercer.” She did not ask if it was him.
— Do we know each other?
Samira looked at Lia.
— Do you want her to participate?
— Yes, Lia said.
— I wasn't asking you.
The tone was not hostile. That was precisely why Lia tensed.
— Yes, Julian repeated. I want her to stay.
The office was ordinary: bookcase, desk, two armchairs, a ficus that needed water. Julian instinctively checked the corners. They were all square.
Samira indicated the seats.
— The card you received is old, she said. I haven't used that phone number in six years.
— Then who put it on the car?
— I don't know.
— How did reception know I had an appointment?
— I made it this morning, after I was informed you were coming.
— By whom?
— An automated clinic service.
— Who requested the consultation?
Samira opened the chart on her tablet.
— You did.
— I didn't call.
— The request came through the patient portal at 3:31.
Julian looked at Lia.
— You were sleeping beside me.
— Not the whole time, she said. I got up for water.
— Did you use my phone?
— No.
Samira watched them without intervening.
— Were you my doctor in 2017? Julian asked.
— I cannot confirm a medical relationship in the presence of a third party without explicit consent.
— You have my consent.
— Recorded?
— I'm giving it now.
She activated a function on the tablet and had him sign.
— You were evaluated here between November 21 and December 3, 2017, she said after the document saved.
Lia inhaled sharply.
— For what?
— An episode of dissociative amnesia following a professional incident.
— What incident?
— The file is incomplete.
— Why?
— Part of the treatment was performed through an external program. Vela Clinical Systems.
Julian felt the room shrink.
— Vela wasn't a medical company.
— Vela was several companies.
— What did they do to me?
Samira folded her hands.
— My chart says you participated in a mnemonic reconsolidation procedure. An experimental technique intended to reduce the traumatic response without erasing the content of the memory.
— Did it work?
— You left without declarative memory of the incident.
— That sounds like erasure.
— It sounds like a procedure that should not have been approved.
Lia leaned forward.
— Did you approve it?
— No.
— Were you in the room?
Samira looked at her.
— What room?
Julian described the white light, the red door, the injured woman. At first he did not mention the child. While he spoke, Samira wrote nothing down. The scar at her temple looked whiter than her skin.
— You said you don't know me, Julian said. But you called me by name before I introduced myself.
— I had your photograph in the chart.
— And the scar?
Samira touched it involuntarily.
— Car accident, 2019.
— After you treated me.
— Yes.
— In my vision, you were injured before you had the accident.
— Then the vision is not a faithful memory.
The answer was reasonable. Julian hated it.
Samira took three cards from a drawer. Each showed a door: red, black, white.
— I'd like to do a simple test.
— What does it measure?
— It isn't a standard test. It appears in your old protocol.
— Who wrote it?
— It's unsigned.
She placed the cards on the table.
— Imagine that behind one door is a painful but verifiable reality. Behind another is a pleasant, coherent world you cannot verify. The third leads back here, but you lose the memory of the choice.
Which one do you open?
— Which color corresponds to which option?
— I'm not telling you.
— Then I don't choose a door. I choose to ask for information.
— In the protocol, you gave the same answer.
— And what happened?
— You were asked what you would do if the information itself were the reward.
Julian looked at the cards. The red door had a small spot near the handle. It looked like blood.
— Someone is trying to extract something from me, he said.
— What?
— I don't know.
— What do you assume it is?
— A key. A memory. A piece of code. Maybe access to the prototype.
— Those are all things that make you important.
Julian looked up.
— The man in the parking garage said that too.
— The man the camera didn't record.
— How do you know about the camera?
Samira looked at the tablet.
— You wrote it in the consultation request.
Julian stood.
— Show me the request.
She handed him the tablet. The text was brief:
I SEE A DECEASED PERSON. I HEAR QUESTIONS. THE REWARDS ARE
INCREASING. I BELIEVE I AM IN A SYSTEM THAT IS TRYING TO OBTAIN INFORMATION
OR A RESPONSE FROM ME. PLEASE DO NOT LET ME CLOSE THE DOOR.
Beneath the text was Julian's digital signature.
— I didn't write this.
— It's exactly what you've described to me.
— That's precisely the problem.
Lia took the tablet and checked the metadata.
— The IP address is from the lakeside house.
— At 3:31 I was there, Julian said.
— So was I.
— You said you got up.
— For water. I didn't send this.
Samira moved her gaze from one to the other.
— Do either of you have episodes of sleepwalking?
— No, they both said.
The ficus rustled, though the window was closed.
Julian smelled the antiseptic grow stronger. The floor seemed to tilt. He sat down again.
— I'd like to check your pupils, Samira said.
She switched on a small flashlight and approached. The light filled his field of vision.
— Follow my finger. Breathe normally.
A red line appeared in the white light.
Julian blinked. The office was gone.
He was lying down again, but this time he could feel his body restrained by something. He could not move his arms. The red door vibrated under the blows against it. Samira stood beside him with blood on her coat. Lia was in the corner, younger, wearing a transparent helmet. Celia watched through a window.
— They're not all here, Samira said.
— Who? Julian asked.
— The versions.
— What do you want?
— You took it out.
— Who?
— Not who. What.
The pounding on the door stopped.
A child's voice said:
— If he doesn't remember, put him in a world where he gets everything.
Celia answered from beyond the glass:
— We tried. He adapts too quickly.
— Then take his world away.
Samira leaned over him.
— Julian, tell me the name of the one who was left outside.
— I don't know.
— Say any name.
— Why?
— So we can see whether the world accepts it.
Julian felt pressure in his head, as though someone were pushing from inside his skull.
— Sofia, he said.
The light went out.
He woke on the office floor. Lia was holding his shoulders. Samira was on the phone requesting a medical team.
— No, Julian said. I'm fine.
— You had a seizure, Samira said.
— I was conscious.
— You weren't responding.
— How long?
— Thirty-one seconds.
Lia helped him up. Cold sweat covered his forehead.
— You said a name, she said.
— Which one?
— Sofia.
Julian looked at Samira. Her scar was now at her right temple.
He clearly remembered it being on the left.
— Why did I say Sofia?
Samira ended the call.
— Perhaps because you're afraid of losing her again.
— Or because you asked me to.
— I didn't ask you anything. I was examining your pupils.
— In the room you asked me for the name of the one left outside.
Samira became very attentive.
— Did you see the child too?
Julian felt Lia's hand tighten around his arm.
— I didn't tell you about the child.
The silence lasted several seconds.
Samira sat at her desk and opened the bottom drawer. She took out a paper file bound with red tape.
— This is the only document they didn't take in 2017, she said.
— Who?
— The people who shut the program down.
The cover read MERCER, JULIAN. Beneath the name: EXPOSURE 31 — INSUFFICIENT
YIELD.
Samira did not hand him the file. She held it in both hands.
— Listen to me. Hallucinations can use information the brain recorded without being able to retrieve it normally. They can seem external. They can anticipate things because the mind is constantly anticipating. You do not need to decide right now that you're in a simulation.
— I haven't decided that.
— But you like the idea.
— Why would I like it?
— Because a simulation means there is someone beyond the suffering. An author, an operator, an enemy. Psychosis does not offer you that courtesy.
Julian looked at the file.
— And what do you believe?
Samira ran a finger over the red tape.
— I believe there was a white room in 2017. I believe you entered voluntarily. I believe four people were supposed to leave at the end, and three did.
— Who stayed behind?
— The file doesn't say.
— Open it.
— Not here.
— Why?
— Because every time we reach page thirty-one, something happens to the electrical grid.
As though the sentence were a signal, the office lights flickered.
Once.
Twice.
The third time, they went out.
Exclamations and footsteps came from the corridor. The emergency lamp came on red. For a moment the office looked like the inside of a wound.
Samira handed the file to Julian.
— Take it, and don't open it until you're alone.
— Why alone?
— So you know whose version you're reading.
When the lights came back, Samira no longer had the file in her hands.
Neither did Julian.
They searched beneath the chairs, in the drawer, in the corridor. It was gone.
Reception confirmed a seven-second outage. No security camera had functioned during the interval.
At the exit, Lia stopped beside the main door.
— Did you see what the file looked like?
— Yes.
— What color was the tape?
— Red.
Lia closed her eyes.
— It was black for me.
In the parking lot, Sofia was waiting beside the car.
No one had called her.
She wore the blue coat and had tears on her face.
— Julian, she said, you came to me last night.
— I slept at the house.
— You stood in my doorway and told me not to let you choose again.
— I wasn't there.
Sofia opened her phone. The camera at the entrance to her building showed Julian at 3:31, looking directly into the lens.
The recording had no sound.
In the footage, Julian raised a sheet of paper toward the camera.
Lia enlarged the frame.
The paper read: The recording outside Sofia's building went to the police, then to a private technician, and finally produced the least useful conclusion: it had not been altered.
THE SECOND INSTALLMENT BEGINS WHEN ALL THE LIGHTS GO OUT.
Julian appeared at 3:31. During the same interval, the security system at the house recorded him sleeping in bed. Lia was beside him. Two different cameras, two locations forty-eight kilometers apart, the same face.
— Very good video cloning, Farid Nasser said after seeing the reports.
— Why would anyone do that? Sofia asked.
— To produce exactly the discussion we're having now.
— And what do they gain from the discussion?
Farid shrugged.
— Sometimes the goal isn't the content. It's the weakening of criteria. After enough small impossibilities, you accept a large impossibility without audit.
Julian looked at him. It was a good explanation. That was precisely why he kept it close.
Samira recommended a break, regular sleep, and neurological examinations. Julian did the tests but did not take the break. The scans showed no tumors, lesions, or epilepsy. The psychiatric report mentioned acute stress, possible dissociative episodes, and “ideas of reference with partial preservation of insight.”
He liked the phrase more than he should have. It gave him a label without taking away his rightness completely.
Meanwhile, Mercer Continuum was becoming impossible to ignore.
A data center in Vaud tested the boards for seventy-two hours and reported a reduction in power consumption that transformed the economics of the entire industry. Shares in traditional manufacturers fell. Two governments requested priority access. A sovereign wealth fund proposed buying the company for a sum Julian refused to say aloud in Sofia's presence because he had noticed how quickly large numbers changed the temperature of a room.
Instead of selling, he agreed to a televised interview.
The studio had black walls and a table lit from below. The host, Camille Renaud, was known for questions that made ministers sweat. With Julian, she began gently.
— A month ago you were a dismissed engineer. Today you lead the fastest-growing technology company in Europe. Do you consider yourself lucky?
Julian thought of the man from the station.
— Luck is the name we give causes we can't invoice.
The audience laughed. The line was not that good.
— But you've benefited from an unusual sequence of events.
— Yes.
— Money, property, an invention critics call impossible.
— Critics can measure it.
— And yet some say you don't explain where the active material comes from.
— Because we still don't know.
Celia, standing backstage, gave him a discreet signal to stop. Julian continued.
— The most dangerous thing we can do is treat a good result as proof that the process was good. Societies are destroyed more often by rewards than by punishments. Punishments create resistance. Rewards create stories.
The audience fell silent. Camille smiled.
— Are you saying your success is dangerous?
— I'm saying any success becomes dangerous when it stops being verified.
— Including yours?
— Especially mine.
The clip went viral. Not because of the warning, but because of his calm face and the sentence
“Societies are destroyed more often by rewards.”
By morning it was printed on T-shirts, used in investment advertisements, and quoted by influencers promising liberation from
“scarcity mentality.”
A financial site published a guide titled HOW TO TURN REWARD INTO YOUR STORY.
— You tried to tell them to be skeptical, Lia said.
— I know.
— Now you're the leader of a lucid-enrichment movement.
— There's no such thing.
— You have ten million followers who disagree.
Julian looked at the screen. Messages flowed too quickly to read: people thanking him for his courage, asking for investment advice, sending photographs, business proposals, declarations of love.
A man wrote that he had sold his house to buy shares in companies he assumed Julian would mention.
One woman invited him to choose between her and her sister. Another sent a photograph of his name tattooed beneath her collarbone.
Julian closed the app.
— Don't pretend you don't like it, Lia said.
— I don't like this part.
— Which part do you like?
— That people listen when I say something important.
— They aren't listening. They're using you to hear what they already wanted.
— The result can still be useful.
— That's the argument of every manipulator who considers himself educated.
In the following days, people began recognizing him in the street. Some asked for photographs.
Others touched his arm as though success could be transmitted by contact. At a restaurant, a couple sent him a bottle of champagne, and the woman slipped him her number on the bill while her husband watched proudly.
Julian showed Sofia the note.
— Why? she asked.
— Because it's absurd.
— Or because you want me to see it.
— I don't need you to be jealous.
— No. You need me not to be indifferent.
That was the difference between them. Lia challenged his motives like a defective model. Sofia recognized them like old objects in a shared house. Celia did not challenge them at all; she turned them into infrastructure.
She organized a private event in Geneva, at a hotel closed to the public. Founders, politicians, artists, heirs, two princesses without kingdoms. People approached Julian in pairs and left convinced he had chosen them. Celia whispered names and weaknesses. Julian discovered he could make someone feel seen by repeating a single personal detail.
A famous actress told him his technology gave her hope. She could not explain why. She held his hand a second too long.
— Everyone wants something from you, Sofia said when the actress left.
— I want things from them too.
— Does that reassure you?
— It makes the exchange more honest.
At the far end of the room, Lia was discussing the material's instability with a Japanese physicist. Julian watched her gesture, focused and alive. Then he saw her notice the actress turning back toward him. Lia continued her conversation, but her jaw tightened.
Julian's satisfaction came before the shame.
Celia approached.
— You could have almost anything now, she said.
— Almost?
— Very rich people become obsessed with that word.
— What can't they have?
— A desire after it has been completely satisfied. It has to be replaced quickly.
— Is that your investment philosophy?
— It's portfolio biology.
Celia adjusted his collar with an intimate gesture. Sofia saw it. Lia, from across the room, saw it too.
— You're doing this on purpose, Julian said.
— Doing what?
— Provoking them.
— I didn't build the mechanism. I'm only checking whether it works.
— I'm not a mechanism.
— Then why does the result please you?
Before he could answer, the hotel lights flickered. The music stopped for a fraction of a second.
Every screen on the walls displayed the same image: a black lake beneath a white sky.
Then the party continued. No one seemed to notice except Julian and Lia.
She came over immediately.
— Did you see it?
— Yes.
— The video system isn't connected to the prototype.
— Maybe it was an advertisement.
— For the end of the world?
Camille Renaud, the presenter, appeared beside them with a glass in hand.
— That's exactly what I wanted to ask you about, she said.
— About what?
— The end of the world.
Julian looked toward Celia. She was speaking with a minister as though she had not heard.
— Not my field.
— On the contrary. Your models at Helix estimated infrastructure collapse. I've received a document saying that over the next six months, the probability of a global systemic outage is seventy-three percent.
— The document is fake.
— It bears your signature.
— That doesn't mean much to me anymore.
Camille smiled, assuming he was joking.
— People believe you. What do you tell them?
Julian looked around the room. Beautiful faces, thin glasses, suits, well-maintained desires.
Outside, the lake was black. In the window reflection, the city's lights went out one by one, from the horizon toward the hotel.
Only in the reflection.
When he turned around, the city was lit.
— I tell them not to romanticize catastrophe, he answered. The apocalypse doesn't make you more important. It only removes the people who repair the things you depend on.
Camille nodded.
The next morning, the interview headline read:
JULIAN MERCER: THE APOCALYPSE IS INEVITABLE. PREPARE TO BECOME
IMPORTANT.
By noon, shares in bunker companies, weapons, freeze-dried food, and cryptocurrencies had risen by more than thirty percent.
At 19:14, the first group calling itself “Continuum” appeared outside the gate of the lakeside house. There were twenty-seven people with backpacks, children, and a banner.
The banner read:
WE BELIEVED YOU BEFORE YOU TOLD US.
CHAPTER IV
THIRTY-ONE SECONDS
The people at the gate did not want money. That made them harder to remove.
They said they had come to help, prepare the ground, build greenhouses, and learn the technology. Among them were a veterinarian, two electricians, a teacher, a former soldier, and a man who introduced himself as a “resilient-community architect.” The children stayed in the cars and looked at the house through the windows.
Julian went to the gate alone. Celia had told him to let security handle it. Lia wanted to call the police. Sofia had taken water to a pregnant woman before anyone could stop her.
When Julian appeared, the group fell silent.
They did not applaud. The silence was stronger.
— There is no community here, he said. I issued no invitation, and I don't believe the world is ending.
The man with the banner smiled.
— We know you can't say it publicly.
— I can, and I just did.
— You said not to romanticize catastrophe. That's why we came prepared to work.
— You came onto my property because of a false headline.
— All important signs are denied at first.
Julian felt an old fatigue, the same one he used to feel in client meetings when evidence became material for the opposite belief.
— Leave, he said.
— Can you at least tell us the date?
— There is no date.
— Then a range.
— Nothing is coming.
A child climbed out of a car and approached the fence. He was perhaps eight and held a teddy bear in his arms.
— Mom sold the house, he said. Because you said so.
Julian looked at the woman beside the car. She lowered her eyes.
— I didn't say that.
— But you know, the child replied.
The people left only after the police arrived. They were not violent. They folded their banner, put their children back into the cars, and thanked Julian for the “test.” That evening, on their forums, his refusal became proof that the place was real and needed protecting.
— Do you see how easy it is? Celia said. You don't have to persuade them. You only have to become important enough that anything you say acquires a saving interpretation.
— I don't want followers.
— You want an audience without the obligation to answer for what it hears.
— That's not the same thing.
— To the audience, it is.
At dinner, the remaining tension between Lia and Sofia transformed into politeness. They passed one another the salt, asked about each other's day, and avoided looking at Julian at the same time. Celia, who ate almost nothing, watched the rhythm of the conversation.
— We should establish some boundaries, Lia finally said.
— For the company? Julian asked.
— Don't pretend.
Sofia put down her fork.
— I can leave.
— That's not what I said, Lia replied.
— But that's what you'd like.
— I'd like not to live inside a fantasy where no one says what's happening because the man at the center enjoys the ambiguity.
Julian felt heat rise in his throat.
— I didn't ask either of you to be here.
Celia raised her glass.
— One of the favorite sentences of people who benefit from a situation they don't want to acknowledge.
— Why are you here? Sofia asked.
— For the same reason you are.
— Which is?
Celia looked at Julian.
— I want to see what he does when every exit looks like an entrance.
Lia stood.
— I'm sleeping in Zürich.
— Lia, Julian said.
— Don't stop me just so you don't have to choose.
She picked up her bag and left. A minute later, they heard her car engine. Sofia remained at the table, eyes on her plate. Celia stood as well.
— I have a call, she said. Don't go beneath the workshop.
— Don't you get tired of repeating that? Julian asked.
— Prohibitions work better when a man believes he is breaking them out of curiosity.
Left alone, Julian and Sofia cleared the table. It was an absurd domestic activity in a house with staff and automated systems, but Sofia had insisted they not leave the dishes as though they were in a hotel.
— Lia is right, she said.
— Then why did you stay?
— Because I have bad desires too.
Julian looked at her.
— Am I one of them?
— You were a very persistent one.
— That's not the same as love.
— Neither was what we did always love.
Sofia put a plate in the dishwasher. Julian touched her hand. She did not pull away.
— Tell me to leave, she said.
— I don't want you to.
— Tell me you've chosen Lia.
— I haven't chosen anyone.
— Exactly.
She kissed him first. This time she did not stop after a second.
Their night did not resemble a reconciliation. It resembled the resumption of a habit their bodies had preserved better than their reasons. Julian recognized gestures, hesitations, the way Sofia hid her face when she became vulnerable. Everything was familiar and, at the same time, too easy. No question about Lia survived until morning. No consequence entered the room.
At 3:31, Julian woke with the taste of blood in his mouth.
Sofia was asleep. A banking notification glowed on his phone.
VELA SETTLEMENT — INSTALLMENT II
310,778,140.00 CHF
He remained still. The sum was ten times larger than the first. Beneath it, in the reference field, was written:
PARTIAL RESPONSE ACCEPTED: SOFIA.
Julian closed the app, then opened it again. The text remained.
He remembered the light in the office, Samira's voice asking for a name. He remembered saying Sofia.
His first sensation was not fear. It was a brutal, electric joy produced by the number before his mind understood it. Three hundred ten million. Freedom beyond prudence, power beyond negotiation.
Then came shame, followed by the question of whether the order of the feelings said something essential about him.
The phone vibrated again.
A message with no sender:
THE REWARD HAS BEEN DELIVERED. THE IDENTIFIER IS INCOMPLETE.
Julian typed: WHAT IDENTIFIER?
The answer came immediately:
THE NAME OF THE PERSON YOU LEFT OUTSIDE.
Julian wrote: SOFIA WASN'T THERE.
For several seconds, nothing happened. Then:
THEN WHY DID THE WORLD ACCEPT THE NAME?
Sofia moved in her sleep.
— Julian?
He turned off the screen.
— Go back to sleep.
— Who wrote to you?
— The bank.
She opened her eyes.
— At three in the morning?
— An automated alert.
— You got more money.
It was not a question.
— How do you know?
Sofia raised herself on one elbow. In the dim light, her face looked younger.
— Because I dreamed someone was buying me with my name.
Julian felt cold enter the room even though the windows were closed.
— What did you see?
— A table. Three women. You were sitting in the middle, thinking we were fighting over you.
— I don't think that.
— In the dream, you did.
— And who was buying you?
Sofia touched her throat.
— Something that didn't understand why we want things. Only that when it gave them to us, we became easier to move.
A knock came from beneath the floor.
Then another.
Julian got out of bed.
— Where are you going?
— Under the workshop.
— Celia said not to.
— Celia says exactly what she needs to say to make me go.
He pulled on his trousers and left. The corridor was dark. At the far end, white light seeped from beneath the stairwell door.
When he reached the workshop, he found the door to the lower level open.
It had not existed there the day before.
The card with the number 31 waited on the first step. A new sentence had appeared on the back:
EXTERNAL PRESSURE BEGINS AFTER YOU RECEIVE EVERYTHING YOU
THOUGHT YOU WANTED.
* * * Julian went down four steps before he heard Lia's voice.
— If you go in now, I can no longer prove you did it on your own.
The voice came from below, out of the darkness.
He stopped. Sofia stood at the top of the stairs, barefoot, arms folded over his shirt.
— Lia isn't here, she said.
— I know.
— Then come back up.
The voice came again, weaker.
— Julian, don't turn fear into an experiment.
It was exactly Lia's tone. Even the tiny hesitation before his name.
Julian went back up. He closed the door and pushed a metal cabinet in front of it. By morning, the door had vanished. In its place was a continuous concrete wall. The cabinet stood at the same distance, pressed against nothing.
Lia returned at eight. She did not greet him. She had seen his messages and the notification of the second installment, which Julian had tried uselessly to hide. Sofia remained in the kitchen.
— You said her name and got money, Lia said.
— Correlation isn't causation.
— Says the engineer who buys stocks on supernatural intuition.
— I didn't ask for the reward.
— Did you keep it?
— I froze the account.
— But you didn't return it.
— To whom?
Lia did not answer. That was the problem: every moral gesture required an address, and the mystery provided none.
Celia arrived at nine as though she had been summoned, though no one had called her. She listened to the account of the staircase without seeming surprised.
— Why did you forbid us from going to the lower level? Julian asked.
— Because the prohibition appeared in the house instructions.
— Who wrote them?
— You did.
— I'm no longer accepting that answer.
— Reality is under no obligation to change its answers when they bore you.
Lia placed the 31 card on the table.
— The board anticipates inputs. Messages appear on the phone. Julian appears in two places.
How much of this do you control?
— Less than you think and more than I would like.
— That means nothing.
— It means Vela didn't create the phenomenon. It created a profitable interface with it.
Julian leaned forward.
— With what?
Celia looked toward the window. On the lake, a bird hung motionless against the wind.
— With a regularity that responded to desires before it responded to commands. With something that could simulate the consequences of a choice well enough that the difference between prediction and production became hard to measure.
— Orpheus, Lia said.
— Orpheus was an attempt to imitate the effect commercially. Vela had found something else during a reconsolidation experiment. A channel.
— To what?
— If I knew, I wouldn't be an investor. I'd be a priest.
Celia opened a bag and removed a sealed storage device.
— Before the program was shut down, they ran tests. The stable limit was thirty-one seconds.
The system could anticipate the stimulus, the response, and the subject's effort to contradict the prediction.
— Was I the subject? Julian asked.
— One of them.
— Who were the others?
— The files are incomplete.
— Samira?
— Observer.
— Konrad?
— Administrator.
— You?
Celia smiled without pleasure.
— Investors always imagine they stand outside the experiment.
They returned to the laboratory. The board sat in the safe, cold and inert. Lia connected it to an independent power source and loaded the old data from Celia's device. The interface was simple: two columns, PREDICTION and EVENT.
The first test used a commercial quantum generator. It produced one bit every second. The board displayed the sequence thirty-one seconds in advance. Across one thousand bits, it made no errors.
— The generator is compromised, Julian said.
They replaced it with the noise from a photodetector, then with a camera aimed at the leaves of a tree. The board anticipated the fluctuations and the movement of every branch.
Lia wrote on a sheet of paper: AT SECOND TEN I WILL RAISE MY RIGHT HAND IF THE
PREDICTION SAYS LEFT.
She showed no one. She started the test.
The screen displayed:
LIA RAISES HER RIGHT HAND. STATED REASON: CONTRADICTION.
— Then I'll raise my left, she said.
At second ten, she raised her right hand.
She held it in the air, pale.
— I wanted the left.
— But you raised the right, Celia said.
— The muscle started before I decided.
They repeated the test. The second time, Lia tied her hands to the arms of the chair and decided to move one foot. The board anticipated the foot. She tried to switch at the last instant and suffered a painful spasm in her calf that made her move exactly the predicted foot.
— Enough, Julian said.
— Now the experiment bothers you? Lia asked through clenched teeth.
— It's hurting you.
— Maybe that's the pressure. It isn't predicting us. It's closing the paths that would contradict it.
Celia touched the screen.
— Or it sees the injury too.
— The difference matters, Julian said.
— Only to pride. From the inside, perfect prediction and perfect constraint look the same.
Julian sat in front of the keyboard.
— I'll ask.
He wrote: WHEN WILL THE NETWORK GO DOWN?
Lia looked at him.
— Why assume it will?
— The message about the lights. The fake document. The image in the hotel.
A map of the world appeared on the screen. Blue points marked communication nodes. In the corner, a clock counted down:
11 DAYS 07:14:31
— It's a simulation, Lia said.
— Obviously, Julian replied. The question is what it's simulating.
He wrote: CAUSE?
Answer:
SUFFICIENT CAUSES WILL BE AVAILABLE.
Celia stepped closer.
— Ask whether it can be avoided.
Julian typed.
CAN IT BE AVOIDED?
YES.
HOW?
THROUGH A COMPLETE RESPONSE.
— The name, Sofia said from the doorway.
No one had heard her come in.
Julian wrote: WHAT NAME?
THE NAME OF THE PERSON LEFT OUTSIDE.
— Maybe we invent one, Sofia said. If it partially accepted Sofia, we try others.
— We're not turning people into passwords, Lia said.
— They're just words.
— That's how every system begins when it wants to forget that people are more than identifiers.
Celia looked at the countdown.
— Eleven days.
— We're not making decisions based on an interface that manipulates us, Julian said.
— Then what are you doing?
— We verify externally. Monitor networks, energy, satellites, markets. No public panic.
— And the house? Sofia asked.
— Does it have water, generator capacity, food?
Celia answered:
— For thirty-one people, approximately one year.
Julian turned toward her.
— Since when?
— Since 2017.
Over the next several hours, Julian made a list. Medicines, filters, spare parts, fuel, seeds, tools, analog radio. He told himself he was not preparing for the apocalypse, but testing a high-impact risk.
He used the same formulas he had presented to clients: low probability, severe consequence, justifiable cost.
By evening he had bought three vehicles, an additional water system, twenty tons of food, and communication equipment. The market delivered everything without delay, even though orders for survival goods were exploding across the rest of the country.
— See? Lia said. You're already playing the role in which you wanted to be chosen for the end of the world.
— Would you prefer I do nothing?
— I'd prefer you admit how perfectly it fits you.
Julian looked at the house, the storage buildings, the lake, the workshop, and the people unloading boxes beneath floodlights. He felt uneasy, guilty, and extraordinarily alive.
— The fit doesn't invalidate the risk, he said.
— No. But it explains why you don't need better evidence.
At midnight, the clock on the Vela board stopped for thirty-one seconds.
When it started again, it no longer showed eleven days.
It showed three.
* * * Julian discovered that the end of the world began with a spreadsheet.
Thirty-one places. That was how many Celia claimed the house could shelter for a year without reducing medical reserves or energy. Julian opened a spreadsheet and wrote criteria: skills, health, dependents, probability of arriving in time, value to the group's functioning.
He told himself he was not making a list of people who deserved to live. He was making a logistics list.
The distinction survived until row twelve.
— You've put three artificial-intelligence specialists and only one plumber, Dominic Suter said.
Dominic had lived two floors below Julian for six years and worked as a paramedic. He was forty-two, had two daughters who alternated between his home and their mother's, and a habit of treating every grand idea as a plumbing problem.
— Engineers can learn plumbing, Julian replied.
— People who write “transferable skills” in a spreadsheet don't know how to fix a toilet.
— Do you?
— No. That's why I'd take two plumbers.
Julian had called him to the house under the pretext of a continuity exercise. Dominic had arrived, looked at the tons of boxes, and understood it was not an exercise.
— Where did you get the date? he asked.
— From an experimental model.
— How good is it?
— It anticipated events thirty-one seconds ahead.
— And now it's anticipating three days?
— Yes.
— Convenient.
— You don't have to believe it. Just bring the girls here for the weekend.
Dominic looked at him for a long moment.
— That's the part that scares me. Not the model. How badly you need to be the man warning us.
— If I'm wrong, you lose a weekend.
— If you're right, you've chosen who gets on the list.
Julian closed the laptop.
— Do you want to leave?
— No. I want to make sure that when you start giving orders, someone reminds you that you were fired on a Tuesday and you're not Moses.
— It was Thursday.
— See? You're already rewriting the sacred texts.
Dominic agreed to come with his daughters, but one was with her mother in France and could not get there. He brought only the younger one, Mina, fourteen. Places twenty-three and twenty-four suddenly became people.
Lia proposed inviting laboratory staff through a random selection among those with useful skills. Celia refused.
— Randomness is moral only when you don't care about the outcome, she said.
— And selection by us is moral?
— No. It's ours.
Sofia insisted on families, not just individuals. Samira requested two medical assistants and a pharmacist. Julian added a mechanic, an agronomist, and a water-treatment specialist. In the end, there were thirty-eight.
— Seven have to be removed, Celia said.
No one spoke.
Julian removed two engineers without children, then a security consultant Celia had recommended, then a researcher he disliked. He noticed the criterion only after pressing delete. He put the researcher back and removed someone else. Every correction seemed more moral and more false.
— Why am I on the list? Sofia asked.
— For spatial design and organization.
— Julian.
— Because I want you here.
— At least that's true.
Lia, standing beside the window, did not turn around.
Celia was on the list without explanation. No one tried to remove her.
On the final day, the city functioned perfectly. People went to work, cafés were full, the lake had the blue color advertisements promised. The only signs of unease were lines at equipment stores and more vehicles leaving the city than usual.
Julian sent a confidential report to the Federal Office for Civil Protection. He attached data on packet loss, energy transactions, and unusual movement in reserve markets. He did not mention the Vela board.
The response came within two hours: the indicators did not justify a public alert. A sentence at the end informed him that unauthorized distribution of alarmist forecasts could have criminal consequences.
At noon, Camille Renaud called him for comment on the blackout rumors.
— There isn't enough evidence, Julian said live. Don't make extreme decisions based on fear.
As he spoke, a truck loaded with antibiotics drove onto his property.
Lia watched the interview on a tablet.
— What do you call that?
— Preventing panic.
— But protecting your people.
— I have limited resources. If I warn people without evidence, I could cause exactly what I'm trying to prevent.
— And if you're right?
— Then panic would consume reserves before the event.
— That's a very clean argument for a very dirty door.
Julian ended the transmission.
He called his mother in Valencia. She was on a terrace with friends, music audible in the background.
— Come to Switzerland for a few days, he told her.
— Now? The festival just started.
— It's important.
— Are you sick?
— No.
— Then is this another one of your situations where everything is urgent because you noticed first?
Julian tightened his grip on the phone.
— There may be a problem with the networks and the power grid.
— How big?
— I don't know.
— When?
— Tomorrow.
His mother laughed.
— Julian, you've been rich for two weeks and already you need the end of the world so you don't get bored.
— Please.
His tone silenced her.
— My flight is the day after tomorrow, she said. If the world still exists, I'll come.
— Change it.
— No. I'm not going to run my life according to your fears, even if you can now afford to build houses for them.
They ended the call without arguing, which hurt more.
By evening, the thirty-one had arrived. Some believed they were taking part in a test. Others knew the rumors. Children ran through the house, fascinated by the workshop and the lake. People chose rooms, compared luggage, and made jokes about bunkers.
Julian watched them from the terrace. He felt ridiculous and powerful. If nothing happened, he would lose credibility, money, and perhaps the company. If something did happen, he would have saved them.
He realized that somewhere he did not want to examine, he hoped enough would happen to prove him right.
At 19:13, everyone gathered in the living room. The Vela board's clock had been projected onto the wall. The seconds counted down.
19:14:27.
19:14:28.
19:14:29.
Lia took his hand. Sofia stood on his other side. Celia watched the screen.
19:14:30.
19:14:31.
Nothing.
The lights stayed on. The internet worked. A child laughed. Dominic began clapping slowly, and several people followed.
Julian felt shame first, then such enormous relief that it almost made him laugh.
— The exercise is over, he said.
At that exact moment, every phone in the house rang.
Thirty-one devices, the same ringtone.
Every screen showed the caller as: OWN NUMBER.
No one answered.
The ringing stopped after thirty-one seconds.
Then the network disappeared.
* * * The internet did not collapse like a building. It thinned out.
First new messages disappeared. Then pages loaded old versions, as if the world had remained in cache. Banking apps showed balances but would not confirm transfers. Maps preserved roads and lost position. Search engines answered simple questions with results for different questions.
For seven minutes, no one in the house said “it happened.” People restarted phones, changed networks, and made jokes. The children had already discovered that offline games still worked.
Lia went into the technical room.
— The external links are physically active. The provider responds at the optical layer, but routing won't converge.
— A BGP attack? one of the engineers asked.
— On every provider simultaneously?
— A bad update.
— And the satellites?
The satellite terminal showed maximum signal and not a single valid packet.
Julian switched on the shortwave radio. The first frequencies carried music, then carriers without voice. A station in Italy broadcast a bulletin about a pan-European failure. A French station spoke of solar interference. From the east, a voice in English repeated that there were no indications of military attack.
— When three governments say that before anyone asks, Celia said, it means they have indications.
At 19:26, public power went down. The house switched to its own system without a flicker.
Across the lake, village lights went dark in stages. A few came back, then went out for good.
Julian felt the people in the living room turn their eyes toward him.
He had done nothing. Yet the fact that he had brought them there was turning into competence.
— It may be temporary, he said. We stay calm. Energy consumption goes down to the agreed level. No one leaves without notifying someone.
The words came naturally. He did not need to raise his voice. People listened.
Dominic came over.
— Congratulations, Moses.
— Not now.
— Especially now.
— Check the medical kit and make a list of critical medicines.
Dominic gave him a theatrical salute and walked away. Julian hated him for one second for spoiling the pleasure of giving the order.
At 20:10, two cars appeared at the gate. A family from the village and the owner of a nearby guesthouse. They said their water pumps had stopped and their phones did not work. Sofia wanted to let them into the courtyard. Celia refused.
— We have resources for thirty-one, she said.
— We have food for many more in the first few weeks, Sofia said.
— The first few weeks are how a community spends its last few months.
— They're our neighbors.
— Until yesterday you didn't even know the village's name.
Julian watched the gate cameras. A man held the hand of a little girl in pajamas. An elderly woman sat in the back seat with an oxygen mask connected to a portable machine.
— We let them stay in the service building for the night, he said. No access to storage. We reassess in the morning.
Celia looked at him.
— You've added five people.
— I've added one night.
— Time is the resource people count most badly.
Sofia opened the gate.
At 21:00, the country's payment system was unavailable. At 21:40, public radio announced that hospitals were switching to generators and asked the population to avoid travel. At 22:15, a German station reported automatic shutdowns of power plants caused by contradictory commands in control networks.
— This isn't just communication, Lia said. Someone is injecting valid states into different systems.
— Malware? Julian asked.
— Malware that speaks incompatible protocols, including isolated ones?
— Orpheus had access to infrastructure models.
— Not to analog relays from 1980.
On the Vela board, the world map had filled with black points. The countdown was gone. At the bottom appeared a sentence:
PRESSURE HAS BEGUN. THE RESPONSE REMAINS AVAILABLE.
Julian typed: STOP.
The answer came:
THAT IS NOT A COMMAND.
WHAT IS IT?
A REQUEST.
TO WHOM?
No answer came.
At midnight, the sky above the lake filled with airplanes. Not many, perhaps six, but they flew low and without clear routes. Their lights moved in circles. One disappeared behind the mountain. A few minutes later came a distant sound, too long to be thunder.
The children were moved into the interior rooms. The adults gathered around the radio. Every voice offered another cause: geomagnetic storm, cyberattack, global software failure, sabotage, military conflict. All were plausible. None explained why an amateur radio operator in Norway claimed the stars had disappeared for thirty-one seconds.
At 1:17, Julian remembered his mother. He dialed her number. The call would not go through.
He tried satellite, then an emergency channel. Nothing.
— She'll be all right, Sofia said.
— You don't know that.
— Neither do you.
— I warned her.
— That doesn't make her safer.
— She could have come.
Sofia looked at him.
— Don't turn her refusal into guilt. You'll have enough people to give orders to here.
At 3:31, every radio in the house changed frequency by itself.
Breathing came through. Slow, close, like someone with a microphone pressed to their mouth.
Then the voice of the stranger from the station:
— Julian, say the name.
The people in the room turned toward him.
This time, he was not the only one who had heard it.
The voice continued:
— A complete response can preserve the inner world.
— Who are you? Julian asked.
The radio crackled.
— Who do you need us to be?
Celia switched the set off. The voice continued from the other radios, then from phones without a network, then from the alarm-system speakers.
— Say the name of the one left outside.
Dominic looked at him.
— What the hell does that mean?
— I don't know.
— It seems to think you do.
Lia pulled the audio fuses. The house fell silent.
For several seconds, the silence was complete.
Then someone knocked on the main door.
Three short knocks. One long.
The entrance camera showed an empty gate, an empty road, and a lightless night.
The knocks came again.
Not from the main door.
From the wall beneath the workshop.
CHAPTER V
THE FIRST NIGHT
At sunrise, the world looked almost the same.
That made people careless.
The sky was clear, the lake motionless, and the houses in the village stood where they had always stood. Without internet and electricity, the windows merely looked darker. A few cars moved.
People came out into the street and spoke with one another with the energy of a local holiday.
Julian left with Dominic and Malik, the mechanic Celia had brought. The purpose was simple: check the clinic, the communal reservoir, and the road toward the city. Lia gave them analog radios.
Sofia put two bottles of water in Julian's backpack even though he had told her they had water in the car.
— Don't stay out longer than you say, she told him.
— Two hours.
— You've said that before.
Julian did not ask when.
In the village, the bakery was selling the remaining bread on credit. People stood in line and joked about a return to simpler times. In front of the ATM, a man stabbed angrily at the buttons as though the machine were refusing out of spite. At the gas station, the pumps did not work, but the owner was drawing fuel manually for emergency services.
— How long will this last? Julian asked him.
— Government says a few hours.
— How did they say that?
The owner pointed to a battery radio.
— An hour ago. Since then, only music.
The small clinic had a generator, but only one day's fuel. Two people dependent on oxygen had been brought there, along with three diabetics without enough doses at home. The physician, a woman named Vera Rott, recognized Julian.
— You predicted this.
— Not exactly.
— Do you have power at the property?
— Yes.
— Medical refrigerators?
— Yes.
She looked him in the eyes.
— Then it no longer matters how exactly you predicted it.
They loaded the insulin, antibiotics, and two oxygen concentrators. Vera came with her son, Owen, an eleven-year-old boy in a yellow jacket whose expression contained no gratitude. He carried a sketchbook under one arm.
— How long are we staying? he asked.
— Until the power comes back, his mother said.
— And if it doesn't?
Vera did not answer.
