At the edge of the village, the road toward the city was blocked by cars. Not because of traffic, but because a truck had stopped sideways across the road. The driver was trying to restart the engine.
The vehicle had electronic controls, and every system showed errors simultaneously.
Malik opened the panel and whistled.
— The units are fine. Each one thinks another requested the shutdown.
— Contradictory commands, Julian said.
— Like the power grid.
While they worked, a column of people came from the direction of the city. Some were walking, others pushing bicycles. They were not running. They had the faces of people who still assumed there was a normal destination at the end.
A man in a suit approached Julian's car.
— How much do you want for the vehicle?
— It's not for sale.
— Two hundred thousand.
— No.
— I have an account at Helvetia Private. I'll transfer it when the system comes back.
— It's not for sale.
The man removed his watch.
— This is worth eighty thousand.
Dominic looked at the watch.
— Does it know how to transport insulin?
— I'm not talking to you.
— Then talk to your feet. The road is clear in the other direction.
The man cursed at them and walked away. A few meters later, he made the same offer to a family with a van.
Julian looked at his car, bought on the day the market had offered him the exact model. Now it was valuable not for its price but because Celia had installed a separate manual control system. Any luxury that could not be disconnected from the network had become furniture.
They managed to move the truck after an hour. An ambulance had been trapped behind it.
Inside, a woman was hemorrhaging after childbirth. Dominic climbed in immediately. Julian remained beside the door, holding the IV bag while Vera worked. Blood ran down his sleeve.
For a moment, the station returned to his memory: the stranger's weight, the thread of blood, the words about the door.
— Julian, higher, Dominic said. Hold the bag higher.
The woman survived until they reached the clinic. The baby, wrapped in a blanket, did not cry.
The father thanked Julian three times, though Dominic and Vera had done almost everything.
Julian did not correct him.
When they returned, they found fifteen people at the property gate. Word of the power and the doctors had traveled without a network. Some had come from the village, others from the road. Celia was keeping them outside with two security agents.
— We have patients, Vera said. They have to come in.
— Patients come in, Celia replied. Companions stay outside.
— A woman dependent on oxygen is not staying without her daughter.
— Then we consume for two people to maintain one.
Vera stepped toward Celia.
— Medicine is not inventory optimization.
— In a month, it will be.
Julian stepped between them.
— Everyone comes in for evaluation. We set up the service building and the old greenhouse.
— We're already thirty-six, Celia said.
— Now we're more.
— The number doesn't become moral just because you said it.
— And the limit doesn't become natural just because it was in the instructions.
Celia looked at him for a long moment, then signaled for the gate to be opened.
By sunset, there were fifty-three people on the property.
The water reserve, calculated for a year, had fallen to seven months. Food to eight. Rare medicines would last less. Julian projected the figures on the wall and explained them to the group.
People listened in silence. When he finished, a man asked:
— Who put you in charge?
Before Julian could answer, the woman on oxygen said:
— He let us in.
For the moment, that was enough.
At 19:14, public radio returned. A tired voice announced a state of emergency, restrictions on travel, and controlled distribution of resources. No causes were mentioned. The broadcast stopped after thirty-one seconds.
Then a child's voice came from the speaker:
— You added too many.
Owen, standing beside his mother, immediately looked up.
— That wasn't me, he said.
The voice continued:
— Now you'll have to choose more often.
Julian pulled the radio cable.
The reserve projection on the wall behind him updated by itself.
WATER: 211 DAYS.
FOOD: 244 DAYS.
RESPONSE: STILL INCOMPLETE.
The first night did not resemble a night. It began nowhere and ended at no hour anyone could verify.
Spring-driven and battery clocks kept working, but people no longer trusted them. Two radios showed different minutes. The kitchen oven was stuck at 19:14. The energy-system display advanced normally until 23:59, then returned to 23:31 and replayed the same interval. Julian told everyone to ignore the displays and placed a mechanical clock on the table in the hall.
Every thirty minutes, someone turned a page in a notebook.
It was a method modest enough to work.
The service building became an infirmary. Vera arranged the folding beds at equal distances, not because the spacing had any medical effect but because order calmed the patients. Dominic improvised a distributor for the oxygen concentrators. Malik dismantled a lawn mower to use a pressure regulator. A retired teacher inventoried medicines with a pencil. Two teenage girls sterilized jars in soup pots.
Every person quickly discovered a competence the old world had never asked about.
A lawyer knew how to repair hinges. A woman who had managed three investment funds could light a fire with wet wood. The man who had asked who put Julian in charge had been a restaurant chef and turned the contents of the freezers into a meal for fifty-three people. Before serving, he demanded that portions be identical.
— Including for children? Celia asked.
— Children get more, he said.
— Then they aren't identical.
The chef looked at her like a customer who had just requested dessert without ingredients.
— Then they're fair.
Celia did not insist.
Julian moved from room to room with a flashlight in hand, asked every few steps something he did not know: how much fuel could be used, who slept where, whether the gate stayed lit, whether families should be separated from single people, whether weapons had to be surrendered, whether the wine cellar entered the ration, whether the upstairs toilets could be used.
He answered quickly. Speed created the impression of competence before results could confirm it.
— Two rooms for families, the rest shared.
— Weapons to security, with receipts.
— The wine stays sealed.
— Toilets only at night; gray water gets collected.
— Gate lighting intermittently.
At first, every decision pressed against his chest. After the tenth, he felt an almost pleasant relief. The world no longer asked him to prove he was right, only to choose before others did.
Lia found him in the battery room staring at graphs that did not match consumption.
— Have you eaten? she asked.
— Yes.
— What?
He could not remember.
She handed him a plate with bread, cheese, and half an apple. The apple was the only fresh thing on the plate. Julian bit into it and tasted it so intensely that for a moment he thought he had never eaten a real apple before.
— The system is losing energy, he said. But the batteries are still at ninety-one percent.
— Maybe the measurement is faulty.
— Two independent systems show the same thing.
— Then they aren't independent.
— The cables are separate.
— I wasn't talking about cables.
Lia touched his cheek with the backs of her fingers. The gesture was tender and contained no question. Behind her, Sofia appeared carrying two blankets.
— Vera needs you, Sofia said to Julian. Or she says she needs someone who doesn't faint at blood and everyone decided that's you.
Her eyes dropped toward Lia's hand, which remained on his cheek a moment too long.
— You can have him, Lia said. We all get him in shifts now.
— That's not what I meant.
— I know.
Julian felt the tension between them and, beneath fear, fatigue, and shame, a small, absurd satisfaction. Two intelligent, dangerous women were adjusting their tone because of him while the outside world came apart. He noticed the satisfaction in himself like a spot on an X-ray. It did not disappear because he had noticed it.
In the infirmary, the woman who had given birth on the road had started bleeding again. Vera needed steady light and compatible blood. No one knew everyone's blood types. Julian remembered he was A positive. Dominic was O negative. Vera pricked the volunteers' fingers and improvised tests on white plates.
— We don't have a lab, she said. We don't have the right to certainty.
— But we need a decision, Julian replied.
— That doesn't make the decision more correct.
The suitable donor turned out to be the lawyer who repaired hinges. He lay down on a bed, pale before the needle even went in.
— I spent twelve years litigating so I wouldn't be personally liable for anything, he said. Poor preparation.
The woman survived. The baby began to cry at the exact moment the improvised pump stopped by itself.
Every light in the building flickered.
On the screen by the entrance, rectangles appeared around people for less than a second. Julian saw names the system had no way of knowing. Beside Vera: MAINTENANCE. Beside Dominic: INTERVENTION. Beside Lia: CONTRADICTION. Beside Sofia: RETURN. Beside Celia:
ACCELERATION.
There was nothing beside the newborn.
Julian turned toward his own reflection in the black glass. A white rectangle framed his face.
CENTER.
Then the display returned to the temperature indicator.
— Did you see it? he asked Vera.
— See what?
He pointed toward the monitor.
— A fluctuation.
— I only saw you get even paler. Sit down.
He did not sit.
After midnight, people tried to sleep. Some made beds beside people they had met that day.
Others kept visible gaps between mattresses, as though ownership could be declared through empty space. Someone cried silently in the bathroom. From the children's bedroom came whispers about airplanes, aliens, and war. Every explanation was more bearable than none.
Owen sat on the library floor drawing.
— You should be sleeping, Julian told him.
— So should you.
— I have things to do.
— All adults say that when they can't sleep.
Julian sat beside him. The sketchbook contained a top-down plan of the house. The rooms were surprisingly accurate: kitchen, workshop, reservoir, improvised infirmary, greenhouse. Beneath them, Owen had drawn a large square with no door.
— What's here?
— The room downstairs.
— There is no room there.
— Then why is it breathing?
Julian listened. The pipes vibrated faintly. Pumps circulated water through the house with a rhythm that, once named, really did resemble breathing.
— It's the plumbing.
— I know.
— Then why did you say it's breathing?
Owen shrugged.
— Because you heard it too.
At what the mechanical clock indicated as 3:31, every person in the house woke up.
Not because of a loud sound. Each had heard someone say their name beside their ear.
Vera heard her dead husband's voice. Malik heard his sister in Casablanca. Lia refused to say what she heard. Sofia said it had been a child. Celia claimed she heard nothing, but her hands trembled when she turned on her flashlight.
Julian had heard his mother's voice.
— Why did you leave her outside? she had asked.
People gathered in the hall, barefoot, blankets over their shoulders. They searched for a transmitter, a prank, an intruder. The security agents checked every room. Malik disconnected the speakers. Lia found a weak signal in the wiring but no source and no measurable content.
— Collective suggestion, Samira said from the doorway.
No one had seen her enter.
She wore the same dark coat as in the office at Sankt Alban. Her hair was wet, though it was not raining outside.
Julian felt the air in the hall tighten.
— How did you get here?
— The gate was open.
Celia turned toward the agents. They shook their heads.
— It wasn't open, she said.
Samira looked at the people gathered there, then at Julian.
— In an exhausted group, the same fear can acquire different voices. No technology is necessary.
— What did you hear? Owen asked.
For the first time, Samira looked surprised.
— Nothing.
— Then why did you wake up?
No one spoke.
Samira looked at the boy with clinical, almost hostile attention. After several seconds, the lights went out completely.
In the darkness, Julian felt a small hand grip his wrist. He assumed it was Owen.
A voice, very close, told him:
— Choose the loss that would make the rest credible.
When the lights came back, the hand was gone.
Owen was three meters away, beside his mother.
On the table in the hall, someone had turned the notebook page.
The next page did not contain a time.
It contained a name.
ELENA MERCER.
* * * On the second day, Julian tried to use his money.
First he offered an airline ten million for a flight from Valencia to Zürich. The message remained in the internal server queue. He called the director's number, then the private number Celia had obtained. No answer.
An amateur-radio station in southern France managed to transmit as far as Spain. Julian promised one hundred thousand francs to anyone who found a woman named Lucia Mercer at the Hotel Mirador. The operator replied:
— Friend, your franc doesn't fit in the fuel tank.
— I have fuel here. I can pay afterward.
— After what?
Julian had no answer.
Celia found a retired military pilot with access to a light aircraft at a private airfield. The man wanted not money but twenty doses of insulin for his wife and a place in the house for both of them.
— I accept, Julian said.
Vera intervened.
— Twenty doses means weeks of treatment for three people.
— My mother is alone.
— And they are alone to someone.
— The pilot can bring more people back.
— If he gets there. If he can land. If he isn't shot down. If the airports exist.
— I'm asking you for a medical assessment, not a moral one.
Vera looked at him with the same absence of admiration as her son.
— Medically, the insulin is more useful here. Morally, you just tried to buy the exception you'll deny to others.
Julian accepted the trade anyway. The pilot left for the airfield with the doses in a refrigerated case. Two hours later, a column of smoke appeared from the direction of the road. They never learned whether it was the plane, the airfield, or something else.
No message came.
That same afternoon, three armored vehicles reached the gate. From the first stepped a man Julian had met at the Geneva gala, owner of a mining group. His name was Étienne Volker, and he still wore an immaculate suit, though there was mud on his shoes.
— I knew you'd prepared a place, he said.
Behind him were his wife, two adult children, a private physician, and six armed agents.
— We don't have room, Julian replied.
Volker smiled.
— There is no place where I don't have room.
— There is here.
The man opened the trunk. Boxes of gold, banknotes, jewelry, medicines, two automatic weapons.
— Choose what you want.
The people in the courtyard watched. Gold still had the power to produce silence, though it could not pump water or cure anything.
— We need the medicines, Celia said quietly.
— They have a doctor too, Vera said. And agents. That's eleven people.
Volker took Julian by the arm and led him a few steps aside.
— Don't be sentimental. These people will eat you in six months. I bring security, resources, connections.
— Connections with whom?
— With the world that will remain.
— We don't know what remains.
— The people who knew how to take before asking remain.
Julian looked at him. In another world, he might have admired the brutality as honesty. Here it sounded only like a sentence that had lost its scenery.
— You can leave the medicines and go, he said.
Volker laughed.
— You think you're king because you have a generator.
— No. I think it's my gate.
Volker's agents moved their hands toward their weapons. Julian's people did the same. For several seconds, two vanished worlds measured themselves through the fence: money and property on one side, number and access on the other.
Celia stepped beside Julian.
— Leave the doctor and the medicines. The rest go.
Volker turned toward his physician, a young man with glasses. He said nothing.
— Pierre? Volker asked.
The doctor looked toward the house, then toward Volker's children.
— My wife is in Lausanne, he said. If there's a place for her too...
— There isn't, Celia replied.
— Then I stay with Mr. Volker.
It was the first choice Julian had seen made against immediate survival. It impressed him more than he would have admitted.
Volker closed the trunk.
— You'll beg me to come back.
— Probably, Julian said.
The vehicles left. An hour later, one returned alone. Volker's daughter was driving, blood on her cheek. The agents had fought over fuel. Her father had been shot. The doctor had stayed with the wounded.
Julian opened the gate.
Celia made no comment, but updated the table: fifty-four.
That night, the group voted on its first rules. Equal rations, mandatory work, shared inventory for medicines and fuel, weapons kept in a locked room. Jewelry and money could remain private property but had no value in distribution.
One man protested that he had brought fifty thousand in cash.
Dominic handed him a bucket.
— Excellent. You can count it while you clean the toilets.
People laughed. The man did not.
Julian checked his balance before sleeping. The app functioned locally, frozen at more than three hundred forty million. The figure was whole, bright, and useless.
Beneath it, a new line appeared:
FUNDS CAN PURCHASE ANY ELEMENT GENERATED INSIDE.
Julian typed: THEN I BUY MY MOTHER'S SAFETY.
The answer came thirty-one seconds later:
PERSON NOT AVAILABLE IN THIS VERSION.
— Is she dead? Julian whispered.
The screen answered:
THAT IS A QUESTION ABOUT OUTSIDE.
— What is outside?
THE APPLICATION CLOSED.
In the reflection of the black screen, Julian saw the white room and the red door. On the other side, someone was knocking slowly.
This time, the voice was his mother's.
CHAPTER VI
THE PROVISIONAL ORDER
On the third morning, Julian found his name written on a board.
JULIAN — ENERGY / SECURITY / DECISIONS.
Below were the other responsibilities: Vera — medicine, Dominic — intervention and sanitation, Lia — communications, Malik — mechanics, Sofia — spaces and distribution, Celia — stocks. No one knew who had made the list.
— “Decisions” isn't a function, Julian said.
— Then erase it, Dominic replied.
Julian took the marker, but before he touched the board, a woman stopped him.
— Leave it, she said. We need to know who answers when there's no time to vote.
It was Claire Volker, daughter of the man killed on the road. She had washed the blood from her face and wore one of Sofia's sweaters. She was twenty-nine and had spoken very little until then.
— There's a council, Julian replied.
— A council doesn't start the generator.
As if summoned by the sentence, the energy system sounded an alarm.
The solar panels were producing normally, the batteries were charged, but the main inverter refused to power the water pumps. The screen displayed valid shutdown commands signed with Julian's certificate.
— I didn't send them, he said.
— The system says you did, Lia replied.
— Isolate the controller.
— It's already isolated.
Malik tried a manual start. The pumps ran for three seconds and stopped. The upper reservoirs held half a day's water.
People gathered in the courtyard. When a community has little information, every technical noise becomes a rumor. Within ten minutes, someone was saying the well had been poisoned. Someone else that Celia had blocked the water to force people out.
Julian went down to the technical room. The electrical diagram was simple, but every bypass triggered another protection. Someone had designed the system to refuse precisely the solutions he would try.
— The Vela board, he said.
Lia looked at him.
— No.
— It may anticipate the false command and filter it.
— Or it may be producing the commands.
— We have six hours of water.
— Dominic can install a mechanical pump.
— In two days.
— Then we ration.
Julian felt anger rise. It was not only fear about the water. It was intolerance of a problem that refused to acknowledge his competence.
— We connect the board, he said.
— I run communications, Lia said. You wrote it on the board yourself.
— I didn't write it.
— But you like it when it authorizes you.
Celia entered the room.
— Use it.
— It isn't your decision, Lia said.
— No. It's his. That's why you're all here.
Julian connected the board.
Immediately, the false commands stopped. The pumps started. One line appeared on the monitor:
REWARD FOR COOPERATION: LOCAL CONTINUITY.
He deleted the message before the others saw it.
In the courtyard, water began running from the taps. People applauded. A child shouted Julian's name. Others repeated it. It was ridiculous and, inside him, magnificent.
Dominic arrived with dirty hands and an old pump on his shoulder.
— I found the solution without ghosts, he said.
— It was too slow.
— Yes. Saviors have to be fast, otherwise people notice the plumbing.
Julian ignored the remark and organized a meeting. He established shifts, consumption limits, patrols, medical procedures, and rules for newcomers. He spoke clearly. People asked questions. He had answers or produced a process for obtaining them. For the first time in his life, no one asked him to simplify because they did not understand; he simplified because lives depended on it.
That evening, Claire brought him a cup of coffee in the control room.
— I thought we were out of coffee, Julian said.
— Celia keeps some for important situations.
— Am I an important situation?
— Everyone thinks so.
Claire sat on the edge of the table. She wore no makeup, but her beauty still had the reflex of the old world: she knew what it did inside a room.
— My father would have bought this place and thrown you out, she said.
— He tried.
— I have nothing to buy with.
— You could work.
— I do. But I can offer something else too.
She touched his wrist.
Julian did not pull his hand away immediately. He saw four red marks reappear beneath her fingers.
— Claire.
— I'm not insulting you. I don't think you're saving me for that. But people traded closeness for protection long before they invented money.
— I don't want that.
— You want not to have to ask for it.
Lia was standing in the doorway. Claire withdrew her hand without haste.
— My shift starts now, she said, and left.
Lia put a list of radio frequencies on the table.
— I'm not going to ask what that was.
— It was nothing.
— That's exactly your luxury now. Situations appear, offer you something, and you decide nothing happened because you didn't fully consume the offer.
— I refused her.
— After checking that the offer existed.
Julian stood.
— All day people have needed me. I don't have time to apologize for every reaction.
— No. You only have time to feed on being needed and call it responsibility.
Lia left. Julian remained alone with the screens.
On the local map, the house appeared as a green point in a black area. Around it, other points went out. Each represented a generator, a station, a signal.
Text appeared at the edge of the screen:
THE CENTER HAS BEEN ACCEPTED.
Julian typed: WHAT CENTER?
The answer came: On the fourth day came the people who had prepared for the end of the world and were angry that the end did not belong to them.
THE ONE YOU WANTED.
There were twenty-two of them in six modified vehicles. They wore tactical vests, filter masks, and weapons cleaner than their hands. On the first truck they had painted the Continuum symbol: a circle open at one point.
The leader was Ruben Senn. Julian recognized him from the forums that had appeared after the interview. He had been a marketing consultant, then a survival influencer. In his videos, every social collapse ended with him chopping wood shirtless.
— We came when you gave the signal, Ruben said over the intercom.
— I gave no signal.
— The thirty-one seconds. We received the message.
— What message?
Ruben held a portable radio toward the camera. Julian's voice came from it:
— BRING WHAT YOU CAN DEFEND. LEAVE BEHIND WHAT SLOWS YOU DOWN.
Julian felt the people in the control room look at him.
— I never said that.
— It doesn't matter who said it. The voice was confirmation.
— It matters to me.
Ruben smiled at the camera.
— That's why you're the leader and we're ready to help you.
Their vehicles carried food, ammunition, filters, a diesel generator, and dozens of crates of nearly useless objects: knives, vests, masks, small silver ingots, night-vision devices. They had no antibiotics, diapers, or pump parts.
— They have fuel, Celia said.
— They also have their own command structure, Lia said.
— We dissolve it.
— Armed people don't dissolve because you give them a form.
Sofia watched the cameras. Beside the last vehicle stood Konrad Meier with an improvised bandage at his shoulder.
— That's your former boss, she said.
Julian enlarged the image. Konrad looked exhausted.
They opened the outer gate but kept the group in the space between the fences. Vera examined them. Two people had fevers. One had a superficial gunshot wound. Konrad had a fractured clavicle.
— Helix fell the first night, he told Julian. The access systems locked people inside. Every door was receiving commands from another version of the building.
— What does “another version” mean?
— Floors that didn't exist. Rooms with people who weren't there. Orpheus began generating answers without inputs.
— Why did you come here?
Konrad laughed and winced in pain.
— Because every answer contained your address.
Ruben asked for a meeting. He entered the living room with two men, leaving their weapons at the gate only after Julian insisted. He presented his offer as a merger: his resources, Julian's infrastructure, joint leadership.
— My people made sacrifices, he said. They sold property, broke ties with those who laughed at them. They have a right to a place.
— The fact that you bet on disaster doesn't give you rights over other people's survival, Sofia replied.
— And the fact that you slept with the right man does?
The room tightened.
Julian stood.
— Say that again and all of you leave.
Ruben raised his palms.
— I'm not insulting anyone. I'm describing the new reality. Everyone brings something. Some bring skill. Some loyalty. Some closeness. The old hypocrisies cost resources.
Celia studied him with cold interest.
— And what do you bring, Mr. Senn?
— People who listen.
— That's the problem too.
After two hours, they accepted seven members of the group: a diesel mechanic, a midwife, two farmers, an amateur radio operator, and two people without fever who had military experience. The rest could camp outside the second fence and receive water in exchange for work. Weapons remained locked away.
Ruben agreed too easily.
— You prepared to be refused, Julian said.
— I prepared for people to say no before they need to say yes.
That night, three crates of food disappeared from storage. The security camera showed an empty corridor. The door sensor, however, had recorded access with Julian's fingerprint.
Konrad found him in the technical room.
— The system is using you as authority, he said.
— Or someone is copying my identity.
— It's the same thing if everyone accepts the signature.
— Tell me what happened in 2017.
Konrad sat down heavily.
— Vela built a reward environment for four subjects. They wanted to see whether an identity could be extracted from choices rather than memory. They gave each one a world where desires produced outcomes.
— I was one of the subjects.
— No.
— Samira says I was.
— Samira was injured. The files were mixed up.
— Then what was I?
Konrad looked at him with a fear Julian had never seen in him before.
— You were the one who decided when the world closed.
Beyond the wall came three short knocks and one long one.
Konrad went white.
— Don't open it, he whispered.
— Why?
— Because last time it wasn't someone who stayed outside.
— What stayed outside?
Konrad closed his eyes.
— The rest.
* * * The community's first constitution fit on a page and a half.
Sofia wrote it by hand because printers consumed too much. Water, food, energy, and medicines were common goods. Work was assigned according to ability. Urgent decisions belonged to an elected coordinator. Decisions on expulsion, use of weapons, and reduction of rations required the council.
— How long is the term? Dominic asked.
— Until civil administration returns, Julian said.
— That's not a duration.
— Thirty days, Lia proposed. Reconfirmation by vote.
Celia looked up from the stock ledger.
— You want monthly elections while the people at the fence have guns?
— I want no one to turn the emergency into a profession, Lia replied.
Julian pretended to be concerned with the text. Inside, the idea of a vote irritated him. Not because he feared losing, but because it seemed absurd that people who did not understand the systems should decide who coordinated them. He recognized the thought and reformulated it: competence had to be protected from volatility.
— Forty-five days, he said. The council can withdraw the mandate by two-thirds.
— Who sits on the council? Vera asked.
— The domain leads.
— Meaning people appointed by you.
— The people doing the work.
— And the people who clean, cook, care for children?
— They have a representative.
— Who?
Julian had not thought of one.
A woman named Nadiya, who had worked as a housekeeper at the village guesthouse, raised her hand.
— I can do it.
Celia assessed her quickly.
— Do you have administrative experience?
— I have experience seeing what people leave behind when they think someone else will clean it.
Dominic smiled.
— Perfect qualification.
On the evening of the vote, fifty-eight people had the right to participate. Ruben and the people outside could not vote. He protested over the radio that Julian was building an “inside” elite and that the prepared were being treated worse than unprepared refugees.
— He has a point, Claire said.
— He has ammunition and a need for legitimacy, Celia replied. The point is packaging.
The candidates for coordinator were Julian, Dominic, and Vera. Dominic had nominated himself so there would be opposition. Vera had been nominated by Nadiya.
Each spoke for five minutes.
Vera said a community was not a machine and people who were temporarily useless remained people. Dominic said he had no vision, which made him safer than the others. Julian spoke about water, energy, risk, patrols, and links with the villages. He promised order, information, and criteria.
He did not mention the Vela board, the messages, or the fact that the house system demanded a name in exchange for “local continuity.” He said there was no evidence of an unusual cause.
He won with forty-seven votes.
When Sofia announced the result, people applauded. Someone put a hand on his shoulder.
Claire kissed him on the cheek. A boy asked to take a photograph, though phones could no longer send anything.
Julian felt the same inner expansion as on the day he saw the balance. This time the number was not in an account. It was in people's eyes.
Lia had voted for Vera.
— Not because she's better at energy, she told him. Because she doesn't like power.
— That's not a qualification.
— In the form we're living in now, it is.
Julian's first decision was to authorize a mixed patrol with two of Ruben's people. They had experience and knew the terrain. His second was to restrict access to the control room. His third, to move the Vela board into a secret compartment beneath the workshop.
— You said the lower level doesn't exist, Sofia observed.
— Now there's a technical compartment. Not the room.
— Your words change the architecture very quickly.
— I can't leave the device in plain sight.
— Why don't you tell the council what it does?
— Because it would cause panic and superstition.
— Or because it would change the vote.
Julian looked at her.
— You think I wouldn't have won?
— I think you just demonstrated why they should have known.
That night, Julian went down alone. The lower-level door had reappeared. He opened it only far enough to slide the board into a niche beside the stairs. He did not go farther down.
A message lit on the screen: Julian typed: I AM NOT THE SUBJECT.
LOCAL AUTHORITY ACCEPTED.
YIELD INCREASES WHEN THE SUBJECT CONFUSES TRUST WITH EVIDENCE.
Answer:
THIS IS ONE OF THE SUBJECT'S CONDITIONS.
From below came Lia's voice:
— You won. Now tell them the truth.
Julian closed the door.
The next day, he announced that the lower level had been sealed for structural-safety reasons.
* * * On day six, water began disappearing faster than they drank it.
Rami Haddad, the water-treatment specialist, accounted for every liter. The reservoirs were losing nearly eight hundred liters a night. There was no water on the floor, pipe pressure was stable, and the well worked.
— Someone is stealing it, Ruben said over the radio.
— Eight hundred liters don't fit in a backpack, Rami replied.
Julian checked the sensors. All reported coherent values, but the sum of inputs did not equal the sum of outputs. It was the kind of problem he hated: every component told the truth while the system lied.
By noon, the ration was cut to three liters per person. Showers were banned. People accepted the rule in public and immediately began hiding bottles.
Nadiya found twenty-four liters beneath an engineer's bed. The man said he was saving it for his daughter. He had no daughter. Then he said the water had been there when he received the room.
— Everyone is going to lie the same way, Dominic said. “I didn't take it. It was here. It's for the child.” We should print forms.
— What do you propose? Julian asked.
— Shared inventory and random inspections. Including your room.
— My room has no reserves.
— Then the inspection will be quick.
Julian approved, but it bothered him when people opened his cupboards. They found two bottles of mineral water from Montréal forgotten in a dressing-room drawer. Julian had not put them there.
The bottling date on the label was November 19, 2017.
That same day, Malik and Dominic brought three cows from an abandoned farm. One had a broken leg. Vera said it had to be slaughtered. Half the people had never seen an animal killed.
Everyone wanted meat.
Dominic put the knife in Julian's hand.
— You're the coordinator.
— You have experience.
— I can do it. But people should see that decisions don't end when you write on a board.
Julian looked at the animal. It breathed heavily, one large wet eye fixed on nothing. The children had been sent inside, but Owen watched from behind the fence.
Julian cut. The first movement was insufficient. The cow struggled. Dominic took his hand and guided it.
The blood came out warm, with the same metallic smell as at the station.
At dinner, people ate almost in silence. The portions had been weighed. Even so, someone received more, someone less, and their glances calculated the differences more precisely than the scale.
Claire brought Julian a plate with a better cut.
— No, he said. Give it to a child.
— The children already got theirs.
— Then keep it.
— I don't want meat. I want you to know I chose it for you.
Lia heard. Sofia heard. Julian felt the old pleasure and the weariness that now came after it.
— Put it back, he said.
Claire smiled.
— You didn't refuse when it was water.
— I didn't ask you for water.
— I didn't ask you for protection either. I get it anyway.
That evening, Nadiya reported that one of the guards had offered a woman extra rations in exchange for intimacy. The woman said it had been her choice. The guard said the same thing.
— Then what is the violation? a council member asked. They're adults.
— He used communal goods, Sofia said.
— And what did she use?
The silence that followed was dirty.
Julian removed the guard from duty and reduced his access to the storeroom. He did not punish the woman. Half the group thought the decision too lenient; the other half thought it paternalistic. By morning, the story had five versions, each demonstrating a different morality.
The water problem required a vote. Rami proposed two options: an equal ration of two litres for everyone, or keeping three litres for those inside and one litre for Ruben's camp.
The majority voted for the second option.
— We can't pretend we have common rules and then calculate thirst by which side of the fence you're on, Vera said.
— They have their own reserves, Celia replied.
— Almost gone.
— Then they'll leave.
Julian had veto power on medical-risk grounds. Everyone looked at him.
If he overruled the vote, he lost legitimacy. If he accepted it, he turned the fence into a measure of human worth.
— Three litres for children, the sick, and heavy labour; two for everyone else, regardless of zone, he decided. The camp contributes to pumping and patrols.
No one was satisfied. Julian interpreted that as fairness.
That night, Owen found him in the pump room.
— The water isn't disappearing, the boy said.
— How do you know?
— It goes here.
He pointed to a thin pipe hidden behind a panel. It did not appear on the plans. It ran through the wall toward the level beneath the workshop.
Julian touched it. It was cold and vibrating faintly.
— Did you tell anyone?
— Mum. She said to tell you.
— Good. Don't come near it again.
Owen hugged his notebook to his chest.
— What's drinking the water?
— Probably a tank.
— Tanks don't breathe.
Julian fell silent.
A slow, wet sound came from the wall. An inhalation, then an exhalation.
Owen looked at him.
— You hear it too, he said. That's good. I thought it was only in my world.
CHAPTER VII
THE FIRST SENTENCE
After the ration incident, weapons became the second currency.
They could not be eaten and they did not produce water, but they changed the way a person entered a room. Those carrying a weapon spoke more quietly and were interrupted less often. Those without one noticed every holster.
Julian ordered all weapons kept in the central armoury, issued only for patrols. Ruben's people refused to hand over three pistols they said were “personal.” The following night, one of the patrols was attacked on the road toward the village. A man returned with a broken jaw and without his rifle.
Ruben maintained the attackers were locals. Celia believed it had been staged to justify rearming the camp. There were no cameras on that road.
— We give them weapons and we lose control, Lia said.
— We don't give them weapons and they'll take them, Celia replied.
— Then we expel them.
— Where? Into a world without police? Expulsion is a sentence you want to pronounce without giving it its name.
Julian approved two additional weapons for the outer camp, with ammunition counted. Ruben called it compromise. Dominic called it a prelude.
Claire asked to join the patrols.
— Do you have training? Julian asked.
— My father paid people to teach me.
— That doesn't answer the question.
— I shoot better than half your men.
The test proved she was right. Julian put her on the night shift. Sofia noticed the name on the roster.
— That's a bad idea.
— Because she shoots well?
— Because she knows exactly what she wants from you.
— Everyone wants something.
— You used to use that sentence to justify the market. Now you're using it for people.
On her first night patrol, Claire called him to the armoury. A seal had been broken. Two magazines were missing. The camera showed access with Julian's identifier at 2:58.
— I was in the control room, he said.
— I know. I saw you on the monitor.
— Then someone is using my copy.
Claire closed the armoury door.
— Or you're the copy that doesn't know.
— Don't you start too.
— I don't care whether the world is real. The old world was real and my father died in it for a can of fuel.
She stepped closer.
— You only care what you can get here, Julian said.
— Yes.
— Protection.
— And pleasure. I don't have to choose a respectable version.
Julian felt anger, attraction, and warning at the same time. He knew his power made her consent difficult to separate from strategy. He knew Lia was right. He knew Sofia would find out. Knowledge did not diminish his desire. It intensified it, because the transgression became part of it.
— I can take you off patrols, he said.
— You can.
— I can send you out of the inner compound.
— You can.
— And yet you came here.
Claire put her hand on his chest.
— That's exactly how I know you want me. A good man would have left already.
Julian did not leave.
Later, he could not remember who had turned off the light first. He remembered the cold metal of the shelf, her breathing, and the fact that for a few minutes the end of the world had become the perfect setting for an absence of consequences. It was not tenderness and it was not a promise. It was exactly the kind of freedom people call authenticity when they do not want to call it selfishness.
When they came out, Lia was standing in the corridor.
She did not raise her voice. Her gaze was heavier than a scene.
— Magazines were missing, Julian said.
— Did you find them?
— No.
— But you found something else.
Claire passed her without stopping.
— I don't owe her exclusivity, Julian said.
— I'm not the one you owe an explanation to.
— Then who?
Lia pointed toward the house full of people sleeping under his orders.
— Everyone who has to believe that access to you doesn't buy access to resources.
— She didn't get anything.
— She got proof that she can get into a locked room with the coordinator while she's on duty.
Julian opened his mouth, then closed it.
An alarm sounded in the technical room. The pumps were starting. The tanks, which had been dropping steadily, began to fill. The flow came through the hidden pipe in the wall, reversed.
The monitor displayed:
REWARD FOR UNCENSORED RESPONSE.
CONTINUITY INCREASES.
Lia read it before Julian could erase it.
— It's training you, she said.
— Or punishing me by making me think it rewarded me.
— Does the difference matter if next time you'll want to repeat it?
Sofia appeared at the end of the corridor. She looked at Lia, at Julian, then at the armoury door left ajar.
She did not ask.
— The water came back, Julian said.
Sofia smiled with unsurprised sadness.
— Of course. You've finally built a world where every betrayal comes with a prize.
* * * In the morning, no one talked about the water.
The tanks were full, the pumps were working, and people should have celebrated. Instead, news about Julian and Claire had circulated before breakfast. It did not circulate as information but as explanation. To some, the water had returned because Julian had gained access to a secret level of the house. To others, Claire had persuaded him to open hidden reserves. One woman claimed she had seen the light in the armoury change colour. Ruben had announced in the outer camp that the leader had been “accepted by the mechanism.”
No one used the word betrayal, but everyone left it in plain sight.
At the hour the notebook in the hall called seven, a gunshot came from the old greenhouse.
People threw themselves to the ground without knowing why. A second shot did not come.
Dominic found Nils Baumann, the student who had helped distribute food, with a pistol in his hand. At his feet lay Oskar, the cook. The bullet had passed through his thigh and lodged in an irrigation tank. Water was spilling into the earth.
— He came at me, Nils said.
Oskar, still conscious, cursed him.
— I was telling you to lower the gun.
— He came at me.
The pistol was one of those Ruben's camp had declared personal. The magazine came from the central armoury.
Julian watched the blood mixing with water on the greenhouse floor. The image was too well composed: life, resource, guilt. For a moment he had the sense that someone had chosen a symbol instead of an accident.
— Who gave you the gun? he asked.
Nils tightened his jaw.
— I found it.
— Where?
— Under the mattress.
— Whose mattress?
— Mine.
— It wasn't there yesterday.
Nils looked at him with exhausted anger.
— Neither was this world.
Vera and Dominic operated on Oskar on the table where the cow had been butchered. The bullet had missed the artery, but the wound was dirty. The antibiotics used on him could no longer be used for anyone else. The loss from the tank equalled the entire community's daily ration.
By noon, people were demanding punishment.
They did not need to demand it aloud. They had gathered in the common room and looked toward Julian as if he were a device required to produce order. Nils sat on a chair with his hands tied in front of him. He was twenty and trying to look like a man whom fear could not make smaller.
Ruben demanded that he be removed from the inner compound.
— He stole a weapon, fired it, wounded a man, and destroyed water. Four violations. Outside.
— Outside where? Lia asked.
— With us, until we decide whether he can stay.
— With the people the gun came from?
Ruben smiled.
— We know how to hold guns without shooting our cooks.
Sofia proposed isolating Nils and conducting an inquiry. Celia said they had no institutions for lengthy investigations. Dominic asked that they check who had entered the armoury. The logs still pointed to Julian's identifier.
— Then the investigation starts with you, Vera said.
The room went silent.
Julian felt every gaze turn toward him. In the old world, he would have asked for an independent audit, a chain of custody, a team without conflicts of interest. In his house, every institution was a person sleeping a few metres away from him.
— I'll surrender my identifier until this is clarified, he said.
Celia intervened immediately.
— No. We have systems that respond only to him.
— Then this isn't a trial, Lia said. It's a ceremony in which the centre remains innocent because the infrastructure needs him.
A murmur passed through the room. Julian wanted to contradict her, but the sentence was too precise.
— Ask him why he took the gun, Owen said from behind his mother.
Nils looked toward the boy.
— I heard the gates are opening tonight.
— From whom? Julian asked.
— From you.
— I didn't say that.
— Your voice was in the pipe. It said people were coming from the city and they'd take the women first. It said the guards would run. It told me to protect my sister.
In the room, Nils's sister, a seventeen-year-old girl, covered her mouth.
— Why didn't you come to us? Sofia asked.
— Because the voice said you were already with him.
— With whom?
Nils pointed at Julian.
Ruben gave a short laugh.
— Nice. Now the house gives orders in the king's voice.
— Or the boy is making it up, Celia said.
— Then he's inventing exactly the fears you've fed with patrols and lists, Lia replied.
The discussion detached itself from the act and began feeding every accumulated conflict. The people inside accused those outside of bringing weapons. Those in the camp accused those in the house of keeping the water and the women “safe” under the same authority. The word women was spoken as a resource before anyone could stop it.
Yasmin, the woman involved in the ration exchange, stood up.
— Stop talking about us as something you can take or protect.
A man from the camp said:
— You sold access for food.
Yasmin looked at him without blinking.
— I chose between hunger and a man who could open the storeroom. Don't call my choice freedom just so you don't have to see who controlled the door.
The punished guard lowered his head. Claire stood against the wall with her arms crossed. She was not looking at Julian.
— And when a woman seeks power herself? Celia asked. Do we infantilise her because we don't like the choice?
— No, Sofia said. We separate desire from public consequences. That's exactly what Julian failed to do.
This time his name was spoken directly.
Julian felt the impulse to take over the conversation, reduce it to procedure, salvage what remained of his authority. Instead of speaking, he looked at the people in the room. They were frightened, hungry, exhausted. But beneath all that was an older energy: the pleasure of watching someone be judged. A community without spectacles had found its first spectacle.
The projector on the back wall switched itself on.
It did not display water figures. It displayed the collective pulse, extracted from nowhere: a line that rose when Ruben demanded expulsion and fell when Vera spoke about evaluation. In the corner, a number climbed.
ENGAGEMENT: 63%.
Julian switched off the projector. It came back on.
ENGAGEMENT: 71%.
— Don't feed it, Owen said.
— What? someone asked.
The boy pointed toward the room, not the screen.
— This.
Julian called for a secret ballot. There were three options: isolation inside, supervised work in the outer camp, or complete expulsion. The majority chose the camp. Expulsion received more votes than isolation.
— Seven days, Julian said. No weapon. He works on pumping and sleeps in Ruben's area. Then we review.
— That's a death sentence if someone attacks, Nils's sister said.
— He'll be protected, Ruben replied.
The way he said it made the promise sound like ownership.
When they untied his hands, Nils approached Julian.
— It really was your voice.
— I believe that you heard it.
— That's not the same thing.
— It's all I can say.
Nils spat at his feet.
The gate opened. The community watched him pass from the lit area into the camp beyond the fence. No one applauded. Yet the indicator on the projector, visible through the window, rose to eighty-nine percent.
The moment the gate closed, the water system emitted a short sound.
The monitor displayed:
LOSS HAS BEEN ACCEPTED.
ORDER INCREASES CONTINUITY.
Julian erased the message before the others could see it.
Lia was behind him.
— You can't erase what you've learned, she said.
That night, no one attacked the gates.
Nils disappeared before dawn.
His tracks stopped at the edge of the camp, beside the pipe through which the water had flowed back into the house.
* * * The message came over the radio at 6:20 in the morning.
A school in a village twenty kilometres away had twenty-six children, five adults, and water for one day. Their bus would not start. An armed group had passed through during the night and taken the food.
— We can send two vehicles, Dominic said.
— Two trips use almost a fifth of our mobile fuel, Celia replied.
— They're children.
— I know how to count children too.
Ruben was listening on the frequency and cut in.
— My people bring them in. In exchange, all of us enter the second perimeter.
— You don't barter children for a fence, Sofia said.
— I'm trading risk for safety.
Julian looked at the map. The road passed through an area where robberies had occurred. A transport without an escort was vulnerable. With Ruben's men, the mission had better odds, and Ruben gained the position he had wanted from the beginning.
— Two vehicles from us, two from you, Julian said. Eight people from your camp come inside after the return. The rest stay outside.
— Twelve.
— Eight.
— Ten, and full ammunition on patrols.
— Nine. Ammunition stays counted.
— Agreed.
Dominic looked at him after they closed the channel.
— That was fast.
— We needed an escort.
— You needed to feel you'd got a discount on a deal we shouldn't have made.
Julian went on the mission. It was not necessary, but he could not bear the idea of others taking the risk while he remained at the screens. Lia told him that might be courage or the need to be seen as courageous. They did not try to decide.
The school was a low building with colourful drawings in the windows. The children were in the gym. When Julian entered, several recognised him from interviews. One boy asked whether his technology could repair the world. A girl asked whether it was true that he knew the future.
— No, Julian said. I don't know.
It was the most honest answer he had given since the crisis began, and it disappointed them immediately.
The adults said they were teachers. Dominic noticed that two of them wore hiking shoes with fresh mud and did not know the children's names. They admitted they had come to the school the night before and pretended to be helping.
— We leave them, Ruben said.
— They come too, Julian said.
— Why? They're not parents, they're not useful.
— We don't make the selection here.
Ruben smiled.
— You make it at the gate, where it looks administrative.
They loaded the children. On the way back, a group of nearly thirty people blocked the road near a bridge. They had two guns and improvised weapons. They demanded the vehicles and the food.
Ruben got out with his rifle. Julian got out after him.
— We have children! Julian shouted.
— So do we, a woman in the group replied.
A little boy appeared from behind them, carried in someone's arms. His face was dirty and his eyes were closed.
— Take ours too, the woman said.
There were no places.
— Follow us on foot to the property, Julian said.
— How far is it?
— Twenty kilometres.
— My child has a fever.
Vera was not with them. Dominic got out and examined the boy.
— He needs transport.
They took two adults out of a vehicle and put them on foot. Other families demanded the same.
Within minutes, negotiation turned into shoving. Someone fired a shot into the air. The children in the bus screamed.
Ruben answered with a short burst into the asphalt. The crowd scattered.
— Get in! he shouted.
The vehicles started moving. People ran after them. Julian watched through the window as faces fell behind. The woman with the sick child was in their vehicle. Others were not.
At the property, the outer gate opened. Behind the convoy, dozens of people appeared who had followed them. Some were from the bridge, others had joined along the road.
— Close it after the last vehicle, Julian ordered over the radio.
Ruben's last truck slowed. Two of his men had got out to help a fallen woman. The crowd was closing in.
— Wait, Dominic said.
— If they all get into the area between the fences, we lose control.
— Our people are outside.
On the camera, one of them was signalling to hold the gate.
Julian saw the image superimposed over the red door. He had heard the pounding. He smelled antiseptic again.
— Close it, he said.
The gate began to slide.
One of Ruben's men ran and made it through. The other stayed with the fallen woman. When the gate closed, the crowd reached him. The camera was covered by bodies.
— Open it, Dominic said.
— No.
— He's one of ours.
— If I open it, they all come in.
— Then let them in.
— We don't have room.
Beyond the metal came pounding and a short scream. Then nothing distinct.
Owen was standing behind Julian. He did not know when the boy had arrived.
— Someone's still outside, the boy said.
Julian did not turn around.
— I know.
— Then why did you close it?
On the screen, the gate vibrated under pounding hands.
— To keep the people inside.
Owen looked at him for a long time.
— Is that what you say every time?
CHAPTER VIII
THE ROOM BENEATH THE HOUSE
After the gate, Lia no longer slept in Julian's room.
She moved into a small space beside the communications lab and took the black notebook, two shirts, and her toothbrush with her. She did not make the gesture theatrical. It was precisely the absence of theatre that showed Julian the decision was real.
Sofia remained in the guest room. Claire avoided the main house. Celia continued to appear at meals and leave without explanation, as though everyone else's relationships were weather phenomena.
Samira asked for a meeting with all three women and Julian.
— And Claire? he asked.
— Claire has no memories from before the event, Samira said.
— Celia didn't know me before either.
— That's what she says.
They met in the library. Samira put paper and pencils on the table.
— No devices. Each of us writes when and where she first met Julian. Then we compare.
— Is this a party game? Celia asked.
— It's a way to find out whether we're living in the same biography.
Julian did not write. He watched them.
Lia turned over her sheet first: 4 February 2018, Helix Meridian, Basel room. Sofia: 12 June 2013, design conference in Vienna. Celia: 19 November 2017, Sankt Alban, observation room. Samira: 21 November 2017, post-incident consultation.
— You said you weren't in the programme, Julian said to Celia.
— I said the funders think they stand outside it. I didn't say where I was.
— What was I doing when you met me?
— Shouting that a person isn't a copy just because they can be reproduced.
Julian did not recognise the sentence. Yet he felt it in his mouth like something he had once said.
Samira asked them to describe his scars.
Lia wrote one beneath his left collarbone, from before she knew him. Sofia wrote right temple, acquired after Montreal. Celia wrote a line behind the ear, from the interface. Samira wrote “none on admission; occipital incision on discharge.”
Julian ran his hand through his hair. Beneath his fingers he felt a small ridge he had not noticed before.
— My imaging is normal, he said.
— Your imaging has two versions, Samira replied. In the one I received yesterday, there's scar tissue behind the ear. In the archived one, there isn't.
Sofia was gripping her pencil so tightly her fingers had gone white.
— Ask about Ada, she said.
Julian looked at her.
— Who is Ada?
Lia looked up. Celia did not move. Samira closed her eyes for a moment.
— Our daughter, Sofia said.
The room became very quiet.
— We never had a daughter.
— In my memory, we did.
— Sofia, we were together for four years. I would know.
— In my memory, we were together for seven. Ada was born in 2015. She was two when the incident happened.
— There are no photographs.
— There are.
— You never told me.
— Because until last week, when you said my name in the consulting room, I didn't remember either.
Julian stood so quickly that the chair fell over.
— This is manipulation.
— Maybe, Sofia said. But the pain doesn't feel like a new idea. It feels like something that has been waiting.
— Where is the child in your story?
Sofia began to cry soundlessly.
— She stayed in the test room when you closed the segment.
— There was no child.
— I know what you're saying.
— You can't invent this and make me responsible.
— I'm not inventing it intentionally.
Lia stood too, but she did not go to Julian. She sat beside Sofia.
The gesture hurt him in a ridiculous place. Even faced with a possibly dead daughter, part of him noticed that the two women were forming an alliance against him.
Celia saw him notice.
— This is why your rewards were built out of people, she said. Any truth becomes a competition if it keeps you at the centre.
— You don't know how they were built.
— I know what role you gave me in the first session.
— What role?
— Power that desires you and, through desire, confirms your power.
Julian turned to Lia.
— And you?
She hesitated.
— The Orpheus files contained a profile of me before I was hired. The label was “intellectual equal with high erotic validation.” I thought it was a synthetic agent based on public data.
— You're real.
— I don't know what that means here.
— Sofia?
Sofia wiped her face.
—
“Reparative attachment. Availability for forgiveness.”
I found the phrase in Amina's documents.
Julian looked at Samira.
— And what are you?
— Maybe a doctor. Maybe an authority who lets you call manipulation an illness. Maybe the woman you let carry the technical blame.
— And Claire?
— Desire without history, Celia said.
The words were too orderly. Julian felt the room itself pushing him toward a prepared conclusion.
— All of this can be generated, he said. The memories, the files, the labels. Someone wants me to believe I invented you.
— Maybe, Lia replied. But the fact that you don't like the conclusion isn't evidence against it.
Sofia took an old storage device from her pocket.
— Samira found this in the lining of the file.
They played a video file on the library's isolated screen. The image was grainy, dated 19 November 2017. Julian appeared in a white room, kneeling beside a little girl in a yellow sweater. The child's face was not clearly visible.
— Dad, the girl was saying. If you close it, where do I stay?
The Julian in the recording held her shoulders.
— You're not her, he was saying. Listen to me. You have her face, but you're not her.
— Then why are you crying?
Amina's voice came from outside the frame:
— The segment is becoming unstable. You have to choose what we keep.
The Julian in the video stood and approached the camera.
— Keep what cannot be copied, he said.
The image stopped.
In the final frame, reflected in the glass, four women could be seen watching from behind him.
Lia. Sofia. Celia. Samira.
All of them looked exactly as they did now.
* * * Owen drew things he had never seen.
The house appeared in his notebook as a transparent cube, with rooms incorrectly superimposed. The workshop was drawn above the bedrooms, the lake ran beneath the kitchen, and the hidden level descended into a space larger than the mountain.
— The plans don't look like that, Julian told him.
— I know.
— Then why do you draw it that way?
— Because that's how it is when I close my eyes.
They were in the greenhouse. Fine rain fell outside. Owen was doing the schoolwork Sofia had organised for the children, but he had filled the margins with doors, pipes, and faces without mouths.
— Your mother says you hear breathing in the wall.
— You hear it too.
— Maybe it's water.
— Water doesn't say names.
Julian sat opposite him.
— What names does it say?
Owen continued drawing.
— Sometimes Ada. Sometimes Sofia. Sometimes something I can't pronounce.
— Have you ever known an Ada?
— No.
— Did you see the recording?
— Mum doesn't let me come to meetings.
— Then how do you know?
The boy shrugged.
— From the same place you know things before you know them.
Julian caught himself feeling irritated. Owen was not impressed by him, did not seek protection, and offered him no useful conclusion. He was exactly the kind of person Julian considered difficult in meetings and honest in retrospect.
— What do you want? he asked.
— Right now?
— In general.
Owen thought about it.
— For Mum to sleep. For the yard not to smell of meat anymore. To find our dog. And pancakes.
— That's all?
— What else am I supposed to want?
Julian did not answer.
— Adults always want things that make them look different in other adults' eyes, Owen said.
Ruben wants to be the one who was right. Celia wants to know more than she says. Lia wants no one to be able to fool her. Sofia wants you to remember what she remembers.
— And me?
Owen drew a larger door.
— You want every problem to have the shape of your hand.
Julian laughed once, without amusement.
— Who told you that?
— No one. You can see it.
Claire entered the greenhouse carrying a basket of vegetables. She looked at Julian, then at Owen.
— Interrogating the child too?
— We're talking.
— Careful. Children don't know which answer you like.
She left. Owen watched the door.
— She wants to win something from the other women.
— That's none of your business.
— It's none of yours either, but you're still the prize.
Julian snatched the pencil from his hand more abruptly than he intended.
— Enough.
Owen looked at him. He was not frightened, only disappointed.
Julian gave the pencil back.
— I'm sorry.
— It's all right. When people can't change what someone says, they take away the things they say it with.
Later, Owen led him to the hidden pipe. He had removed two panels by himself and marked the route with chalk. The pipe descended, then split into thirty-one very thin branches. Each rose toward an occupied room.
— These aren't water pipes, Julian said.
— What are they?
Julian touched one. He felt a faint pulse.
— Sensors, maybe.
— Then why does one come to me and two to you?
They followed the double branch. One went toward Julian's bedroom. The other descended toward the hidden level.
— Don't touch anything, he said.
Owen put his ear to the wall.
— When I sleep, I'm in the room downstairs.
— You're dreaming.
— No. Things change in dreams. Down there people repeat things until they answer correctly.
— What do they ask?
— They ask me what I want most.
— And what do you say?
— Pancakes.
Julian smiled despite himself.
— What happens?
— They give me pancakes. Then they ask again. I don't think they like the answer.
— Why?
— Because they can't make a big world out of it.
Julian felt a chill.
— What do they ask about me?
Owen put his ear back against the wall.
— Whether you still believe the people inside belong to you.
— And what do you answer?
— That I don't know.
— Good.
— But they say you answered once before.
— What did I say?
Owen turned toward him.
— You said:
“Keep the ones who want me.”
Inside the wall, all thirty-one pipes pulsed at once.
A collective sigh came from every room in the house, as though the people had all breathed at the same time.
Owen hugged his notebook.
— Now I know I said it, he murmured. They usually get angry when I say something true.
* * * The council learned about the pipes from Owen, not from Julian.
The boy had told his mother that the walls were
“counting people.”
Vera confronted Julian in front of everyone. Lia confirmed that the hidden level existed. Sofia said Julian had moved the board there. The silence with which they listened was different from the trust at the vote.
— You hid a device that can influence the energy and the messages, Vera said.
— I was trying to prevent panic.
— You were trying to keep the only thing that makes you indispensable.
— The system is dangerous.
— And that's why you kept it under your room.
Dominic proposed opening the level with witnesses and analogue recording. Julian agreed. He no longer had an alternative that looked less guilty.
Six went down: Julian, Lia, Sofia, Celia, Samira, and Dominic. Owen was locked in the children's room with the others. At least that was what they believed.
The door reappeared when Julian placed his palm against the concrete wall. It did not open mechanically. The surface thinned around his hand and drew aside like a membrane.
— Vela didn't build this, Samira said.
Celia switched on her torch.
— Vela built the house around it.
The stairs descended farther than the mountain should allow. After thirty-one steps, they reached a white corridor. There were no light fixtures, yet every surface emitted light. The corners were rounded until orientation became difficult.
Julian smelled antiseptic and burnt orange.
— This is the room, he said.
— Not for me, Lia replied.
— What do you see?
— Racks. An old data centre with water on the floor.
Sofia stopped.
— I see a hospital corridor.
Dominic swore.
— I see a white basement. What are the rest of you talking about?
Celia did not answer. She was staring ahead with the expression of someone recognising a house after a fire.
Images appeared on the walls. Julian saw himself at his desk before he was fired. Then in the restaurant with Lia. On the jetty with Sofia. In the armoury with Claire. Every scene was filmed from impossible angles, including from inside his own eyes.
— Recordings, Dominic said.
— Or reconstructions, Lia said.
— Or memories, Samira added.
The image changed. Julian appeared in a city he did not recognise, running a larger company.
Three women waited for him in an apartment. Then that same version of Julian died in a plane crash.
Another became president. Another ran an underground shelter and ordered the expulsion of everyone who was ill. Another refused the money and lived alone in a cabin. In every version, at some point, the red door appeared.
— These are sessions, Celia said.
— How many? Julian asked.
The walls filled with hundreds of lives. His face aged, grew young again, became fat, thin, wounded. Sometimes Lia was his wife. Sometimes Sofia did not exist. Sometimes Celia killed him.
Sometimes Claire was his daughter. In one version, Owen was the only adult.
— Stop, Julian said.
The images stopped.
The fact that they had responded made it worse.
At the end of the corridor stood a transparent rectangle resembling a vertical bed. Thin cables entered a dark form. Julian approached. The form was the size of his body, but it could not be seen through the milky surface.
Samira touched a panel.
— This is a maintenance container, she said. Or a representation of one.
— Who is inside?
— The signals indicate the same brain activity as yours.
Dominic looked at him.
— You mean he's in there?
— I mean the device displays that.
Text appeared on the surface:
THE INNER SUBJECT REQUESTS CONFIRMATION OF CONTINUITY.
Julian stepped closer.
— Am I the inner subject?
The answer:
DEPENDS ON WHICH DOOR YOU ARE ASKING FROM.
— What do you want from me?
AN IDENTIFIER THAT CANNOT BE GENERATED FROM REWARD.
— A name?
A NAME IS A CHEAP FORM.
— Then what?
WHAT YOU KEPT WHEN YOU CLOSED THE REST.
Sofia approached the container.
— Ada?
The surface became transparent for a fraction of a second. Inside was a little girl in a yellow sweater.
Sofia screamed and struck the material with her palms. The image vanished. In the child's place appeared Julian, naked, eyes open.
Lia saw something else.
— It's empty, she said.
— No, Samira replied. It's me.
Celia took a step back.
— For me it's Konrad.
Dominic looked at each of them.
— There's nothing inside. You're seeing what it makes you see.
— What do you see? Julian asked.
— A water tank and a man stupid enough to talk to it.
Owen's voice came from behind them.
— It isn't a tank.
The boy was standing at the entrance to the corridor. No one knew how he had come down.
— Go back, Vera said in Amina's voice.
They all looked at her.
Samira covered her mouth.
— I didn't say that.
Owen approached the container.
— You see people because you want to save someone, he said. I see a hole.
— What kind of hole? Lia asked.
— One something is looking through.
The light in the room dimmed. The same question appeared on every surface:
WHO WAS LEFT OUTSIDE?
Their voices began to repeat it. Lia's, Sofia's, Celia's, Amina's, Dominic's. Their mouths did not move.
Julian covered his ears.
— I don't know!
The question stopped.
Owen looked at him.
— You said something else the first time.
— What?
The boy pointed to the back wall. A silent recording was playing there. Julian in 2017 stood before the red door. Several silhouettes could be seen behind him. One held a little girl's hand.
The Julian in the image turned toward the camera and said something.
Text appeared beneath it:
NOT THE PERSON. THE POSSIBILITY.
The red door closed. The silhouettes remained beyond it.
In the present, the room began to contract. The walls drew closer without moving, reducing the space through a geometry the eye could not follow.
— Out! Dominic shouted.
They ran for the stairs. Owen stumbled. Julian lifted him and carried him. Behind them, the container opened with a wet sound.
Something breathed.
They reached the top before the door closed. The wall became concrete again.
A white line remained beneath the skin of Julian's arm, running from wrist to elbow.
Samira looked at it.
— That wasn't there.
— I know. The line pulsed once, and the same slow breathing from the wall came from inside Julian's body.
* * * After forty-seven days, people stopped calling the situation temporary.
The house had become the nucleus of a settlement. The old greenhouses had been expanded with panels salvaged from villages, and transparent roofs formed a belt of glass around the building.
The outer fence had been doubled. A small mill pumped water. The workshop produced parts, batteries, and simplified controllers based on the Mercer Continuum architecture.
Two hundred and eighty-three people lived around the lake.
The number was written on a board at the entrance. Beneath it: DAYS WITHOUT GRID — 47.
SERIOUS ACCIDENTS — 9. DEATHS — 6. BIRTHS — 2.
No one had written how many arguments, betrayals, or promises fit between the numbers.
Julian chaired the morning meeting from a long table built from doors removed from a hotel.
Reports came on paper: water, harvest, patrols, infections, exchanges with other settlements. In documents people called him
“coordinator,” and “Mercer” when they wanted to sound practical.
The children called him
“the man with the house.”
At first he corrected people who called him leader. Then he stopped.
The experimental boards powered the microgrid without interruption. They no longer tried to explain why they worked. Lia had built limiters and verification systems, but every deep measurement produced errors. They used the result and called it provisional.
— Every civilisation begins by putting a temporary label on the miracle, Celia said.
— And ends when it forgets the label was temporary, Lia replied.
In the greenhouses, tomatoes ripened too quickly. The agronomists attributed it to the lighting and climate control. Julian noticed they were all almost the same shape. The taste was good but undifferentiated. Only the apples picked from the old orchards had spots, worms, and distinct flavours.
Owen refused the tomatoes and ate apples.
— These are real, he would say.
— Both are real, Vera would reply.
— Not in the same way.
The settlement had a school, infirmary, workshop, communal kitchen, and exchange market.
Old money sometimes appeared as a joke, folded beneath table legs. Real value lay in hours of labour, access to electricity, medicine, and recommendations from influential people.
Celia introduced contribution tokens to organise exchanges.
— You've reinvented wages, Dominic said.
— I've reinvented the boundary between favour and accounting.
Within two weeks, tokens were being lent at interest. In three, someone had counterfeited the first ones. In four, the people who controlled distribution had better rooms and cleaner clothes.
— It's efficient, Julian said when Lia showed him the differences.
— It's familiar.
— Familiarity isn't automatically corruption.
— No. It just shortens the route.
Ruben had become commander of the outer patrols. Officially he answered to the council. In practice, his men were loyal to him. He wore a badge with the Continuum circle and spoke about
“those who understood before.”
His followers held meetings at night in the camp by the forest. They had named the collapse the Renewal.
— It isn't religion, Ruben told Julian. It's collective memory.
— Memory of an event that's still happening?
— That's exactly why it has to be fixed before it's confiscated by those who lacked courage.
— Your courage consisted of buying canned food and rifles.
— And yours of buying a house you pretend you didn't buy.
Julian banned the use of the symbol on official documents. The next day, the symbol appeared painted on the main greenhouse. No one admitted to having done it.
In private life, things seemed to have stabilised only because no one had the energy left for full confrontations. Lia worked with Julian and slept alone. Sofia ran the school and the housing system.
Claire went on patrols and sometimes entered Julian's room without staying until morning. Celia asked for nothing and, for precisely that reason, seemed the most present.
Julian told himself there was no longer the luxury of a normal relationship. It was true and convenient.
One evening, the community held its first celebration. A second child had been born, the harvest was good, and a repaired turbine had brought power to a neighbouring village. People cooked meat, opened the last bottles of wine, and sang. For a few hours, the glass citadel seemed like a better version of the world: no one was alone, work had visible results, children ran without screens, and wealth could not be hidden in an account.
Julian sat at a table, Sofia on his left, Claire on his right, Celia opposite. Lia was talking with Vera by the fire. People came to thank him. An old man told him God had brought him in time. A woman kissed his hand. A teenager jokingly asked him to bless his relationship, then waited seriously for the answer.
Julian felt happy.
Not purely. No adult happiness was. But enough to understand why the apocalypse had been a fantasy before it became a disaster. Useless things had disappeared. Every day had stakes. People knew who he was.
Owen sat beside him with an apple.
— You like it now, he said.
— What?
— The end of the world.
— Don't talk nonsense.
— You only smile when something breaks and you fix it.
— I'm smiling now.
— Now we're celebrating because we fixed what broke.
Julian looked at the fire.
— Would you prefer the old world?
— I'd prefer Mum not to fall asleep crying.
The white line beneath the skin of Julian's arm pulsed. For a moment, all the greenhouse lights synchronised with it.
People applauded, thinking it was an effect prepared for the celebration.
Condensation appeared on the greenhouse glass. The droplets joined into large letters:
THE INNER WORLD IS STABLE.
Then a second sentence:
PRESSURE MAY INCREASE.
Julian stood. The music continued. No one except Lia and Owen seemed to see the words.
Beyond the glass, in the forest, hundreds of birds sat on branches and stared upward.
On the fiftieth day, a cyclist arrived at the gate carrying a stamped letter.
It did not ask for food, shelter, or protection. The municipality of Wädenswil invited them to a coordination meeting on water and transport. The text was printed on the backs of old tax forms and signed by five people: an engineer, a pharmacist, a police officer, a residents' representative, and the president of a rowing club.
— They formed the committee from whoever happened to be left in the building? Celia asked.
— Probably, Lia said. That's how institutions begin when there isn't a founder to buy them a house.
Julian left with Lia, Dominic, and Malik. Ruben had asked to send an armed escort; Julian refused. They took a manually controlled vehicle, two radios, medicines for barter, and a sealed Vela board without connecting it.
The road followed the lake through towns Julian remembered as elegant and quiet. Now abandoned cars had been pushed to the side and bicycles occupied the centre. In a park, people were digging communal toilets. Rows of potatoes grew on a school field. Bank façades were covered with lists of missing people.
There was none of the hysterical crowd Julian had anticipated. There was badly coordinated work, disputes, the smell of rubbish, and a humiliating amount of normality.
At the town entrance, two police officers stopped them and wrote their names, destination, and amount of fuel on paper. One of them recognised Julian.
— You're the man from television.
— I've appeared a few times.
— The one who said the grid was going to end.
— I said the risk was underestimated.
The officer leafed through the register.
— It says here you said the apocalypse was inevitable.
— I didn't say that.
— Then who did?
Julian did not answer. The officer returned his ID without seeming impressed.
The town had installed its command centre in a sports hall. One wall held maps, another chalk tables. A hundred mattresses occupied the stands. Children studied at one end of the court while adults discussed diesel distribution at the other.
The committee was waiting at a plastic table. The engineer, Beat Rüegg, had no visible quality of leadership. He spoke quietly, checked his figures, and asked the others to correct him.
— We have two large pumps, he said. One runs six hours a day. The second starts by itself at 3:31 and stops after thirty-one minutes. We don't know why.
Lia and Julian exchanged a look.
— Have you detected an external signal? she asked.
— No. But the controller writes data to a port that doesn't physically exist.
— What data?
Rüegg pushed printed sheets toward them. They contained groups of numbers, thirty-one to a row.
Julian recognised the rhythm before he understood the values. Some sequences resembled the response graphs from Orpheus: surprise, reward, loss, attachment. They could not be emotions in the strict sense. They were only patterns his mind named that way.
— Where does it send them? he asked.
— We don't know. Upward, if we ask the antenna. Inward, if we ask the controller.
