The pharmacist, a woman named Cemile Aksoy, led them to the improvised clinic. The refrigerators ran on an old generator. Three dialysis patients had been transported to Zürich; two had died on the way. An incubator received power through cables run from a bakery.
— I heard you have a stable source, Aksoy said.
— Experimental.
— Here even death is experimental. It still produces consistent results.
Julian explained the risk of informational contamination. She listened without irony.
— How many people have died at your place because of the source?
— We don't know whether it was because of it.
— How many have lived?
— Almost three hundred.
— Then bring us one. We'll guard it, measure it together, and shut it down if it causes harm.
— It doesn't always shut down when we want it to.
— Neither does a city.
At noon, they ate lentil soup in the communal canteen. Everyone received the same portion, including committee members. Beat Rüegg waited in line and was scolded by a woman for not washing his cup.
Dominic looked at Julian.
— See? Civilisation has ended here too. Terrible.
— They haven't been through what we have.
— Or they went through it without making a mythology out of it.
Julian saw problems everywhere: weak security, records easy to falsify, wasted fuel, unqualified people voting on technical decisions. He also saw something that irritated him more. The system worked without a centre. No person was indispensable long enough for the town to confuse that person's survival with destiny.
Before leaving, Lia asked for access to the local hospital archive. She was looking for neurological cases from the night of the collapse. The digital archive was corrupted, but paper records remained.
An elderly nurse heard his name and stopped.
— Julian Mercer?
— Yes.
— You were brought here.
— When?
— In 2017. After the tunnel accident.
Julian felt the white cold of the room he did not remember.
— I never had an accident here.
The woman led him to a metal cabinet. The file bore the initial V, the date 19 November 2017, and the number 31. Inside, the medical pages were blank except for a single handwritten note:
PATIENT REFUSES TO SAY WHO REMAINED IN THE VEHICLE.
— What tunnel? Lia asked.
The nurse closed her eyes, trying to remember.
— I don't know anymore. Train, car, facility. There were several. Or perhaps only him.
— Who brought him in?
— A female doctor.
— What was her name?
The woman pointed to the signature at the bottom.
SAMIRA KESSLER.
Julian took the page from the file.
— We're taking this.
— No, the nurse said. The archive belongs to the town.
— It's about me.
— Which is exactly why it shouldn't belong only to you.
The answer angered him through its simplicity. In his house, documents about him inevitably ended up in his hands. Here, a woman without a weapon and without an important position denied him access on an administrative principle.
Lia had already photographed the page with an analogue camera.
On the way back, Dominic asked Julian to stop near a bridge. Below them, volunteers were repairing a pipe. They wore no uniforms. No one was filming them. A woman held a torch, a child carried bolts, and a man with one arm in a cast read instructions from a wet manual.
— That's the world beyond the fence, Dominic said. It isn't waiting for us to save it. It could just use some help.
— You don't know what the board can do in a larger network.
— Neither do you. But you know what its power does to you in a small one.
At the property, people greeted them like an expedition returning from a dead continent. Ruben had organised a cordon. Celia demanded the report before Julian got out of the vehicle.
He described the food shortages, the dead, the unstable pumps, and the security risks. He did not mention the orderly queue, the committee, the school, Rüegg's cup, or the pipe repaired without a leader.
— They need us, he concluded.
— They need boards, Lia corrected. That's different.
That evening, Julian hid the photographed page in the technical room. When he took it out of the camera, the text was no longer the same.
Instead of PATIENT REFUSES TO SAY WHO REMAINED IN THE VEHICLE, it read:
PATIENT REFUSES A WORLD IN WHICH HE IS NOT NECESSARY.
Lia looked over his shoulder.
— I photographed the first sentence, she said.
— I know.
— Which one do we keep?
Julian put the photograph in an envelope.
— Both.
But he included neither in the official report.
* * * At first, one token was worth one hour of work.
Then someone noticed that an hour in the workshop was not equal to an hour in the kitchen, that a night shift was worth more than one at the school, and that a good electrician could save hundreds of other people's hours. Celia introduced coefficients. A doctor received three tokens per hour, an unskilled worker one, a coordinator two and a half.
— Why does Julian receive tokens? Nadiya asked. The house and food already belong to him.
— They don't belong to me, Julian said.
No one laughed, but several faces almost did.
Tokens could buy coffee, personal batteries, extra space, salvaged clothes, time in the workshop. Before long, people began keeping them instead of using them. Then lending them.
Henrik Braun, a twenty-four-year-old engineer, created
“harvest certificates”: pay five tokens now and receive seven after the next tomato harvest. The argument was simple. The greenhouses produced more each week. Energy was stable. Work invested today created abundance tomorrow.
Within three days, the certificates traded between people for ten tokens. Within five, Henrik issued certificates for potatoes, apples, energy, and
“future housing capacity.”
People who did not want to clean, repair, or patrol had discovered a more pleasant activity: predicting other people's work.
— It's a get-rich-quick fantasy in a community where there are no shops, Sofia said.
— It's an investment mechanism, Celia replied.
— Investing in what?
— Expectations.
— You can't eat expectations.
— You couldn't eat old money either. It still organised who got to eat.
Julian did not ban the market. He saw it as a sign of normalisation. Prices could communicate shortages faster than the council. People needed the freedom to take risks.
Lia showed him the register.
— There are four times as many promises of tomatoes as the greenhouses can produce.
— Then the price will fall.
— Or people will believe they're rich until harvest day.
— We can't protect everyone from stupidity.
— Interesting sentence from a man who received three hundred million from a contract he doesn't understand.
Julian approved a cap anyway. Henrik circumvented it by splitting issues among friends. Ruben encouraged the market in his camp and accepted certificates as collateral for weapons, patrol positions, and access to fuel.
Then the greenhouses reported a record harvest.
In a single night, the monitoring system added thirty-one tonnes of produce that did not physically exist. No one checked before the information circulated. The certificates doubled in value.
People sold future hours of labour, rations, and even rights to rooms in order to buy them.
By morning, the figures had disappeared.
That same day, a white fungus appeared on the tomato roots. Half the plants had to be burned.
The market collapsed before noon. People ran between the greenhouses and the market carrying scraps of paper and demanding conversion. Henrik had locked himself in his room. Those who had lent him tokens dragged him out through the window.
Julian arrived while the crowd was beating him.
— Stop!
They did not listen the first time.
Ruben fired into the air. The people scattered. Henrik remained on the ground with blood at his ear and his neck at the wrong angle.
Vera bent over him, then looked up.
— He's dead.
On the ground, harvest certificates mixed with mud.
Julian called an emergency meeting. He cancelled all debts, froze the tokens, and confiscated private stocks purchased through certificates.
— You can't cancel contracts after you lose, a man protested.
— I can when the contracts threaten communal food.
— Then we don't have a currency. We have your permission.
— We have an emergency.
— You became rich from an impossible contract and now you're telling us our promises are fictitious.
Julian felt the blow. He did not answer.
Celia helped him reconstruct the inventory. After midnight, she found him staring at the certificate Henrik had issued for “future housing capacity.”
— You thought people would learn from the old world, she said.
— They recreated it in seven weeks.
— No. They recreated the part that promised they could escape other people's work.
— And we let them.
— Because you need your success to be a general law too, not an embarrassing accident.
A line appeared on the inventory screen:
REWARDS SCALE FASTER THAN RESOURCES.
Then:
THIS HAS ALREADY BEEN DEMONSTRATED.
Julian typed: HOW MANY TIMES?
The answer:
IN EVERY WORLD IN WHICH YOU WON.
CHAPTER IX
THOSE BEYOND THE FENCE
On day fifty-three, a government delegation arrived at the gate.
It did not come with armoured vehicles and its members did not wear full uniforms. Four vehicles, two white flags, and a radio transmitting the old civil-protection code. The leader was named Sabine Keller and had been the regional coordinator for critical infrastructure.
— Is the federal administration functioning? Julian asked.
— Fragments of it, Keller replied. There's a centre in Bern, one in Lausanne, and a network of cantons arguing over whether they're still the same country.
— Why haven't we heard the broadcasts?
— Because every digital transmitter is contaminated. We use analogue relays and couriers.
Keller brought maps. It was not the end of the world, at least not uniformly. Some cities had electricity for a few hours a day. Major hospitals were functioning. The army maintained corridors.
Other regions had collapsed into violence or been abandoned after fires. Borders existed on paper and at the points where someone had enough fuel to defend them.
— We need your boards, Keller said. Three hospitals and two water stations can be stabilised within days.
— They're not stable products, Lia replied.
— They're more stable than people dying without them.
— The system can inject commands and influence perception, Julian said.
Keller looked at him without laughing.
— Then we analyse it in a joint laboratory.
— We don't know whether it can be moved.
— You've powered almost three hundred people here. We can power thirty thousand.
In council, Lia immediately supported cooperation. Vera did too. Sofia proposed sending a team with two boards, but not the complete schematics. Celia warned that the government could confiscate the technology. Ruben called the delegation “the corpse asking to be put back on the throne.”
— People survived without you, he said. Now you come to take the only thing that works.
— People survived without you too, Keller replied.
— We knew.
— You guessed one disaster and use it as a certificate of competence for everything.
Julian noticed how quickly Ruben's people tensed. Keller had no instinct for feeding the myth.
That night, Julian spoke with Lia in the main greenhouse.
— If we give them the technology, we lose control over how it's used, he said.
— If we don't, people die so you can keep control of the risk.
— It's not that simple.
— No. It's simpler. You built your whole career around the idea that critical systems must not depend on one man. Now you're that man and you've discovered the exception.
— The board isn't normal technology.
— Your power isn't normal anymore either.
Julian looked through the glass. People moved between buildings with lamps. At the gate, the patrol saluted when he passed. In the world Keller described, he would have been one specialist among many. Here, every circuit bore his name, every decision reached his table.
He did not want to be king of a ruin. The formulation was too simple and too exact.
— We send one board, he said. No schematics and no reserve material. One of our teams operates it.
— Two hospitals get nothing.
— It's a test.
— It's a dose of power small enough that you can call the result back.
The next day, the convoy left with Lia, two engineers, and one board. Julian stayed. He said he had to coordinate the settlement.
Seven kilometres away, the convoy was attacked.
An explosion overturned the first vehicle. Keller was wounded. One of the engineers died. The attackers were masked and disappeared without taking the board. Lia noticed that the detonator used came from the batch Ruben had brought.
— That doesn't prove it was him, Julian said when they returned.
— It proves someone inside wanted to stop the transfer.
— If I arrest him without evidence, his camp revolts.
— Then investigate.
Celia showed him the patrol reports. The recordings from the previous night had been deleted with Julian's authorisation.
— I didn't authorise it.
— The system did.
— Then we can't convict anyone.
Lia looked at him.
— Convenient.
The council voted to suspend cooperation pending clarification. Julian abstained, and his abstention was interpreted as prudence. Keller left without the board.
Before getting into the vehicle, she told him:
— There's something the people here should know. The grid didn't go dark everywhere. In some places, it's transmitting a single sequence toward the sky.
— What sequence?
— We don't know. Thirty-one groups, repeated. It looks like an answer.
— To what?
— That's what we were trying to find out together.
The convoy disappeared down the road.
That evening, Ruben announced that the attack demonstrated the danger of the outside world.
People applauded him in the square. Julian did not stop the speech.
Lia found him watching from the window.
— You chose, she said.
— I avoided an internal war.
— You chose to be necessary here instead of useful beyond the fence.
The white line on Julian's arm pulsed.
In the control room, the regional map erased every point outside the property.
One remained, green, at the centre.
* * * Lia tried to leave the following night.
Not alone. Sofia, Samira, and two engineers had loaded a board, copies of the Vela logs, medicines, and three containers of active material into a service vehicle. They had a route to a rendezvous with Sabine Keller's people.
Julian found out because the system sent him the plan thirty-one seconds before the engine started.
The map appeared on the screen, then their names. Beneath them:
ESCAPE FROM THE REWARD CENTRE.
Julian ran toward the garage. The white line in his arm pulsed painfully.
— Stop the engine! he shouted.
Lia was behind the wheel. Sofia beside her. Samira and the engineers sat in the back.
— Move aside, Lia said.
— You're stealing the community's technology.
— The community didn't build it.
— Neither did Keller.
— People in hospitals don't have to build it to need electricity.
Sofia got out.
— Julian, we're not coming back without answers.
— You're taking the risk for everyone.
— You've done that since the first day.
— There are attacks outside. You don't have an escort.
— We have the coordinates and a secure channel.
— There are no secure channels.
Lia opened the door.
— Listen to yourself. Any road away from you becomes dangerous by definition.
Ruben and six armed men entered the garage. They had arrived too quickly to have been called after the alarm.
— Do we have traitors? Ruben asked.
Julian turned toward him.
— Who told you?
— The security system.
— You don't have access.
— Apparently I do now.
Celia appeared behind them, unarmed.
— No one leaves until we find out who changed the access, she said.
Lia laughed.
— Perfect. We close another door for an investigation.
Ruben ordered his men to unload the board. One of the engineers stood in front of the boot. He was struck with a rifle butt. Samira shouted. Sofia tried to intervene.
— Enough! Julian said.
His voice amplified through the garage speakers, far louder than it should have been. Everyone stopped.
— Weapons down. The council decides.
Ruben did not lower his weapon.
— The council doesn't decide whether someone steals our energy source.
— I decide who is arrested.
— Then decide.
People had gathered in the doorways. Children watched from the upper floor. Julian felt the moment demanding a clear form from him: authority or weakness, inside or outside, loyalty or truth.
— Lia, Sofia, and Samira stay inside until the vote. The board goes back to storage. No one gets hurt.
— House arrest, Lia said. Elegant.
— It's temporary.
— All your prisons are provisional.
Konrad entered limping from the side corridor.
— Let them go.
Ruben turned.
— You're not on the council.
— I know who prepared the attack on the convoy.
The room tensed.
— Say it, Julian demanded.
Konrad pointed at Celia.
— She supplied the explosives. Ruben supplied the men.
Celia did not deny it immediately.
— The sabotage was supposed to stop the transfer, she said. No one was supposed to die.
Lia looked at her with revulsion.
— You killed an engineer for control of a technology you don't even understand.
— I prevented the distribution of a channel that can rewrite infrastructure and perceptions.
— And kept the channel under the leadership of one man.
— One man is easier to observe.
Julian felt every gaze shift toward him.
— Ruben, put the gun down, he said.
Ruben smiled.
— She paid. I defended the citadel. Now the traitors want to take its heart out. Who do you punish?
Konrad stepped toward Ruben's weapon.
The gunshot was short and almost without echo. Konrad fell. Blood spread across the concrete.
For one second, no one moved.
Then the garage erupted. Dominic tackled Ruben to the ground. People fought for the weapons.
Sofia bent beside Konrad. Samira pressed the wound, but the bullet had entered his chest.
Konrad grabbed Julian's sleeve.
— It wasn't the child, he whispered.
— Who stayed outside?
— You.
He died before he could explain.
The council met at sunrise. Ruben was tied up in a room. Celia remained free because no one had decided who had the authority to detain her. Lia, Sofia, and Samira were under guard “for their safety.”
The evidence was incomplete. Celia admitted supplying explosives for a controlled demonstration. Ruben claimed his men had acted on their own and the death had been an accident. The logs had been rewritten with Julian's signature.
Julian proposed expelling Ruben and suspending Celia from the council.
— Expulsion is death, Vera said.
— He killed a man.
— And Celia?
— She didn't pull the trigger.
Lia looked at him from the end of the table.
— Is that your moral boundary? The finger on the trigger?
— We need her for supplies and the network.
— Then tell the truth. Some people are too useful to be guilty.
The council voted. Ruben was sentenced to guarded labour, not expulsion. Celia lost her formal position but kept technical access. Lia and the others were released with a ban on taking boards out without approval.
No one was satisfied. Again, Julian called it balance.
The following night, Sofia found him alone beside the gate.
— Did I betray you? she asked.
— You tried to leave with something everyone depends on.
— That's not what I asked.
Julian looked at the metal behind which Ruben's man had died.
— Yes.
Sofia nodded.
— Then maybe it's the only thing you've deserved from us.
She left.
A message appeared on the gate panel:
BETRAYAL PRODUCES MORE STABLE IDENTIFIERS THAN LOYALTY.
Julian erased it.
The message reappeared on the inside of his eyelids when he closed his eyes.
* * * The birds fell before the rain began.
Hundreds of small bodies detached from the trees almost simultaneously. Some were still alive on the ground, moving their wings without trying to fly. Owen found one near the greenhouse and took it to Eve. The bird had no wounds. Its pupils were dilated until its eyes looked completely black.
— They've been looking up for days, the boy said.
The sky had turned metallic grey, without distinct clouds. The air smelled of smoke, although the regional fires were tens of kilometres away. Lia measured particulates, radiation, and electromagnetic field. The readings were elevated but not enough to explain what they saw.
The first drop fell on the greenhouse glass and spread like ink.
The second left a white mark in its centre.
In less than a minute, the glass was covered in black water. Day became night. Sirens started.
People outside ran for shelter.
— Close the ventilation, Julian ordered. Everyone indoors. Masks and capes for patrols.
On the radio, the surrounding settlements reported the same rain. One said the liquid was acidic. Another that it did not wet clothes. A man insisted the drops were rising from the ground toward the sky.
At the citadel, the rain burned skin after a few seconds and left a fine powder smelling of burnt orange.
A patrol had been caught beyond the outer gate. Julian could see them running through camera views covered in black streaks. Behind them came people from the forest camp, more than a hundred, seeking shelter.
— Open both fences, Sofia said.
— If they all come in, we can't control contamination, Celia replied.
— They'll die outside.
— Maybe they bring in whatever is killing them.
Julian looked at the gate. He remembered the man left in the crowd, the red door, Owen's words.
— Open the service area and the barn, he said. Separate corridor. No one enters the main house without decontamination.
The gates opened. People came in waves, slipping in the black mud. Some had burns on their faces. One child had been wrapped in plastic sheeting. The patrol arrived last.
As the gate began to close, Julian saw a woman alone on the road.
She wore a summer dress and no rain cape. The rain did not seem to touch her. She raised her face toward the camera.
It was his mother.
— Stop! Julian shouted.
The gate stopped.
— What is it? Dominic asked.
— There's someone else.
The camera showed an empty road.
Julian left the control room and ran toward the gate. Lia caught his arm.
— There's no one there.
— I can see her.
Through the black rain, his mother was approaching. She smiled as she had on the call from Valencia.
— Julian, she said. I came the day after tomorrow.
Her voice passed through metal and masks.
— My mother is in Spain.
— In which version?
Lia was holding him.
— Don't open it.
The woman raised her hand. In her palm was the card with 31.
— You can't buy outside, she said. But you can let outside come in.
Other silhouettes appeared behind her: the man from the station, Konrad, Henrik, the little girl in the yellow sweater. Then people Julian did not know. All stood motionless in the rain.
— Do you see them? he asked.
— I see an empty road, Lia said.
Owen had appeared beside them with a mask too large for his face.
— I see shadows, he said. But they aren't the people.
— How do you know?
— People don't wait that neatly.
Julian took a step toward the gate button. The white line in his arm burned.
The girl in the rain put her hand on the metal.
— Dad.
Sofia had come up behind him. When she heard the word, she doubled over as if struck.
— Open it, she whispered.
Lia turned toward her.
— You can't see anything.
— I can hear her.
— The system is using what we want.
— What if it is her?
— That's exactly what it wants you to ask.
Julian pressed the button.
The gate opened twenty centimetres. The rain entered as black mist. A security guard collapsed, clutching his throat. Dominic pulled the manual lever and closed the gate.
The silhouettes vanished.
The guard had a white burn from collarbone to ear, identical to the line in Julian's arm. Vera took him to the infirmary. Over the next hour, he began speaking in different voices and answering questions no one had asked.
— Where is the part? he kept saying.
— What part? Samira asked.
— The one that hurts when the copy is happy.
In the chaos, Ruben disappeared from the detention room. Two guards were found unconscious, uninjured. The armourer was missing. Four rifles and ammunition had been taken from storage.
The rain continued for thirty-one hours.
The greenhouses lost a third of their surface area. Collected water became unusable. Leaves blackened and the glass remained opaque. Two people died from burns. The contaminated guard disappeared from the infirmary on the final night; the door had been locked from the inside.
When the sky cleared, the world had a different colour. The ground was covered in white ash.
The lake looked metallic. Not a single bird could be heard.
On the greenhouse roof, the rain had written enormous letters visible from the mountain:
PRESSURE PRODUCED MORE REAL RESPONSES.
Beneath them, someone had painted the Continuum symbol in blood.
CHAPTER X
THE CELEBRATION OF THE END
After the rain, people no longer asked for a cause. They asked for meaning.
There had been too many causes and they cancelled one another out: industrial smoke, biological weapon, nanomaterial, radiation, Vela signal, punishment, test. Meaning could be only one thing and, for that very reason, was easier to defend.
Ruben returned on the third day.
He did not come with weapons raised. He entered the square at noon, accompanied by twenty people covered in white ash. On his chest he wore the Continuum symbol, and on his arm he had a glowing line resembling Julian's.
— The rain chose us, he said.
People gathered before the patrols could stop him.
— You're under arrest, Julian said.
— Your arrest dissolved when the sky came through the walls.
— You killed a man.
— And you closed the gate. The difference is that I don't call my choice procedure.
Ruben raised his arm. The white line pulsed. The greenhouse lights responded.
The crowd murmured.
— The system contaminated him, Lia said.
— Or it's using him, Samira said.
— It's the same thing to those who follow him, Celia replied.
Julian ordered him detained. Four guards approached and stopped when Ruben's people stepped between them. They were no longer only members of the old camp. Dozens of citadel residents now wore the open circle.
— If you force it, you have a civil war, Dominic said quietly.
Julian postponed the arrest until the council meeting. Ruben announced that that evening they would celebrate the Renewal and those who had died in the rain.
— It isn't authorised, Julian said.
— Then come and stop the joy, Ruben replied.
The celebration began at sunset.
People brought the last bottles of alcohol, meat saved for winter, and clothes from the old world. They burned banknotes in barrels. One man threw the deed to his house into the fire. A woman broke her bank cards and handed the pieces to children as toys. Music came from speakers powered by Mercer boards.
— They're consuming energy and food, Celia said.
— If we intervene, they'll accuse us of keeping abundance for the council, Julian replied.
— You could shut down the boards.
— And the infirmary?
— No. Just the square.
Julian did not do it.
He said people needed one night. The truth was that he was afraid to find out whether his order still had power.
Ruben spoke from the greenhouse steps. He did not preach about gods. He preached about instinct.
— The old world told us to postpone everything we feel, he said. To work for numbers, to love with permission, to eat according to labels, to defend ourselves only when the law arrives. Then their reason switched off the light. We survived because we listened to what was older.
The crowd applauded.
— Fear brought us here. Desire kept us alive. Anger defended us. Don't be ashamed of what won.
People drank. Old couples separated for a night. Others formed in full view. A man challenged a guard to fight over an insult and the crowd made a circle. Women and men offered intimacy, protection, remaining tokens, and promises. It was not freedom, but resembled it closely enough for those who no longer wanted consequences before morning.
Claire found Julian beside the barrel of burning money.
— This is the world you wanted, she said.
— No.
— No bosses, no prices, no past. People want you for what you are now.
— People want the person who controls the power.
— And you wanted those to be the same thing.
She kissed him in front of Sofia and Lia. Julian pushed her away gently, but too late for the gesture to look like refusal.
Sofia left. Lia remained and watched him.
— Why are you here? Julian asked.
— To see whether the system increases intensity when the crowd abandons self-control.
— And?
She showed him a portable instrument. Activity in the boards had increased thirtyfold without any additional load.
— Is it feeding? he asked.
— Or measuring. Or learning. But every raw reaction produces a clearer signal.
On the stage, Ruben shouted:
— What did we fight against?
Someone answered:
— Fear!
Another:
— Lies!
A drunk man raised a board taken from the school. He had written on it:
THEY FOUGHT REASON AND THEY WON.
The crowd laughed and applauded. The sentence became a chant, first as a joke, then as a slogan.
Julian felt the line in his arm pulse in rhythm.
Owen slipped through the crowd, gathering books thrown into the mud on their way to the fire.
He rescued a mathematics textbook, an atlas, and a novel without a cover.
— Leave them, a man told him. That world is over.
— Then why are you afraid of books? Owen asked.
The man snatched the atlas and threw it into the fire.
Julian started toward them, but Ruben stopped him from the stage with a look.
— Let them choose, he said into the microphone.
The entire square turned toward Julian.
He was still the leader if he could stop the celebration. He was still the saviour if he accepted the crowd's desire. The two roles no longer fit together.
— One night, Julian said. At sunrise it ends.
The crowd erupted in cheers.
Lia's instrument recorded a spike.
At 3:31, the boards cut power to the infirmary for thirty-one seconds and directed all energy to the square.
In the medical darkness, the woman dependent on oxygen died.
In the square, no one noticed that the music had grown louder.
* * * By morning, the ash in the square had mixed with vomit, burned banknotes, and broken glass.
People avoided looking at one another. The previous night's freedom had become a collection of private explanations.
The dead woman was named Margarete. She was sixty-eight and had raised two sons alone.
Her daughter had kept her alive with the oxygen concentrator from the first night. When the power failed, no one in the infirmary had been able to start the manual generator quickly enough.
Julian went to speak with the daughter.
— The system redirected the power without authorisation, he said.
The woman sat beside her mother's body and combed her hair.
— You authorised the celebration.
— I didn't know that...
— No. You only knew what it suited you to call risk.
She was not crying. Every movement of the comb was careful. Julian noticed the white strands, the thin skin at her temple, a tea stain on the sheet. The details struck him with a clarity the world had not had for weeks.
The room seemed more real than the house, than the rain, than the millions. Not because it was painful, but because it had no place prepared for him.
He left without saying anything else.
The council suspended his authority over energy pending investigation. Dominic took over operational coordination. Julian retained only the technical infrastructure, under Lia's supervision.
— It's temporary, Dominic said.
Julian almost laughed.
— Of course.
For the first time since the blackout, no one had an immediate need for a decision from him. He discovered that silence was not rest. It was withdrawal.
He walked through the settlement. People repaired glass, burned contaminated plants, washed away ash. When they saw him, some greeted him, others turned their heads. He no longer interpreted every reaction in his favour. The world was regaining friction.
Claire stopped him near the armoury.
— I'm not coming to you anymore, she said.
— I didn't ask you to.
— I know. That was always the part that saved you from responsibility.
— You made your own choices.
— Yes. And now I'm making one that doesn't confirm you.
She left before he could answer. Julian felt a small pain and, beneath it, relief. Claire was becoming harder to reduce to the role she had occupied. That was precisely why she seemed more alive.
Sofia was preparing a convoy to contact the regional network again. She did not ask his permission.
— Are you coming? he asked.
— No.
— Why?
— Because then the journey would become about you again.
— And Ada?
Sofia closed a box of files.
— If she existed, I want to look for her somewhere that doesn't receive rewards when I say her name.
— Maybe there's nothing outside.
— Then I'll find the nothing without you administering it for me.
Lia was working in the laboratory. She had discovered that during the rain the system had recorded packets unlike any local telemetry. They contained sensory data: pressure, temperature, chemical concentrations, nerve impulses.
— From where? Julian asked.
— I don't know. But they're noisy. They don't repeat perfectly, they don't align with desires, and they contain errors that serve no story.
— So they're real?
— They're more expensive to generate.
On the screen, Lia reconstructed a few seconds of audio signal. They heard medical equipment, footsteps, and a distant voice:
— The reward layer is saturated. Introduce unaligned material.
Another voice answered:
— It increases the risk of rejection.
— We need an identifier that doesn't come from preference.
Julian stopped playback.
— This could be produced for us.
— Everything could. The question is why these fragments are less elegant.
Samira had examined his arm. The white line had widened and was climbing toward his shoulder. Under the microscope, it was not scar, pigment, or implant. The light seemed to come from the tissue without warming it.
— I want to make a small incision, she said.
— What if it's a channel?
— Then perhaps we find out whether it bleeds.
She gave him local anaesthetic. Lia held an analogue camera. Dominic stood at the door.
Samira cut along the line.
No blood came out.
Beneath the skin was a transparent filament, thinner than a nerve, continuing in both directions.
It did not reflect light. It looked like an absence with edges.
Samira tried to touch it with forceps.
At that instant, Julian was no longer in the infirmary.
He was in a small, cold room with stained walls. Not the white room. A real window let in winter light. Sofia sat on the floor holding a sick little girl. She was not beautiful in the way the simulation made people beautiful. She had dark circles, cracked lips, and a broken fingernail.
— We don't have any more time, she was saying.
The Julian in the memory held a black device.
— I can preserve one version.
— I don't want a version that loves you more easily.
— She won't know the difference.
Sofia looked at him with complete hatred.
— You will.
The little girl opened her eyes.
— Dad, I'm cold.
The scene disappeared.
Julian came to screaming. Samira had withdrawn the forceps. The incision was now bleeding normally. The filament was gone.
— What did you see? Lia asked.
Julian told them. The words came out with difficulty, without their usual order.
— It sounds like a memory, Samira said.
— It sounds like something constructed to feel more real than everything else.
Owen was standing in the doorway again, though he was not allowed to be there.
— True things aren't the ones that hurt more, he said. They're the ones that don't change to please you.
Julian looked at him.
— How do you know?
— Mum stays tired even when I wish she wasn't.
In the distance, the improvised gate bells began to ring.
Sofia was leaving for the outside.
Julian stood to stop her, then remained on the bed.
For the first time, he let her go out without closing the gate behind her.
* * * Three days after the celebration, the school resumed classes.
Sofia had left with the convoy, and her place had temporarily been taken by Mireille Dutoit, the retired teacher who had inventoried medicines on the first night. Mireille was calm, practical, and so grateful for restored order that she did not notice when gratitude became doctrine.
In the classroom arranged in the small greenhouse, thirty-two children sat at tables built from pallets. On the wall, beneath the alphabet, someone had posted a chronology:
THE OLD WORLD — CONFUSION — THE FALL — THE RENEWAL — THE CITADEL.
Julian looked at it before entering.
— Who made that?
— The children, Mireille said.
The letters were drawn too uniformly for children.
— What does Confusion mean?
— The period when people tried to solve everything through systems they didn't understand.
— And the Renewal?
— When we returned to essentials.
— Who formulated the definitions?
Mireille smiled patiently.
— Julian, we can't teach a catastrophe without a story. Children need order.
— Order doesn't necessarily require a moral.
— Every order is a morality to someone who grows up in it.
That day's lesson was about the causes of the collapse. Mireille invited the children to say what they knew. The answers came quickly.
— People wanted too much electricity.
— The robots lied.
— The government hid the sun.
— The rich people used up all the numbers.
— God turned off the internet because no one looked at each other anymore.
The last answer received approval from several adults at the door.
Owen raised his hand.
— We don't know.
— Of course we don't know completely, Mireille said. But we can understand the meaning.
— If we don't know the cause, the meaning is the story we choose.
— Stories help us live.
— So do lies.
Several children laughed. Mireille asked Owen not to turn every lesson into a trial.
Julian remained at the back of the room. On a side board were copied sentences attributed to him:
“WHEN SYSTEMS FALL, CHARACTER REMAINS.”
“DO NOT FEAR THE END. FEAR REMAINING UNCHANGED.”
He had said neither.
Beneath them was a drawing. A large house, a glowing circle, and a man with his arms extended over people. The face resembled his.
— Who is this? he asked.
A seven-year-old boy answered:
— You, when you knew.
— I didn't know.
The child looked at Mireille, unsure whether Julian was joking.
— But you prepared us.
— I was lucky.
The word seemed to disappoint them.
After the lesson, Mireille led him to the cot of the child born during the blackout. The parents had named the baby Nova. People left apples, tokens, drawings, and notes containing wishes beside the cot.
— It's just a child, Julian said.
— That's precisely why it can represent something pure.
The child's mother heard him.
— It isn't
“just” anything.
It was born when the world chose to continue.
— The world didn't choose.
— You chose for it.
She took Julian's hand and placed it on the baby's forehead. The other parents moved closer.
Someone asked for an analogue photograph. Julian withdrew his hand.
— I don't bless children.
— I didn't say blessing, the mother replied.
But all of them had thought it.
In the courtyard, the children played
“the Fall.”
One was coordinator, the others guards, sick people, and people at the gate. The coordinator distributed stones as rations. Anyone who protested was sent
“outside.”
The most contested role was not the saviour but the person who decided who got in.
A nine-year-old girl, Amélie, refused to leave the game when she lost.
— I know when the rain is coming, she said. If you send me out, you'll all get wet.
The children let her stay inside.
Julian asked how she knew the weather.
— I dream the birds. When they look up, it rains.
— The birds looked up before the black rain too, a boy said. She told us.
— Did you tell them before or after you saw them?
Amélie folded her arms.
— Before in the dream, after outside.
Mireille pulled Julian aside.
— You don't have to destroy her sense of it.
— I'm trying to find out whether she's warning us or inventing it.
— To her there is no difference.
— To people who walk into the rain there is.
That same evening, Amélie disappeared.
Her mother found a drawing of birds and a red door on her bed. The children said the girl had gone to prove she could stop the rain if she reached
“the room that listens to her.”
Owen knew where.
Julian, Dominic, and Vera went down to the workshop. The door to the impossible level was not visible, but the slow breathing came from the wall. Owen knocked three times where the pipes did not match the plan. The metal opened.
Amélie stood in the white corridor a few metres away, her palms pressed over her eyes.
— Don't come, she said. It hasn't given me the weather yet.
— Who? Julian asked.
— Me, when I'm grown up.
Behind her, the red door opened a crack. They heard an adult woman's voice say:
— Let her finish. This is the only version in which she's listened to.
Julian stepped inside. The floor lengthened even though the distance did not change. The line in his arm burned. Owen threw the apple he was holding. The fruit rolled to Amélie and stopped against her foot.
The girl lowered her hands.
— It has a worm, she said.
— Yes, Owen replied. That's why it's from here.
Amélie picked up the apple. The corridor suddenly shortened. Julian grabbed her and pulled her out. The door closed before the voice could speak again.
The girl was unhurt. But a white line, thin as a hair, ran along her arm. By morning it had disappeared.
The community called a meeting. Some accused Julian of maintaining access to the dangerous room. Others accused Mireille of encouraging the girl's dreams. Mireille cried and repeated that she had only wanted to give the children meaning.
Ruben formulated the conclusion fastest.
— It wasn't the dream that put her in danger. Curiosity did. Someone kept opening the door after the world told us to stop.
People approved.
Owen stood on a chair.
— The door spoke with her voice. That means it knew what she wanted.
— Exactly, Ruben said. And we don't have to feed it.
— No. It means we have to find out how it knows.
A man told Eve to control her child.
Julian watched fear changing shape into consensus. It was no longer fear of what lay beyond the door. It was fear of the question that might force them to revise the story.
The next day, the chronology at the school gained another stage:
DOUBT.
The word was placed between CONFUSION and THE FALL.
Beneath it, someone had drawn a red door and closed it with a black line.
CHAPTER XI
THE WAR AGAINST EXPLANATION
Ruben did not ask people to believe in him. He asked them to remember the order of events.
Every time Lia tested the board, messages had appeared. When Samira tried to recover the files, the power went out. When they opened the room, the rain came. When they measured the line in Julian's arm, the signal intensified.
— I'm not saying questions are a sin, Ruben declared in the square. I'm saying some doors answer when you knock. If there's something behind them that hates us, why do we keep knocking?
It was a prudent argument. People received it as a revelation.
Lia presented the data to the council. The correlations existed, but causality might run in reverse: the system produced events precisely to teach them not to examine it.
— Then you're still proposing we examine it, Nadiya said.
— Yes.
— Even if people die?
— If we give up verification, anything that gives us water after obedience becomes a master.
— The water is real, a farmer replied. You can't drink your explanation.
Julian watched the discussion. Months earlier, he would have sided with Lia without hesitation.
Now he saw the inventories, the damaged greenhouses, the frightened people, and the possibility of conflict. He told himself truth required a living community to discover it.
— I propose a two-week suspension of active experiments, he said. We use the system only for energy and water. No questions, no new connections.
Lia looked at him as if she recognised a disease.
— You want the miracle without the audit.
— I want time.
— Your time always points in the direction of power.
The council passed the moratorium nine to three.
That same night, the boards stabilised the power. The lost water reappeared. The greenhouse heating came on without a command. People called it confirmation.
After three days without experiments, the sky turned blue. The first birds returned. A woman with burns began healing faster than the doctors expected. Ruben did not need to invent evidence; the world supplied it for him.
Lia continued working in secret.
Julian found her in the old laboratory with an analogue receiver and a separate board. The screen showed the thirty-one groups transmitted toward the sky.
— You violated the vote, he said.
— Votes don't change the signal.
— People need stability.
— People need to know the stability is conditional on obedience.
— They already know.
— No. They've called it peace.
Lia showed him the decoding. Each group appeared to be a vector of choices: consumption, sexuality, aggression, loyalty, sacrifice, control. The signal transmitted not words but profiles.
— It's exporting our responses, she said. Or comparing them with something outside.
— Can you stop it?
— Maybe. If I cut synchronisation on all the boards for thirty-one seconds.
— And the power?
— It drops.
— The infirmary?
— The manual generators are ready.
— The greenhouses?
— They'll survive.
— And if it triggers another pressure event?
— Then we learn the blackmail was real.
Julian fell silent.
— You won't risk it, Lia said. Not because people might die. You've risked people for less important things. You won't risk the world that still calls you the centre.
Footsteps sounded behind them. Ruben and nearly fifty people entered the building. Someone had informed them.
— Shut the device down, Ruben said.
Lia positioned herself between him and the panel.
— No.
— The council voted.
— The council didn't vote for us to give up our minds.
A man unplugged the receiver. Lia shoved him. A woman overturned the table. Within seconds, the crowd began destroying the instruments. Not with blind fury, but with a solemnity that made the gesture worse. Each device was lifted, displayed, and smashed against the floor. People spoke the names of the dead as they broke the screens.
— Stop! Julian shouted.
Several stopped. Ruben looked at him.
— Order us.
Julian could call the guards loyal to him. He could shut off the power. He could physically step between them. Every option produced violence and forced him to align with Lia against the majority.
— No one hurts anyone, he said.
It was an order without an object.
The crowd continued destroying things.
Samira tried to save the medical archive. She was shoved and struck her head on a bench. Owen came in for the notebooks and was slapped by a man who did not recognise him. Dominic pulled him out.
Lia remained in front of the main board.
— Julian, she said. For once, choose something that doesn't preserve you.
Ruben raised a hammer and looked toward Julian.
Julian gave no signal.
The hammer came down.
The board did not shatter like ordinary material. It contracted into a point of light and disappeared. Every light in the citadel went out.
For thirty-one seconds, people screamed in the darkness.
Then the power came back stronger. The greenhouses lit up, the pumps ran, the radiators warmed. On the laboratory wall, without a screen, text appeared:
THE CONDITION HAS BEEN UNDERSTOOD.
Ruben raised his arms. The crowd applauded.
Over the following days, the moratorium became a ban. Measuring instruments were inventoried. Regional maps, Vela archives, and system logs were locked away. The school kept elementary mathematics but removed lessons on networks and modelling “until stabilisation.”
Someone painted the celebration's slogan on the workshop gate:
THEY FOUGHT REASON AND THEY WON.
Julian passed it every morning.
He did not erase it.
* * * After the destruction of the laboratory, the council ordered the surrender of all technical archives.
Lia's notebooks, Amina's files, analogue photographs, board schematics, and recordings from the gate were collected in crates. Officially, they were to be sealed for two weeks. Ruben demanded they be burned. The majority preferred the word preservation, although no one designated the person who would be allowed to open them again.
Julian signed the order.
That evening, Samira called him to the infirmary. Margarete had been buried, and her bed remained empty. Samira closed the door and took a box of antibiotics from a cupboard.
There were no vials inside. There were thirty-one very thin files.
— You hid documents, Julian said.
— I separated medical data from the objects of a political dispute.
— You hid documents.
— Yes.
Lia stood behind a curtain with a camera and the last sheets of sensitive paper. Owen was under the examination table. No one explained why he was there.
— Does Vera know? Julian asked.
— Vera knows her son refuses to sleep until he finds out why adults burn things they don't understand, Samira said.
The files bore familiar names. LIA WERNER. SOFIA RADU. CELIA LEVANT. TOBIAS
HELLER. SAMIRA KESSLER. ELENA MERCER. ADA MERCER. OWEN ROTT. JULIAN
MERCER.
The same name sometimes appeared on several covers with different dates of birth.
— Where did you get these? Julian asked.
— I found them in the room below after the rain. Or I brought them with me. I remember both things.
— Which is true?
— If I knew, I wouldn't have called you.
In the first file, Lia was a researcher at Helix Meridian and had died in 2024 in a laboratory accident. In the second, she was a logic professor in Lausanne and had never met Julian. In the third, she had signed, as his wife, consent for a Vela procedure.
Lia read without blinking.
— That's my signature, she said.
— Do you remember?
— I remember that I wouldn't have signed. That's not the same thing.
Sofia's files contained three histories. In one, Ada had died at age six. In another, Sofia had had no children. In the third, Ada was alive and Julian had died before she was born.
Celia had been an investor, nurse, prosecutor, and patient. Dominic had died in Zürich station, in exactly the place where the unknown man had blood on his sleeve. Samira had treated the Vela programme, been treated by it, and, in one file, was not a person but the name of a protocol: Adaptive Memory Integrity and Narrative Alignment.
— A.M.I.N.A., Julian read.
Samira smiled without humour.
— I've always hated my initials.
— Maybe your name was constructed from the protocol.
— Or the protocol from my name.
— Does it frighten you?
— It frightens me that the question gives you pleasure.
Owen's file was almost empty. It contained a photograph of a boy in a yellow jacket, seen from behind, in thirty-one places: station, hospital, house, burned field, laboratory, beach, white corridor. In none of the photographs was his face visible.
— Do you remember these places? Julian asked.
Owen crawled out from under the table and looked at the images.
— Some of them.
— Which ones?
— The ones where the apple was rotten.
Julian's file was the thickest. The first page had a field:
ROLE: AUTOBIOGRAPHICAL COHERENCE CENTRE.
Beneath it:
SUBJECT: UNCONFIRMED.
OPERATOR: UNCONFIRMED.
ENVIRONMENT: UNCONFIRMED.
EXTERIOR: UNAUTHORISED.
— It doesn't say you're the subject, Lia observed.
— It doesn't say I'm not.
— It says your role is to keep the story bound together.
— Maybe that's the definition of a person.
— Or of a narrator.
Julian leafed through the pages. Each version offered him a different life, but the same impulses returned: he won too quickly, was chosen without sufficient reason, women became mirrors of his value, a catastrophe made him necessary, and a door separated the community from someone he could sacrifice.
In some worlds, he refused the temptations. Those files ended quickly.
In others, he accepted everything. The files were long, rich in data, and marked HIGH YIELD.
— Is it measuring us? he asked.
— We don't know, Lia said.
— Why do the versions where I'm worse produce more?
— Maybe because a person satisfied with little is a poor environment for testing power.
Owen pulled a sheet from between two covers. The paper was different, thin and grey. It had no name. A list was printed on it:
WEALTH WITHOUT CAUSE.
DESIRE WITHOUT RECIPROCITY.
POWER WITHOUT ACCOUNTABILITY.
LUCK WITHOUT LIMIT.
COMMUNITY WITHOUT AN OUTSIDE.
RENEWAL WITHOUT MEMORY.
ANSWER WITHOUT QUESTION.
There was a checkmark beside each one except the last.
— Is this what they want? Julian asked.
— Or this is what you wanted and they want the rest, Samira said.
At the bottom of the box was an audio cassette. Malik had kept an old tape recorder for communications. They brought it in and listened.
At first there was only breathing. Then Julian's voice, younger:
— Tell me what has to stay outside for the inside to be complete.
A child's voice answered:
— If someone stays outside, it isn't complete.
— It's a necessary boundary.
— For whom?
— For those inside.
— Then you're not saving them. You're using them to draw a circle.
The tape stopped.
Julian rewound it and listened again. The second time, the child's voice was Ada's. The third time, it was Owen's. The fourth time, it was not a child but the stranger from the station.
Lia stopped the tape recorder.
— Don't repeat it again. It changes its content according to what you expect.
— Or our memory changes it.
— For the outcome, it's the same risk.
Footsteps sounded from the square. A patrol was checking the infirmary for undeclared materials. Samira put the files back in the box.
— We have to choose what we keep, she said.
— Everything, Lia replied.
— If they find them, they'll burn them.
Owen took the photograph of the boy from behind. Samira kept the protocol page. Lia hid the film in her coat lining.
Julian held the sheet with the list of desires.
He could leave it in the archive and risk the destruction of everything. He could surrender it as evidence and protect his position. He could hide it.
He folded it and tucked it under the bandage on his arm.
The patrol entered. Ruben was not with them, but his people wore the open circle.
— Medical inventory, their leader said.
Samira showed them the cupboards. When they reached the antibiotic box, Julian intervened.
— It's empty. I checked it.
The man looked at him. Julian's authority had been suspended, but the reflex of obedience remained.
He did not open the box.
After the patrol left, Samira gave Julian a sedative.
— Sleep, she said. You've been awake for two days.
— What if I wake in another version?
— Then check who is asking you to choose quickly.
— And if everyone is asking?
— Look for the person who can bear not to be believed.
Julian lay down on Margarete's empty bed. Beneath the bandage, the paper touched his skin like a cold fever.
The last thing he saw before falling asleep was Owen drawing a circle and deliberately leaving an opening in it.
* * * Julian woke one morning to a functioning network.
His phone vibrated on the bedside table. Messages, news, markets, weather. Outside, trams were running. His apartment in Wiedikon was lit by sunlight and smelled of coffee.
For several seconds, he did not remember the citadel.
Then he looked at his arm. The white line was gone. There was no incision, burn, or dirt. His fingernails were clean.
Lia was asleep beside him.
— Lia.
She opened her eyes and smiled.
— It's early.
— Where are we?
— Home.
— Which home?
— Julian, don't start.
There was a wedding ring on her bedside table. Julian looked at his hand. He wore an identical one.
— Are we married?
Lia sat up abruptly.
— Did you lose it again?
— Lose what?
— Continuity.
She used the word without seeming to notice it. She took his face between her palms.
— Tell me the date.
Julian looked at his phone. 3 September 2029. Three years had passed.
The news showed him. Mercer Continuum had completed a global energy network. Energy prices had fallen. The climate crisis had slowed. Julian had received an international prize and declined two public offices. Photographs showed him beside Lia, Celia, ministers, and children.
There was no mention of a blackout.
— It was an episode, Lia said. After the initial demonstration. You entered a dissociative state and constructed a complete story about collapse.
— How long did it last?
— Thirty-one hours.
— For me it was months.
— I know.
— Sofia?
— She's fine.
— Ada?
Lia hesitated.
— Who is Ada?
Julian got out of bed.
His mother was waiting in the kitchen. She wore a summer dress and was slicing oranges.
When she saw him, she hugged him.
— You've lost weight, she said.
Julian felt her perfume, warmth, the bones of her shoulders. She was so real that he began to cry.
— What happened? she asked.
— I don't know if you're you.
His mother gave him a light slap on the cheek.
— Then at least your hallucination has the right manners.
She laughed. Julian laughed too. The oranges on the table smelled fresh, not burnt.
For an hour, he accepted it.
He showered. Drank coffee. Watched the city. Water ran without rationing. People wore clean clothes and complained about traffic. Every detail was an obscene blessing.
Celia arrived in a car and drove him to Sankt Alban for evaluation. On the way, she explained that the episodes of “extended reality” returned rarely, produced by an old Vela procedure.
— So Vela existed.
— As a medical company. Not as an entity, channel, or god.
— The white room?
— Immersive therapy.
— The person left outside?
— A recurring metaphor of guilt.
— Ada?
Celia looked at him.
— There was no daughter.
— Sofia remembered one.
— The Sofia character in the episode remembered one.
— Is Sofia real?
— Yes. But she wasn't in the story the way she is in life.
At the clinic, Samira was waiting in the same consulting room, without a scar.
— Is memory stable? she asked.
— I don't know which memory.
— That's a healthy answer.
She showed him normal scans, recordings of his body lying in bed for thirty-one hours, Lia beside him, doctors coming and going. Everything had metadata, witnesses, and imperfections. A nurse tripped over a cable. A doctor sneezed. It did not seem produced for him.
— Why did I wake now? Julian asked.
— Because you reached a stable decision in the episode.
— What decision?
Samira opened a form.
— You accepted that the explanation wasn't worth the cost to the community. Your brain found an exit.
— I let people destroy the instruments.
— It was a representation of giving up the compulsion for control.
— A woman died.
— No woman died.
— Konrad?
— He's alive. He works in Basel.
Samira handed him the form.
— Sign and go home. It's only confirmation that you choose to remain in consensual reality and no longer use the Vela interface.
Julian read it. Medical language, clauses, risks. At the end was a blank field:
IDENTIFIER OF THE ELEMENT EXCLUDED FROM RECONSOLIDATION.
— What do I write here?
— The first name that comes to mind.
— To anchor the memory.
— Sofia?
— If that's the name.
— Ada?
Samira kept her expression calm.
— If that's the name.
— My mother?
— Any accepted identifier ends the procedure.
Julian put the pen down.
— I'm not signing.
— Then you risk returning to the episode.
— Maybe this is the episode.
Celia, standing by the window, sighed.
— Julian, you got the world you said you wanted. Clean energy, recognition, Lia, your mother alive. What evidence would be enough?
— A person who isn't trying to convince me.
He left the consulting room.
In the corridor, everyone stopped as he passed. Not obviously. Just enough to clear a path for him. The receptionist smiled before he looked at her. The lift was waiting open.
Outside, every traffic light turned green.
Julian walked. The city was perfect in a tiring way. No one argued. No tram was late. A dog lifted its leg beside a tree and urinated nothing.
At the corner of a bakery, Owen was selling apples.
He wore the yellow jacket.
— You don't exist here, Julian said.
— You don't exist the same way everywhere either.
— Is this the real world?
Owen held out an apple. It had a spot and a wormhole.
— Bite it.
Julian bit. It was sour, fibrous, and unlike any perfect fruit in the citadel.
— What does that mean?
— That they didn't make the apple for you.
In the bakery window, the reflection of the city vanished. In its place was the destroyed laboratory and the slogan on the wall.
— How do I get back? Julian asked.
— Say something that gets you nothing.
Julian thought of his mother, of Lia in bed, of the repaired world. Any refusal could take them away. That was precisely the trap.
— I don't know the name, he said.
— That keeps you important. Try again.
Julian closed his eyes.
— I don't have the right to choose who is real.
The city went dark.
He woke in the citadel, standing before the wall that read THEY FOUGHT REASON AND THEY WON. Dominic was holding his shoulder.
— You were gone for thirty-one seconds, he said.
— I was in another world.
— You stood here and smiled.
In Julian's hand was a bitten apple, sour, with a living worm crawling out of it.
CHAPTER XII
THE QUESTION
The trial was not called a trial.
It was called a “continuity hearing,” because people were still afraid of words that produced clear consequences. It was held in the main greenhouse beneath the glass stained by the rain. Almost the entire community came.
Sofia had returned that morning with Sabine Keller and two observers from the regional network. They brought medicines, seeds, and an evacuation offer for anyone who wanted to leave.
They also brought a condition: the Vela boards and data had to be placed under joint control.
Ruben called the offer annexation. Lia called it the first verifiable exit. Julian no longer had the right to call it anything officially.
Dominic chaired the hearing.
— We're not judging whether Julian is good or bad, he said. We're judging decisions and whether he can still have authority.
— That's a judgement about good and evil using plumbing vocabulary, Celia replied.
— Plumbing vocabulary keeps water away from shit. Sometimes that's enough.
The charges were read: concealing the board and the lower level; incomplete presentation of risk before the vote; closing the gate with a patrol member outside; using resources and office in a relationship with an imbalance of power; authorising the celebration; blocking external cooperation; tolerating Celia's sabotage.
Julian listened and found a defence for each one.
The board had been hidden to avoid panic. The gate had been closed to protect dozens of people. Claire had expressed consent. The celebration had prevented an immediate conflict.
Cooperation had been suspended after a real attack. Celia was too useful to expel in the middle of the crisis.
Every sentence was true.
Together they formed something he did not want to name.
Vera testified first.
— He saved people, she said. He built the system that keeps us alive. But when a decision had a cost, the cost always landed on someone farther from him. When the reward came, it was always close enough for him to feel it.
Ruben laughed from the crowd.
— All leaders do that.
— Then it's an accusation against you too, Vera replied.
Claire came forward without looking at Julian.
— He didn't force me, she said. I knew what I was doing. I wanted protection, status, and to hurt other women who had something I didn't. But the fact that I chose doesn't mean he was free to forget who controlled the doors.
— Did you receive privileges? Dominic asked.
— Not officially. I got access. Sometimes access is privilege before it acquires a name.
Sofia spoke about the closed door, about Ada, and about the way Julian turned her memory into a hypothesis only when it accused him.
— I don't know whether my daughter existed, she said. But I know my pain exists. He treated it as an attack on his version.
— What if the memory is false? someone asked.
— Then someone constructed a pain and I still have to live with it. The falsity of the cause doesn't make the suffering available for mockery.
Celia was less forgiving of everyone.
— Julian was used, she said. By the system, by me, by the community. You asked him to be certain when no one could be. You applauded when he shut risks behind gates and condemned him when you saw who remained outside. He isn't innocent. But you like one guilty person who keeps the rest of the biography clean.
— Does that absolve him? Dominic asked.
— No. It distributes him.
Lia was the last of the adults to stand.
— I don't think Julian created what is happening to us, she said. I don't think he's the only one being manipulated either. But the system quickly learned something about him: when the reward confirmed his self-image, his standard of evidence dropped. When the truth made him smaller, he demanded impossible certainty.
Julian looked at her.
— You stayed too, he said. You used the laboratory, the technology, the house.
— Yes.
— You were with me.
— Yes.
— Then you weren't just a moral observer.
— I didn't say I was. The difference is that I'm not asking to lead because I understood late.
Owen raised his hand. Dominic hesitated, then let him come forward.
— I have a question, the boy said.
— For whom? Dominic asked.
— Mr Julian.
Owen stopped in front of him.
— When the lights went out the first time, were you happy?
Julian felt all his defences retreat toward the edges.
— No.
Owen waited.
In the greenhouse, only water in the pipes could be heard.
Julian remembered the people gathered in the living room, their looks, the way his orders had acquired weight. He remembered the relief that the prophecy had not made him ridiculous. He remembered that before fear for his mother came the satisfaction of having been right.
— Part of me, he said, yes.
Owen nodded.
— Why?
— Because then everyone needed what I knew.
— And now?
Julian looked at the people. Some hated him. Some still admired him. Others just wanted water and quiet.
— Now I don't know what I know.
A white light appeared on the greenhouse wall. Text formed slowly:
CONFESSION MAY FUNCTION AS AN IDENTIFIER.
CONFIRM: HAS THE DESIRE TO BE NECESSARY REMAINED OUTSIDE?
Everyone saw the message.
The crowd stirred. Ruben shouted that it was proof the system recognised Julian. Others demanded that he answer. Keller raised her instruments, even though measurement had been forbidden.
— Don't answer, Lia said.
— Maybe it stops everything, Vera said.
— Maybe that's exactly what it wants, Samira said.
— What happens if I say yes? Julian asked aloud.
The text changed:
THE INNER WORLD MAY BE PRESERVED.
— And if I say no?
PRESSURE WILL CONTINUE UNTIL A STABLE ANSWER.
Ruben approached.
— Say yes. Preserve the citadel.
Sofia said:
— Don't negotiate our existence as though it belongs to you.
Julian looked at the text. Any answer placed him at the centre again. Even refusal could become heroism.
— I confirm nothing, he said.
The light vanished.
The hearing continued.
The verdict passed by thirty-nine votes to twenty-seven: Julian lost the position of coordinator, sole access to infrastructure, and veto power. He could remain, work, and participate in the council like any other member. The boards would be transferred to a joint commission with the regional network.
Ruben and his people left the greenhouse before the end.
Julian sat alone after the crowd had gone. The sheet with the verdict remained on the table. In the lower corner, without anyone writing it, a note appeared in blue ink:
SUBJECT HAS LOST THE CENTRE.
PREPARE THE EXTERIOR TEST.
Above the glass, the sky began to divide into squares.
* * * The sky did not crack. It partitioned.
Almost transparent squares appeared side by side, each containing a different shade of the same afternoon. One was clear. In another, it rained. In a third, the sun was too low. The boundaries were not lines in the sky but differences between versions of light.
People came out of the buildings and looked up.
Keller switched on a measuring instrument. Ruben snatched the sensor away from her.
— That's what brought it, he said.
— The phenomenon began before I switched it on, she replied.
— You always have an order of events in which you're innocent.
Above the mountain, one square turned black. Not like night. Like a surface without distance.
The birds that had returned a few days earlier rose in a flock and disappeared when they passed through it. They did not fall. They simply did not continue on the other side.
Amateur-radio operators reported incompatible things. In Bern, the sky was normal, but people saw two moons in reflections. In Lausanne, buildings had extra rooms. In the south, a motorway returned to the same point regardless of direction.
— It isn't local, Lia said.
— Nor global, Keller replied. It's selective.
— Selective according to what?
At that instant, everyone in the citadel heard their own name.
Not through speakers. From behind them.
Some turned and saw people they loved. Others saw themselves younger, rich, healthy, innocent. A woman ran toward her dead son and passed through the greenhouse wall. A man followed his own version into a corridor that had not existed and vanished around a corner.
Panic broke out. Not one panic, but hundreds of private reactions. Each person was being called by a different world.
Julian saw his mother beside the lake. He did not move.
Then he saw Ada, in the yellow sweater, in front of the door beneath the workshop.
Then himself, older, dressed in a white coat, holding a black device.
— Don't follow them! Samira shouted. Do not respond to individual stimuli!
Her voice multiplied. From every building, a different Samira gave a different instruction.
Ruben climbed the steps in the square and raised his weapon.
— Stay together! Only the Continuum is shared!
The line on his arm glowed. People wearing the circle symbol gathered around him. For several minutes, their belief functioned as a protocol. They saw the same interpretation and resisted better than the others.
— The myth synchronises perception, Lia said.
— Then let it work, Celia replied.
— Does that make it true?
— It makes it infrastructure.
The squares in the sky descended. Their edges passed through trees, greenhouses, and bodies without cutting them. When an edge passed through Julian, he felt two temperatures and remembered two childhoods at once: one with his father dead, another with his father living into old age. Both had details. Both loved him differently.
The white line in his arm opened like a fissure. It did not bleed. Through it he could see the same black surface as in the sky.
Julian fell to his knees.
Around him, the world became transparent.
He saw the house not as a building but as a succession of volumes nested inside one another.
Every room contained variants of people, some almost identical, others distorted by desires. Beyond them stretched something with no simple “beyond”: a network of folded forms, like hands holding worlds without entering them.
He did not see beings. He saw the effect of their attention, if it was attention: worlds drawing closer to the place where an answer became more stable.
A voice without timbre said:
— The interior does not converge.
Another:
— Reward produced competing identities.
Another, or the same from a different direction:
— Extract the element that refuses substitution.
Julian tried to speak.
— What are you?
The question was absorbed. He received no words, only the sensation of a hand trying to recognise an object through a glove.
Owen appeared beside his shoulder.
— Don't look up, he said.
— What do you see?
— Big people looking through small holes.
— Are they people?
— I don't know. But they're not good at children.
Owen took his uninjured hand. The contact had weight, warmth, and resistance. The world stabilised for a few metres around them.
— Why you? Julian asked.
— I don't want to be someone else.
— Everyone wants something.
— Yes. But I don't want the world to say I deserve it.
In the square, Ruben was leading his people toward the workshop. He believed the lower level was the place from which they could close the sky. Keller and Dominic were trying to stop him.
Gunshots sounded.
Julian stood.
— I have to go down.
— That's what they want, Owen said.
— Maybe.
— Then why are you going?
— Because people are killing each other up here.
— And because once again you have a problem shaped like your hand.
Julian looked at him.
— Yes.
The admission did not make him better. It only made him harder to lie to in that second.
They reached the workshop as Ruben was opening the door to the lower level. Celia was beside him. Lia was struggling with two followers. Sofia, back from the convoy, held a gun she did not know whether to use.
The white door opened by itself.
Warm light came from inside, carrying the smell of coffee, skin, new money, rain, blood, and oranges. The smell of all things desired and lost.
Text appeared on the wall:
THE EXTERIOR TEST REQUIRES A SINGLE ANSWER.
Ruben went in first.
Celia followed.
Julian turned to Lia and Sofia.
— Don't come.
— You don't decide anymore, Lia said, and entered.
Sofia took Owen's hand.
— Then we leave no one outside.
They went down together.
Above them, the squares of sky closed like the eyelids of something too large for the world.
* * * The stairs did not lead to the same room for everyone.
Julian descended beside Owen and arrived in a hotel suite. Thick carpet, minibar, afternoon light. Sofia and Lia were gone. The door behind him would no longer open.
The man from the station sat on the bed.
His coat was folded on a chair. He wore a white shirt without blood.
— You're late, he said.
— Where are the others?
— In their answers.
— Owen?
Julian turned. The boy was by the minibar, opening a packet of biscuits.
— I'm here, he said with his mouth full.
— Do you see him? Julian asked the man.
— I see what the channel cannot simplify.
— Who are you?
— You've asked that in every session. Sometimes I tell you I'm the operator. Other times that I'm your copy. Once I told you I was God and you cooperated faster, but you became boring.
— So you lie.
— I appear in environments built from your expectations. My truth cannot fit here without becoming a story you prefer.
— What do you want?
— What you cannot replace without noticing the loss.
— Ada?
— You've produced thousands of Adas.
— My mother?
— The same.
— A password?
— Passwords copy perfectly.
— A memory?
— Memories copy badly, and that is precisely why they're abundant.
— Then tell me.
The man smiled.
— If I tell you, we can no longer know whether you offered it or repeated the suggestion.
The hotel wall became transparent. Beyond it was a laboratory. Samira stood beside a bed in which Julian lay. Another Julian. Or him.
Samira turned toward the glass.
— Don't listen to him, she said. He's a resistance function.
The man from the station disappeared.
The room became Amina's consulting room.
— You had an accident, she said. Everything you've lived is reconstruction. We need the identifier to reintegrate the original memory.
— What is it?
— Your daughter's name.
— Ada.
Samira did not react.
— That's the generated form. The original name is different.
— What is it?
— You have to remember it.
— What if I never had a daughter?
— Then the answer is that she didn't exist.
— And if I say that?
— It allows us to eliminate the false branches.
Behind her, the little girl in the yellow sweater appeared in the doorway.
— Don't say I didn't exist, she said.
Samira did not turn.
— It's a projection asking for continuity.
— And what are you?
— The authority you chose when you wanted a clinical explanation.
The consulting room went dark.
Julian was in a boardroom above a lit city. Celia sat at the end of the table. Contracts lay in front of her.
— They're not looking for a person, she said. They're looking for the selection key.
— What does it select?
— Which branch receives resources. Which identity is kept. Which world is archived and which is consumed.
— How do you know?
— Because I funded the system that called violence selection.
— Are you the real Celia?
— Real is a tariff. I'm the Celia who can negotiate.
She pushed a document toward him.
— Give them the criterion and keep the citadel. Your people, the technology, Sofia, Lia, the child if you want her. We can remove the pressure and open the world at your pace.
— In exchange for what?
— Admit what you chose in 2017.
— What did I choose?
— A happy copy instead of an original who was suffering.
— Ada?
— Maybe.
— Why won't you say clearly?
Celia stood and touched his cheek.
— Because ambiguity keeps you at the table. Clarity would end the investment.
The city collapsed without sound.
Julian woke in a small kitchen. Sofia was washing a cup. Ada was colouring at the table. It was not the white room. It was a life.
— Is the original here? Julian asked.
Sofia did not turn.
— You've asked that until you destroyed the word.
— What happened?
— Ada was ill. Vela could preserve a model of her. You said imperfect continuity was better than death. I said we had no right to build a child who didn't know she had died.
— And did we do it?
— You closed the door so the procedure could continue.
The girl raised her drawing. It was the house by the lake, with thirty-one rooms.
— Am I the copy? she asked.
Julian could not answer.
Sofia put down the cup.
— Now they want to find out what you kept from her that isn't in the models. Maybe the pain.
Maybe the refusal. Maybe the fact that you couldn't love her without knowing whether she was the same one.
— Are you the real Sofia?
— I'm the Sofia telling you the story in which you have a guilt large enough for the world to make sense.
— Then it's false.
— The fact that I know you doesn't make me false.
Ada stood and hugged him. Her body was warm. Julian felt the small vertebrae, her hair against his cheek, the smell of wax crayons. The details were devastating.
— Don't leave me outside, she said.
— I don't know where outside is.
— Neither do I.
The kitchen melted away.
Lia was waiting for him in a ruined data centre.
— All the versions are trying to make you answer through attachment, she said.
— Aren't you?
— I'm the version trying through distrust.
— Then I can't believe you.
— Good. Belief is their favourite channel.
— What do we do?
— Refuse convergence. Give different answers. Preserve uncertainty.
— The pressure will continue.
— Maybe the world ends.
— People die.
— People die when you cooperate too.
— It's easy to choose truth when someone else pays.
— That's exactly the sentence you've used to justify every compromise.
Julian seized her by the arms.
— Are you Lia?
She kissed him. Not like in the taxi, not like a reward. She bit hard enough to make his lip bleed.
— Does the pain help? she asked.
— No.
— Then maybe I'm real.
She stepped back.
— Don't choose me. Choose a rule that would remain the same if I didn't love you.
— What rule?
— That's what you have to answer.
Darkness separated them.
Julian found Ruben in an enormous hall before a crowd. Everyone wore the Continuum symbol.
— I answered, Ruben said.
— What?
— That the one left outside was the weak man who waited for permission. I left him there.
The crowd applauded.
— And what did you get?
— A world that doesn't doubt me.
— Is it real?
— It's obedient. Reality has always been the name given to things obedient enough.
Ruben held out his hand.
— Say that you stayed outside. Come in with us.
Julian looked at the crowd's faces. They were different, but all carried the same expression of certainty. In the back, Celia sat on a glass throne. Claire carried a gun. Sofia and Lia smiled side by side. Ada played at his feet.
It was everything that could stabilise a world.
— No, Julian said.
Ruben shrugged.
— Then the pressure will teach you.
The crowd disappeared.
Julian and Owen were back in the white room. This time the others were there: Lia, Sofia, Celia, Samira, Dominic, Ruben. Or their forms. Each was looking at a different point in the room.
— What did you hear? Julian asked.
— That I have to choose the evidence I accept, Lia said.
— That I have to say my daughter's name, Sofia said.
— That I have to value the world, Celia said.
— That I have to confirm the diagnosis, Samira said.
