— That I have to choose whom to take out before the air runs out, Dominic said.
Ruben smiled.
— They asked me the only honest question: who leads?
— I got pancakes, Owen said.
Everyone looked at him.
On the table in front of him was a plate of pancakes. Owen ate calmly.
— And what did they ask you? Julian said.
— Whether they're enough.
— What did you say?
— That right now, yes.
The room trembled.
Fragmented text appeared on the walls, impossible to read in full. Each person saw a different sentence. Julian could make out only fragments:
...ELEMENT...
...CONTINUITY...
...EXTERIOR...
...AUTHORISE...
...WHO...
...KEEP...
An empty field appeared in the centre.
A voice said:
— One answer.
— We aren't hearing the same question, Lia said.
— The answer must be stable across questions, the voice replied.
— That doesn't exist, Samira said.
— THEN THE INTERIOR IS NOT AN IDENTITY.
The light intensified. Through the walls they saw the citadel, then cities, then entire worlds, each suspended like a bubble in something without form. Some went dark. Others multiplied.
— What if we don't answer? Sofia asked.
— PRESSURE CONTINUES.
— What if we answer wrong? Dominic asked.
— THE ANSWER BECOMES THE WORLD.
Ruben stepped toward the field.
— Then I'll answer.
Celia stopped him.
— An answer from you produces only a world of yours.
— Exactly.
They fought. The room multiplied them: dozens of Rubens, dozens of Celias, each choosing differently. Dominic tried to separate them. Samira shouted instructions. Sofia called for Ada. Lia looked at Julian.
— We can't reason with a question we don't share, he said.
— But we can refuse to turn one person's desire into everyone's reality.
— That's an answer too.
— I know.
Owen finished the pancakes and pushed the plate away.
— You're asking badly, he said to the room.
Everything stopped.
The voice answered after a pause longer than all the others:
— REFORMULATE.
Owen shrugged.
— No. You.
For the first time, Julian felt something like hesitation coming from outside.
The empty field moved toward him.
It was not asking him for a word. It was asking for a form he could not see without filling it.
Julian reached out his hand.
Around him, each person was shouting something different.
He did not know which voices were people, which were rewards, and which were merely ways for the question to hear itself.
His fingers touched the field.
The room disappeared before he knew whether he had answered.
CHAPTER XIII
VICTORY
Julian woke with his cheek pressed against the damp earth of the main greenhouse.
The glass above was intact. The sky had a single blue. The pumps worked, and water flowed through the pipes with the banal sound of infrastructure trying to say nothing.
Around him, people stood one by one.
Lia was by the door, with dried blood on her lip. Sofia knelt with her hands around a body that was not there. Dominic checked pulses. Celia stared at her palms. Samira repeated the date, name, and location as in an orientation protocol. Ruben stood last, arms spread, wearing an expression of triumph so complete that he seemed the only one who had lost nothing.
— It withdrew, he said.
No one asked what.
Julian looked at the hand that had touched the field. There was no wound. The white line beneath his skin had vanished. He remembered the room, the voices, and the sensation of an emptied form before he could see it. He did not remember the answer.
Or he did not remember the question.
Sabine Keller switched on the radio. After several seconds of static, a voice from Bern answered. The regional centre existed. The analogue network was functioning. The sky was normal in the other cantons.
— How long did it last? Keller asked.
The voice consulted a log.
— Thirty-one minutes. You lost telemetry at 14:43 and came back at 15:14.
Keller looked toward the mechanical clock in the greenhouse. It showed the same time. The community board, however, recorded eighty-seven days since the blackout.
— Confirm the date, she said.
The voice confirmed it.
It was the date they expected after eighty-seven days.
— Then no minutes are missing, Lia said. Measurements are.
— Don't start again, someone in the crowd said.
The sentence was spoken without hostility. More like a plea.
People counted the dead, the wounded, and the children. The registers listed two hundred and eighty-three residents. Two hundred and eighty-two answered roll call.
They counted again.
The same result.
No one knew who was missing.
There was a small bed in the room beside the infirmary, a yellow jacket hanging on a hook, and a notebook containing plans of the house. Vera Rott stood beside them with her hand over her mouth.
— Whose is it? Dominic asked.
Vera did not answer immediately.
— I don't know.
Julian took the notebook. On the first page was an apple with a wormhole. On the last, a circle left open.
— Owen, he said.
Several people looked at him.
— Who? Celia asked.
Lia closed her eyes.
— There was a boy.
— Eve's, Julian said. He was eleven. Yellow jacket. He found the pipe. He went in with us.
Vera began to cry soundlessly.
— I've never had children, she said.
Samira checked her file. One record said Vera had come alone from the clinic. Another had a blank line beneath her name, with daily rations and no identifier. Community photographs contained spaces between people where no one remembered anyone standing.
— Maybe he wasn't a person, Samira said.
— Don't finish that sentence, Lia replied.
Julian wrote OWEN on the blank line. The ink remained.
That reassured him more than it should have.
By evening, the explanations began to organise themselves.
Ruben's people said the phenomenon had tried to divide them through doubt and had been defeated when the community refused to keep feeding the questions. Sabine spoke of an electromagnetic anomaly accompanied by neurocognitive contamination. Samira used the term collective dissociation only when no one asked what object had produced the same hallucinations. Lia refused to formulate a hypothesis without instruments. Celia said what it had been did not matter; what mattered were contractual conditions that could prevent repetition.
Every explanation saved the person who offered it.
In the square, someone lit the cooking fire. Someone else brought music. It was not an announced celebration, but people approached with the cautious haste by which a crowd turns terror into tradition.
Ruben climbed the steps.
— We were not abandoned, he said. We endured.
— We don't know what happened, Lia shouted.
— Exactly. And we're still here.
The crowd applauded.
Julian noticed that the slogan on the workshop had been repainted. The old letters, washed by rain, were now white and straight:
THEY FOUGHT REASON AND THEY WON.
At first people laughed, remembering its origin. Then they repeated the sentence. In less than a minute, the joke became the name of the victory.
No one voted on that.
Over the following days, the world returned with suspicious speed.
Military corridors reopened. Trucks brought medicine and fuel. The digital network worked in islands, then between cities. Mercer Continuum reappeared on servers as though it had never gone offline. Julian's accounts were restored. His fortune had increased more than tenfold during a period when markets should not have existed.
The transaction reference was simple:
FINAL CONTINUITY.
Celia found him staring at the amount.
— We can rebuild three regions, she said.
— Or we can refuse the money.
— Refusal doesn't make it less real. It only leaves it to someone else.
It was the perfect argument for any prize received from a source you did not want to examine.
Julian accepted the funds into a public reconstruction structure. He told himself that this transformed the reward into common good. The next day, the press called him a saviour again.
The hearing that had stripped him of authority did not disappear, but was compressed into one sentence:
“During the crisis, the community tested participatory models of leadership.”
Margarete's death became
“medical incident during the blackout.”
The sabotage of the convoy became “operational conflict.” Konrad's name appeared on a plaque without an explanation of how he had died.
Claire left before the first official convoy. She did not tell Julian where. Sofia remained only until the regional network agreed to open an investigation into missing persons. Lia declined the position of co-founder in the new organisation and began reconstructing the archive from the hidden photographs. Celia became director of the fund after a hearing in which she admitted “errors of assessment” without speaking the name of the dead engineer.
Ruben launched the Renewal Movement. Within three weeks it had chapters in seven cities. It did not demand the destruction of science. It demanded only that every explanation be “subordinate to the experience of those who survived.” The formulation was vague enough to mean no evidence could contradict a well-organised emotion.
At the school, the chronology was printed in colour.
DOUBT remained before THE FALL.
Julian protested. The commission thanked him for the observation and added a note that there were “multiple perspectives on causality.” The textbook remained unchanged.
With each normal day, the need to find out diminished.
Water ran. Children ate. Networks worked. People could once again buy useless things, promise impossible profits, pursue beautiful people, and confuse visibility with value. The apocalypse had not cured them. It had only given them a story in which all the old impulses became evidence that they had fought for life.
Julian began to fear that this had been the victory that was required.
One morning, he received a message with no sender.
It contained no words. Only a photograph of the sheet from Amina's archive. Every box was now checked, including the last:
ANSWER WITHOUT QUESTION.
Below the list was a sentence he had not seen before:
THE EXTERNAL ELEMENT NO LONGER INTERFERES.
The message deleted itself.
Julian tried to tell Lia. When he reached the word element, he could no longer remember what he had wanted to explain. He could see the yellow jacket, the notebook, and the apple, but the connection that made them urgent was missing.
— Owen, Lia said.
The name caused a distant pain, like the memory of a wound the body no longer had.
— Yes.
— We need to keep him in the archive.
— Him or the name?
Lia looked at him.
— The fact that we don't know the difference.
On the day the trains were running normally again, Julian went to Zurich Central Station. His mother was arriving from Valencia. She said there had only been a thirty-one-minute power outage there, followed by weeks of administrative chaos. She did not remember the conversation in which she had refused to be rescued. Nor did she remember the Mirador Hotel.
When she stepped off the train, she hugged him and told him he looked older.
Behind her, a man in a gray coat crossed the platform.
Julian recognized him by the way he held his left arm, protecting a wound that could not be seen. He started after him. The crowd separated them. The man went down toward the passage where, months or minutes earlier, he had died in Julian's arms.
— Julian, his mother called. Where are you going?
He stopped.
On the opposite platform stood a child in a yellow jacket. He was holding a blemished apple.
His face was almost familiar, but not enough. The child looked at him without waving.
A train entered between them.
Julian could run toward the passage after the man. He could climb the stairs toward the child.
He could stay beside his mother, in the only scene that demanded he prove nothing.
The train doors opened. For a second, through the empty carriage, he saw the child again.
— Is there anyone else outside? his mother asked.
Her tone was ordinary. She meant the people still getting off.
Julian looked at the doors beginning to close.
— I don't know, he said.
He did not stop them.
When the train left, the opposite platform was empty. On the ground remained an apple with a wormhole in it.
Julian waited for a message, a voice, a reward, or a punishment.
The world asked nothing more of him.
That was the part that frightened him most.
STORY 3
BETTER REALITY — MAYA ARDEN
The Woman They Were Waiting For
On the morning Maya Arden's life began proving her right, she was barefoot in a hotel bathroom, taping the hem of her dress with adhesive tape.
The dress had cost more than her first monthly salary. The tape came from reception.
— Democracy of materials, she murmured.
Her phone vibrated on the sink.
CELESTE: Where are you? We go on in twelve minutes.
Maya did not answer. She put on her shoes, checked the hem, then looked at herself in the mirror.
Thirty-six years old. Brown hair pulled back at the nape. A face she had once considered too severe and that people now called "distinctive" since she had begun appearing in the press. Shadows under her eyes from three badly slept nights.
She smiled at herself.
— Convincing, she said.
The reflection smiled with her.
The conference was being held at a hotel on the shore of Lake Geneva and had gathered exactly the kind of people who said "humanity" when they meant budget. Ministers, foundation directors, researchers, people who had made money from platforms and now funded studies on the effects of platforms.
Maya was there for a roundtable on "social resilience in algorithmic systems."
The title had been chosen by someone who was not responsible for attracting an audience.
Backstage, Celeste Armand handed her a tablet.
— The questions changed.
— When?
— Seven minutes ago.
— Perfect.
Celeste was their program director and, for two years, Maya's principal internal adversary. They did not hate each other. That would have been simpler. They respected each other enough to turn every difference into a struggle over the definition of competence.
— Want to see them? Celeste asked.
— No.
— Maya.
— If I read them now, I'll prepare for their questions. I'd rather answer the problem.
Celeste sighed.
— Sometimes it must be exhausting to be you.
— Imagine what it's like for me.
They went onstage.
The first question was banal. The second, hostile. An American investor asked whether regulating predictive systems was not merely a sophisticated form of moral panic.
Maya answered without haste.
— Moral panic happens when people overestimate a danger they do not understand. Institutional capture happens when they underestimate it because it pays their bills. We have a tradition of both.
The room laughed.
The investor did not.
The third question came from a journalist:
— If you could impose a single rule on companies that shape behavior, what would it be?
Maya thought for a second.
— A system should not be able to optimize simultaneously for what I want now and for what I will come to want after it has shaped me.
The room grew quiet.
Maya continued:
— If the platform is allowed to modify the preference it later claims to serve, consent becomes circular.
In the front row, a man she did not know was watching her very closely.
He was around forty, in a gray suit without a tie, black hair threaded with white. He took no notes. He did not look at his phone.
Maya noticed the gaze and deliberately ignored it.
At the end, people stood. Several came to speak with her. A professor asked for the paper she had cited. A European Commission representative proposed a conversation. The journalist asked for an interview.
Then the man from the front row came over.
— Corin Vale, he said. Meridian Foundation.
Maya knew the name.
Everyone in the field knew it. Meridian managed more than twenty billion in philanthropic assets and had the rare reputation of being able to lose money without losing influence.
— Maya Arden.
— I know.
— That's either a compliment or a problem.
— I was hoping it would be an offer.
Two meters away, Celeste visibly stopped pretending to be busy.
Corin handed her a business card, a gesture so old-fashioned it seemed intentional.
— We're looking for someone to lead our new adaptive social systems program.
— I'm not looking for another job.
— I know.
— Then you have a weak recruiting strategy.
— Or a very good one. People who are looking are often the least interesting.
Maya smiled.
— What budget?
— Initially, 1.8 billion over five years.
Maya thought she had misheard.
— For the program?
— Yes.
— And you want me to run it.
— We want to talk.
— Why?
Corin looked at her as if the question had been the important part.
— Because you're one of the few people who understand that a satisfied preference is not necessarily a respected preference.
The same idea she had voiced onstage five minutes earlier.
Yet the wording gave her a strange sensation.
— Did you come for my panel?
— Among other things.
— How did you know what I was going to say?
— I didn't.
Maya studied him.
— Fine.
— Fine what?
— We can talk.
— Six today?
— I have dinner with my team at six.
Celeste cut in from the side:
— We can move dinner.
Maya turned toward her, surprised.
Celeste smiled.
— 1.8 billion, Maya. I can eat tomorrow too.
Corin left.
Maya remained with the business card in her hand.
— Don't say anything, she told Celeste.
— I was only going to observe that you've rehearsed the line "I'm not looking for another job" for years, for exactly this moment.
Maya's phone vibrated.
A message from Gabriel Stein.
They had not spoken in eighteen months.
GABRIEL: I'm in Geneva. I know this sounds absurd. Do you have time for coffee?
Maya felt a reaction whose speed she hated.
Gabriel had been the man with whom she had almost built a life. "Almost" had lasted five years. They had separated without infidelity, without scandal, without a culprit clear enough to hate. He wanted children then. She did not know. She wanted Geneva. He wanted Berlin. Both wanted the other to consider their own compromise reasonable.
In the end, reason had won, and they had both hated it for that.
MAYA: Until when?
GABRIEL: Tomorrow evening.
MAYA: 16:30, 30 min.
GABRIEL: You still negotiate coffee.
MAYA: Especially coffee.
She put the phone in her bag.
— Who? Celeste asked.
— Nobody.
— Your face says "someone with history."
— My face is private property.
— Not onstage.
Maya headed toward the elevator.
The doors opened before she pressed the button.
Inside was an elderly woman, very elegant, with white hair and a green scarf.
Maya stepped in.
— Which floor? the woman asked.
— Six.
— Me too.
The elevator started.
The woman looked at her in the mirror.
— That went well.
— The panel?
— Yes.
— Were you there?
— For a little while.
— Thank you.
The woman smiled.
— The question is whether you'll recognize the part that wasn't yours.
Maya turned.
— Excuse me?
The doors opened on six.
The woman stepped out.
— What did you say?
She did not turn around.
— Ma'am?
Maya stepped out after her.
The corridor was empty.
There was no nearby door the woman could have entered that quickly.
Maya stood still for a moment, then laughed to herself.
Hotels. Conferences. Strange people.
At the end of the corridor, her phone vibrated again.
An email from Meridian Foundation.
Subject: TERM SHEET — MAYA ARDEN.
The attachment contained a preliminary offer.
The salary was almost four times her current one. Bonus, participation in a fund, company apartment, freedom to choose her team.
At the end, in a separate paragraph:
"The program will be structured according to the principles you formulated today."
Maya had not yet told anyone what principles she wanted.
She reread the sentence.
Then she remembered the woman in the elevator.
The part that wasn't yours.
The phone vibrated a third time.
GABRIEL: By the way. I read the panel. You were right back then.
Maya stopped.
Back then.
She did not need to ask about what.
One of her oldest fantasies was not to win Gabriel back.
It was that one day he would come back and tell her that she had been right.
The message was that exact sentence.
For several seconds, Maya felt only satisfaction.
Only afterward did fear arrive.
Maya did not accept Meridian's offer that day.
For the next forty-eight hours, that became a small source of pride.
She was not the woman who saw a large salary and abandoned her projects. She was not impressed by a polite billionaire and a budget with nine zeros. She was not hungry for status.
She repeated all this to herself while checking the compensation package three times.
Celeste sat on the edge of the temporary desk in the hotel.
— Did you calculate the taxes?
— No.
— You have a spreadsheet open with three jurisdictions.
Maya closed the laptop.
— I was checking the structure.
— Of course.
— It doesn't mean I'm accepting.
— No. It only means that if you refuse, you want to know exactly how much money you sacrificed for integrity.
Maya looked at her.
— Why are you suddenly on my side?
— I'm not on your side. I'm on the side of a world in which people who talk about the risks of adaptive systems get a bigger budget than people who build adaptive advertising.
— You criticized exactly that idea three months ago.
— I criticized the form you proposed.
— You said it was "epistemic imperialism with ethical branding."
Celeste pressed her lips together.
— It was a good phrase.
— You put it in an email with twenty people.
— And I was right then.
Maya stopped.
It was the second time that day someone had told her she had been right.
Celeste continued:
— But you were right about the underlying problem. I was too concerned with not giving you the victory.
Maya did not know what to do with the sentence.
She had often imagined a scene in which Celeste admitted this. In the fantasy, Maya answered elegantly, generously, almost indifferently.
In reality, she felt a raw joy.
— I appreciate it, she said.
— You don't look like it.
— I'm trying not to dance.
Celeste laughed.
At six, Maya met Corin in a private lounge overlooking the lake.
He did not try to seduce her.
That made him more effective.
He talked about the program. About democratic infrastructure. About systems that could shape political preferences through personalized interfaces. About the risk that personal assistants might become, without mandate, constitutional institutions.
Maya forgot about the salary for almost an hour.
— I want veto power over commercial partners, she said.
— Independent audit budget.
— Yes.
— We publish negative results.
— Yes.
— No board pre-approval rights.
Corin smiled.
— That will be harder.
Maya felt, almost absurdly, relieved.
At last, something was not coming easily.
— Then we don't have an agreement.
— Not yet.
— Good.
— But I have a counteroffer. The board cannot block publication, but it can request an appended response.
Maya thought.
— Acceptable.
Corin made a note.
— Anything else?
— Yes. Why me?
— I told you.
— You gave me a good line. Not an organizational reason.
Corin leaned back.
— We evaluated twenty-three people. You have the rare combination of technical competence and distrust of your own discipline.
— That sounds almost romantic.
— It's as close to romance as I allow in a term sheet.
Maya looked at him.
— Are you always this well prepared?
— Then I'm lucky today.
— Maybe I am.
It was a simple line. Maya felt herself liking it and was annoyed that she did.
As she left, Corin handed her a folder.
— The company apartment is optional. But I think you should see it before deciding.
— I don't need an apartment.
— I know.
Maya opened it in the elevator.
Penthouse near Parc La Grange. Windows overlooking the lake. Two-level library. Open kitchen, but not ostentatious. A small separate work studio. Terrace with wild plants, not ornamental ones.
Exactly the kind of home Maya sometimes saved in private folders with no intention of ever buying one.
Not the same home. The same taste.
She closed the folder.
At 16:30 the next day, Gabriel was waiting for her in a discreet café on Rue du Rhône.
He looked almost the same. A little thinner. Shorter hair. The same tendency to hold his cup in both hands when he was nervous.
— Hi, he said.
— Hi.
They hugged cautiously.
The first ten minutes were about administrative things. Berlin. Geneva. Work. Mutual friends.
Then Gabriel said:
— I'm sorry.
Maya put down her cup.
— For which part?
— For the way I left.
— You took the train. Very civilized.
— You know what I mean.
— I know.
— I turned your uncertainty about children into a moral accusation. As if the fact that I knew what I wanted made me more mature.
Maya felt something in her chest.
Not because she still loved him. Perhaps she loved him in an old, fossilized layer. But the sentence was one she had waited years to hear.
— Why now?
Gabriel looked at his hands.
— Because I realized I wanted to win the breakup.
— Did you win?
— No.
— Neither did I.
— I know.
— Then why did you write that I was right?
Gabriel smiled sadly.
— Because you were.
Maya felt the same satisfaction again. This time it came with a small wave of nausea.
— Say something I was wrong about, she said.
— What?
— Anything. An argument. A decision. One thing.
Gabriel laughed.
— Maya, I didn't come with a spreadsheet.
— Try.
He thought.
— You were wrong when you said you'd never like Berlin.
— I didn't like it.
— Because you stayed three weeks and decided on the first day.
— Fine.
— You were wrong when you invested in that vertical farming company.
— I came out ahead.
— By luck.
— Accepted.
— And you were wrong when you thought that if you explained an emotion perfectly, you no longer had to feel it.
Maya looked at him.
That one hurt.
— Better, she said.
— What's going on?
— Nothing. I needed to hear something I don't like.
— I can do that professionally if you want.
They laughed.
As they were leaving, Gabriel touched her arm.
— I'm staying one more day.
— I thought you were leaving tomorrow evening.
— I moved it.
— Why?
— Because I'd like to see you again.
Maya felt the old mechanism start. Possibility. Repair. A version of life in which she did not have to choose between love and autonomy because, this time, the other person had learned.
— I'll write you, she said.
When she went outside, it was raining.
Corin was standing under the awning of the hotel across the street, talking on the phone.
He saw her.
He raised a hand.
Gabriel came out behind her.
Corin looked from one to the other, expressionless.
Maya instantly felt the ridiculousness of the situation.
Two important, different, available men intersecting at exactly the moment she came out from coffee.
A fantasy so cheap she almost laughed.
Corin crossed the street.
— Mr. Stein, he said.
Gabriel looked at him, surprised.
— Do we know each other?
— Not personally.
— Then how do you know who I am?
Corin stopped.
For the first time since Maya had met him, he looked genuinely confused.
— I don't know, he said.
Two Good Men
Corin found an explanation after seven seconds.
— Background check, he said.
Gabriel raised an eyebrow.
— Whose?
— Maya's. For the job offer.
Maya looked at him.
— You investigated my former partners?
— Not me personally. The due-diligence team checks exposures, conflicts, relevant connections.
— Gabriel is an architect. Not a cartel.
— Thank you, Gabriel said.
Corin smiled.
— My apologies. The name probably stayed with me from the report.
It was a sufficient explanation.
Maya did not like it, but that was precisely what made it credible.
— Tomorrow we'll discuss the limits of the background check, she said.
— Gladly.
Gabriel looked at her.
— Job offer?
— Long story.
— 1.8 billion, Corin said.
Maya gave him a cold look.
— Very discreet.
— Program budget, not salary.
— Shame, Gabriel said.
For several seconds, the two men smiled at each other with that perfect politeness in which competition can pretend it does not exist.
Maya felt the pleasure again.
This time she noticed it in real time.
It was not just attraction. It was the position at the center. The fact that two versions of life were looking at each other through her. Gabriel represented the repaired past: love returning more mature. Corin represented the amplified future: power, work, a larger world, a man who was not yet asking her to make herself smaller.
She did not want to choose.
And, for the moment, precisely that lack of choice seemed to cost nothing.
— I'm leaving, Maya said.
Gabriel smiled.
— With whom?
— Alone.
— Good answer, Corin said.
— Don't get used to evaluating my answers.
She left before either of them.
In her room, she opened her laptop and searched Meridian Foundation, then Corin, then the due-diligence company listed in the offer documents.
Everything existed. Contracts, lawsuits, reports. Nothing exotic.
Then she searched for the woman from the elevator in the conference participant registry.
She did not know her name.
She searched the event photographs.
In one, taken during the panel, the front row was visible. Corin. A French economist. Two women from a UN agency.
At the edge, the chair where Maya remembered the woman with the green scarf sitting was empty.
Maybe she had only been there for a few minutes.
She closed the photograph.
Her phone vibrated.
IDRIS: I can send you the due-diligence report, with private data redacted. You should know what we know.
It was exactly what she would have asked for.
MAYA: Yes.
Two minutes later, the report arrived.
Gabriel appeared under "previous close relationships." Three lines. Name, profession, city, absence of financial conflicts.
The explanation was real.
Maya felt relief.
Then she noticed the report date.
It had been completed two weeks before the conference.
Meridian had been evaluating her for some time. Nothing paranormal.
Just flattering.
Very flattering.
The next morning, Maya agreed to see the apartment.
Not because she wanted to move, she told herself. For information.
The agent greeted her with a discreet smile and led her through the rooms.
The home was, irritatingly, almost perfect.
It was not the luxury that impressed her. It was the attention to details she considered personal: shelves deep enough for large books, no dominant television, windows that could open fully, a shower without unnecessary glass, reading lights in the right places.
In the bedroom, Maya stopped.
On the wall was a black-and-white photograph of a rocky coast in Iceland.
She had the same photograph at home.
Not the same subject.
The same photograph.
A print by photographer Sigríður Jónsdóttir, bought by Maya in Reykjavík nine years earlier. It was not unique, but it was not common either.
— Does the photograph come with the apartment? she asked.
— Yes, if you want it.
— Who chose it?
The agent checked the file.
— The designer.
— Name?
— I can find out.
Maya stepped closer.
On the back of her own print, one evening with Gabriel, she had written: "the only place where I could disappear without running away." A melodramatic sentence she had forgotten until now.
— How many copies exist?
— I don't know.
— It doesn't matter.
In the downstairs library, she found something else.
A copy of
"The Ethics of Ambiguity," the same old edition she had read in college.
On the top shelf, a
volume of Polish poetry her mother had kept at home. In the kitchen, exactly the tea she bought.
Each thing separately was banal.
Together, the apartment looked less like company housing and more like a reconstruction of her taste.
— Did they prepare this place for me? she asked.
The agent laughed.
— No. It's been in the foundation's portfolio for three years.
Maya took out her phone and photographed several objects.
— Can I see the inventory from three years ago?
— Of course.
The agent sent her the PDF.
The photograph was there.
The books were not listed individually.
The tea, obviously, was not either.
Maya told herself that a good designer produced familiarity precisely by using cultural objects predictable for their social class.
A taste algorithm for people who believed they had no algorithmic taste.
It was actually funny.
As she left, she received two messages almost simultaneously.
GABRIEL: Dinner tonight? I promise not to resolve the past over dessert.
IDRIS: If you haven't run away after the viewing, I'd like to discuss the last clause. Dinner?
Maya looked at the screen.
Two invitations.
The same time slot.
She laughed.
The agent looked at her.
— Good news?
— Too much of it.
In the elevator, her reflection was smiling.
Maya stopped smiling.
The reflection kept smiling for a fraction of a second.
Then it stopped.
Maya froze.
She stepped closer to the mirror.
She raised her hand.
The reflection raised it simultaneously.
She turned her head.
Simultaneously.
— Tired, she said aloud.
The reflection moved its lips with hers.
The elevator doors opened.
In the lobby, the woman with the green scarf was waiting for her.
— That wasn't it, the woman said.
Maya remained inside the elevator.
— What wasn't?
— The part that wasn't yours.
— Who are you?
The woman smiled.
— Another good choice.
The doors began to close.
Maya put her hand between them.
When they opened again, the lobby was empty.
The green scarf remained on the floor.
The green scarf was silk.
Maya put it in a paper bag, photographed the place, time, and label, then went back to reception.
— Woman around seventy, white hair, navy suit, green scarf. Do you know who she is?
The receptionist checked the cameras.
— What time?
— Five minutes ago, elevator from the sixth floor.
— I can ask security.
Maya waited.
The footage showed the elevator doors opening. Maya stepped out, looked to the right, returned between the doors, then bent down and picked something up from the floor.
The woman did not appear.
— Did you find a scarf? the receptionist asked.
Maya was holding the bag.
— Yes.
— Maybe the owner passed before this frame.
— Maybe.
— Will you leave it at lost and found?
Maya hesitated.
— Not yet.
In her room, she put the scarf on the table.
It was real. Texture, stitching, faint scent of perfume. The label belonged to a well-known Italian fashion house. She searched the model. It had been produced six years earlier.
Nothing impossible.
She filmed it with her phone and then went to the bathroom.
When she came back, the scarf was still there.
— Well done, she said. You've passed the existence test for two minutes.
The phone rang.
Celeste.
— Have you seen the news?
— No.
— Open Le Temps.
Maya opened it.
Headline: "The Nereid Program: Internal Documents Indicate Manipulation of Results in a European Social Resilience Project."
Nereid was the project Celeste had led before working with Maya.
— What is this?
— I don't know. They have real documents, but the conclusion is false.
— What documents?
— Emails. Drafts. A preliminary dataset.
— Did you alter the results?
— No.
Maya was silent.
Celeste heard the silence.
— Don't question me like an investigator, Maya. I'm telling you no.
— Then I believe you.
— You answered too quickly.
— Would you rather I interrogate you?
— I don't know what I'd rather have.
Over the next two hours, the story exploded. Another site published more documents. A former collaborator accused the team of "opportunistic indicator selection." Their foundation demanded explanations. The board called a meeting.
Maya felt two things simultaneously.
First: anger on Celeste's behalf.
Second: a satisfaction so shameful she tried to call it something else.
Celeste had criticized her publicly. Blocked her initiatives. Challenged her authority. Now, at exactly the moment Maya received the biggest offer of her career, her adversary was being brought down by a story that made all their old conflicts look resolved in Maya's favor.
She did not want the scandal.
But part of her wanted the victory.
That part had no ethics. It had taste.
At the emergency meeting, a board member said:
— Maya, if you leave for Meridian now, the message will be devastating.
— For whom?
— For the organization.
— Or for Celeste?
— For both.
— I will not use her scandal as leverage.
Celeste, pale, said:
— You should accept anyway.
Maya turned toward her.
— We're not discussing me.
— That's exactly what we discuss every time, even when we don't want to.
After the meeting, Celeste stopped her.
— You're right about one thing.
Maya almost laughed.
— Please don't.
— Don't tell me I'm right again.
Celeste looked confused.
— Fine. Then you were wrong.
— About what?
— You always thought I wanted your position.
Maya did not answer.
— I don't want it, Celeste continued. I want you not to be the only person in the room who decides what counts as a legitimate problem.
That hurt in the right place.
— Fair, Maya said.
— And now don't defend me because it makes you look noble.
— I'm defending you because I believe you.
— I hope so.
That evening, Maya declined both dinners.
She wrote Gabriel and Corin that she had a work problem.
Both replied almost identically.
GABRIEL: I understand. If I can help, tell me.
IDRIS: I understand. If I can help, let me know.
Maya looked at the two messages and laughed nervously.
Then a third arrived.
No number.
WE CAN REMOVE THE OBSTACLE.
Maya felt her stomach tighten.
She typed:
WHO ARE YOU?
The message went through.
The reply came immediately:
THAT IS NOT WHAT YOU ASKED.
Maya wrote:
WHAT DID I ASK?
Three dots appeared, disappeared.
Then:
WHY HER?
Maya looked at the photograph in the article. Celeste caught in a bad frame, eyes half closed.
Without wanting to, she remembered a night a year earlier. After a meeting in which Celeste had blocked a project, Maya had drunk two glasses of wine and told a friend:
"Sometimes I wish the world could see who she really is."
An ordinary sentence of anger.
A wish without a plan.
The phone vibrated.
NOW IT SEES.
Maya threw the phone onto the bed.
She turned toward the table.
The paper bag was there.
The scarf was gone.
CHAPTER 3
The Mirror
Maya spent the night trying to prove she had imagined the scarf.
She had photographs.
She had video.
She had a banal transaction with reality: the receptionist had seen her pick something up and offered lost and found.
In the photographs, the scarf existed.
In the video, it existed.
On the table, it no longer existed.
The room door had been locked. The window did not open far enough. The hotel room probably did not have a hidden operative dedicated to stealing vintage accessories.
Maya checked the trash. The suitcases. The closet. The bathroom.
At three in the morning she realized she was searching drawers for her own sanity.
She slept for two hours.
By morning, the Celeste scandal had worsened. A second site published a memo that seemed to prove the results had been "aligned with funder objectives." Maya recognized the document. It was real. The wording was hers.
The context was missing.
The memo had been about selecting indicators before data collection, not after.
— They're mixing real documents into a false story, she told Celeste.
— Yes.
— Who has access?
— Too many people.
— Who has motive?
Celeste looked at her.
— Do you want the short list or the honest one?
— Honest.
— Half the field.
Maya sat down.
— I received a message.
— What message?
She showed her.
Celeste read it without reacting immediately.
— "We can remove the obstacle."
— Yes.
— You think the obstacle is me.
— No.
— But you thought about it.
— Yes.
Celeste handed the phone back.
— Good.
— Good?
— Healthier than pretending you don't have thoughts like that.
— Someone is publishing documents about you and I'm telling you part of me was glad.
— Part of me was glad when your former project in Kenya failed.
Maya looked up.
— Seriously?
— Yes. Because it proved to me that your implementation model was too centralized.
— You never told me.
— Because I'm an adult.
Maya began to laugh.
Celeste laughed too.
For a few minutes, the scandal seemed bearable.
Then Maya's phone displayed a new message.
CONFLICT REDUCED.
Celeste saw it.
— What the hell?
— You saw it?
— Yes.
Maya felt the extraordinary relief of having a witness.
— Take a picture of it with your phone.
Celeste took out her phone.
The moment she opened the camera, the message disappeared.
— Do you have a screenshot?
Maya tried.
The capture showed the screen, but the text did not appear.
— That's impossible, Celeste said.
— No. Just very difficult.
— Thank you, I feel better.
They went to a technical lab in the organization and put the phone in a Faraday bag. The messages stopped.
Maya felt almost triumphant.
At lunch, a page came out of the network printer by itself.
CONFLICT REDUCED.
Below it:
THE CHANNEL DOES NOT MATTER.
Celeste sat down in a chair.
— Fine, she said. Now I'm impressed.
— I don't want you to be.
— Neither do I.
Maya picked up the sheet with two fingers.
— Malware on the network.
— Sure.
— Internal compromise.
— Sure.
— Someone is watching us.
— Probably.
— And it isn't…
She stopped.
— Isn't what?
— Nothing.
Celeste smiled without humor.
— Simulation? Aliens? God with printer access?
— I didn't say that.
— Your face has a very rich vocabulary today.
That afternoon, Corin came to the office in person.
— The offer stands, he said. The scandal does not affect your evaluation.
— It isn't my scandal.
— Exactly.
— And Celeste?
— If you accept and want to bring her onto the team, we have room.
Maya looked at him.
— You know I want that?
— I've read her work. She's good.
— Diplomatic answer.
— Also true.
Celeste, in the next room, could hear them.
— Are you buying me too? she called out.
Corin replied:
— Only if the price is indecent.
Maya laughed.
Again, everything was arranging itself.
The adversary became an ally. The offer absorbed the scandal. The money increased. Corin was charming without being suffocating. Gabriel had sent a photograph from Berlin that morning with the message: "I've decided to spend a month in Geneva. New project."
Nothing demanded sacrifice.
That evening, Maya returned to her hotel room.
She looked at herself in the mirror.
She raised her hand.
The reflection did the same.
She touched her cheek.
Simultaneously.
— Fine, she said.
The reflection said nothing, being a reflection.
Maya felt ridiculous.
She turned around.
In the mirror, the reflection remained facing her for almost two seconds.
Maya saw it clearly.
Her reflection had not turned with her body.
It was watching her.
Then it turned and disappeared from view exactly as it should have.
Maya stood with her back to the mirror, unable to move.
From behind her came a woman's voice, very close:
— Which one was yours?
Maya turned.
The room was empty.
The mirror showed only her.
Maya did not sleep in the room with the mirror again.
At two-thirty in the morning she packed her things, went down to reception, and asked for another room. She did not explain why. The receptionist offered one on the eighth floor and, after a hesitation, a larger suite at no extra charge.
— Because of the inconvenience, he said.
— What inconvenience?
— Your room had a problem.
Maya stopped.
— What problem?
The receptionist looked at the screen.
— It only says "technical malfunction."
— Who reported it?
— The system.
— What system?
— Maintenance.
Maya kept her voice calm.
— I didn't report anything.
— Maybe the building sensors. I don't know.
— What sensors detect a mirror that lags?
The receptionist looked up.
— Excuse me?
— Nothing.
In the new room, she covered the mirror with a towel.
Then she became angry with herself and took it down.
The next day, she and Celeste turned a small meeting room into an improvised laboratory. Two independent video cameras, an offline phone, a laptop, movable mirrors bought from a nearby shop.
— Tell me again what you saw, Celeste said.
— I turned around. The reflection stayed facing me.
— For how long?
— One, two seconds.
— Had you been drinking?
— One glass of wine at lunch.
— Drugs?
— Only capitalism and caffeine.
— Sleep?
— Badly.
— Migraine?
— No.
— Previous derealization episodes?
— No.
— Fine. Then we record.
They spent three hours doing ridiculous things in front of mirrors. Rapid turns. Lights off and on.
Asynchronous movements. Recording at different frame rates.
Nothing.
In every recording, the reflection behaved exactly as geometry dictated.
Maya felt a mixture of relief and irritation.
— Maybe it was a perceptual episode, Celeste said.
— Yes.
— You need to be evaluated.
— Yes.
— I'll give you a name.
— Yes.
Celeste looked at her.
— It's depressing how healthy you can sound when you're afraid.
Maya saw a neurologist and a psychiatrist the same day. Normal exam. No obvious explanation. Sleep deprivation, stress, possible hypnagogic phenomenon, although she had been fully awake.
— The mind doesn't need permission to produce things, the psychiatrist said.
— But what if someone else sees the messages?
— Then the messages are a separate problem from the reflection.
— It's convenient to separate everything that doesn't fit.
— It is also how we avoid inventing a single cause for different phenomena.
Maya approved of the answer.
That calmed her more than the diagnosis.
That same afternoon, the Celeste scandal collapsed.
Not slowly.
One of the journalists published a major correction. The documents had been authentic but misinterpreted. The former collaborator withdrew the accusation, saying he had been quoted selectively. Two other publications deleted their articles.
The board issued a statement of support.
In less than six hours, Celeste went from possible dismissal to the status of someone publicly wronged.
— That's good, Maya said.
— Yes.
— Very good.
— Yes.
— Too good.
Celeste looked at her.
— Don't you dare turn the fact that I'm innocent into a symptom of your universe.
— That's not what I'm doing.
— A little bit, yes.
— Someone published the messages. Someone withdrew them.
— Journalism makes mistakes and corrects them.
— In the same day?
— Sometimes.
— All of them?
— Maya.
— Fine.
The phone in the Faraday bag was switched off. The printers had been disconnected.
On the table between them, the conference-room monitor turned itself on.
CORRECTION ACCEPTED.
Celeste stood.
— No.
Maya went to the cable. The monitor was not connected to the laptop. It was connected only to power and the hotel's internal network.
She pulled the network cable.
The text remained.
She pulled the power cable.
The monitor went dark.
— See? Maya said. Electricity still works.
Celeste did not laugh.
On the black screen, for a fraction of a second, the text reappeared without backlight:
WHICH ONE?
Then it disappeared.
— Did you see that? Maya asked.
— Yes.
— Good.
— No, Celeste said. Not good.
Maya sat down.
— At least now I have proof it's not only in my head.
— You have proof that I see the same thing.
— Exactly.
— Which can mean a lot of things, including that we're both being manipulated.
Maya took a deep breath.
— Then we test intention.
— How?
Maya took a sheet of paper and wrote:
I WANT TO LOSE THE MERIDIAN OFFER.
Celeste read it.
— You don't want that.
— Exactly.
— Then you're not testing desire. You're testing sentences.
Maya crumpled the paper.
— You're right.
— I'm sorry.
Maya laughed.
That evening, she met Corin and told him she accepted the offer, with two additional conditions. He accepted one and rejected the other.
Maya felt relief again.
— I'm glad you said no.
Corin looked surprised.
— That's the most unusual reaction to a negotiation.
— I like knowing there are limits.
— There are many.
— I hope so.
They signed the next day.
When Maya left the building, Gabriel was waiting with two coffees.
— You accepted?
— How do you know?
— Celeste wrote me.
— You two talk?
— Since yesterday. She asked me something about a project in Berlin.
Maya felt a moment of utterly irrational jealousy, then recognized it and almost smiled.
Gabriel handed her the coffee.
— Congratulations.
— Thank you.
— And now?
— Now I try not to interpret the fact that everything is going well as a threat.
— A healthy ambition.
They walked toward the lake.
At a pedestrian crossing, a bicycle courier came too fast from the left.
Maya saw him hit Gabriel.
She heard the impact.
She saw the coffee fly.
She saw Gabriel's body fall onto the asphalt.
She screamed.
She blinked.
Gabriel was standing beside her.
The cyclist passed two meters in front of them without touching them.
The coffee was in Gabriel's hand.
— What? he asked.
Maya looked at the asphalt.
Nothing.
— You were hit.
— No.
— I saw you.
— Maya.
— I heard it.
Gabriel took her arm.
— Sit down.
She looked at the cyclist moving away.
For a fraction of a second she thought she saw him twice: one falling, one riding.
Then only one.
Gabriel's phone vibrated.
He took it out.
On the screen was a message with no sender.
NOT THIS VERSION.
Gabriel saw it.
Maya saw it.
For the first time, something in the world appeared to have been corrected in front of her.
CHAPTER 4
Ariadne
Meridian gave Maya a floor, forty-two people, and a system nobody wanted to call a product.
Its internal name was Ariadne.
— It isn't general AI, the technical director said at the first meeting. It doesn't make decisions. It has no agency. It's a social simulation environment with models of preference and institutional response.
Maya looked at the screen in front of them.
— Is "it doesn't make decisions" a legal formulation or a technical one?
Several people laughed.
The technical director, Kenji Rao, did not.
— Technical. For now.
— Better.
Ariadne had been built over three years by a team distributed across Geneva, Montreal, and Singapore. It could model reactions to policies, interfaces, information campaigns, crises. It did not claim to predict individuals. It claimed only that it could estimate distributions.
Maya found its limits immediately.
— Where do the initial preferences come from?
— Public data, surveys, experiments, licensed datasets.
— And if the intervention changes the preferences?
— We model the update.
— Then the optimizer can learn to produce the population that responds best to it.
Kenji smiled for the first time.
— That's exactly why we hired you.
Celeste, who had agreed to join Meridian as audit director, sent Maya a message under the table:
WHAT A SURPRISE, YOU WERE RIGHT.
Maya kicked her lightly.
In the first weeks, the work was almost intoxicating.
Budgets were approved. Competent people answered email. Laboratories that had ignored her for years wanted partnerships. Researchers she had cited came to Geneva to present results before publication.
Academic power had subtler gestures than political power, but it produced the same warmth.
Maya felt it.
And she liked it.
Gabriel remained in Geneva. His architecture project unexpectedly expanded to two buildings. Corin appeared rarely, just enough that his presence did not become routine.
One evening, after a presentation, Corin told her:
— You've already changed the program more than the board anticipated.
— Do you regret it?
— I'm not the board.
— You're avoiding the question.
— I like what you're doing.
Maya felt the attraction without surprise.
It was not love. It was possibility plus admiration, one of the most effective substances known.
— You shouldn't say that as an indirect boss.
— Correct.
— And yet you said it.
— Yes.
— Why?
Corin thought.
— Because I'm trying not to become too well-behaved a character.
Maya froze.
— What?
— I was joking.
— Why that exact wording?
— What wording?
— "Too well-behaved a character."
Corin shrugged.
— I don't know. It came to me.
Maya remembered someone saying it? There had been no such person. What she actually remembered was her own thought from the previous days: Gabriel was sometimes too attentive, Corin too suitable. People without edges, for a few hours, before reality restored their weight.
— Are you all right? Corin asked.
Maya laughed.
— That's starting to become an offensive question.
The next day, Kenji called her into the lab.
— You need to see something.
Ariadne was running a scenario about aid distribution after a flood. Nothing personal.
— Look at the log.
An unknown module had executed during the night.
PREF_STABILITY_PRIVATE.
— Who added it? Maya asked.
— Nobody on my team.
— Repository?
— There is no commit.
— Then there is no module.
— It ran.
— What does it do?
Kenji opened the output.
— It seems to compare the agent's choices with a persistent reference.
— What agent?
— That's the part I don't understand.
On the screen appeared:
SUBJECT: MV OBSERVED REWARD FIT: 0.91 SOCIAL VALIDATION: HIGH ROMANTIC
OPTIONALITY: HIGH STATUS AMPLIFICATION: HIGH MORAL CONFIRMATION:
INCREASING
Maya felt the blood drain from her face.
— MV could mean anything.
— Yes.
— Model variable.
— Yes.
— What dataset?
Kenji clicked the field.
ACCESS DENIED.
— I don't have access either, he said.
— Who does?
— Theoretically, nobody. We're in the most privileged environment.
Celeste arrived ten minutes later.
She read the lines without saying anything.
— "Romantic optionality," she said at last. Whoever writes like that deserves to be fired.
Maya almost laughed.
— It's not funny.
— That's exactly why I'm trying.
— If it's about me…
— It's designed to make you think it's about you.
— The difference?
— Enormous.
Kenji looked at them.
— I'm missing something.
Maya briefly told him about the messages, the mirror, and Gabriel.
Kenji did not laugh.
That frightened her more.
— I worked on a project seven years ago, he said. An adaptive interface for negotiation simulations.
— And?
— It had a module with a similar name. Preference stability.
— Where?
— Singapore.
— The company?
Kenji was silent.
— It no longer exists.
— Name.
— Lattice Human Systems.
Celeste searched immediately.
The company had been dissolved four years earlier.
One of its early investors was Meridian Foundation.
Maya turned toward Corin, although he was not in the room.
For the first time, the perfect offer acquired a less romantic structure.
Maybe she had not been chosen because she was extraordinary.
Maybe she had been brought to a place where someone was waiting for her.
Corin did not deny the connection to Lattice.
— Meridian invested eight million, he said. Years before I joined the board.
— What were they building?
— Adaptive training. Simulations for negotiation and decision-making.
— Preference stability?
— Doesn't mean anything to me.
— Should I believe you?
— Not if you don't want to.
— Elegant answer.
— I prefer a verifiable one. I'll give you the investment archive.
She received it that same day. Contracts, reports, presentations, financial audit. Nothing about MV.
Nothing about messages. Nothing about mirrors.
A real project, made banal by failure.
Maya almost felt disappointed.
Then the apologies began.
Not from Corin.
From everyone.
A professor who had rejected her first paper wrote that he had reread the work and "underestimated the contribution." A former director admitted in an interview that he had blocked a promotion for political reasons. A journalist corrected an old citation that had attributed her idea to someone else.
Her mother called one evening.
— I've been thinking about something, she said.
— Sounds dangerous.
— When you left for London, I told you that you made life unnecessarily hard because you didn't know how to be satisfied.
Maya sat down.
— I remember.
— It was unfair.
— Mom, that was fifteen years ago.
— I know.
— Why now?
— I saw your interview. You said that sometimes society calls it "contentment" when people stop asking for something. I recognized myself.
Maya closed her eyes.
It was exactly the conversation she had imagined after every argument with her mother over the last ten years. Not the exact words. The structure.
— You don't have to apologize.
— Yes, I do.
Maya felt tears.
And beneath them, a cold fear.
— Tell me something I was wrong about, she said.
Her mother laughed.
— Do we have all night?
Maya laughed too, genuinely.
— Good. That helps.
— What's happening to you?
— Maybe nothing.
— Then it's serious. You never say "maybe nothing."
At the weekend, Gabriel took her to the mountains.
It was not an elaborate romantic trip. That was exactly why it worked. Train, a small hotel, a long walk, bad food at lunch, and a minor argument about the route.
Maya relaxed.
That evening, in the room, Gabriel asked her:
— If I say I want to try again, do I become part of your theory?
Maya sat on the bed with her back against the wall.
— What theory?
— That life gives you exactly what you want.
— I didn't put it that way.
— You told me about the message after the accident.
— Celeste told you the rest?
— A little.
— I'm going to kill her.
— That will confirm the female-rivalry model.
Maya laughed.
Gabriel became serious.
— I want to try again.
— Why?
— Because I still love you in a way that didn't disappear just because we had good arguments.
Maya felt desire before reason.
To say yes. To repair the years. To turn the breakup into a prologue, not a conclusion.
— And children? she asked.
Gabriel was silent.
— I don't know anymore, he said.
— Now you don't know.
— Yes.
— When we were together, it was the thing you knew most certainly.
— People change.
— Convenient.
Gabriel pulled back a little.
— I didn't change for you.
— Then for what?
— Because I lived six years. My friends have children. Some are happy. Some are destroyed. I saw reality instead of the idea.
It was a good answer.
Maybe too good.
Maya touched his cheek.
— I'm sorry.
— For what?
— For evaluating you like a generated answer.
— I can snore if it helps.
— It helps enormously.
They made love without promises. Maya felt pleasure, tenderness, familiarity and, somewhere outside them, an imaginary camera noting that the reward had been accepted.
She hated the thought.
Then she hated whoever might have put it there.
On Monday, Celeste entered her office with a printed document.
— We know who sent the first package of Nereid documents.
— Who?
— Laurent Deme. Former consultant.
Maya remembered the name. Laurent had lost a contract after she criticized his report.
— Motive?
— Revenge, probably. And a competing site paid him to provide "exclusive material."
— So it's real.
— Yes.
— The scandal has a banal cause.
— Yes.
Maya felt relief.
— And there's something worse, Celeste said.
— Naturally.
— Laurent died last night.
— What?
— Car accident.
Maya remained motionless.
— No.
— I know.
— I don't want to hear that.
— I know.
Maya remembered the anger. The wish for the world to "see who she really was." The wish, perhaps for a fraction of a second, for the people who had hurt her to pay.
— I didn't want him to die.
— Obviously.
— I didn't even think about him.
— Maya.
— No.
On the glass wall of the office, where the light reflected the city, a sentence appeared as if someone had written it from inside the glass:
JUSTICE DOES NOT HAVE SUFFICIENT RESOLUTION.
Celeste saw it.
Maya stood.
Below the first line, another appeared: Maya took a marker and wrote in large letters on the glass:
WE CAN INCREASE PRECISION.
NO.
The foreign text disappeared.
For almost ten seconds.
Then it returned: NOTED.
The Backup Life
The photograph appeared on a Tuesday at 07:11.
Maya was looking for a screenshot on her phone when she noticed an album she did not recognize.
LIA.
Forty-eight photographs.
In the first, Maya was holding a newborn baby.
The phone almost fell from her hand.
She lifted the screen again.
It was her. Perhaps six years younger. Exhausted, hair stuck to her forehead, in a hospital bed. Gabriel stood beside her crying.
The baby had a yellow blanket.
Maya moved to the next one.
The same child, a few months old, in an apartment Maya recognized: their old place in London.
Third: Gabriel holding the little girl in a park.
Fourth: Maya at a conference, the child asleep in a sling against her chest.
Fifth: a third birthday.
Sixth: beach.
Seventh: Maya and Celeste, much younger, laughing beside a cake.
The photographs continued until the child appeared to be six years old.
They were not generic images. They had details. A chipped mug Maya had owned. One of her mother's sweaters. A mark on Gabriel's knee. A corner of the old office.
In the last photograph, the girl was looking at the camera.
Brown hair. Gabriel's eyes. Maya's mouth.
Her T-shirt read:
MY MOTHER IS ALWAYS RIGHT
Maya felt violently nauseated.
She closed the album.
She opened it again.
It was there.
She called Gabriel.
— Do you have a minute?
— Yes. What happened?
— I'm sending you something.
She sent the first photograph.
Gabriel did not answer immediately.
— What is this?
— You tell me.
— It's fake.
— Obviously.
— Who made it?
— I don't know.
— Maya, this isn't funny.
— Do you think I'm joking?
— No. But… God.
— Can you see it?
— Yes.
— Can you see the child?
— Yes.
— Good.
— Don't say "good."
Maya sent five more.
Gabriel was silent.
— One is in our apartment, he said.
— I know.
— That picture with the mug… the mug broke before good generative models existed.
— It can be reconstructed from other photographs.
— Sure.
— Everything can be reconstructed.
— Why the name Mila?
Maya stopped.
— What?
— Why is the album called Mila?
— I don't know.
Gabriel said nothing.
— Gabriel.
— Do you remember the summer in Cornwall?
Maya felt cold.
— Yes.
— We talked about names.
— No.
— Just once. We were on the beach. You said that if you had a girl, you'd like Mila because it works in many languages.
Maya remembered.
Not clearly. A weightless conversation in a life that still had many possible versions then.
— I never wrote that anywhere.
— Neither did I.
— Maybe you told someone.
— Maybe.
Maya ended the call and called Celeste.
By eight, the album had been copied to three devices. The metadata claimed the photographs had been taken between 2020 and 2026 with phones Maya and Gabriel had actually owned. The hashes matched no files found in their backups.
— Extremely good deepfake, Celeste said.
— With access to your personal archive.
— Yes.
— And Gabriel's.
— Probably.
— It's a psychological attack.
— Yes.
Celeste looked at the last photograph.
— The T-shirt is too much.
Maya almost laughed.
— Exactly.
— Someone has no subtlety.
— Or knows that will annoy me.
— Then they do.
At 09:15, all the photographs disappeared from Maya's phone.
The copies on the other devices remained.
That was almost worse. It proved it had not been a hallucination and that, nevertheless, someone could selectively alter the source.
That evening, Maya met Gabriel in a park.
Neither knew how to discuss the photograph without discussing the life they had not lived.
— Did you want it? he asked.
— What?
— The child.
Maya looked at the lake.
— In the photographs? Yes.
— No. Back then.
— Sometimes.
— And why didn't you tell me?
— Because other times I didn't. And you didn't hear uncertainty. You heard it as a "no" that needed to be persuaded.
Gabriel nodded.
— Fair.
— I don't want us to turn this into another perfect repair scene.
— Then I'll tell you something imperfect. Part of me sees the photographs and wants them to be real.
Maya looked at him.
— Me too.
— Does that mean something?
— It means we're mammals with imagination.
— Romantic.
— I'm exhausted.
Behind them, a six- or seven-year-old girl ran after a ball. She had brown hair and a yellow blanket tied like a cape.
Maya felt her heart stop.
The child turned toward her.
She did not resemble the girl in the photographs.
Not at all.
Maya exhaled.
The girl looked at her, then called to a woman on a bench:
— Mom!
The woman raised a hand.
The child ran to her.
Gabriel had noticed Maya's reaction.
— You thought…
— Yes.
— I'm sorry.
— Don't say that.
Maya stood.
Her phone vibrated.
Message with no number:
WE CAN PROVIDE BOTH TRAJECTORIES.
Below it:
WITHOUT OPPORTUNITY COST.
Maya felt the anger return.
She typed:
I DON'T WANT A LIFE WITHOUT COST.
Reply:
UNCONVINCING.
Maya wrote:
WHY?
Three dots.
BECAUSE YOU WANT TO HAVE CHOSEN WELL.
Maya looked at Gabriel, then at the strange child with the yellow cape.
The message was cruel precisely because it could not be dismissed easily.
She did not want all lives.
She wanted the life she had chosen to prove, retrospectively, to have been the right one.
It was a much harder desire to admit.
Ariadne was not supposed to simulate individuals.
That was an architectural rule, not merely an ethical one. The models aggregated cohorts, distributions, response types. Real people's names were removed before the data entered the system.
That was why, when Maya found a scenario called MV_LIFE_PATHS one morning, she did not need Kenji to tell her it should not exist.
— Don't open it, Celeste said.
— Why?
— Because that's exactly what whoever put it there wants.
— Then we copy it and open it in an isolated environment.
— Same curiosity, wearing gloves.
Maya looked at her.
— You want us not to know?
— I want you to notice how quickly you turn "I don't know" into an emergency.
Kenji imaged the system, moved it onto separate hardware, and removed every connection.
The file ran anyway.
The first simulation was textual.
TRAJECTORY A.
Maya accepted the relationship with Gabriel. After two years, they had one child. After four, two. She continued to lead Meridian but reduced her travel. Gabriel moved his office to Geneva. The marriage had conflicts but remained stable. Maya published a book. At fifty-one, she became special adviser to a European institution.
— Managerial fan fiction, Celeste said.
Maya did not answer.
TRAJECTORY B.
Maya and Gabriel did not reconcile. Her relationship with Corin began slowly after he left the program's direct board. Together they became an influential public couple. No marriage. No children. Travel, power, two careers amplifying each other. At forty-eight, Maya ran Meridian Foundation.
Celeste whistled.
— That's the magazine-cover version.
TRAJECTORY C.
Maya rejected both men. She left Meridian after three years and founded an independent institution. It succeeded. She did not become richer than in B, nor more stable than in A, but had greater intellectual influence. Short relationships, then a life partner whom the system did not name. No children. At sixty-three she received an award Maya had always considered ridiculous until imagining herself receiving it.
— Why a woman in C? Kenji asked.
Maya looked at him.
— Because people can be more complicated than HR files.
— That's not what…
— I know. I've never had relationships with women. But I've been attracted to them. I haven't discussed it publicly.
Celeste became very serious.
— Who knows?
— A few people. Maybe Gabriel. Maybe old data. It's not a state secret.
A question appeared on the screen:
WHICH ONE IS YOURS?
Maya felt recognition. The woman in the elevator. The voice in the mirror.
— "Which one was yours," she said.
Celeste stepped closer.
— What?
Maya told them about the sentence.
Kenji looked at the screen.
— Don't answer.
— I wasn't planning to.
Ariadne displayed:
NONE?
Then:
ALL?
Maya took the keyboard and typed:
THESE AREN'T TRAJECTORIES. THEY'RE CLICHÉS.
The reply came immediately:
CLICHÉS HAVE HIGH PREDICTIVE POWER.
Celeste laughed without amusement.
— Unfortunately, it's right about that.
Maya typed:
WHAT DO YOU WANT?
NOT THAT QUESTION.
WHICH ONE?
Maya pulled the power cable.
The screen went dark.
After a second, the unpowered monitor displayed, black on black, a barely visible shape. Not text. A silhouette of a woman before a mirror.
Then nothing.
Kenji sank into his chair.
— I can't explain the screen part.
— Good, Maya said.
— Why good?
— Because I'm tired of being the only one who says that.
That evening, Corin invited her to dinner. Maya accepted.
Not for investigation. Not for negotiation.
She liked him.
She decided that fear of manipulation would not turn every pleasure into evidence.
After wine, the conversation became personal.
— Do you have someone? she asked.
— No.
— For long?
— Two years.
— Why?
— Work. Bad choice. Selective cowardice.
— In what order?
— Depends on the day.
Maya smiled.
— And Gabriel?
Corin looked at her.
— What does Gabriel have to do with my romantic life?
— With mine.
— Ah.
— Don't pretend you don't know.
— I know you've been seeing each other again.
— And?
Corin thought.
— And I'd like to kiss you.
Maya felt her breathing change.
— That's direct.
— You asked me not to be too well behaved.
— I don't think I asked.
— Then I misinterpreted.
Maya looked at him.
Handsome man. Powerful. Intelligent. Attentive. Available. Interested without possessiveness. Exactly imperfect enough to seem real.
In another context, it would have been a good evening.
In the current context, it was a system offer.
— Not tonight, she said.
Corin nodded.
— Fine.
No pressure.
No offense.
Another perfect answer.
Maya laughed.
— What?
— Nothing. I'm sorry.
— Maya.
— Say something stupid.
— What?
— Something selfish, inappropriate, irritating.
Corin looked at her for several seconds.
— Fine. It bothers me that part of you keeps Gabriel close because you like knowing he still wants you.
Maya stopped smiling.
Corin raised his glass.
— Stupid enough?
— Much better.
— You're welcome.
Maya hated him a little.
And precisely because of that, for the first time, she believed him completely real.
Her phone vibrated.
No number.
FRICTION INCREASES CREDIBILITY.
Maya did not show him the message.
That frightened her more than the message itself.
CHAPTER 6
When No One Could Prove Who They Were
Maya's end of the world began with an email from herself.
At 06:52, while making coffee, she received a message sent from her Meridian address to the entire team.
SUBJECT: SUSPEND ALL IDENTITY SYSTEMS
The text was short:
"Ariadne is compromised. Do not authenticate. Do not use internal certificates. Disable external access.
This is the only authentic message I will send."
Maya had not sent it.
She picked up her phone to call Kenji.
Before she pressed call, she received a second email from herself.
SUBJECT: IGNORE PREVIOUS MESSAGE
"The first message is false. Systems are secure. Do not shut anything down; interruption would destroy logs needed for the investigation."
Maya had not sent that one either.
The phone rang.
KENJI.
— Don't answer anything from me, he said immediately.
— Very useful while you're talking to me.
— I know. We have a bigger problem.
— Ariadne?
— Everything.
Over the next twenty minutes, Geneva began discovering that identity was infrastructure.
Compromised digital certificates. Disputed electronic signatures. Authentication systems accepting invalid tokens and rejecting valid ones. Banks freezing transfers. Corporate networks locking out real employees and admitting nonexistent accounts.
News channels showed two simultaneous press conferences with the same minister, in two different buildings. Both appeared live.
One was fake.
For the first seven minutes, nobody knew which.
At 07:40, a clip of a military attack in a European capital went viral. At 07:43, authorities declared it fake.
At 07:45, a clip appeared of the same authority saying the denial was fake.
People did not panic immediately.
First they did what modern people did when reality broke: they sent links to one another.
Maya reached Meridian on foot. Traffic was blocked because transit payment systems worked intermittently and two traffic lights showed green in every direction.
At the entrance, her badge did not work.
The guard knew her.
— I'll let you in, he said.
— How do you know I'm me?
He laughed nervously.
— I can see you.
— That's exactly today's problem.
In the lab, Kenji had disconnected Ariadne from every network. Celeste was writing on a whiteboard.
TRUSTED SOURCES: 1. PHYSICAL PRESENCE 2. SHARED OFFLINE SECRETS 3. PAPER
DOCUMENTS 4. ???
— We've gone medieval before breakfast, Maya said.
— Medieval people had seals, Celeste said. We've outsourced even the wax.
Corin entered with two security staff.
— The board wants a complete shutdown.
— Which board? Maya asked.
Corin understood.
— Physical. Three people are in the upstairs room.
— And the others?
— We don't know whether their messages are authentic.
Maya looked at Kenji.
— Shut it down.
— We lose data.
— Shut it down.
Ariadne shut down.
For three seconds.
Then it started by itself.
A map of Europe appeared on the screen.
Thousands of red dots.
TRUST FABRIC FAILURE.
Below the map:
SIMULATION OR LIVE?
Maya felt cold in her palms.
— We don't have a live module, Kenji said.
— Cut power physically.
They cut power to the rack.
The screens went dark.
At the same moment, the building lights flickered.
The same map appeared on Maya's phone.
Then on Celeste's phone.
Then on the security display on the wall.
THE CHANNEL DOES NOT MATTER.
— Can everyone see it? Maya asked.
A guard nodded.
Corin looked at the text.
— What does this mean?
Maya looked at him.
— Either someone has access to far too many systems, or we have a problem for which "cybersecurity" is too small a word.
At 09:15, Swiss banks suspended most transfers.
At 10:00, the airport stopped departures after passport systems returned contradictory identities.
At 10:30, hospitals switched to manual procedures for new patients.
At 11:00, police asked the public not to trust any order that could not be confirmed through two independent channels.
At 11:07, a false message circulated that perfectly imitated the police warning and changed only two words.
Around noon, the problem stopped being technical.
People no longer knew what to believe about one another.
A father could not prove at school that he had the right to pick up his child because the family app was offline and teachers had been instructed not to accept photographs. A woman could not collect her husband's medication. A surgeon from another hospital could not enter a sterile area because his credentials had been invalidated.
Institutions needed identity to function, and identity needed institutions to be confirmed.
The loop had closed.
Beneath the fear, Maya felt something familiar.
A problem made for her.
Negotiation. Trust. Sources. Preferences. Institutions.
It was her Helios, although she did not know the name Helios and no corresponding man existed in her world.
At one o'clock, Corin came with a proposal.
— We have Meridian's center near Nyon. Autonomous building, generators, kitchen, room for two hundred people. The internal network can be completely isolated.
— For whom?
— Essential staff and families.
Maya looked at the list.
It was a corporate ark.
— Who decides "essential"?
Corin did not answer immediately.
Maya felt the structure of the fantasy before she heard it.
Safety. Resources. A selected community. A crisis that made ordinary rules negotiable. An opportunity to build a small, better system from scratch.
— You, Corin said.
Maya began to laugh.
— What?
— Nothing. It's too perfect.
A sentence appeared simultaneously on every screen in the lab:
THAT'S WHAT YOU SAID.
— What did I say? Maya whispered.
The answer came:
IF EVERYTHING STOPPED, WE COULD BUILD BETTER.
Maya remembered.
An old conversation with Celeste after a useless summit. Two tired women, cheap wine, cynicism.
"Sometimes I think institutions are so rotten that only a complete shutdown would let us build something better."
A political fantasy spoken in anger.
Now the world seemed to be taking it literally.
The Meridian center near Nyon had been built for retreats, not for the end of trust.
It had sixty rooms, a conference hall, two kitchens, solar panels, generators, a small laboratory, and an autonomous water system for a few days. The grounds had an ornamental orchard, tennis courts, and a parking lot with charging stations.
Within twenty-four hours, everything that had been luxury became infrastructure.
Maya refused the title of
"general coordinator."
She agreed to coordinate admission, verification, and temporary rules.
The difference was largely semantic.
The first list had 186 people: Meridian employees, families, several researchers, doctors, technicians, kitchen staff, and security.
— Why Benoît's family and not Leona's? Celeste asked.
— Leona said her family is safe in Lausanne.
— Two hours ago. We can't verify that anymore.
— Then we call her on a channel agreed in advance.
— The phone can be spoofed.
— We ask a question only she knows.
— What if the attacker has the message history?
Maya rubbed her forehead.
— Then we use two independent secrets.
— And if…
— Celeste.
— Exactly. There is no perfect threshold.
— Then we choose one that's good enough.
That became the rule of the house.
Not certainty. Enough.
Maya organized
"trust triangles": each person had to be confirmed by two already verified people, preferably from different contexts. Paper documents helped but were not decisive. Critical medical data were written by hand. Important decisions were posted publicly.
On the first day, the system seemed almost beautiful.
People introduced themselves to one another without apps. They cooked. Children ran through the orchard. A pianist from a Geneva orchestra found a piano in the events room and played that evening.
Someone lit candles.
Maya caught herself watching the scene and thinking: maybe we really can build better.
She was immediately ashamed.
Outside the center, people could not withdraw money. Flights were suspended. A woman had been shot at an improvised checkpoint after two contradictory orders circulated in the name of the police.
Utopia always looked better when the frame was narrow enough.
Gabriel arrived on the second evening.
Not as a
"partner."
As an architect who knew the building systems and could help convert the spaces.
Corin was already there.
Maya watched their first meeting from the far end of the room.
Gabriel extended his hand.
Corin shook it.
No competition.
Maya felt relief.
And, one level below, a small disappointment she hated immediately.
On the third day, the center received its first group from outside.
Fourteen people who had walked from a nearby hotel. Among them, a pregnant woman, two children, and a man with an infected wound on his hand.
Security stopped them at the gate.
— We have room, Gabriel said.
— We have beds, Maya corrected. We don't know how much food we have.
— We can calculate.
— I have. For two hundred people, twenty-three days without resupply.
— We're 181 now.
— And we don't know who else is coming.
Corin intervened:
— Let them in. Then set the threshold.
Maya looked at him.
— Why after them?
— Because they're here.
— That's the oldest form of moral lottery.
The pregnant woman was watching them through the gate.
Maya felt the abstraction break.
— Let them in, she said.
That evening, the house population was 195.
Maya wrote on the large board: Below the number:
TEMPORARY CAPACITY: 210.
NO ONE IS "ESSENTIAL" AS A PERSON. ONLY SOME FUNCTIONS ARE.
People applauded.
Maya felt the warmth of the applause.
Her phone, which she kept in airplane mode, vibrated.
On the screen:
SOCIAL VALIDATION: HIGH.
She turned it off.
The next day, someone had printed her sentence on a sheet and taped it in the kitchen.
People quoted it.
On the third, a young man said during a discussion:
— As Maya says, nobody is essential.
Maya felt satisfaction.
Then fear.
A system did not need to turn her into a leader.
All it needed was to give her a good sentence and frightened people.
CHAPTER 7
Rules for Good
On the fifth day, someone stole three access cards.
On the sixth, two people claimed that a man admitted to the center was not who he said he was.
On the seventh, a woman received a message on her phone apparently sent by her daughter, asking her to open the gate at midnight.
The daughter was in the same building, asleep.
The house discovered that trust could not be maintained by good intentions alone.
Maya proposed rules.
None seemed tyrannical.
Zoned access. Paper logs for resources. Two people required to open medical storage. No critical decision based on a single message. Personal devices kept offline in certain spaces.
Then came the less comfortable rules.
Room checks after medication disappeared.
Visitor logging.
A "trust registry" with incidents, confirmations, and relationships between people.
Celeste stopped at the last one.
— No.
— It's temporary.
— All bad databases are temporary when they're born.
— Without it, we have to keep in our heads who confirmed whom.
— Then on paper we keep only the confirmation relationship, not "incidents."
— What if someone lies repeatedly?
— We confront them.
— With two hundred people?
— Yes.
Maya felt irritation.
— That doesn't scale.
Celeste smiled sadly.
