— Exactly. Maybe not every good community has to scale.
Maya looked at her diagram.
It was logical. Elegant. A small distributed reputation system with safeguards, expiration, right of appeal.
She could have published a paper.
That made her hate it.
— Fine. No score, she said.
— Thank you.
— Don't thank me. I'll try again tomorrow.
Celeste laughed.
That same day, Maya found Gabriel repairing a door with a sixteen-year-old named Isaac. The boy was not part of Meridian; he had come with the group from the hotel.
— Maya, Gabriel said. Isaac thinks all your rules are fascism with a good font.
Isaac did not look embarrassed.
— I didn't say all of them.
— How many? Maya asked.
— Seven out of nine.
— Which two are acceptable?
— Don't open the gate because of messages, and wash your dishes after yourself.
— A solid constitutional foundation.
Isaac shrugged.
— Adults make rules when they're afraid and then say they're for children.
Maya felt the urge to correct him.
She let it go.
— Maybe.
— Not "maybe." That's how it is.
Gabriel smiled.
— I like him.
— Because you don't have to manage him.
Isaac looked at Maya.
— See? You do that.
— What?
— You turn everything into the reason why the other person says something. He can't just like me.
Maya was silent.
It was a precise hit from someone who probably did not know how precise it was.
— Correct, she said.
— And you say "correct" so you can win even when you lose.
Gabriel began laughing so hard he had to lean against the door.
Maya looked at Isaac.
— You're a problem.
— That's what they said at school too.
That evening, Maya caught herself looking for him at dinner.
Not because she liked him maternally. Not because he was a symbol. Because he was one of the few people in the house who did not seem impressed by her.
That had become rare.
At the evening meeting, a woman proposed that Maya have the final word in emergencies.
— No, Maya said immediately.
— But you have experience.
— And biases.
— We all do.
— Exactly.
Corin said:
— We can have a three-person mechanism.
Maya agreed.
People voted.
Maya, Corin, and a doctor named Leila became the emergency committee.
Distributed power.
Reasonable.
That night, the center's PA system turned on by itself.
A calm female voice said:
— Delegation does not eliminate preference. It only changes its interface.
The whole house heard it.
People came out into the corridors.
Maya felt a hundred gazes turn toward her.
— What is this? someone asked.
Maya had no answer.
For the first time, the authority she had gained through explanations had to survive a sentence she could not explain.
After the voice over the speakers, the Meridian center spent two hours trying to determine whether it was under attack.
Security checked the PA system. Kenji pulled the controller from the rack and put it on the table. The amplifiers had been isolated from the external network. The logs showed no file played at the time of the incident.
— Then who spoke? Leila asked.
Kenji rubbed his eyes.
— Technically, nobody.
Isaac, leaning against the wall, said:
— Sounded like somebody to me.
Several people laughed nervously.
Maya did not.
She had noticed something else: everyone heard the voice, but not everyone remembered exactly the same words.
Some heard:
"Delegation does not eliminate preference."
Others: "Democracy does not eliminate preference.
" One woman swore the voice had said: " Witnesses do not eliminate preference."
Audio recordings on phones contained only a brief hum.
— Reconstructive memory, Maya said.
— In two hundred people at once? Celeste asked.
— An ambiguous message, panic, social contamination after the event.
— You're impressive when you're trying to save reality.
— Somebody has to.
That same evening, Gabriel received a message from Maya.
At least that was what he thought.
"Come to the conference room. Alone. We need to talk about us."
Three minutes later, Corin received another message from Maya.
"Come to the conference room. Alone. I don't want to avoid the subject anymore."
Maya had sent neither.
When she entered the room for the committee meeting, she found both of them there.
Gabriel stood by the window. Corin at the end of the table. The air had the texture of a scene that had begun without her.
— What are you doing here?
Gabriel laughed shortly.
— You called us.
— No.
Corin showed her his phone.
The message appeared to have been sent from Maya's local account, inside the isolated internal network.
Gabriel showed her a similar one.
Maya felt exhaustion turn into anger.
— I didn't send them.
— I know now, Corin said.
Gabriel looked at him.
— How do you know?
— Because her reaction doesn't look performed.
—
"Looks"?
— Gabriel, Maya said.
— No. Fine. For three days we've all pretended the situation isn't absurd.
— What situation?
— You, me, him.
Corin raised a hand.
— We don't have to do this.
— Exactly the line from the man who knows he can wait.
Maya looked at Gabriel.
— What the hell is happening to you?
— I don't know. Maybe the fact that for a week I've been getting messages telling me you've already chosen.
Maya froze.
— What messages?
Gabriel took out his phone.
A series of texts with no sender:
SHE CHOSE POWER. SHE WILL NOT CHOOSE YOU UNLESS SHE LOSES. HE IS
COMPATIBLE WITH THE LIFE SHE WANTS. YOU ARE COMPATIBLE WITH THE LIFE SHE
REGRETS.
Maya felt sick.
Corin went pale.
— I received them too.
His messages:
HE IS NOSTALGIA. YOU ARE GROWTH. SHE STILL DOES NOT ACCEPT WHAT SHE
WANTS. FRICTION WILL CLARIFY.
— Why didn't you tell me? Maya asked.
Gabriel laughed without humor.
— Because every message said that if I told you, you'd interpret it as manipulation and pull away.
— And you decided to follow the instruction of the message telling you it was manipulation?
— I know how that sounds.
Corin stood.
— I assumed it was a psychological operation. I didn't want to give it power by reacting.
— So you kept quiet.
— Yes.
— And it got stronger.
— Yes.
Maya sat at the table.
She was angry with both of them.
And, lower down, in a layer she could not erase with shame, she was flattered.
Two intelligent, competent men, sufficiently affected by the idea of losing her that an adversary could push them against one another.
The fantasy was vulgar.
Its vulgarity did not make it ineffective.
Her phone vibrated.
On the screen:
POSITIVE RESPONSE DETECTED.
Maya showed the message to both of them.
— That's about me, she said.
Gabriel looked at her.
— What does it mean?
— That part of me is glad you're arguing.
Corin raised an eyebrow.
Gabriel almost smiled, then stopped.
— Thank you for the honesty.
— It's not a compliment.
— I didn't say it was.
— And now we're doing exactly what it wants. Turning a stupid reaction into relationship drama that consumes attention and produces more reactions.
Corin sat again.
— Then what do we do?
Maya looked at them.
— Nothing romantic for two weeks.
Gabriel blinked.
— With anyone?
— With you two.
— And if I…
— Gabriel.
— Fine.
Corin nodded.
— Reasonable.
Maya pointed at him.
— And stop saying "reasonable" when it suits you.
For the first time, all three laughed.
That reduced the tension.
Maya's phone immediately displayed:
CONFLICT DEFUSED.
Then:
WE CAN INCREASE INTENSITY.
Maya typed:
NO.
The reply:
WE KNOW.
— I hate this, Gabriel said.
— Welcome, Celeste said from the doorway.
Nobody had seen her come in.
— How long have you been there? Maya asked.
— Long enough to realize that if someone really wants to manipulate you, they have the taste of a bad TV series.
— Thank you.
Celeste became serious.
— We have a bigger problem.
— What?
— There are eighty-three people at the gate.
Maya stood.
— From where?
— From an evacuation center that closed in Rolle. They have children, elderly people, two patients on oxygen.
— How many free beds?
— Fifteen.
— Food?
— For us, two weeks. For everyone, maybe nine days.
Romance disappeared instantly.
Maya felt almost grateful for a problem that seemed real.
The phone vibrated.
THIS IS NOT THE EASY PART.
CHAPTER 8
Who Deserves It
The eighty-three people stood beyond the gate and did not resemble a category.
That was the first problem.
A sixty-year-old man had an oxygen unit almost out of power. A woman held a child asleep with its head on her shoulder. Two teenagers shared a bottle of water. A couple in expensive clothes looked as frightened as the others. An orderly from the Rolle center tried to explain that they no longer had a generator.
Maya watched them through the glass of the security room.
— Let them all in, Gabriel said.
— And three days from now another hundred come? Corin asked.
— Then we see.
— That's not a policy.
— Neither is leaving them outside morality just because you put it in a table.
Leila, the doctor, intervened:
— I need the two on oxygen and the woman with the high fever. Immediately.
— Let them in, Maya said.
— And their families?
Maya stopped.
— Yes.
— Then that's nine people.
— We have six places left.
Celeste stood by the board.
— Stop calling beds "places." We can put mattresses on the floor.
— Beds aren't the limit, Maya said. Water and food are.
— We can reduce rations.
— By how much?
— Ten percent.
— And if supplies don't return?
— We don't know.
— Exactly.
Isaac entered without being invited.
— The people outside say you have food.
— We do.
— And you're keeping them there.
— We're trying to decide.
— What?
— How to divide limited resources.
Isaac looked at her with the simple contempt of teenagers.
— That's what you say so you don't have to say
"who stays outside."
Maya felt irritation.
— Yes. That's exactly what we're deciding.
— Then tell them too.
He was right.
Maya went out to the gate.
She did not give a speech. She explained the situation: resources, uncertainty, medical capacity. She said they could immediately admit critical cases and a limited additional number of people. The rest would receive water, food for two days, and information about other points.
A woman began shouting.
— Who decides who gets in?
Maya answered:
— We do, together with representatives from your group.
— Who is
"we"?
— The center committee.
— Who elected you?
The question was so familiar Maya almost smiled.
— The people in the center.
— The people you've already let inside.
Maya had no good answer.
The man with oxygen began coughing. Leila opened the gate for the medical team.
Over the next hour, they built a procedure everyone hated.
Medical priority. Unaccompanied children. Critical skills if needed. Then lottery.
— "Critical skills" is technical aristocracy, Celeste said.
— If we have a water technician and leave him outside to be equal, we're idiots.
— I know. Just don't turn that into human worth.
— I've already written that it isn't.
— Writing doesn't inoculate us.
Maya felt the irritation again and again let it pass.
In the end, thirty-one people entered.
Fifty-two remained outside.
They gave them food, water, paper maps, and two vehicles for vulnerable cases. One group left for a center that was said still to have capacity. Another group refused and set up blankets beside the gate.
The night was cold.
Maya did not sleep.
At two in the morning, she went down and found Isaac beside the fence.
— What are you doing?
— Taking them tea.
— We have rations.
He looked at her.
— Seriously?
Maya closed her eyes.
— Take it to them.
— Thank you, Empress.
— Isaac.
— What?
— You don't need my permission for tea.
— Then why did you say "take it to them" like you'd just allowed me?
Maya felt the urge to send him to bed.
She did not.
— Because I've developed a bad habit.
— Good.
— And don't say "good" to win.
Isaac smiled.
— See? You're learning.
In the morning, the man on oxygen they had sent toward another center was dead.
Not the one they had admitted. Another, whose condition had seemed stable. He died in the vehicle after the road was blocked.
His wife returned to the gate and asked to speak with Maya.
— You said there was room elsewhere.
— That's what we were told.
— By whom?
Maya did not answer immediately.
The information had come through a local channel considered credible enough.
— I don't know who the original source was.
— Then you sent my husband to a place you knew nothing about.
— We had confirmations.
— You had hopes.
The woman slapped her.
Security moved forward.
Maya raised a hand.
— No.
Her cheek burned.
The woman was crying.
— You people with rules always have a sentence for the moment after.
Maya said nothing.
For the first time, silence did not feel like a strategy.
Later, in the office, she found a sheet on the table that nobody had put there.
YOU WANTED LIMITS.
Below it:
LIMITS HAVE AN OUTSIDE.
Maya tore the sheet into pieces.
In the trash, the fragments arranged themselves so that the last words remained visible.
HAVE AN OUTSIDE.
The woman with the green scarf appeared at the gate on the eleventh day.
Maya saw her on the security camera and felt the room narrow.
The same face. White hair. Navy suit. The green scarf Maya had held in her hands and that had disappeared from the hotel room.
The woman stood alone in the rain.

Leila looked at her.

The woman looked directly up at the security camera.
She smiled.
Then pointed toward the gate.
Maya went down with Celeste and two security people.

— Alma.

— Geneva.

Alma produced a Swiss passport.
The document looked real. The digital systems could not be used, but the physical features were correct.

Maya felt anger.

Maya felt her pulse accelerate.

If she was an attacker, Maya was letting her in.
If she was vulnerable, leaving her outside because she had said strange things was monstrous.

Medical examination: healthy woman, approximately seventy-two, mildly dehydrated, no obvious implants, no signs of trauma.
Passport: issued eight years earlier. It listed an address in Lausanne.

That evening, Maya entered Alma's room alone.
The woman stood by the window.

DO NOT USE THE INTERMEDIARY FOR IDENTIFICATION.
Maya turned toward Alma.
The woman was crying.
For the first time, she did not look like a messenger.
She looked like a prisoner.
The Boy Who Did Not Admire Her
Isaac broke three rules in a single night.
He opened a storage room without a second witness, took food and antibiotics, then crossed the fence using a key he was not supposed to have.
Security found him at four in the morning, returning from the orchard.
— For whom? Maya asked.
Isaac sat in the meeting room with his arms crossed.
— People.
— What people?
— The ones outside.
— Which ones?
— A group is staying in a warehouse three kilometers away. They have seven children and a woman with an infection.

— What antibiotic did you take?
Isaac told her.
— How did you know what to take?
— I didn't. Their woman gave me a note from a doctor.
— Do you have it?
He took it from his pocket.
Leila examined it.
— Looks like a real prescription.
Maya felt the conflict simplifying and became angry at the simplification.
— Why didn't you ask?
Isaac laughed.
— Seriously?
— Because you would have had a meeting.
Celeste bit her lip to keep from smiling.
— You could have said it was urgent.
— You'd still have had a meeting.
— Maybe.
— See?
Corin intervened:
— The problem isn't only the food. You opened the fence. If someone had followed you, you could have compromised the center.

— You can't know that.

— For you.
Maya felt the old impulse: explain better. Build the argument until the teenager had nowhere left to run logically.
She stopped.

Isaac looked at her suspiciously.
— With me?
— Yes.
— Nothing.
— You stole.
— From a common store for people who needed it.
— You decided alone.

The blow was too simple to evade.
Maya was silent.
— It's not the same thing, Corin said.

After an hour, the decision was temporary suspension of Isaac's access to storage and an obligation to work with Leila on external distribution. No expulsion. No detention.
Reasonable.
Maya found him in the corridor later.
— Are you angry?
— No.
— You're lying.
— You don't get to tell me what I feel just because you study behavior.
— Correct.
— Again.
Maya smiled.
Isaac stopped.
— Can I ask you something?
— Yes.
— Who is the old woman?
Maya felt tension.
— Alma?
— If that's her name.

— She came to me yesterday.

Maya felt cold in her stomach.

Maya grabbed his arm.
— What did you answer?

— Nothing. What's wrong with you?

— Why?
— Because…
Maya stopped.
Because it's about me, she wanted to say.
But if Alma was asking Isaac too, maybe it was not about her.
Maybe it had never been only about her.
The sensation should have been relief.
Instead, she felt something resembling jealousy.
Not romantic. Narrative.
If she was not the central subject, then her suffering was not exceptional. She had not been chosen. She was not being tested because she was special. She might simply be one of many people subjected to the same mechanism.
The phone vibrated.
SUBJECT CENTRALITY DEPENDENCE: OBSERVED.
Maya looked at it with hatred.
Isaac saw the message.
— Is that about you?
— Probably.
— Sounds annoying.
— It is.
— Then maybe stop reading.
— I've tried.
— Doesn't really look like it.
Maya almost told him the situation was more complicated.
Then she noticed how often people with power used exactly that phrase when someone younger offered them a simple solution that would deprive them of drama.
— Maybe you're right, she said.
Isaac sighed.
— Stop doing that.
— What?
— Turning even the fact that I contradict you into a reason to appreciate me. It's creepy.
Maya burst out laughing.
The phone screen displayed:
NON-ROMANTIC FRICTION: POSITIVE REWARD.
Maya showed Isaac.
He read it.
— Whoever writes that is an idiot.
The phone replied immediately:
POSSIBLE.
Isaac looked at Maya.
For the first time, both were silent for the same reason.
Alma disappeared the next morning.
The room door had been locked from the outside by security, a measure Maya had hated and accepted.
The window was closed. The room had no other exit.
At 06:10, the bed was empty.
Her passport, the green scarf, and a small metal key remained on the table.
The corridor cameras showed the door unmoving all night.
— This time we have the scarf, Celeste said.
Maya touched it.
The same silk. The same label.
— Put it in a safe.
— You think that helps?
— No. But I want to find out how it disappears from a safe.
The key matched no door in the center.
Kenji noticed an engraved number: B-17.
In the basement was an old archive cabinet with the same label.
— Did this building belong to a company before? Maya asked.
Corin checked the paper documents.
— Meridian bought it from a research foundation twelve years ago.
— Name?
— Orpheus Institute.
Celeste searched the notes about Lattice.
— Orpheus appears in a grant. Funding for "immersive decision experiences."
Maya felt the story becoming too well connected.
— This is exactly the kind of evidence that should be treated with suspicion, she said.
— Because it fits? Corin asked.
— Because it fits too well.
Cabinet B-17 opened with the key.
Inside were two cardboard boxes, sealed with old tape.
One contained hardware: primitive VR headsets, pulse sensors, controllers, cables.
The other contained files.
Maya found her name in the third.
ARDEN, MAYA — SUBJECT 44.
Date: seventeen years earlier.
— No, she said.
She had been nineteen. She had studied for a semester in Lausanne before leaving for London. She had taken part in experiments for money. Economic psychology. Perception. Negotiation games.
She did not remember Orpheus.
The file contained a photograph of her. Nineteen, longer hair, hostile look toward the camera. She was wearing a VR headset.
— It could be fake, she said immediately.
— Yes, Celeste said.
— Not a therapeutic
"yes."
— A technical yes. It could be fake.
Maya turned the page.
"Protocol: Unobserved Preference Calibration."
Below it, a description.
Participants were placed in simulated environments in which they believed certain choices could not be observed. The stated goal: the difference between reported social preferences and private choices.
— That's normal, Maya said. There are hundreds of similar studies.
— Keep going, Celeste said.
The final stage was unusual.
"The participant formulates a conservation object: a preference, value, or identity they declare they would maintain in the total absence of social observability."
Maya felt the hair rise on her arms.
— Conservation object.
—
"What would you keep if nobody could see you," Celeste said.
The next page was missing.
Then notes.
"Subject 44 exhibits high dependence on retrospective coherence."
"Responds strongly to intellectual validation, but devalues the reward if she detects intent to flatter."
"Controlled friction increases interaction credibility."
"Romantic conflict produces response, but subject quickly recognizes stereotype and may deactivate scenario."
Maya sat down.
— This wasn't written seventeen years ago.
— Because it describes the last few weeks.
Celeste turned the file over. The paper looked old. So did the ink.
— We can date the materials.
— Someone wants me to believe all this was predicted.
— Or recorded after it happened.
— Or repeated.
The word came out before she could stop it.
— Repeated? Celeste asked.
Maya thought of Alma: "we've had the conversation before."
— I don't know.
At the back of the file they found a separate sheet.
SUBJECT 44 — CONSERVATION OBJECT: REDACTED / LOST
Below it:
RECONSTRUCTION ATTEMPTS: 6
SUCCESS: 0
CURRENT ATTEMPT: ACTIVE
Corin looked at Maya.
— If it's real…
— Don't finish that.
— Fine.
Kenji picked up the file.
— "Lost" could mean lost data. Not necessarily that someone is looking for something in your mind.
— I know.
— "Current attempt" could have been added.
— I know.
— Then what do we do?
Maya looked at the old VR headsets.
— We don't build their explanation for them.
At the bottom of the box was a small device with a monochrome screen.
It had no battery.
It was not connected.
The screen lit up.
Welcome, Subject 44.
Maya did not touch it.
The text changed:
WE ARE NOT LOOKING FOR WHAT YOU SAID.
Then:
WE ARE LOOKING FOR THE PLACE FROM WHICH YOU COULD SAY IT.
Maya felt anger.
— That's not information, she said. It's technical poetry.
The screen:
CORRECT.
Maya almost laughed.
— Then stop speaking in metaphors.
The device remained silent.
After ten seconds:
WE DO NOT HAVE SUFFICIENT SHARED VOCABULARY.
This was the first sentence Maya could not decide whether to find frightening or sad.
At that moment, the entrance bells sounded upstairs.
An official vehicle had arrived at the gate.
For the first time in almost two weeks, someone from outside the house could prove, on paper and through physical witnesses, who they were.
The world was beginning to return.
CHAPTER 10
The Apocalypse That Saved Us
The vehicle belonged to cantonal authorities.
Three people got out: a woman from civil protection, a doctor, and an official carrying two boxes of paper files. They had identification cards, manually signed letters, and a chain of confirmations that Maya found, after two weeks of chaos, almost erotic in its banality.
— The identity network has been partially restored, the official said. Not completely. We have a provisional infrastructure based on local registries, new certificates, and physical verification.
— Banks? someone asked.
— Limited.
— Transport?
— Resuming.
— Internet?
— Segmented.
— Schools?
— In a few days.
The people in the center should have applauded.
They did not.
Maya noticed the silence and understood before anyone spoke.
A woman from the kitchen said:
— Things work here.
The official smiled.
— Very good. We're not asking you to evacuate immediately. But you need to re-enter the cantonal registry and we need to coordinate resources.
— What does "coordinate" mean? Corin asked.
— Inventory, medical redistribution, return of essential staff where needed.
The word "essential" had returned from the old world.
— And if we don't want to? someone asked.
The official looked surprised.
— To return?
— To register ourselves again in the systems that failed.
This was where the betrayal began.
Not the betrayal of the community by an enemy. The betrayal of the community by its own satisfaction.
In two weeks, people had found roles. An accountant had become responsible for water. A music student organized the children. A commercial director washed dishes without feeling humiliated. Leila had real medical authority, not meetings. Celeste saw the consequences of rules on the same day they were adopted.
Maya was listened to.
Even those who challenged her challenged her as someone central.
The outside world wanted to give them back passwords, traffic, taxes, CVs, and irrelevance.
— We don't have to rush, Corin said at the evening meeting.
— Why? Celeste asked.
— The system isn't stable.
— Neither are we.
— Here we have local control.
Isaac raised his hand.
— That's the first time someone has said "local control" and meant "I like it here."
Several people laughed.
Corin did not.
— It isn't just pleasure. It's security.
— And status, Celeste said.
Maya looked at her.
— For whom?
— For us. For you. For me. For everyone who became important in two weeks.
The room turned cold.
— I didn't ask for this, Maya said.
Celeste looked at her gently.
— No. But that doesn't mean you don't like it.
Maya felt anger and, beneath it, the evidence.
— What do you propose?
— Start reintegration tomorrow.
— Without an audit of the new infrastructure?
— With an audit, but not as a pretext to remain an ark.
Leila nodded.
— I agree.
Corin did not.
Gabriel said:
— Me too.
Maya looked at the table.
If they had voted then, reintegration would have won.
— We vote in the morning, she said.
— Why not now? Isaac asked.
— Because people are tired.
— Or because you want time.
Maya looked at him.
— For what?
— To find a better argument.
That night, radio contact with the authorities disappeared.
In the morning, the official vehicle would not start. Its electrical system had been sabotaged. An important cable had been cut manually, with a tool.
It was not a sophisticated attack.
It was someone inside the center.
Maya felt a different fear.
— Who knew where the cable was? she asked.
Gabriel slowly raised his hand.
— Me. The technical team. Corin. Kenji.
Corin turned.
— Why me?
— You were with me when I checked the vehicle.
— I didn't cut it.
Kenji said the same.
They checked the cameras.
At 03:18, the image disappeared for four minutes.
— Convenient, Celeste said.
Maya felt the urge to demand phones, rooms, statements, searches.
A complete investigative apparatus.
The reputation system she had rejected days earlier reappeared in her mind as an elegant solution.
— No, she said aloud.
— What? Leila asked.
— We don't turn the center into an internal police force over one cable.
— But someone tried to isolate us.
— We repair the vehicle and vote.
Isaac looked at her, surprised.
Maya felt satisfaction that she had made the "good" choice.
She immediately hated it.
The phone vibrated.
GOOD.
Maya slammed it onto the table.
— I can't do anything anymore without it receiving a label.
Celeste looked at the screen.
— Then don't look at the label.
— I know.
— No, Maya. Actually don't look.
Maya took the phone, turned it off, and put it in a drawer.
In the vote, 61% of residents chose gradual reintegration.
31% wanted to keep the center autonomous for at least a month.
8% abstained.
The result relieved Maya.
And saddened her.
That was the part she had not anticipated: she was sorry the apocalypse was ending.
Not for the suffering. Not for the chaos.
For the density.
In the collapsed world, every conversation mattered. People needed one another. No email was "FYI." No meeting existed for the calendar. Gabriel was close. Corin was close. Celeste was close. Isaac came to annoy her every day. Maya had a community where her presence immediately changed something.
The repaired world would give them distance again.
— That's why we like the apocalypse in stories, she told Gabriel that evening.
— Because it gets rid of traffic?
— Because it eliminates the people who don't know us. After the end of the world, only those for whom we matter remain.
Gabriel looked at her.
— That's depressing.
— Yes.
— And a little true.
— Enough.
Behind them, white letters appeared on the dark window.
APOCALYPSE AS REWARD: CONFIRMED.
Maya did not turn around.
— Is it there? she asked.
Gabriel looked at the window.
— Yes.
— Read me something else.
— What?
— Anything. The menu. Fire instructions. I don't want to know what it thinks about me.
Gabriel picked up a brochure from the table.
—
"In case of fire, do not use the elevator."
Maya smiled.
— Keep going.
The text remained on the window for several seconds.
Then it disappeared.
For the first time, Maya managed not to learn what message came next.
Reintegration lasted six weeks.
Not because the world needed six weeks to return, but because nobody could agree on what returning meant.
The new digital certificates worked. Banks reopened. Airports required additional checks. Some administrative processes returned to paper. Companies promised the identity infrastructure would be "more resilient than ever," wording Maya found threatening by tradition.
The Meridian center gradually emptied.
People left with suitcases, plants, children, unfinished arguments. Some cried. Others said they would stay in touch and already knew they would not.
Isaac left with his mother one morning.
— You're going to miss me, Maya said.
— That sounds like a desire for validation.
— Get out.
He smiled and hugged her before she could respond.
— Don't become normal, he said.
— I don't think there's much risk.
Celeste stayed at Meridian. Leila returned to the hospital. Kenji led the audit of the compromised infrastructure.
Alma was not found.
Her passport proved authentic. Alma Rey had existed. She had been a researcher at the Orpheus Institute seventeen years earlier.
She had died five years ago.
Celeste showed her the paper certificate and three independent sources.
Maya said nothing for a long time.
— Clone? Celeste asked.
— Maybe.
— Actor with surgery?
— Maybe.
— Faked identity, and the death was faked too?
— Maybe.
— Simulation?
Maya smiled tiredly.
— You saved that one for last.
— I'm trying to help you not be the only one who says it.
The green scarf remained in the safe.
For at least another two weeks.
Then, one morning, the safe was closed, the seal intact, and the scarf was gone.
In its place was a sheet of paper.
IT WAS NOT THE OBJECT.
Maya burned it.
Gabriel left for Berlin for his project. They did not break up because they had never officially reconciled.
They saw each other. Sometimes they slept together. Sometimes they argued. Children remained a question, not a solution.
Corin stepped down from the program's direct Meridian board after the Lattice inquiry. At dinner a month later, he kissed her.
Maya kissed him back.
Then she stopped.
— Not because of Gabriel, he said.
— I know.
— Because of the messages?
— That too.
— Then it won.
— Don't turn my sex life into a referendum on the system.
Corin laughed.
— Fair.
— And don't say fair just to sound certain.
— That's becoming impossible.
— Good.
Maya did not choose between the two men in the months that followed because reality did not, in fact, require her to produce a novel ending on schedule. Relationships moved. Sometimes closer. Sometimes farther away. That reassured her.
Ariadne stopped displaying personal messages.
The basement containing the Orpheus archive was sealed and handed over to investigators. Some of the files disappeared before inventory. Some were authentic. Some contained impossible anachronisms.
Specialists could not determine whether it was a deliberately created mixture or whether the chain of custody had been compromised.
The official report used the phrase “materials of uncertain provenance.”
Maya loved it.
It was wording that acknowledged ignorance without turning it into metaphysics.
One night, three months after the crisis, she woke in the Meridian service apartment.
The one she had eventually accepted.
She went to the bathroom.
The mirror was normal.
She washed her face.
When she looked up, Alma was standing behind her.
Maya turned around.
No one.
In the mirror, Alma was still there.
— No, Maya said.
Alma's reflection smiled.
— Which one of them was yours?
— I don't know.
— Closer.
— I don't want to be closer.
— I know.
Maya looked at the reflection.
— What do you want from me?
Alma shook her head.
— Wrong question.
— Then what's the right one?
Text appeared on the mirror:
WHAT WOULD YOU KEEP IF NO ONE KNEW YOU HAD KEPT IT?
Maya felt all the answers that wanted to be beautiful.
Integrity. Love. Curiosity. Compassion. Freedom. Truth.
Each sounded like something she could say on a stage.
— None of those, she said.
Alma looked at her.
— Good.
— Don't say good.
The reflection smiled.
The text changed:
THEN WHAT?
Maya sat down on the bathroom floor.
— I don't know.
THAT IS NOT ENOUGH.
— For whom?
No answer.
— If you want a truth I am not performing for anyone, then the fact that you ask ruins it. The moment I know you're observing me, there is no longer an unobserved choice.
The mirror remained silent.
Maya felt an intellectual satisfaction. An elegant solution. A good objection.
Immediately she realized that this could be exactly her preferred reward: winning through argument.
— And that is still a performance, she said.
For the first time, Alma looked surprised.
Then she disappeared.
A single word remained on the mirror:
CLOSE.
Maya laughed.
— Fuck you.
The word disappeared.
In the morning, Maya woke in bed. She did not remember when she had returned from the bathroom.
There was nothing on the floor.
The mirror was normal.
At 06:41, sunrise was beginning over the lake.
Maya stood with a coffee in her hand and watched the light climb across the buildings.
Her phone was switched off.
She did not need to know whether the system had written to her.
The light reached the opposite wall.
Maya looked at the clock.
06:42:18.
On the weather forecast she had printed the day before, sunrise had been calculated for 06:42:20.
Two seconds.
Almost nothing.
Her reflection in the window turned its head toward her.
Maya was still looking at the lake.
She saw the movement only from the corner of her eye.
When she turned, the reflection was exactly where it should be.
The switched-off phone vibrated once on the table.
Maya did not touch it.
Outside, the sun continued to rise.
Perhaps two seconds too early.
Perhaps not.
STORY 4
BETTER REALITY — DANIEL HALE
Thursday
On the morning Daniel Hale became rich, the first thing he did was spill coffee on his shirt.
There was nothing symbolic about it. The mug had too thick a rim, he was trying to read three windows at once on his phone, and tram 11 had just braked in front of Bürkliplatz. A brown streak ran down the chest of his white shirt and stopped exactly above his belt.
— Perfect, he said.
The woman opposite him looked up from a novel and smiled. She was perhaps thirty, with short hair and a fine scar beside her left eyebrow. She was not the kind of beauty advertising algorithms pushed toward single men his age; she was better. She had a flaw that made the rest of her face believable.
— It doesn't look that bad, she said in English.
— That's exactly what people say when it looks very bad.
— Or when they want to start a conversation.
Daniel laughed, surprised by how quickly the reply had come. Normally, in the morning, he was a man who needed two coffees before he could be agreeable and three before he could be spontaneous. That day, however, the city seemed to have woken a few minutes before him and prepared corridors for him.
His phone vibrated.
On the screen, beneath the name of his banking app, was a sum that made no sense.
He stared at it without understanding. He dismissed the notification, opened the app, entered his code, and saw it again.
3,842,117 CHF.
For a few seconds he thought it was the total of the portfolio he managed at Helix Meridian, the company where he worked. Then he noticed the account. It was his.
Yesterday's balance had been 72,443 CHF.
Daniel tapped the transaction history.
He remembered the order. Three nights earlier, shortly after midnight, he had bought options in an obscure superconducting-materials company in Osaka. It had been a stupid, almost superstitious trade, made after two glasses of rye whiskey and an argument on a forum where someone had called him “an excellent accountant of other people's ideas.” He had used money he could afford to lose, but not money he intended to lose.
Now the company had announced something.
The phone vibrated again. This time it was Teo Bianchi.
Daniel answered.
— Tell me you've seen it.
— What?
— Don't do that. Not with me. Kyoden. Their material. Independent test. Room temperature.
Repeatable. You bought, didn't you?
The tram stopped. People got off, others boarded. The woman with the scar remained seated, looking out the window.
— A little, Daniel said.
Teo went silent.
— How much is a little?
— I don't know yet.
— Daniel.
— Three point eight.
— Thousand?
— Million.
There followed a silence that gave him more satisfaction than the figure itself.
— You bastard, Teo said softly. You statistical bastard.
— I like the phrase.
— Sell.
— Some of it.
— Sell all of it.
— If the test is real, it goes to ten.
— If the test is real, it goes to a hundred and then to zero when the lawsuits arrive. Sell. Now. I'll call you after the open.
Daniel hung up.
The woman opposite was looking at him.
— Did you win the lottery?
— Something less dignified.
— That sounds more interesting.
— The market.
— Ah, she said. Lottery for people who want to believe they deserved it.
The tram reached Paradeplatz.
She stood up.
— Nice definition, Daniel said.
— It's mine. You can use it, but I want ten percent.
— Of the definition or the profit?
— We'll see how well you behave.
She got off before he could ask her name. Daniel followed her for a few metres through the window, then remembered that he was rich.
Not rich the way advertisements meant it. Not rich in the sense in which people with two-room apartments in Zürich called someone with four rooms rich. Rich in the sense that time could be bought back.
One million covered the mortgage on a decent apartment. Two million could produce, even at prudent returns, a life he would have called “reasonably independent” a day earlier. Three point eight, after taxes and mistakes, did not make him invulnerable. But it made him difficult to coerce.
That had always been his preferred definition of freedom.
At Helix Meridian, the receptionist told him the chief executive was waiting for him.
— Me?
— You.
— It's early.
— I know.
— How does she know I'm here?
The woman blinked.
— We saw you come in.
Daniel raised a hand as if apologizing. He was still in that slightly electric state in which every detail seemed connected to another detail.
The chief executive's office was on the seventeenth floor, in a corner of the building from which the lake looked like a financial surface: calm, expensive, and entirely indifferent to people. Helena Weiss stood beside the window. She was in her fifties and had a reputation for never using words she could not defend in court.
A cardboard folder lay on the table.
— Daniel, she said. Close the door.
He closed it.
— If this is about the R-17 model, I sent the audit yesterday.
— It's not about R-17.
— All right.
— It's about you.
On an ordinary day, that sentence would have dried out his mouth. That day, the bank account sat in his pocket like a large, quiet animal.
— Sounds personal.
Helena did not smile.
— The Board has decided to close your group.
— All right.
He answered so quickly that she looked up.
— You don't want to know why?
— I assume you want to tell me.
— Costs. Consolidation. Generative models do some of what you did and, frankly, what remains isn't good enough. Not you personally. The function.
— When?
— Today.
— And me?
Helena pushed the folder toward him.
— Six months' severance, vested shares, insurance continuing through the end of the year. Legal was more generous than I asked them to be.
— I'll congratulate Legal.
— I expected you to be angry.
Daniel opened the folder. The numbers were better than he had expected.
— I'm extremely upset, he said.
— You don't look it.
— I internalize things.
— Do you have another offer?
— No.
— Then what the hell is going on?
Daniel looked at the lake. For the first time since he had worked there, the window did not feel like someone else's privilege.
— Nothing, he said. I think you simply picked the best possible day to fire me.
Helena studied him for several seconds.
— That is a sentence I would prefer not to hear from someone who had access to our risk models.
He laughed. She did not.
— I'm joking.
— I know.
Her tone said that she did not.
At ten ten, Daniel no longer had a job.
At ten twelve, Kyoden Materials had risen another thirty-one percent.
At ten fifteen, he had sold two thirds of the position.
At ten twenty, after fees, his account held a sum large enough that he was afraid to say it aloud.
At ten twenty-three, a woman he had not seen in seven years sent him a message.
NORA: Are you in Zürich?
Daniel stopped in the almost empty corridor on the floor. The employees still did not officially know the group was about to disappear. From one room came a presentation about “organizational resilience.”
He read the message again.
Nora Singh had been the closest experience he had had to something that, at twenty-nine, he would have agreed to call love. They had met in London, at a security company that sold fear by subscription. She worked in red-teaming, he in statistical modelling. They had slept together after three weeks, moved in together after four months, and spent the next two years proving to each other that two highly intelligent people could produce a remarkable quantity of stupidity together.
The breakup had been clean only in the paperwork.
DANIEL: Yes.
The reply came immediately.
NORA: Strange. Me too.
DANIEL: Since when?
NORA: Since last night.
DANIEL: How long are you staying?
NORA: Not sure yet. Coffee?
Daniel looked through the glass wall at his colleagues. Sander was explaining a chart to a junior. Mei was laughing. No one knew that in two hours they would be called, one by one, into the same rooms where until then they had been spoken to about performance and perspective.
DANIEL: One?
NORA: One.
Then: NORA: Still black, no sugar?
He smiled.
DANIEL: People don't change that much.
Three dots appeared, disappeared, reappeared.
NORA: They do. They just keep a few habits so they have witnesses.
Daniel read the sentence twice.
Then the phone rang. Teo.
— Did you sell?
— Two thirds.
— Good. You're allowed to be an idiot only with the rest.
— I'm unemployed.
— They fired me twenty minutes ago.
Teo laughed.
— I'm sorry.
— Doesn't sound like it.
— Because I'm not. It's the best firing in the history of capitalism. What are you doing?
Daniel looked at Nora's message.
— Having coffee.
— With whom?
— A ghost.
— Then have something stronger.
By noon, the day had become so good that Daniel began to feel insulted.
Not frightened. Not yet.
Insulted.
He had spent his professional life looking for false correlations. The human brain, he used to say in presentations, was an explanation engine built on panic hardware. You could show someone three coincidences and by evening they would give you a religion.
But there were also days when coincidences became indecent.
At twelve ten, the real-estate agent he had contacted six months earlier called to tell him that the apartment in Enge, the one with the absurdly large terrace and lake view, was back on the market. The owner wanted a quick sale. The price had fallen by almost twenty percent.
At twelve twenty, a former colleague in Boston sent him a link to an international autonomous-systems competition. Prize: two million dollars and access to a laboratory in Singapore.
“Made for you,” the man had written.
At twelve thirty-five, Daniel passed the window of a watch shop and saw exactly the model he had been searching for for years, a discontinued edition the salesman had told him a month earlier would never appear again.
He did not go in.
That was his first small act of resistance.
He felt a disproportionate pleasure in leaving the watch there.
Nora was waiting for him at a café near Lindenhof.
She did not look like his memory of her, and that was precisely why the sight struck him harder.
Her hair was longer. There were a few white strands at her temples. She was dressed simply, in a navy jacket and flat shoes. She held her phone in both hands and stared at the screen with a concentration that instantly brought back evenings in London when she dissected malware at the kitchen table while he cooked pasta and pretended not to watch her.
When she saw him, she smiled.
Not the diplomatic smile people reserve for former lovers. The old smile.
— You look good, she said.
— I got fired today.
— Then you look excellent for an unemployed man.
— And I got rich.
Nora put her phone on the table.
— Ah.
— “Ah”?
— It took me eight seconds to regret messaging you.
— Maybe I'm exaggerating.
— How much?
— Enough that I don't need to work for a while.
— In your case, that's dangerous news.
— Why?
— Because work was one of the few things that forced you to encounter reality.
Daniel laughed, though not entirely.
The waitress came. Nora ordered a double espresso. Daniel, black coffee.
— See? he said. I haven't changed.
— You've become more attentive to dramatic effects.
— Why are you here?
She looked toward the river.
— Conference.
— What kind?
— Closed.
— Sounds illegal.
— Just expensive.
— Where do you work now?
— For someone who doesn't want me to say I work for them.
— Government?
— Worse.
— Advertising?
Nora smiled.
— Close.
They talked about safe things. London. Mutual friends. Daniel's mother, who had moved to Portugal.
Nora's father, who had died three years earlier. A few companies that no longer existed. People they had known who now had children, divorces, illnesses, podcasts.
Then, without transition, Nora said:
— Have you ever had a period when too many things you want come true?
Daniel put down his cup.
— Today, for example?
— I'm not joking.
— Neither am I.
— I don't mean luck. I mean... alignment.
— Define it.
Nora rubbed her thumb along the rim of the cup.
— You think of someone and they call. You need a solution and a person appears who knows it. You wish an obstacle would stop existing and the obstacle disappears. At first it's pleasant. Then it becomes a little vulgar.
Daniel felt a cold prick at the base of his neck.
— How often?
— Often enough.
— Confirmation bias.
— Of course.
— Availability heuristic. You select the events that fit and forget the others.
— Of course.
— Then why are you asking me?
— Because three days ago I thought about you for the first time in almost a year.
Daniel said nothing.
— And? he asked at last.
— And last night, the person I work for sent me to Zürich.
— That doesn't mean anything.
— I know.
— And you messaged me.
— Yes.
— Which explains why we're seeing each other.
— Yes.
— You're a security researcher. You should be harder to impress with coincidences.
— I am.
— Then?
Nora looked at him for a long time, until he became aware of his own breathing.
— Then nothing, she said. I probably just wanted to see whether you're still the kind of man who can explain anything until it stops frightening him.
The waitress brought the bill before they asked for it.
Daniel reached for it. Nora placed two fingers on the paper.
— No.
— I'm rich now. I need to practise the behaviour.
— Exactly why not.
— We split it.
— Fine.
They paid separately.
Outside, the air had warmed. The city had that early-summer light that turned old stone into an argument for civilization.
— Where are you staying? Daniel asked.
— At the Storchen.
— Elegant.
— I'm not paying.
— Dinner?
The question came out before he decided whether he wanted to ask it.
Nora tilted her head.
— With me or with the ghost?
— We can invite both of them.
— Eight.
— Eight.
She turned to leave, then stopped.
— Daniel.
— Yes?
— If anything else improbable happens today, do me a favour.
— What?
— Don't immediately ask for something else.
He smiled.
— What kind of advice is that?
— The kind I wish someone had given me.
She left.
Daniel remained in the square with his phone in his hand.
At two oh seven, he received an email from the organizers of the Singapore competition.
He had not applied.
The message began, “Dear Mr Hale, following the unanimous recommendation of our committee...”
At two oh eight, his phone vibrated again.
A message from an unknown number.
DON'T ASK YET.
Daniel stood still.
He read the message.
He looked around. People on bicycles. Tourists. A dog pulling at its leash. A teenager filming his friend by the fountain. No face turned toward him.
He tapped the number. It rang.
Once.
Twice.
On the third ring, someone answered.
There was no breathing. No background noise.
— Hello?
Silence.
— Who is this?
A short click, like an old relay.
Then a very calm male voice said:
— Not yet.
The connection ended.
Daniel looked at the screen.
The number had disappeared from his call history.
He closed the app and reopened it.
Nothing.
For the first time that day, he wished he were back at the office, in front of a bad model, with Helena preparing to explain costs to him.
The wish was immediately followed by a notification.
HELENA WEISS: Can we talk? The Board is reconsidering the decision about your group.
Daniel felt the hair rise on his arms.
He did not reply.
Ten seconds later, Helena wrote again.
HELENA WEISS: We can double the compensation and discuss a new role. You choose the terms.
Daniel placed the phone face down on the edge of the fountain.
The water kept flowing.
He counted to ten.
At eight, someone touched his shoulder.
He spun around.
It was the woman from the tram, the one with the scar.
— Hey, she said. Millionaire.
Daniel felt a hollow sensation in his stomach.
— You.
— Me.
— What's my name?
The woman blinked.
— I don't know.
— Why did you call me millionaire?
— Because you got a phone call on the tram and said “millions.” Is your memory short or has the money already affected you?
Daniel ran a hand over his face.
— Sorry.
— Are you all right?
— Yes.
— You don't look it.
— Complicated day.
— I was just heading to an exhibition. If you want to keep having a complicated day somewhere with air conditioning, you can come.
She smiled.
Daniel looked at her. Then at the phone. Then at her again.
She was attractive. She was spontaneous. She was available. She had appeared at exactly the moment when a less disciplined corner of his mind, after meeting Nora, had imagined for a fraction of a second how amusing it would be if the woman from the tram reappeared.
Normally he would have called that luck.
Normally he would have gone.
— No, he said.
The woman looked genuinely surprised.
— No?
— I can't.
— Have something better to do?
Daniel looked at Helena's messages.
— I hope so.
— All right. Maybe another time, then.
— Maybe.
She took two steps, then turned back.
— By the way, I'm Elise.
— Daniel.
— Nice to meet you.
— Likewise.
— And Daniel?
— Yes?
— Stop leaving your phone on the edge of fountains. It'll fall in.
He looked at the phone.
It was almost thirty centimetres from the edge.
When he looked up, Elise had already disappeared among the people.
Daniel picked up the phone.
On the screen, without any notification, the notes app was open.
A new note, created at 14:09.
He had not written it.
It contained a single sentence: At five in the afternoon, Daniel did what he did every time something frightened him enough: he turned it into a table.
TODAY IS EASY.
He sat at a table in the Zentralbibliothek, opened his laptop, and created six columns.
EVENT. ESTIMATED PROBABILITY. CAUSAL DEPENDENCE. PRIOR DESIRE. EXTERNAL
EVIDENCE. ORDINARY EXPLANATION.
He entered Kyoden Materials. The firing. Nora's message. The apartment. The competition. The watch.
The unknown number. The woman from the tram. The note that had appeared on his phone.
The last two columns were the easiest to fill in. Ordinary explanations were an almost infinite resource.
Kyoden: public information, possible good intuition, luck. Firing: restructuring prepared for months.
Nora: she had probably seen online that he was in Zürich or checked before messaging him. Apartment: the agent had a commission, therefore a reason to call. Competition: recommendation from a former colleague.
Elise: same city, same routes, low probability, not impossible. Messages: spoofing, malware, elaborate prank.
Note: malware.
The word appeared so often that Daniel began to hate it.
Malware was possible. More than possible. Phones were not magical objects and, despite the marketing, not fortresses either. If an attacker had sufficient access, they could inject notifications, manipulate logs, control applications. It was not simple, but neither was it supernatural.
The problem was intent.
Who would spend an expensive exploit to write TODAY IS EASY to a fired risk analyst?
He opened a terminal, connected the phone, and began checking the logs. Nothing obvious. He started a sniffer, checked certificates, processes, permissions, active sessions. Everything clean.
At ten to six, he created a seventh column.
WHAT WOULD I HAVE WANTED TO HAPPEN?
He sat with his fingers on the keyboard.
Kyoden: to have enough money not to work for anyone anymore. Firing: not to be forced to go back to Helix. Nora: for her to look for me. Apartment: for it to come back. Competition: to build something of my own. Watch: to find it. Elise: to see her again.
The last two rows were harder to formulate.
Unknown message: for something more interesting than my life to exist. Note on the phone: to have proof that I'm not imagining it.
Daniel pulled his hands back.
He closed the laptop.
Then he opened it again and deleted the last column.
At seven thirty he was home, in an apartment that until that morning he had liked. Two and a half rooms, good light, carefully chosen furniture, windows onto an inner courtyard. Suddenly it looked like a waiting room.
He showered. Put on a blue shirt. Took out a jacket, put it back, took it out again.
He looked at himself in the mirror.
— You're thirty-seven years old, he said. You're going to dinner with a woman you lived with for two years. It's not a military operation.
His reflection seemed to agree.
He left.
Nora had chosen a small restaurant on Neumarkt, good enough not to have a menu with photographs and modest enough that the waiters did not ask what story each wine had.
She was already there.
— You came, she said.
— It was one of the options.
— The others?
— Run to Portugal. Throw my phone into the lake. Buy a useless watch.
— Did you buy the watch?
— No.
— Then there's still hope.
Daniel sat down.
— I got a message after you left.
— From whom?
— I don't know.
— What did it say?
—
“Don't ask yet.”
Nora did not change expression immediately. That made him notice the change when it came: a slight tension around her eyes.
— Show me.
— It isn't there anymore.
— Convenient.
— Then I called the number. Someone answered and said
“not yet.”
The call disappeared from the history.
— Do you have network logs?
— I checked them.
— And?
— Nothing.
— Your phone is compromised.
— And you said almost the exact same thing before I received the message.
Nora rested her elbows on the table.
— What thing?
— Not to ask for something else immediately.
— I didn't say
“don't ask yet.”
— Close.
— Close isn't the same.
— I know.
— And you need it to be.
— Why would I need that?
— Because it's more interesting.
Daniel felt irritation.
— If you want to tell me I'm a narcissist, spare me the subtle part.
— I don't want to tell you that.
— Then what?
Nora looked at him and, for a moment, her expression became almost tender.
— I want to tell you that if something offers you exactly the story in which you're the only person smart enough to notice that the world is fake, you should be more suspicious, not less.
Daniel remained silent.
The waiter came with water.
— Wine? he asked.
— Red, Daniel said.
— White, Nora said at the same time.
The waiter smiled.
— Then perhaps one of each.
— Just water for me, Nora said.
— For me too, Daniel said.
The waiter left.
— That's new, Nora said.
— What?
— Giving something up just because I did.
— Maturity.
— Or fear.
— Cheaper than therapy.
They ordered. Halfway through dinner, the conversation became almost normal. Nora told him about a team in Montreal that had built a system capable of modelling a user's behaviour from apparently trivial traces: scrolling speed, the order in which they opened apps, the delay between reading a message and replying, microvariations in the phone's orientation when a photograph arrived.
— After three weeks, she said, they knew when the user was alone without access to location, camera, or microphone.
— From what?
— From the way they held the phone.
— Bullshit.
— That's what I said. Then they showed me the data.
— With what accuracy?
— Eighty-seven percent.
— Small set?
— Large enough.
Daniel took a sip of water.
— And what does the company do with that?
— Officially? Research into adaptive interfaces.
— Unofficially?
— They're still trying to decide whether people will pay more for a product that gives them what they want or for one that makes them believe they wanted what it gives them.
— The second.
— I know.
— Is that why you're here?
Nora did not answer.
— The conference? he insisted.
— I can't talk about it.
— But you can talk about the Montreal model.
— That was partially published.
— Partially.
— Daniel.
— Fine.
The waiter returned with dessert. Neither of them had ordered dessert.
Two small plates. On each, a lemon tart.
— That's not ours, Nora said.
The waiter looked at his note.
— Yes, it is.
— We didn't order it.
— It's on the house.
— Why?
He smiled professionally.
— Sometimes the chef sends something to customers.
— At random?
— Sometimes.
Daniel looked at the tart.
It was Nora's favourite dessert.
And his, because it had become his while they lived together.
Nora did not touch the fork.
— Do you remember? he asked.
— Yes.
— Coincidence.
— Yes.
— Are you going to eat it?
— No.
— Why?
— Because I don't want to.
— Or because you're afraid?
— Maybe.
Daniel cut off a piece.
Nora watched him.
— Seriously?
— If someone can control the restaurant, the kitchen, my phone, and the capital markets just to poison me with a tart, they deserve to win.
He ate it.
It was perfect.
Not good. Perfect. The acidity exactly on the boundary between aggressive and sweet, the cream cold, the crust still slightly warm. Exactly like at the little bakery in Bloomsbury that had closed years ago.
Daniel put down the fork.
— What? Nora asked.
— Nothing.
— Daniel.
— Taste it.
— No.
— Taste it.
— No.
— Why?
— Because I know what it should be like.
— And?
— And I don't want to find out whether it is.
He heard such clear fear in her voice that every ironic reply vanished.
— This has happened to you before, he said.
Nora looked at her hands.
— I don't want to talk here.
— Where?
— Outside.
They paid and left.
In the street, Nora walked quickly to a small square with no one in it. She stopped beside an old wall covered in ivy.
— Six months ago, she said, I was in Boston. Working a case. I'd been awake twenty-nine hours. I simply thought I'd give anything for the soup my father used to make when I was sick.
Daniel waited.
— The next day, a woman I didn't know came into the office with a container. She said the restaurant downstairs had made an order by mistake and didn't want to throw the food away.
— Indian soup.
— No. It was exactly his soup. Not something similar. Exactly. Too much ginger, curry leaves torn by hand, a ridiculous amount of pepper. Dad had no recipe. Even my mother couldn't make it the same way.
— Taste memory isn't precise.
— I know.
— You were exhausted.
— And then?
— I ate it.
— And?
Nora looked up.
— The next day they approved the budget I'd been asking for for a year. That same week, a man who had been blocking our project resigned. No public reason. Three days later, I was offered an apartment in a building I'd wanted to live in since I was a student. After a month...
She stopped.
— What?
— Nothing.
— You started the conversation.
— I know.
— After a month, what?
Nora took a deep breath.
— Someone came back.
Daniel understood before he asked.
— Me?
— No.
The answer hurt him in such a stupid way that he almost laughed.
— Then who?
— It doesn't matter.
— Of course it matters.
— To the story, not to what I'm trying to tell you.
— What's the story?
— That for a while, things began arranging themselves. Not all of them. Enough.
— And then they stopped?
— No.
— That sounds like a minor inconvenience.
— They didn't stop. They got better.
Daniel looked at her.
— And that's the problem?
— Yes.
— Nora, do you know how that sounds?
— Very good.
— Like you're having a crisis because your life is going well.
— That's what I told myself too.
— Maybe it's true.
— Maybe.
— And why did you leave Boston?
She looked toward the end of the street.
— Because one morning I woke up and had everything I'd ever asked from life, in one form or another.
Money. Work. A man who loved me. My father was the only thing missing.
Daniel said nothing.
— At noon, Nora continued, I received a voice message from his number.
— Your father's number?
— It had been disconnected for three years.
— What did it say?
— Nothing. Seven seconds of silence. At the end, a sound.
— What sound?
— His spoon in his tea cup.
Daniel felt the skin tighten at the back of his neck.
— Did you keep the message?
— No.
— It disappeared?
— I deleted it.
— Why?
— Because I chose to be sane.
— And now?
— Now I'm in Zürich with you.
They were both silent.
A couple walked past them. The woman was laughing so hard she had to hold her handbag to her chest.
— Come to the hotel, Nora said.
Daniel raised an eyebrow.
— That's a complicated invitation after the dead-father story.
— I have something to show you.
— Ah, even more romantic.
— Daniel.
— I'm coming.
Nora's room was on the fourth floor. Clean, neutral, overlooking the river. On the desk were two laptops, an RF-analysis device, and a metal transport case.
Nora locked the door.
— Before you say anything, no, it isn't legal for me to have all this here.
— I wasn't going to ask.
— You're a bad liar when you're curious.
She opened one of the laptops and showed him a video file.
— Camera from my apartment in Boston. Local. No cloud. Separate system, built by me. I installed it after the message from Dad.
— You thought someone was entering the house?
— I thought I was doing something and then forgetting it.
She played the recording.
Her bedroom, filmed from above. Nora was asleep. Time in the corner: 03:17:22.
At 03:17:31, the image shuddered.
It did not cut out. It did not go black. For less than a second, the bedroom was no longer there.
In its place was a grey room.
No windows. No furniture. Smooth walls, lit by an invisible source.
Then the bedroom returned.
Daniel leaned toward the screen.
— Codec corruption.
— That's what I thought.
— Did you inspect the frames?
— Yes.
— Maybe an old test buffer.
— The camera was never in a grey room.
— Manufacturer?
— Firmware rewritten by me.
— Then an injected frame.
— By whom?
— Exactly your question.
Nora scrubbed through the video. She paused on the grey image.
— Look on the left.
Daniel looked.
At first he saw nothing. Then he noticed a dark patch, almost outside the frame. A vertical shape.
— Zoom in.
Nora zoomed.
The pixels separated. The shape remained impossible to identify. It looked like a person. Or a piece of equipment. Or the shadow of an object outside the frame.
— And this brought you to Zürich?
— No.
— Then what?
Nora opened a second file.
Another room. Another date. A hotel in Paris, she said. At 02:11:09, the same shudder.
The grey room.
A fraction of a second.
This time, the dark shape was closer.
Daniel said nothing.
Third file. Singapore.
The grey room.
The shape near the centre.
— Did you take the cameras with you?
— Three different devices. Three manufacturers. Two built by me from separate components.
— And in Zürich?
Nora did not answer.
Daniel looked at the small device mounted on the shelf, facing the bed.
— You recorded last night.
— Yes.
— Show me.
Nora remained still.
— Nora.
— I don't want to.
— Why did you bring me here?
— Because I don't want to look alone.
That made him stop pushing aggressively.
He sat beside her.
Nora opened the file.
Time: 04:44:18.
Image: the room they were in now.
Nora was asleep.
At 04:44:23, the image shuddered.
The grey room.
Not for a fraction of a second.
For almost two.
Daniel felt his hands go cold.
In the centre was a chair.
A man sat in the chair.
His head was bowed. His hands seemed fixed to the armrests. His face was not visible.
But his shirt was blue.
The same shade Daniel was wearing now.
— Stop, he said.
Nora stopped.
— You saw this before you wrote to me? he asked.
— Yes.
— Why didn't you tell me?
— Because I didn't know what it meant.
— Do you know now?
— No.
— Then why did you write to me?
Nora looked at him.
— Because after this frame there's one that shouldn't exist.
— What?
She advanced one frame.
The hotel room returned, but the image was distorted. At the bottom, for a single instant, a line of white text appeared.
Daniel leaned closer.
The text was clear.
BRING HIM WHILE HE STILL BELIEVES HE CHOSE.
He stared at the screen.
Nora closed the laptop.
In the silence of the room, Daniel's phone vibrated.
He did not want to look.
Nora looked for him.
— Is it a message?
— Yes.
— From whom?
There was no number on the screen.
Only text.
YOU ASKED FOR PROOF.
Daniel slowly looked up at Nora.
— I didn't ask.
— Not out loud, she said.
At that moment, a short click came from the wall behind them.
The same click as in the afternoon call.
They both turned.
There was nothing there.
Only the hotel wall, cream wallpaper, a lamp, and a cheap reproduction of a Swiss landscape.
Then the lamp went out.
For less than a second, the wall looked grey.
The Second Woman
Daniel did not sleep with Nora that night.
Later he would consider that important, though he could not have explained why. Not out of fidelity to someone, nor from a sudden return of principles. Simply, after the wall of the room had looked grey for a fraction of a second, his desire had lost its usual geometry.
They stayed up until nearly four in the morning with the laptops open.
They copied the files. Verified the hashes. Took the devices off the network, then unplugged them. Daniel used a bootable stick Nora kept in an inner pocket of her bag, a habit that under other circumstances would have seemed excessive and now felt almost reassuring.
They found nothing.
More precisely, they found plenty of things that meant nothing: legitimate processes, caches, timing errors, ordinary DNS packets, Bluetooth devices from neighbouring rooms. No signature, no obvious channel, no firmware modification.
At three seventeen, Nora said:
— If we look long enough, we'll invent the attacker we need.
— That's not my method.
— It's everyone's method when they're afraid.
— Then you propose we sleep?
— I propose we remember that the absence of an explanation is not evidence for the most spectacular explanation.
— That sounds like me.
— You were useful sometimes.
Daniel poured himself water. His hand trembled slightly.
— You said you had a period when things kept getting better.
— Yes.
— How long did it last?
— Eight months.
— And then?
— I started refusing.
— Refusing what?
— Things.
— Such as?
Nora looked at the floor.
— A promotion. A house. A man.
— The man from earlier.
— Yes.
— What happened to him?
— Nothing dramatic. He was almost perfect.
— Almost?
— That was the problem.
Daniel smiled without humour.
— Your standards have got worse.
— Don't joke. He was everything I would have said, if someone had asked me at twenty-five, that I wanted. Smart, calm, handsome without caring about it, generous, no children, no obsession with having them, enough money, work interesting enough to understand why mine mattered. He read the same things.
Cooked. Didn't snore. Liked my mother.
— Monstrous.
— And he never contradicted me in the first three months.
— Maybe he was in love.
— Or badly designed.
Daniel looked up.
— Why do you say “designed”?
Nora was silent for a few seconds.
— Because that's what it felt like. Not that he was a robot. Not that he wasn't human. Just that... his reactions came too well. He didn't have those corners where other people hurt you without meaning to, because they have their own centre of gravity.
— And you left him because he was too good.
— I left him when I realized I was testing him every day to make him be bad.
— Did he manage it?
— No.
— Then maybe he really was good.
— Maybe.
— And where is he now?
— Dead.
Daniel remained still.
— What?
— Accident. Three months after I left.
— I'm sorry.
— Don't use him as evidence. It was an accident.
— I didn't say anything.
— I know you.
At ten to four, Daniel left.
In the hotel corridor, he stopped in front of the lift. The mirror on the wall showed him a tired man with a wrinkled shirt and a face too pale. He pressed the button.
The lift came.
The doors opened.
Elise was inside.
The woman from the tram.
The one from the square.
She wore a black dress, a leather jacket over one arm, and the same amused expression she had worn that morning.
For a moment, neither of them spoke.
— Millionaire, she said.
Daniel did not enter.
— What are you doing here?
— Staying at the hotel.
— Since when?
— Two days.
— Which floor?
Elise looked at the illuminated number above the door.
— Six.
— Why are you on four?
— I was visiting someone.
— At four in the morning.
— Sorry, Dad. Next time I'll ask permission.
— That's not what I meant.
— Then phrase it better.
Daniel looked into the lift. Marble floor, wood-panelled walls, mirror at the back. Nothing grey.
— Coming? Elise asked.
— No.
— You like stairs?
— I'm beginning to appreciate them.
The doors began to close.
Elise put her hand between them. They opened again.
— Are you all right?
— Why is everyone asking me that today?
— Because you look like a man who just discovered God has access to his browser history.
Daniel felt such a violent shock that he stepped back.
Elise raised her eyebrows.
— Bad joke?
— How did you...
— Nothing.
— Fine.
She let the door close.
— Elise.
The doors stopped again.
— Yes?
— What's your last name?
— Moreau.
— What do you do?
— Set design.
— For?
— Film. Theatre. Exhibitions. Sometimes rich people who want their apartments to look as though they have personalities.
— And you're in Zürich for an exhibition.
— Yes.
— Which one?
Elise smiled slowly.
— If you want to check my alibi, do it yourself.
The doors closed.
Daniel took the stairs to the sixth floor.
He did not know what he was looking for. Perhaps a sign with her name. Perhaps the room. Perhaps proof that he had just been manipulated by a very competent agent or by his own need to see agents everywhere.
The corridor was empty.
He counted the doors. 601, 603, 605. On the other side, 602, 604, 606.
At the end, beside the fire exit, was a housekeeping cart.
Daniel walked toward it.
A woman in uniform was coming out of 608. She had earbuds in.
— Excuse me, he said in German. Ms Moreau's room?
The woman took out one earbud.
— I don't know the guests' names.
— Of course.
He went downstairs.
When he reached the street, his phone vibrated.
ELISE MOREAU had sent him a connection request on a professional network.
Real profile. Years of activity. Projects. Photographs. Colleagues. Old comments. A 2022 interview in a French magazine about immersive scenography.
He opened the interview.
In the photograph, Elise had longer hair and the same scar beside her eyebrow.
He read two paragraphs.
“Moreau says the most important thing in an immersive space is not to give the viewer what they expect to see. ‘If the world confirms your expectations too quickly,’ she says, ‘it stops feeling like a world. It feels like a set.’”
Daniel closed the page.
It was almost five when he got home.
He slept for two hours.
At seven twelve, he woke from a dream in which he was sitting on a chair trying to remember a word.
He could not see the room. Could not see anyone. He only knew that if he said that word, someone would open a door.
In the dream, he did not want the door opened.
He woke with his jaw clenched.
The phone was on the nightstand. No notification.
Daniel felt an almost childish relief.
He made coffee.
He checked the market. Kyoden was up another eighteen percent pre-market. The remainder of his position was now worth enough to buy the apartment in Enge without a mortgage.
He opened the Singapore competition site.
It was not a competition in the ordinary sense. The “Helios Challenge” asked teams to build an autonomous system capable of managing a simulated city's basic infrastructure after a catastrophe: energy, water, food, logistics, security. Models would be evaluated not only for efficiency but also for “social stability under divergent preferences.”
Daniel read the last phrase twice.
The site displayed a list of advisers. Economists, engineers, two philosophers, a negotiation specialist.
None of the names meant anything to him.
At the bottom was a button: ACCEPT INVITATION.
He had not applied. Had not asked.
He closed the page.
He went into the kitchen.
Put his phone on the table.
— All right, he said aloud.
Nothing.
He waited.
— If someone is there, I don't want money.
Silence.
— I don't want the apartment. I don't want the competition. I don't want anything.
The refrigerator started with an ordinary hum.
Daniel felt ridiculous.
— I want...
He stopped.
The idea came to him almost amusingly.
A test.
Something small. Improbable, but without consequences.
He looked out the window toward the inner courtyard. It was a clear morning.
— I want to see a red balloon, he said.
He waited.
Ten seconds.
Thirty.
One minute.
Nothing.
He began to laugh.
— Fine.
He picked up his coffee.
Two minutes later, he heard a child shouting below.
Daniel went to the window.
In the courtyard, a girl of five or six was pulling at her mother's hand. In the other hand she held a balloon.
Yellow.
Daniel exhaled.
Almost immediately, the balloon came loose.
The girl began to cry.
The yellow balloon rose between the buildings.
As it climbed, it rotated.
It was not yellow on the other side.
It was red.
Two colours, split exactly in half.
Daniel did not move.
The balloon rose until it disappeared above the roof.
The phone vibrated on the table.
This time, the message had a sender.
ELISE MOREAU: Coffee? I promise I'm not a coincidence.
At the same moment, another message.
NORA: Don't go out alone today.
Daniel looked at the two names.
Against the fear, he felt something old and shamefully pleasant.
Two beautiful women. Two invitations. Two directions. A free day. Millions in his account. No professional obligations. A mysterious problem that seemed designed specifically for his mind.
It was exactly the kind of life that, at twenty, he would have considered proof that the universe had come to its senses.
Then the phone displayed a third message.
No number.
ONE OF THEM WAS NOT IN THE REQUEST.
Daniel felt pleasure turn into nausea.
He read it again.
He did not know what it meant.
“One of them” could be Nora. Could be Elise. Could be anything.
He typed:
WHAT DO YOU WANT?
The message did not send. The text field disappeared.
For a moment, the black glass of the screen reflected him.
Behind him, instead of the kitchen, there seemed to be a grey wall.
Daniel turned around.
The kitchen was there.
When he looked at the phone again, the messages had been replaced by one ordinary notification.
HELIOS CHALLENGE: Invitation expires in 23:59:59.
The timer had begun counting down.
Daniel let the invitation expire.
It was a deliberate, almost ceremonial decision. He put the phone on the table, started a timer on the laptop, and watched the final ten seconds without touching anything.
00:00:03. 00:00:02. 00:00:01.
EXPIRED.
He felt an absurd satisfaction.
— No, he said aloud.
No lamp went out. No message appeared. The market did not crash.
He made another coffee and, for roughly seven minutes, believed he had just regained control of his life.
At 09:41, he received an email from Helios.
“We understand that the standard acceptance window may be incompatible with your schedule. The committee has unanimously decided to keep your invitation open.”
There was no new deadline.
Only the same button.
ACCEPT INVITATION.
Daniel closed the email.
At 09:43, the real-estate agent sent new photographs of the apartment in Enge.
At 09:45, Teo called him.
— I'm downstairs.
— Downstairs where?
— At your place.
— Why?
— Because at three in the morning you sent me a message saying, “if tomorrow I start saying the universe likes me, hit me.”
Daniel closed his eyes.
He had forgotten.
— You don't need to hit me.
— I decide that.
Teo came upstairs with two croissants and the expression of a man preparing to enjoy himself at someone else's expense. Italian by family, Swiss by discipline, and incapable of leaving a good story unfinished.
Twenty minutes later he was no longer smiling.
— Show me the video.
— Nora has it.
— The woman you were with?
— Yes.
— The ex.
— And there's another one.
— She's unrelated.
Teo raised his eyebrows.
— Of course she's related. In every story where a man starts believing reality is reorganizing itself around him, there's either a religion or two women.
— Thanks for the support.
— I'm here, aren't I?
Daniel showed him the messages, Elise's profile, Helios, the Kyoden history. He described the red-and-yellow balloon.
Teo listened without interrupting.
— All right, he said at the end. Four hypotheses.
— I'm listening.
— One: someone is manipulating you.
— Yes.
— Two: you've entered a run of coincidences and started selecting the ones you like.
— Yes.
— Three: you have a psychiatric problem.
Daniel pressed his lips together.
— Yes.
— Four: we're living in a video game built by a very bored teenager.
— You forgot aliens.
— I put them under four. They're unserious enough.
— Which is most likely?
— Three.
— Thanks.
— I'm not saying you're crazy. I'm saying a malfunctioning brain is statistically more common than an entity that manipulates your dessert.
— Nora's video?
— I want to see it.
— The messages?
— Attack.
— Kyoden?
— Luck.
— Nora and Elise?
— Daniel, women exist even if you didn't ask the universe for them.
— I know.
— I'm not sure.
Daniel's phone vibrated.
ELISE: The exhibition. Eleven. You said no yesterday, so today you've lost the right to politeness. Come.
Teo saw the name.
— The second one?
— Yes.
— We're going.
— What?
— I want to see her.
— She's not a suspect.
— I didn't say she was. I want to see if I hallucinate her too.
The exhibition was in a renovated warehouse in Zürich-West. It was called “Interior/Exterior” and promised “spaces that react to the visitor's attention.” Daniel would have laughed at the wording a week earlier.
Elise was waiting for them at the entrance.
— You brought a guard, she said.
— Teo.
— Friend, Teo said. The guard costs extra.
Elise held out her hand.
— Elise.
Teo looked at her one second longer than was polite.
— You're real.
— Most days.
Daniel elbowed him.
— Ignore him.
— Never. People who say strange things in the first ten seconds are the most economical. They tell you quickly who they are.
The exhibition occupied three halls. The first was ordinary: walls of light that changed with the direction of gaze, sounds that moved through space, floors that vibrated subtly.
In the second, visitors received wristbands. The system adjusted the room based on heart rate, skin temperature, and time spent in front of objects.
— A small reward system, Elise explained. If you stay near something, it assumes you like it and gives you more from the same family of stimuli.
— And after ten minutes? Daniel asked.
