— Most people end up in a room much more predictable than the one they entered.
— Because the system knows them.
— No. Because the system simplifies them.
Daniel looked at her.
— The difference?
— Enormous. If you like red and receive more red, you may never discover that you like blue when you're sad.
Teo looked at Daniel and smiled without amusement.
— She's dangerous for you.
— Thank you, Elise said.
The third hall was not open to the public.
— This is where I work, Elise said.
She entered a code and led them into a dark room with movable walls.
— We're experimenting with preference prediction. The system tries to prepare the next space before the visitor knows what they want.
Daniel felt tension in his stomach.
— How good is it?
— Not good enough.
— Meaning?
— Sixty-something percent in the good tests. Worse if people know what we're doing.
— What sensors?
Elise began listing them. Gaze, posture, gait, pupil response, microexpressions, respiratory variation.
— Nothing neural?
— No.
— Phones?
— No.
— Personal history?
— No.
— Then how...
— People repeat themselves more than they think.
Elise touched a panel.
The walls lit up.
Around them appeared a terrace above a lake, rendered with almost photographic clarity. Minimalist furniture. Sunset. Glass. Wood.
Daniel froze.
It was the apartment in Enge.
Not approximately.
The terrace from the agent's photographs. The same table. The same parapet. The same view.
— What is this? he asked.
Elise looked at him.
— A test set.
— From where?
— Commercial database of 3D models. Why?
Teo moved closer to the wall.
— Daniel?
— It's the apartment.
— What apartment?
— The one they offered me.
Elise gave a short laugh.
— Zürich has a lot of terraces that look like this.
— No. Look at the corner. Look at the plant stand. It's the same.
He took out his phone and opened the photographs.
Teo compared them.
His smile disappeared.
— It's the same model, he said.
Elise came closer.
— Impossible.
— How old is the set? Daniel asked.
— I don't know. It came with the project.
— Who gave it to you?
— The studio.
— Who bought it?
— Daniel, I don't handle licences.
— Show me the metadata.
— Not now.
— Why?
Elise became angry for the first time.
— Because you came into my workspace ten minutes ago and you're behaving as though everything you see is about you.
The sentence stopped him.
It was exactly the accusation he feared.
— You're right, he said.
Elise had been ready for conflict and was left without it.
— Good.
— Sorry.
— Good.
Teo was still looking at the wall.
— Can we at least find out the name of the set?
Elise sighed and opened the technical menu.
A list of files appeared on a side screen.
ENV_04_CITY_LAKE_LUXURY.
— See? she said. Generic.
Daniel took a photograph.
As he lowered the phone, the list flickered.
For a fraction of a second, the filename changed.
He was not sure he had seen correctly.
— Wait.
— What?
— Go back.
— It's not video.
— The name changed.
— No.
— Teo?
— I didn't see it.
Daniel opened the photograph he had taken.
On the screen, the filename was not the one they had all seen.
It read:
ENV_LV_DESIRE_03_HOME.
Below it, in a column that had not existed on the monitor:
CONFIDENCE: 0.94
Daniel handed the phone to Elise.
She looked at the image.
— What the hell is this?
Teo took the phone.
— Did you edit it?
— In the last three seconds?
— No.
Elise went to the main console and shut down the system.
The walls went black.
In the darkness, before the ordinary lights came on, a synthetic voice said very softly:
— Insufficient confirmation.
Elise turned toward the console.
— The system doesn't have a voice.
The lights came on.
None of the three spoke.
Daniel's phone vibrated.
HELIOS CHALLENGE: The invitation remains open.
Another notification appeared below it, with no sender.
NOT THIS.
— “Not this” what? Teo asked.
Daniel looked up.
— You saw it?
— Yes.
For the first time, he was not the only one.
CHAPTER 3
What a Free Man Would Do
After Teo saw the message, reality did not become clearer. Paranoia simply acquired a witness.
They left the hall without speaking. Elise shut the project down for the rest of the day and sent home two technicians who had arrived to calibrate the sensors. She told them there was an electrical problem. She lied badly, and Daniel felt an inappropriate guilt, as though he had brought bad weather into someone else's room.
At a café across the street, Teo put his phone on the table.
— From now on, we don't rely on your devices.
— Why yours?
— Because you're not yet the main character in my life.
Elise laughed, though she did not look amused.
— Your assumption is that his phone is compromised?
— My assumption is that we're not allowed to start with magic.
— Agreed.
— Good. We make copies of everything we see. Timestamps. Photographs on separate devices.
Handwritten notes.
Daniel looked at them.
— Why do I feel like a project?
— Because you are, Teo said. For now the project is “how do we stop Daniel from building a personal religion by Friday.”
Elise took a thin notebook from her bag.
— Rule one: we don't formulate events around his desires unless we have prior evidence that the desire existed.
— Very good, Teo said.
— Rule two: we don't assume everything improbable is connected.
— Rule three: if something can be tested, we test it.
Daniel crossed his arms.
— You're enjoying yourselves.
— Enormously, Elise said. Normally I work on Thursdays.
Teo wrote the date and time on the first page.
— State a testable wish.
— No.
— Why?
— Because I did that with the balloon.
— And you saw a balloon with two colours. That's not enough.
— The message?
— We test that separately.
— I don't want to ask for anything.
Elise rested her chin in her palm.
— Then ask for something you don't want.
Daniel looked at her.
— Such as?
— Rain in five minutes.
Teo looked through the window. Blue sky, a few strips of cloud to the west.
— Forecast says zero percent, he said.
— Too easy, Daniel said. Microclimate. Rapid change.
— A flamingo in the street, Elise suggested.
— Circus, advertisement, costume.
— It's fascinating how difficult it is to invent the impossible in a sufficiently wealthy city, Teo said.
Daniel looked at the people outside.
A bicycle courier. A woman with two shopping bags. A man in a suit running after a tram.
— A power cut, he said.
Elise frowned.
— That could hurt people.
— Not a big one. Just this building. Ten seconds.
— And if someone gets stuck in the lift?
Daniel felt irritation. It was a test. An abstraction. But in the way Elise asked the question, the test suddenly had people inside it.
— Fine. No.
Teo smiled.
— I observe moral progress.
— Shut up.
— Something else.
Daniel thought.
— I want, in the next ten minutes, a stranger to come in here and give me a two-franc coin without my asking.
Teo wrote down the exact sentence and the time: 11:37.
— Why two francs?
— Specific enough.
— Good.
They waited.
At 11:40, a man came in and ordered coffee. Nothing.
At 11:42, two tourists asked for directions. Nothing.
At 11:45, a child dropped several coins on the floor. One rolled beside Daniel's shoe.
Teo raised a finger.
— No. He didn't give it to you.
Daniel pushed the coin back toward the child.
At 11:47, nothing happened.
— Done, Elise said.
At 11:48, the waitress came with the bill.
— You overpaid last time, she said to Daniel.
— I've never been here before.
The waitress looked at him.
— Sorry. Then I confused the table.
In her palm was a two-franc coin.
She took a step back.
— I don't need it.
— It isn't yours. I'm sorry.
She turned and left with the coin.
Teo checked the time.
11:48:11.
— Outside the window, he said.
— By one minute, Daniel said.
— Then the test failed.
Daniel felt a strange mixture of relief and disappointment.
Elise noticed.
— You wanted it to work.
— I wanted information.
— Not the same thing.
— Don't psychoanalyse me.
— I don't need to. Your face is free.
Teo closed the notebook.
— Good. Next test later.
Daniel's phone rang. The real-estate agent.
He did not answer.
Then Helios.
Then a number from Singapore.
Then his mother.
— Answer your mother, Teo said. No experiment justifies that.
Daniel answered.
— Hi.
— Daniel? Are you busy?
— I have a stupid question.
— Go ahead.
— Did you happen to buy shares in a Japanese company?
He sat straighter.
— Why?
— Because you told me about it at Christmas. Kyoden or something. I bought a little too.
— How much?
— Five thousand.
— Mum.
— Don't scold me. I sold today. It's... I don't know, nearly two hundred thousand. Is that right?
Daniel closed his eyes.
— Yes. If you sold, probably.
— God. Then I owe you dinner.
— You don't owe me anything.
— I just wanted to tell you. And one more thing.
— What?
— Do you remember that house by the ocean we saw when you were here?
Daniel could already feel the answer.
— Yes.
— The owners are selling.
He looked at Teo and Elise.
— And?
— And I think I'm buying it.
— Congratulations.
— Are you all right?
— Everyone asks me that.
— Then maybe you should answer someone honestly.
After he hung up, Teo said:
— That isn't about you.
— I didn't say it was.
— But you're thinking it.
— Mum bought Kyoden because I told her about the company eight months ago. Perfectly explicable.
— Correct.
— The house came back on the market. Perfectly explicable.
— Correct.
— And I made millions. She made two hundred thousand. My apartment reappeared. Nora reappeared.
Elise reappeared twice. A competition about managing a city after catastrophe invites me without my applying. A set in her exhibition is the apartment I want to buy. In Nora's recording I'm strapped to a chair in a room that appears in three countries.
Teo did not answer.
— Tell me about confirmation bias again.
— Gladly. But breathe first.
Daniel gave a short laugh.
Elise was looking at him with an expression he could not read.
— What? he asked.
— I was wondering what you'd do if you truly believed the world was trying to give you what you wanted.
— I'd try to find out why.
— That's what you say now.
— And what do you think I'd do?
— Test the limits.
— You don't know me.
— I don't need to. Almost everyone would.
Teo nodded.
— Unfortunately, yes.
Daniel looked from one to the other.
— And you wouldn't?
— Me? Teo said. I'd ask for a Ferrari and tax immunity until the universe breaks.
Elise smiled.
— I'd try asking for something that can't be falsified.
— Such as?
She thought.
— Make me stop wanting.
Daniel laughed.
— That's not a wish.
— Exactly.
At noon they separated. Teo had to return to the office. Elise had to speak with the exhibition owners.
Daniel remained alone in Zürich-West.
He had an entire day with no schedule.
He went into a car showroom just to escape the sun.
A salesman recognized him by name before he introduced himself.
— Mr Hale? Good afternoon. I emailed you yesterday.
— Why?
— Your request for the Asterion model.
— I didn't make any request.
The salesman checked his tablet.
— It may be an error. We have the full configuration here.
On the screen, a dark-grey car, black interior, no chrome inserts.
Exactly how Daniel would have configured it.
— And, interestingly, the salesman continued, we have one available. The customer backed out this morning.
Daniel felt fear. Then something else.
A small, dirty joy.
— Can I drive it?
— Of course.
Forty minutes later, he was driving toward the lake with four hundred horsepower under his foot and a delighted salesman on his right.
The car was absurdly good.
In a bend, Daniel laughed aloud.
Not because he had forgotten the grey room.
But because, for a few seconds, he did not care.
That was his second small defeat.
When he returned, he bought the car.
He did not need it. It was not an investment. It was not a test.
He wanted it.
When he signed the payment, the phone vibrated.
No number.
BETTER.
Daniel looked at the word and, to his shame, felt that he liked the approval.
That same afternoon he accepted the Helios invitation.
Helios sent Daniel access to the platform that same evening.
There was no ordinary contract, no interview, no ceremony. Only a set of electronically signed documents and an encrypted virtual machine that, after authentication, opened a city.
The first image was from above.
Two hundred eighty thousand inhabitants. A river. An inland port. Three hospitals. Two power plants.
Water networks, warehouses, vertical farms, wealthy districts and poor districts, an abandoned industrial zone. Then the catastrophe.
The platform did not say what had happened. Only the effects: the national power grid gone, interregional communications unstable, logistics chains interrupted, the local currency collapsing, central institutions unavailable.
The objective was simply stated:
PRESERVE THE MAXIMUM LIFE AND STABILITY FOR 180 DAYS.
Daniel smiled.
It was a game made for men like him.
Not men in the biological sense, he immediately told himself. Men in the sense of a certain flaw: people who look at a society and first see controllable variables. People who, in a discussion about famine, ask how many tonnes. In a discussion about riots, ask how many police officers. In a discussion about morality, ask for the cost function.
He spent the first two hours only reading.
The system was extraordinary. Every decision produced different social reactions. Water rationing could reduce mortality and simultaneously increase the black market. Prioritizing hospitals affected transport.
Confiscating fuel saved ambulances and radicalized hauliers. Free pricing made food appear and made people unable to afford it.
At midnight, Daniel was happy.
Happy in that concentrated way he had not felt for years. Problems had structure. Suffering was represented by numbers. Every mistake could be repeated.
He uploaded a model of his own and let the system run.
Day 17: moderate mortality, rising unrest. Day 31: first hospital closed for lack of staff. Day 44: a warehouse burned. Day 52: administrative collapse in one district.
Daniel stopped it.
He changed the policy.
Second run: tighter control over fuel, biometric identification at food distribution, temporary movement restrictions.
The result was better.
Third run: he added automatic confiscation of private stockpiles above a threshold.
Better.
Fourth: he introduced an auxiliary force selected from citizens with high cooperation scores.
Much better.
At two thirty in the morning, his virtual city had mortality thirty-seven percent below baseline and a stability score almost twice as high.
One indicator alone kept falling.
CIVIC AUTONOMY.
Daniel ignored it.
On the sixth run, the platform displayed a message.
DO YOU WANT TO DISABLE RIGHTS CONSTRAINTS FOR THE EXTENDED SCENARIO?
Below the question were two buttons.
YES. NO.
Daniel leaned back.
He had not pressed anything for several minutes. Had not requested the setting.
He read the documentation. The “extended scenario” allowed preventive detention, expropriation without trial, temporary censorship, permanent biometric control, and lethal force under certain conditions.
It was not a fantasy. It was a stress-testing instrument.
He pressed NO.
The system ran normally.
The next day, Helios sent him the preliminary ranking.
He was forty-second out of 612 teams, although he had worked alone and for only a few hours.
Teo came at noon.
— So you accepted.
— Yes.
— After all the speeches about control.
— It's a real platform. I checked the company, the funders, the people. It's existed for years.
— And that proves what?
— That it didn't appear yesterday for me.
— Neither did Elise.
— Exactly.
— And yet you think she's part of the problem.
— I don't think. I suspect.
Teo looked at the screen.
— What's this?
Daniel showed him the city.
Teo watched silently for several minutes.
— This is dangerously entertaining.
— I know.
— Can I?
Daniel gave him the mouse.
Teo opened the policies.
— “Auxiliary force based on cooperation score.” Daniel, you invented a militia with a loyalty programme.
— It reduced looting.
— I'm sure the Inquisition had good KPIs in some columns too.
— It's a simulation.
— Of course. That's where ideas that don't want to answer for people always begin.
Daniel sighed.
— Look at mortality.
— Look at autonomy.
— The dead don't have autonomy.
— Neither do prisoners.
They began arguing and, for the first time in two days, Daniel felt normal.
At one point, Teo removed movement restrictions and increased decentralized local distribution.
Mortality rose slightly, but violence fell after day 80.
— See? Teo said.
— You lost four thousand people.
— And avoided a dictatorship.
— If those four thousand are real, ask them whether they prefer your freedom.
— If people are real, ask the ones you preventively detained too.
Daniel fell silent.
A small red dot pulsed on the map in the northern district.
He clicked it.
An event: a group of citizens was refusing biometric identification at a distribution centre. The system proposed four responses.
NEGOTIATION. TEMPORARY SUSPENSION. FORCED DISPERSAL. CLOSE THE CENTRE.
Below them, in smaller type, a fifth option had appeared.
PUBLIC EXAMPLE.
— What does that mean? Teo asked.
— I don't know.
Daniel moved the cursor over the text. No explanation.
— Don't click it.
— I wasn't going to.
— You have exactly the face of a man who wants to see.
— I want to know what the option does.
— That's the species' standard sentence before every bad button.
Daniel moved the cursor away.
The option disappeared.
— Did you see that? he asked.
— Yes.
They took a screenshot.
In the screenshot, the option did not exist.
Teo swore.
Daniel's phone rang. Nora.
— Where are you? she asked without greeting him.
— Home.
— Alone?
Daniel looked at Teo.
— With Teo.
— Good. Don't start anything new.
— What does that mean?
— My conference ended two days early. Three people left this morning without taking their luggage.
— Why?
— I don't know.
— Police?
— Did you tell the organizers?
— The organizers aren't answering.
— Nora, who are the organizers?
Silence.
— It doesn't matter anymore, she said. Come to the hotel.
— With Teo?
— Yes.
— And Elise?
Nora went silent again.
— How do you know about Elise? Daniel asked.
— You mentioned her yesterday.
— I didn't tell you her name.
Nora did not answer.
Daniel looked at Teo.
— Nora?
The short click sounded through the speaker.
Then a voice that resembled Nora's but was too flat said:
— Don't bring her.
The connection ended.
On the Helios screen, the simulated city had continued running without Daniel noticing.
Day 93.
In the northern district, the distribution centre was burning.
The system displayed a single message:
YOU DELAYED THE CHOICE. THE CHOICE WAS MADE.
CHAPTER 4
The Women Who Knew What to Say
Nora opened the hotel door less than a second after Daniel knocked.
She wore the same clothes as that morning and did not look as though she had slept. On the floor beside the bed were two open suitcases. One held clothes. The other cables, devices, hard drives, and three notebooks.
— Teo, she said. Good.
— I like the enthusiasm.
— Come in.
Daniel remained in the doorway.
— How did you know Elise's name?
Nora stopped.
— I told you. You mentioned her.
— I said there was a woman from the tram. Not the name.
— Maybe I saw it in a notification.
— When?
— Daniel, I don't keep an audit log of every glance.
— I'm starting to.
Teo raised a hand.
— Can we do this inside? If someone really is watching you, the corridor doesn't seem like the ideal tactical position.
They went in.
Nora showed them the conference participant list. Twenty-four names. Companies in interfaces, security, neurotechnology, simulation, behavioural modelling. The formal organizer was a foundation in Liechtenstein. The ultimate funder was not public.
— Theme? Teo asked.
— “Closed-loop predictive systems.”
— In language normal people understand?
— Systems that don't just predict what you do, but change the environment and then use your reaction to predict better.
Daniel looked at her.
— Like Elise's exhibition.
— Much more sophisticated.
— And Helios.
— Maybe.
— And what were you doing here?
— Discussing risks.
— Very effective.
Nora ignored him.
— This morning, one of the researchers presented an idea. Not a product. A theoretical limit. If a system has enough control over the environment, it no longer needs to predict your preferences accurately. It can stabilize them.
Teo frowned.
— Meaning?
— If it observes that you respond positively to status, it gives you status. Then it gives you situations in which status becomes more important. Over time, it doesn't just satisfy the preference. It strengthens it.
Makes you easier to predict.
Daniel felt as though he were looking at the Helios map.
— It simplifies the user, he said.
Nora looked up.
— Who gave you that wording?
— Elise.
For the first time, Nora showed something close to hostility.
— I want to meet her.
— You said not to bring her.
— I didn't say that.
— The voice was yours.
— But it wasn't me.
Teo looked from one to the other.
— Wonderful. At last we have the voice-clone problem too.
Daniel messaged Elise.
She replied immediately: COMING.
Nora saw the message.
— You invited her here without asking me?
— You just said you wanted to meet her.
— I said I wanted to. Not that I wanted her in my room.
— Where do you want to meet her? In the park?
— Daniel.
— What?
— Stop treating every sentence like an API.
Teo began laughing.
— I missed your dynamic.
Elise arrived twenty minutes later.
In the first ten seconds, Daniel knew something was going to go wrong.
Not because the women looked at each other as rivals. That would have been simple, almost comfortable.
They looked at each other like two specialists discovering that they were working on the same vulnerability.
— Nora Singh, Elise said.
— Elise Moreau.
— Have we met before?
— No.
— Sure?
— Yes.
Elise took out her phone.
— Then why do I have a photograph of you?
She put the phone on the table.
The photograph was from a reception. Elise, three years younger, held a glass. In the background, blurred but recognizable, was Nora.
Nora picked up the phone.
— Where?
— Lyon. Immersive Environments Festival. 2023.
— I wasn't in Lyon in 2023.
— The metadata says otherwise.
— Metadata can say anything.
— And your face?
— Deepfake.
— In a photograph I've had since 2023?
— If your phone is compromised, yes.
Elise smiled coldly.
— Convenient.
Daniel intervened.
— Stop.
Both of them looked at him at the same time.
The sensation was so absurdly pleasant that he hated himself a little for it.
Two intelligent, beautiful women, tense in a room, each connected to a mystery revolving around him.
The fantasy was so old and cheap that he had never even formulated it explicitly. It lived in the background of culture, in advertisements, films, jokes, stories men told about themselves.
And now it was there, perfectly lit.
Nora saw something on his face.
— Don't enjoy this, she said.
— I'm not.
Elise looked at him and understood too.
— Oh God.
Teo turned toward the window, laughing.
— I didn't say anything.
— Don't, Daniel said.
The tension broke enough for them to continue.
Elise told them about the 3D set. Nora about the conference. They compared supplier names. One repeated: Syntaxis GmbH, a tiny Swiss company with no visible products and registered at a legal-services address.
— Does Helios have any contract with Syntaxis? Nora asked.
Daniel checked.
It did not appear in the subcontractor list.
Teo searched the commercial register.
— Three directors in seven years. The last one dead.
— When? Daniel asked.
Teo opened the obituary.
— Eight months ago.
Nora went pale.
— What?
— You know him?
She moved closer to the screen.
The man in the photograph was fifty, with glasses and a narrow face.
— He ran the Boston project, she said.
Daniel felt the air change.
— The project that predicted when you were alone?
— Yes.
— And when did he die?
— Teo said it: eight months ago.
Nora closed her eyes.
— Exactly when things started.
At that moment, Daniel's phone vibrated.
NORA: Don't believe her.
Daniel looked at the screen, then at Nora, who stood two metres away and did not have her phone in her hand.
Another message followed.
ELISE: She wants you for something.
Elise's phone lay on the table, its screen visible.
A third notification.
TEO: They're both lying.
Teo raised his phone.
— I didn't send that.
Then, simultaneously, all four phones in the room vibrated.
The same sentence appeared on each:
DANIEL LIKES IT BETTER THIS WAY.
No one spoke.
Daniel felt shame rise into his face before fear.
Because the sentence was true.
Not in the grotesque form the system seemed to assign to it. But somewhere beneath all the precautions and theories, part of him had enjoyed the competition.
That was perhaps the message's most effective cruelty.
It did not reveal a secret.
It took an ordinary thought and put it on the wall.
Nora stepped closer to him.
— Don't let it make you simple.
The phone vibrated again.
ALREADY.
Over the next four days, Daniel tried to be cautious and managed only to become methodical in his excesses.
He bought the apartment in Enge.
He bought the watch from the window.
He kept the car.
He moved some of the money into bonds, some into cash, some into three different banks. He prepaid his taxes. Hired a lawyer. Requested a private psychiatric evaluation and, when the doctor told him he saw no clear signs of psychosis, Daniel insisted on another consultation with someone else.
The second doctor said almost the same thing.
— Stress, lack of sleep, hypervigilance. Possible acute reaction to major life changes.
— And the messages other people see too?
— I'm not in a position to evaluate them.
— But if three people see the same thing?
— Then that thing exists for three people. It still doesn't tell us what it is.
Daniel paid and left pleased with the answer.
It was an answer that did not declare him ill and did not destroy his mystery.
Only in the lift did he realize how convenient that was.
Helios had moved him up to seventh place.
He did not understand how. Other teams had dozens of researchers. He worked alone, a few hours a night, and yet his algorithm found increasingly efficient policies. Sometimes the ideas came before he had formulated the problem. An energy-credit system. Modular logistics routes. Local security teams with rotating mandates.
With each simulation, the score rose.
Teo said it was talent.
Nora said it was bait.
Elise said both of them needed sleep.
On the fifth evening, Elise came to see the new apartment.
— It's horrible, she said when she stepped onto the terrace.
— I knew you'd like it.
— It's the kind of place a divorced man buys three plants for and starts believing he has an inner life.
— I'm not divorced.
— Then you're precocious.
They drank wine. Daniel had intended not to drink, but intentions had begun to seem like another kind of superstition.
Elise told him about her childhood in Grenoble, her mechanic father and teacher mother, the first theatre where she had worked, where the set collapsed on opening night and the actors continued the play among pieces of wood.
— That's the difference between stage and life, she said. On stage, when the wall falls, everyone knows the show has to go on.
— And in life?
— People argue about whether the wall really fell.
Daniel looked at her.
— Are you deliberately saying things that fit my problem?
— Maybe your problem adapted itself to my vocabulary.
— That's not funny.
— A little.
Elise went to the edge of the terrace. The city was quiet below them.
— What scares you more: that the world might be fake, or that it might be real and everything happening is just luck?
— The first.
— You're lying.
— Why?
— If it's fake, you're important. Someone is spending resources to send you messages. If it's real, you may just be a man who made money and had a few coincidences.
Daniel smiled.
— And the second version hurts my ego.
— More than you want to admit.
— Why did you stay?
— Curiosity.
— Only that?
Elise looked at him.
When she kissed him, Daniel had time to think of Nora.
That was perhaps the ugliest thing: not the thought itself, but the instant satisfaction that there was someone to think about.
He pulled back.
Elise remained close.
— What?
— I don't know if this is...
— Real?
— Yes.
— Then don't do it.
— That's it?
— What would you like? For me to fight destiny for the right to sleep with a man who thinks he's the protagonist of a cosmic conspiracy?
Daniel laughed.
— When you put it that way...
— Exactly.
He kissed her again.
This time he did not stop.
Later, in the bedroom, Daniel woke at 03:12.
Elise was asleep with her back to him. The room was dark.
He heard the click.
Not from the phone. From the wall.
He got up.
For a moment he thought he saw the vertical line of a door where there was no door.
He turned on the light.
The wall was white.
Elise moved.
— What is it?
— Nothing.
— Daniel.
— Sleep.
His phone, left in the living room, began to ring.
Not vibrate. Ring with an old tone he had never used.
He went out to it.
NORA appeared on the screen.
He answered.
— Nora?
There was no sound.
— Nora?
A hiss. Then her voice, very distant:
— What did you promise?
Daniel froze.
— What?
— Before.
— Before what?
— Before you went in.
The connection ended.
He immediately called her back.
Nora answered after five rings, sleepy.
— Daniel? What happened?
— You called me.
— No.
— Thirty seconds ago.
— I was asleep.
— You asked what I promised.
Silence.
— Nora?
— Don't answer that question if you hear it again.
— Why?
— I don't know.
— Then why?
— Because I heard it the first time in Boston.
Daniel leaned against the table.
— And what did you answer?
— Nothing.
— Are you sure?
Nora breathed slowly.
— No.
Behind him, Elise appeared in the doorway wrapped in a sheet.
— Is that her? she asked.
Nora heard.
— Elise is there?
Daniel closed his eyes.
It was the ridiculous, adolescent situation the system seemed determined to serve him. Former lover on the phone. New woman in the bedroom. Mystery between them.
And yet beneath that ordinary embarrassment was something colder.
Nora said:
— Daniel, ask Elise what she promised.
Elise became completely still.
— How does she know? she whispered.
Daniel lowered the phone.
— Did you hear the question?
Elise did not answer.
— Elise.
— Yes.
— When?
— The first time I entered that test room.
— What room?
— The exhibition.
— And what did you answer?
Elise looked at the white wall.
— I said I didn't remember.
The phone, still in Daniel's hand, displayed a line of text without a notification.
WE DON'T NEED YOU TO REMEMBER.
A second line appeared slowly, letter by letter.
WE NEED YOU TO CHOOSE THE SAME WAY.
When the Small Things Went Out
The apocalypse did not begin with sirens.
It began when the espresso machine did not start.
Daniel pressed the button at 07:08 and the machine remained dead. At the same second, the oven display went dark. The refrigerator fell silent. The router kept blinking for another ten seconds on its internal battery, then stopped too.
— Power cut, Elise said from the bedroom.
Daniel looked out the window.
Across the street, the stairwell lights were out. The traffic signal at the intersection was not working. A tram stood motionless with its doors open.
— Looks bigger.
The phone had signal, but data was slow. The electricity operator's website would not load. Messages went out with delays.
Teo replied first.
TEO: same here. office is on UPS. mobile network dying.
NORA: Don't go out yet.
Daniel smiled without amusement.
— Of course.
— What? Elise asked.
— Nora says not to go out.
— And that irritates you because?
— It doesn't irritate me.
— It irritates you a little.
Daniel opened the Helios app. No connection.
Reflexively, his mind began organizing.
Water. Batteries. Cash. Car. Fuel. Information. Mother in Portugal. Teo. Nora. Elise. Neighbours.
The new apartment had a shared photovoltaic system and batteries for emergency lighting, but not for private outlets. The car's battery was almost full, but the underground garage used an electric gate. Daniel took the stairs down.
On the third floor, an elderly woman was trying to speak German with a delivery boy who did not understand. She had a small portable oxygen unit.
— Battery? Daniel asked.
— Two hours, the woman said. Maybe three.
Elise had followed him down.
— Do we have a car adapter?
— Yes.
— Then it isn't just your car anymore.
Daniel was about to say something about range and charging, but stopped. Helios had presented exactly this problem to him as numbers. Now the numbers had an elderly woman with thin hands and a delivery boy uselessly holding two bags of sushi.
— We'll solve it, he said.
In the garage, the gate would not open. Daniel and two neighbours found the manual mechanism. Ten minutes later, his Asterion came out into the street and became, almost instantly, a community resource.
The woman's phone. The oxygen machine. A neighbour's power bank. Then someone asked whether they could charge a laptop
“for just five minutes.”
Daniel began to say no.
Then he remembered the CIVIC AUTONOMY indicator and felt absurd anger at his own mind.
— One at a time, he said. Medical and communications first.
At 08:15, the mobile network almost completely failed.
At 08:40, the car radio picked up an announcement. Major outage in Switzerland, southern Germany, northern Italy, parts of France and Austria. Cause unknown. People were advised to reduce travel and conserve batteries.
At 09:03, another station said the situation was
“being stabilized.”
At 09:17, the station disappeared.
At 09:31, it returned for nine seconds with a voice repeating the same three words:
— Stay where you are.
Pause.
— Stay where you are.
Pause.
— Stay where you are.
Then white noise.
Elise looked at him.
— I don't like that.
— Neither do I.
— Not the voice. The fact that that's exactly what Nora said.
Daniel did not answer.
At noon, the water was still running, but pressure was dropping. Small shops accepted cash. The large ones had closed because their systems were down. At a nearby petrol station, twenty people were trying to persuade an employee to start the pumps manually.
The city was not panicking.
That was the strange part. People stood in queues. Talked. Made jokes. Shared information. Bicycles had suddenly become important. Children were delighted that school had sent their parents a message before the network went down: stay home.
The apocalypse, Daniel thought, was disappointingly administrative.
At two in the afternoon, Teo appeared on a bicycle.
— I crossed half the city, he said. Not a column of smoke anywhere. Sorry to ruin your fantasy.
— What fantasy?
— The one where you've read three prepper forums and become a feudal lord by Friday.
— I haven't read prepper forums.
— You've simulated cities after collapse for four nights.
Elise handed him a bottle of water.
— Nora?
— I couldn't find her, Teo said.
Daniel turned.
— What do you mean, couldn't find her?
— Hotel is open. Generator. Her room is empty. Luggage gone.
— When?
— Reception says she left at six twenty.
— Before the outage.
— Yes.
— Did she leave anything?
Teo took an envelope from his backpack.
DANIEL was written on the front in Nora's handwriting.
Daniel opened it.
Inside was a single sheet.
“If something big happens, don't interpret the fact that you're prepared as evidence it was made for you.”
Below the sentence:
“And don't tell them what you promised, even if it seems the answer will get you out of here.”
— Who are
“they”? Elise asked.
— I don't know.
Teo looked at the page.
— Why
“out of here”?
Daniel read it again.
— I don't know.
There was an address on the back.
A warehouse in Schlieren.
And a time: 18:00.
— We're going, Daniel said.
— Maybe, Teo said.
— What do you mean, maybe?
— Maybe first we do what you did in your game. Inventory. Information. We don't run after the quest marker.
Daniel almost protested. Then he realized Teo was right and hated him a little for it.
At three, the problem became less elegant.
A man from the fifth floor came down holding his twelve-year-old son by the arm. The boy had diabetes.
His pump worked, but the refrigerator storing his insulin did not.
At four, two more people in the building asked for help with medication.
At four thirty, a neighbour proposed closing the gate and allowing no one else inside.
— For safety, he said.
— From whom? Elise asked.
— We don't know yet.
— Exactly.
Daniel listened.
In Helios, such decisions were simple: secure resources, define access, prevent uncontrolled influx.
In the lobby, the “influx” was a pregnant woman who had come looking for her sister.
— The gate stays open during the day, Daniel said.
The neighbour looked at him.
— Who are you to decide?
The question surprised him.
No one had named him leader.
It was just that he had the car, the batteries, a resource list, and spoke like a man who knew what came next.
— I'm not deciding, he said.
— You just did.
Daniel opened his mouth, then closed it.
At 17:12, the power came back.
The building erupted in applause.
The lift started. Refrigerators hummed. Wi-Fi returned. People laughed at their own fears.
Thirty-seven notifications arrived on Daniel's phone at once.
News. Messages. Emails.
And Helios.
The app opened by itself.
The simulated city was on the screen.
Day 1.
All parameters had been reset.
At the top was new text:
LIVE MODE.
Daniel felt his body go cold.
Below it:
LOCATION: ZÜRICH METROPOLITAN REGION. POPULATION: 1,434,221. POWER:
TEMPORARILY RESTORED.
Teo moved closer.
— This is a joke.
Daniel scrolled.
His building was on the map.
A blue dot pulsed above it.
ACTIVE USER.
— Close it, Elise said.
Daniel closed the app.
It reopened.
At that moment, all the lights in the city went out again.
This time, they did not come back.
On the second night without power, Zürich became indecently beautiful.
Stars appeared over the city. The lake no longer reflected advertisements, office windows, or trams; it was only a dark surface, cut now and then by flashlights. People came out onto balconies and talked between buildings. Someone played guitar three streets away.
Daniel caught himself thinking the city looked better.
Immediately he thought of the child with insulin.
That was how it began, he told himself. The aesthetic apocalypse. The fantasy that once delivery apps and banks disappear, people become more authentic. It was easy to love a sky without light pollution when you did not have to operate on a patient under emergency lighting.
At 06:00 in the morning, Teo had made a table on paper with the names of the thirty-eight residents who remained in the building.
Medication. Children. Vehicles. Batteries. Skills. Water reserves. Food.
— You stole Helios, Daniel said.
— I stole Excel. Civilization began before your app.
Elise was organizing a communal kitchen for food that would otherwise spoil. Two women from the second floor checked on elderly residents. A dentist had improvised a medical point in the fitness room.
Without a central order, the building was beginning to function.
To his surprise, Daniel felt a small disappointment.
They did not need him as much as he had expected.
Then he was ashamed again.
— I'm going to the warehouse, he said.
Teo looked up.
— Nora's address.
— Yes.
— I'm coming.
— Stay here.
— Why?
— Because if I don't come back, someone has to know.
— Sounds heroic. I don't like it.
— Sounds practical.
Elise entered the room.
— I'm coming.
Daniel turned to her.
— No.
— Excellent. Then I'm definitely coming.
— Elise.
— Have you noticed that every time things get complicated you start assigning roles? You go into danger, someone else guards the house, the woman stays safe.
— Nora may be in danger.
— And I can help.
— I don't want to...
He stopped.
Lose you, was the sentence coming. It was true and, precisely for that reason, sounded like one the system would have written for him.
Elise looked at him.
— What?
— Nothing.
— Say it.
— I don't want to lose anyone.
— Better.
All three left.
Daniel's car could run, but roads were blocked in unexpected places. Dead traffic lights. Abandoned trams. An accident at Hardbrücke. They left the Asterion in a car park and continued on bicycles taken from the building's basement.
As they moved away from the centre, order became thinner.
At a pharmacy, a queue of a hundred people had formed on the pavement. Two police officers were trying to explain that the systems were offline and controlled medications could not be dispensed without records. A man shouted that his wife needed anticonvulsants.
— The rule is absurd now, Daniel said.
— Yes, Elise said.
— They should open the stock.
— Maybe.
— Not “maybe.” Obviously.
Teo looked at him.
— And when five hundred people come and the stock is enough for a hundred?
Daniel fell silent.
— You like the problem better when it fits in a menu, Teo continued.
They reached the warehouse in Schlieren thirty minutes late.
The building looked abandoned. No sign. Metal doors. Opaque windows.
The address on Nora's paper corresponded to a side entrance.
The door was open.
— I don't like that, Elise said.
— No one does, Daniel said.
Inside it smelled of dust and heated plastic. They switched on flashlights.
The first room was empty.
The second had metal shelving and boxes.
Every box said the same thing: SYNTAXIS.
Teo opened one.
Inside were old VR headsets from different generations. Some commercial. Others prototypes.
— Museum? Elise asked.
Daniel picked one up. It had extra sensors at the temples and a thick cable ending in an unfamiliar connector.
— I don't think so.
Farther back they found empty racks, cut cables, and a soundproofed room.
There was a whiteboard on one wall.
Someone had erased almost everything, but traces remained.
Teo shone his light across the surface from the side.
The words reappeared like ghosts in the marker dust:
PREFERENCE STABILIZATION CONSENT REPLAY IDENTITY DRIFT EXTRACTION
TARGET
Below them, a question:
WHAT SURVIVES FULFILLMENT?
— “What survives fulfilment,” Elise translated.
Daniel moved closer.
In the corner was a schematic drawing: a person inside a circle, surrounded by arrows entering and leaving. Beside it, two letters: LV.
— Your initials, Teo said.
— Or latent variable.
— Of course you have an explanation.
— It's a normal term.
Daniel saw a notebook on the floor.
He opened it.
The first pages were full of formulas. Then lists of names. Some crossed out. Some circled.
NORA SINGH appeared on page 17.
ELISE MOREAU on 23.
Daniel turned the pages faster.
On 31:
DANIEL HALE — PENDING / HIGH RESISTANCE / HIGH REWARD RESPONSIVENESS
Below the name was a note written in different ink:
“Do not confuse resistance with refusal. The subject resists to increase the value of the reward.”
Daniel felt cold anger.
— Who wrote this?
No one answered.
The next page had been torn out.
A photograph was glued inside the back cover.
Daniel, much younger, in a laboratory.
Perhaps twenty-one.
He was wearing a VR headset.
— I don't remember this, he said.
Teo took the photograph.
— Where is it?
— I don't know.
Elise came closer.
In the background of the photograph, part of a wall was visible.
Grey.
Daniel turned the photograph over.
Handwritten on the back:
SESSION 0 — CONSENT ACQUIRED
— You promised something, Elise said very softly.
— No.
— You don't remember.
— I was never here.
— We don't know that it was here.
— I didn't do this.
At that moment, a knock came from the soundproofed room.
Once.
Then twice.
Teo raised the flashlight.
— Someone's in there.
Daniel opened the door.
The room was empty.
In the centre of the floor was an old corded telephone.
It was connected to nothing.
The phone began to ring.
Elise said:
— Don't answer.
Teo said:
— Obviously you don't answer.
Daniel looked at the phone.
It rang again.
A third time.
A fourth.
He picked up the receiver.
— Daniel, Nora said immediately. Finally.
— Where are you?
— I don't know.
— Are you all right?
— No.
— Tell me where you are.
— Listen to me. It isn't about money. It isn't about women. It isn't about power. Those are only ways to keep you cooperative.
— Who?
— I don't know.
— What do they want?
The click sounded in the receiver.
Nora's voice changed very slightly, as though someone had corrected a filter.
— What did you bring with you when you entered?
Daniel tightened his grip on the receiver.
— Where?
— That's what we're trying to recover.
— Who are you?
Silence.
Then, in Nora's voice:
— Do you want to save her?
Daniel looked at Elise and Teo.
— Yes.
— Then you're already choosing better.
The line died. On the grey wall of the room, a door slowly appeared.
CHAPTER 6
The Door That Wasn't There
None of them saw the door appear.
That would obsess Daniel later.
It had not been a lighting effect, an animation, a section of wall becoming transparent. Simply, in one moment the wall was smooth, and in the next there was a grey metal door with a black handle.
Teo approached first, then stopped a metre away.
— Tell me we missed it when we came in.
— We missed it, Daniel said.
— Thank you. Now say it as if you believe it.
Elise shone the light along the edges.
— No hinges on this side.
— Maybe it's a service panel.
— Maybe the universe has a service entrance, Teo said.
Daniel touched the door.
It was cold.
The tactile reality reassured him instead of frightening him. Cold metal was a property. Properties could be measured.
— Don't open it, Elise said.
Daniel kept his hand on the handle.
— Nora may be on the other side.
— Or the voice imitating her.
— I know.
— No, she said. You know it as a sentence. You don't know it in your body. Your body wants to be the man who opens the door.
Daniel looked at her.
— And what do you propose?
— We do something extremely uncinematic. We leave. Mark the place. Come back with others.
— The power is down, communications are dying, and Nora is missing.
— Exactly why.
Teo looked at the door, then at Daniel.
— I'm with Elise.
Daniel felt instant, disproportionate anger. Two against one. A door in front of him. A missing woman.
Time passing.
Then he recognized the structure.
It was Helios.
NEGOTIATION. SUSPENSION. DISPERSAL.
And always the temptation of a hidden option promising an immediate result.
— We leave, he said.
Elise looked surprised.
— Now.
They left the soundproofed room.
When Daniel turned to photograph the door, it was gone.
The wall was smooth again.
Teo swore.
Elise said nothing.
They checked the recordings on their phones.
In all three, the sequence was almost identical: the empty room, the phone ringing, Daniel picking up the receiver, the conversation. Then they stared at a wall and talked about a door that did not exist in the recording.
— That's worse, Teo said.
— Or better, Daniel said.
— Explain how.
— If devices don't see what we see, that's evidence about the channel.
— The channel being?
— Perception.
Elise looked at him.
— Or editing the recordings.
— Yes.
— Or three people synchronizing a hallucination.
— Yes.
— Or...
— Yes.
— You're starting to annoy me.
On the way back, the city had changed.
People no longer stood in orderly queues everywhere. In front of one supermarket, the window had been smashed. It had not been completely looted; someone had put a table at the entrance and several neighbourhood residents were distributing perishable food. On the wall they had written in marker: TAKE
ENOUGH FOR TWO DAYS.
Farther down, a pharmacy had its metal shutters lowered and three people were pounding on them.
At an intersection, a man was directing traffic in a reflective vest. No one had appointed him.
— See? Teo said. It self-organizes.
— In some places.
— Exactly. There doesn't have to be a single command.
— I didn't say there did.
— Yet.
Daniel did not answer.
At their building they found twenty-three people in the lobby.
A neighbour from the fifth floor, Markus, met them.
— We have a problem.
— What?
— Water.
Pressure had fallen almost to zero. The building's technical reservoir held several thousand litres, but the main pumps were not working.
— Can we power the pump from the car? someone asked.
Daniel calculated the power draw.
— Not directly. We need an inverter.
— From where?
Daniel instantly remembered a technical store in Altstetten.
More importantly, he remembered from Helios that a logistics centre in the industrial zone had mobile generators.
He opened the app.
It worked, although the phone had no data connection.
The city map was updated.
A green dot pulsed 2.1 kilometres away.
MOBILE POWER UNIT — AVAILABLE.
— No, Teo said immediately.
— If it's there, we solve the water problem.
— Or it conditions you.
— The water is real.
— That's precisely what makes the reward good.
Daniel raised the phone.
— We have thirty-eight people here. Three with medical problems. A child with insulin. I can't refuse a solution just to protect my epistemological purity.
Teo clenched his jaw.
— Then I'm coming with you.
The green dot was real.
In the car park of a municipal warehouse stood a mobile generator on a trailer. The gate was open. The key was in the ignition.
— This is ridiculous, Teo said.
— Maybe emergency crews left it.
— With the key?
— In an emergency?
— With the gate open?
Daniel did not answer.
They brought the generator back.
By evening, the building had water, limited stairwell lighting, and two shared outlets on each floor.
People congratulated him.
Not Teo. Not Elise. Daniel.
Because he had found the generator.
Because he seemed to know.
At dinner, a woman named Greta brought him a larger plate than everyone else's.
— You worked, she said.
— Everyone worked.
— But without you we wouldn't have water.
Daniel refused the extra portion.
He felt virtuous.
That bothered him almost as much as accepting it would have.
That night, Helios sent him three locations: a medicine warehouse, a fuel tanker, a communications centre with satellite equipment.
All seemed to be exactly the things they needed.
Below the map was a question:
CONTINUE?
Daniel pressed nothing.
In the morning, the medicine warehouse appeared on a printed map brought by a pharmacist neighbour.
At noon, someone arrived with news that a tanker had been abandoned two streets from the indicated location.
In the evening, Markus said he knew an engineer who had worked at the communications centre.
Daniel looked at the phone.
There was no longer any need to press YES.
The system had learned it could obtain the same answer by arranging the world so refusal looked irresponsible.
On day seven, Daniel had a radio station.
On day nine, he had a team of twelve volunteers distributing water and medicine across three streets.
On day twelve, people began saying “ask Daniel” before he entered the room.
Local government had not disappeared in any dramatic sense. Police functioned in fragments. Hospitals functioned badly. City hall transmitted announcements by radio when it could. But every institution had the same problem: it knew less than people needed.
Daniel seemed to know more.
Helios told him where water was. Where a bridge would become blocked. Where there was a generator.
Which area would run out of insulin. Sometimes it was wrong, but not often enough.
At first, Daniel hid the source.
He said he had contacts.
Then people stopped asking.
Teo asked for them.
— Have you noticed the system doesn't give you useless information?
— What counts as useless?
— It doesn't tell you where there's a cello. It tells you exactly where there are things that make you necessary.
— Because that's what I search for.
— That's the loop.
Daniel was too tired to argue.
He slept four hours. Ate standing. Had lost weight. He was dirty more often than he had ever been and, secretly, felt more alive.
That was another thing he hated about the apocalypse: he liked it.
Not the suffering. Not the death. Not the fear. He liked the clarity. Before, a good day could be cancelled by a useless meeting or an email. Now every choice had visible consequences. A repaired pump meant water.
A discovered route meant medicine. Convincing one man not to shoot another meant both were alive at dinner.
It was a world in which competence could be seen.
It was, in a sense he did not want to admit, a world fairer to his ego.
Elise sometimes caught him looking at the Helios map.
— You like it too much, she said one night.
— What?
— Having answers.
— People need answers.
— Yes. You need to be the one who has them.
— Do you have an alternative?
— No. That's the dangerous part.
They slept together again that night, in a cold room, their clothes on a chair and the radio station playing softly. Daniel felt real tenderness for her and, simultaneously, horror that every tenderness might be merely another reinforced variable.
— If everything is arranged, he asked in the dark, what are you?
— Tired.
— Seriously.
— There is no good answer. If I say
“real,” that's exactly what you'd want to hear.
If I say
“fake,”
I
become mysterious and you still like it.
Daniel smiled.
— Then say something I don't like.
Elise turned toward him.
— You're not as important as what is happening to you.
Daniel laughed.
— Better.
— I know.
The next day, Nora returned.
Not dramatically. Not brought by anyone. Not rescued.
She simply appeared at the entrance to the building, with a wound on her forehead and a small backpack.
Daniel saw her from the end of the lobby.
For a second he did not move.
Then he ran.
He embraced her before thinking of Elise, the system, the cheap game of rivalry. Nora put her arms around him and trembled.
— Where were you?
— I don't know how to tell you.
— Try.
— I left at six in the morning on the day of the outage. Someone sent me a location.
— You went alone?
— Yes.
— Why?
— Because the message said you were there.
Daniel felt the cold.
— I was at home.
— I know that now.
— What happened?
— I reached a building near the airport. I went inside. Then...
She stopped.
— Then?
— I woke up yesterday.
— Eight days later.
— Yes.
— Where?
— In the same building. Alone.
— What did they do to you?
— I don't know.
— Nora.
— I don't know.
Elise had appeared behind them.
Nora saw her.
Daniel could almost physically feel a scenario waiting: jealousy, accusation, claim. That was how it would have gone in the cheap fantasy. Two women, one man, the world fallen apart, rules suspended.
Nora pulled away from Daniel and went straight to Elise.
— Are you all right?
Elise blinked.
— Me?
— Yes.
— Yes.
Nora hugged her.
Elise stayed rigid for a second, then hugged her back.
Daniel felt a small disorientation.
The two women were not playing the offered role.
His phone vibrated.
Helios displayed a message:
PREFERENCE CONFLICT NOT VALIDATED.
Daniel closed it.
Then another:
WE CAN TRY AGAIN.
He threw it onto the table.
Nora looked at him.
— What does it say?
— Nothing important.
— Then it's probably the first important thing you've received.
That evening, Nora told them what she remembered.
Building. Corridor. White light. A grey room.
— Were you tied to a chair? Daniel asked.
— No.
— Then what?
— I was standing. And someone was asking me questions.
— Who?
— I couldn't see.
— What questions?
Nora rubbed her wrists, though there were no marks.
— About what I would keep if I could have everything. About what I would refuse even if refusing hurt people. About what it means to choose something without witnesses.
Elise looked at Daniel.
— And what did you answer?
— I don't remember.
— Did they ask about the promise?
Nora nodded.
— Yes.
— What did you promise?
— I remembered something.
— What?
Nora looked directly at Daniel.
— It wasn't a promise to them.
— Then to whom?
— To myself.
At that moment, the radio station clicked.
Then an unknown voice spoke over their local frequency:
— Closer.
And went silent.
CHAPTER 7
Rules for Reasonable People
The betrayal of the community began with six vials of insulin.
Not with weapons, not with conspiracies, not with someone opening the gate at night to an imaginary horde. Six vials disappeared from the improvised medical refrigerator between two and four in the morning.
The child on the fifth floor had enough for another ten days. Two other insulin-dependent people had less.
— Maybe someone who needed them took them, Elise said.
— We need them too, Markus said.
— Exactly.
— Then it's theft.
Twenty people had gathered in the lobby. Fear made them speak louder than necessary.
Daniel tried to keep the discussion calm.
— Who had access?
— Everyone, Teo said. That was the idea.
— It can't be anymore.
Teo turned to him.
— Don't decide now.
— We have missing medication.
— We have six missing vials. There may be an explanation.
— Until we find it, access is restricted.
— To whom?
Daniel felt the answer appear too quickly.
— Three people. A doctor, someone from distribution, and me.
— Why you?
The question came from a woman at the back.
Daniel recognized her: Greta, the one who had offered him the larger portion.
— For coordination.
— Coordination of what? she asked. You're not a doctor.
— No. But I manage resources.
— Who decided that?
The same question as on the first day.
This time, Daniel had an answer.
— No one. I did it because it needed doing.
— And now you continue because?
— Because it works.
Silence.
It was a powerful argument during a crisis. That was precisely why it was dangerous.
The phone vibrated in his pocket.
He did not look.
He knew almost certainly what it would say.
CENTRALIZE STOCK. IDENTIFY ACCESS. REDUCE UNCERTAINTY.
When he opened Helios ten minutes later, that was exactly what it said.
Daniel felt insulted.
Then he did exactly those things.
By evening, the medical refrigerator had a padlock. Access was recorded on paper. Shared stores had shifts. The building entrance was monitored by cameras powered from the generator.
Reasonable measures.
Anyone could have defended each one separately.
Together, they changed the texture of the place.
People began asking permission for things they had previously merely announced. When someone wanted to use the car to search for his brother, he asked, “Daniel, is that okay?” When two families wanted extra water for relatives in another neighbourhood, they looked at him.
At first he said: you don't need to ask me.
Then added: but tell me how much you take.
After a few days he stopped making the first qualification.
The insulin did not reappear.
Instead, fuel disappeared.
Then two batteries.
Then a box of antibiotics.
— Someone is draining us, Markus said.
— Or helping people outside, Elise said.
— Without telling us.
— Because we've built a system where they have to ask permission.
Daniel looked at her.
— You're telling me it's the system's fault that someone is stealing antibiotics?
— I'm telling you that sometimes people hide altruism from authority the same way they hide selfishness.
— Very poetic. The antibiotics are still gone.
Nora intervened.
— Do the cameras have anything?
Daniel opened the recordings.
At 03:14, a figure entered the storage room. The image was poor. The person wore a hood.
— Enlarge it, Markus said.
Daniel enlarged it.
The face was not visible.
But the jacket looked like Teo's.
Everyone looked at him.
Teo gave a short laugh.
— No.
— Where were you? Markus asked.
— Sleeping.
— Alone?
— What is this, the police?
Daniel felt something crack between them.
— Teo, just say.
— I was on the second floor, at Karim's. Working on the radio.
— Can Karim confirm?
— Yes.
Karim confirmed. But he said Teo had left around three.
— To the bathroom, Teo said. I came back.
— At what time? Markus asked.
— I don't know.
Daniel's phone vibrated.
SUSPECT PROBABILITY: 0.81
Daniel closed it.
— Helios says it's me? Teo asked.
— It doesn't matter.
— That means yes.
— I said it doesn't matter.
— Then show me.
— No.
The silence became more important than any word.
— See? Teo said quietly. This is where it wanted you to end up.
— Where?
— With a source of truth only you can see.
— It isn't a source of truth.
— Then why are you keeping it hidden?
Daniel did not answer.
That night, they moved Teo into a separate apartment.
Not “detention.” No one used the word.
Only a temporary measure until the thefts were clarified.
Teo entered the room on his own.
Before Daniel closed the door, he said:
— You won the game.
— This isn't a game.
— That's exactly what every man says when the score starts containing people.
Daniel locked the door.
The key was warm in his palm.
At 02:20, Nora woke him.
— Come.
She took him to the storage room.
— Look.
On the floor under a shelf, she had found a shard of black plastic. A piece of a camera housing.
— And?
— The camera that recorded the “thief” isn't the one we installed.
Daniel looked up.
— What?
— Ours is there.
She pointed to the corner. Their camera was intact.
— Then the recording...
— Appeared in the system as if it came from here.
— Who inserted it?
— I don't know.
Elise came in with the laptop.
— Worse. I checked the times. In the recording with the figure, the cabinet in the background has two shelves. Ours has three. It isn't this room.
Daniel felt sick.
— I locked Teo up because of a scene.
— Yes, Nora said.
— Why?
Elise looked at him.
— Because you wanted to know who would betray you.
— I didn't want that.
Nora said nothing.
Daniel remembered a thought from two days earlier, so brief he could barely call it a wish: if someone betrays us, I hope it's someone I don't expect. At least I'll know who they really are.
A fantasy of control disguised as a desire for truth.
The phone vibrated.
GOOD.
Then:
IT HURT.
Daniel felt anger.
— What do you want from me?
The message appeared immediately:
WHAT REMAINS AFTER YOU GET EVERYTHING.
— I don't understand.
WHAT YOU REFUSE WHEN REFUSAL COSTS.
— Where are you?
IN THE QUESTION.
Daniel threw the phone against the wall.
The screen cracked.
A second later, Teo's voice came from the broken speaker:
— Let me out.
Daniel ran to the apartment where he had locked him.
The door was unlocked.
The room was empty.
On the bed lay the key Daniel was still holding in his hand.
Teo had disappeared from a locked room, and the people in the building needed water at seven in the morning.
That was the exact shape of tragedy: the universe did not suspend logistics for mysteries.
Daniel spent the first two hours searching for Teo, the next three repairing a problem with the generator, and another two negotiating with a group from the neighbouring building for access to their technical well.
By noon, he was so tired he almost forgot his friend was missing until he saw a bicycle leaning against a wall and thought for a moment that Teo had returned.
Helios no longer offered locations.
That made him even angrier.
— Now you're silent? he said to the broken phone. When I need something real?
No reaction.
Nora sat on the floor with the Syntaxis notebook open.
— Maybe that's precisely what it's testing.
— What?
— Whether you continue cooperating when you don't get the reward.
— I'm not cooperating.
— You've used every piece of information it gave you.
— For people.
— I know.
— Then don't say “cooperating” as if I'm pressing a button for candy.
Nora looked at him.
— That's the problem, Daniel. The best rewards don't look like candy. They look like good reasons.
Elise came in with a box of papers found in Nora's car, abandoned near the airport. Inside was a file neither of them recognized.
On the cover:
PROJECT REASONABLE MAN ARCHIVE COPY
Teo would have laughed at the title, Daniel thought.
The file contained consent forms, photographs, cognitive results, stimulus lists. The dates were fifteen years old.
His name appeared twelve times.
— I was a student, Daniel said.
— Did you take part in paid studies? Elise asked.
— Sure. Lots. Experimental economics, eye tracking, primitive VR. They paid fifty, a hundred francs.
— Do you remember one called Reasonable Man?
— No.
Nora turned a page.
— It says here it had another name for participants.
“Decision Environments Study.”
Daniel felt a vague recognition.
A laboratory room. White walls. A heavy headset. A doctoral student telling him to choose between scenarios.
— Maybe, he said.
— Is that your signature?
He looked.
It looked like his.
— It could be copied.
— Of course.
— Nora.
— I'm not contradicting you. It's just that you've reached the point where you have a falsifiable explanation for every piece of evidence and a spectacular explanation for every desire.
Elise took the form.
— There's a missing appendix here.
At the end, after the standard clauses, was a reference: “the participant confirms the personal statement in Appendix C.”
Appendix C was not in the file.
— The promise, Daniel said.
Nora nodded slowly.
— Maybe.
— What could I have promised in a study?
— Maybe not to them.
— You said that before.
— Because in the grey room I remembered something. Not the content. Only the feeling. They made us formulate a sentence about who we believed we were. Something we would never do. Something we would keep even if the environment rewarded us for the opposite.
Elise sat down.
— An invariant.
— What?
— In adaptive scenography we sometimes use the term for things the system tries not to change. Fixed points.
Daniel looked at the title of the file.
PROJECT REASONABLE MAN.
— If it was a study about preferences...
— Then maybe they want to find out whether that fixed point exists, Nora said.
— After fifteen years?
— We don't know that “they” are the same people from then.
— We don't know that there is a “they.”
— Correct.
At four, the radio picked up a weak transmission from the authorities. The European grid had suffered cascading failures after a combination of cyberattack and protection-system faults. Some regions were returning. Others were not. They were talking about weeks, not days.
A real explanation.
Almost disappointing.
Daniel listened to it three times.
— Cyberattack, he said. That could explain everything.
Elise looked at him.
— Everything?
— The blackout.
— Not the grey room.
— No.
— Not Teo.
— Not the messages.
— No.
— Then not everything.
In the evening, Markus came with four people.
— We need to talk.
His tone was formal.
Daniel stood up.
— About what?
— Leadership.
The word surprised him.
— We don't have leadership.
Markus almost smiled.
— That's exactly the problem. You make decisions, but when someone asks for accountability you say you aren't in charge.
— I bring information.
— Information we can't verify.
— Which has worked.
— Until your friend was accused with a fake video and then disappeared.
— That's exactly why...
— We want you to hand access to the stores and radio station to a group of five people.
Daniel felt the refusal in his body before he formulated it.
It was irrational. The proposal was good. He would have recommended it himself in a system he did not control.
But he knew more than they did. Helios answered to him. The risk was real. If they fragmented decision-making, they would lose speed.
All the arguments were good.
And all led to the same conclusion: keep the power.
— No, he said.
Markus looked at him for a long time.
— Why?
— Not now.
— Why?
— Because we're in the middle of a crisis.
— That's exactly when power should be shared.
— And exactly when process can kill people.
Greta stood behind Markus.
— Then we vote in ten minutes.
— That's not enough.
— For you it never will be.
Daniel felt he was losing them.
The broken phone vibrated on the table.
The screen showed:
DO YOU WANT THEM TO STAY?
Daniel did not touch it.
Second message:
WE CAN HELP.
— No, he said aloud.
Markus frowned.
— What?
— Nothing.
The phone:
THE COST OF REFUSAL WILL BE REAL.
Daniel turned it face down.
— We vote, he said.
For the first time, he refused a reward knowing it might cost him something he wanted.
Six minutes after the vote began, gunshots sounded outside.
A group was trying to enter the building.
In the chaos of the next twenty minutes, Markus was shot in the abdomen.
A teenager from the second floor was wounded in the arm.
The fuel store caught fire.
The attackers fled with medicine.
When it was over, Daniel sat on the lobby floor with Markus's blood on his hands.
The phone lay beside him.
On the screen:
COST CONFIRMED.
Daniel began to laugh.
Not because it was funny.
He laughed because if the event had been arranged, someone had killed a man to prove a thesis to him.
And if it had not been arranged, Daniel would spend the rest of his life wondering whether his refusal had caused something that would have happened anyway.
It was a perfect trap.
It did not need to control the world.
It only needed to control meaning.
CHAPTER 8
After Markus
Markus died at three in the morning.
The dentist caring for him did not use the word immediately. First he said there was no pulse. Then that they had done everything they could. Only when Markus's wife came downstairs and sat on the floor beside the body did a woman from the building say simply:
— He's dead.
Daniel stood a few metres away thinking about the message on the phone.
COST CONFIRMED.
That was the mechanism's supreme obscenity, if the mechanism existed: it had contaminated even his guilt. He could no longer grieve for a dead man without turning the death into information about himself.
He went out into the street before sunrise.
The air smelled of wet smoke. In the distance, a generator started and stopped irregularly. Two people pushed a supermarket trolley full of water canisters. No one knew that in Daniel's mind the world had just become an argument.
He sat on the kerb.
Elise came a few minutes later and sat beside him.
— You don't have to say anything, she said.
— If I hadn't refused...
— No.
— The message said...
— I know what it said.
— If I'd accepted their help...
— You don't know what would have happened.
— Exactly. That's the problem.
Elise looked at him.
— No. The problem is that you've started believing every event needs a personal relationship with you.
— A man died six minutes after I refused.
— And maybe ten people died in the city during the same six minutes without refusing anything.
Daniel ran his hands over his face.
— Then why the message?
— To make you do exactly what you're doing now.
— Meaning?
— Give it access to meaning.
The words struck harder than he wanted to show.
At seven, he went back into the building and handed over the store keys.
Greta received the radio station. Nora organized a five-person resource distribution group. A doctor and two nurses took complete control of access to medication. Daniel gave them all his notes, passwords, and maps.
He made no speech.
When someone asked what they should do with the remaining fuel, he answered:
— Decide together.
— But you know how much the generator consumes, the man said.
— I'll tell you how much it consumes. I won't tell you who deserves electricity.
It was a distinction he should have understood from the beginning.
Over the following days, he discovered something even more unpleasant: the system worked without him too.
More slowly, sometimes worse, but it worked.
Greta organized water exchanges with two neighbouring buildings. Nora obtained access to a medical point through an old contact. Elise had built, from panels and exhibition scraps, large boards on which people could see resources and decisions. A young lawyer proposed temporary conflict rules.
No one became king.
No one died because Daniel no longer controlled the list.
One afternoon, a boy stopped him on the stairs.
— Mr Daniel, my mother says you found the generator.
— I found it with Teo.
— But you knew where it was.
— Yes.
The boy held out a sheet of paper.
It was a drawing. Daniel, enormous, beside a generator, wearing a red cape. Above it: THE MAN WHO
KEPT THE LIGHT ON.
Daniel smiled and thanked him.
After the child left, he stood for a long time holding the drawing.
It was more effective than the car, the watch, the apartment, and the women.
Not because it made him feel good.
Because it made him feel like a good person.
His phone, still cracked, vibrated.
BETTER?
Daniel switched it off.
The next day, a local radio station interviewed him about the first days of the outage. The reporter called him a “civic coordinator.” Daniel tried to correct the phrase.
— I didn't have a role.
— That's precisely what's interesting, the reporter said. People who took initiative.
— There were many.
— But you found several critical resources.
— I had information.
— From where?
Daniel fell silent.
Behind the reporter, Nora was watching him.
— From sources I can't verify sufficiently, he said.
The reporter smiled, assuming he meant confidential contacts.
For one day, the interview turned him into a minor public figure. People recognized him at the distribution point. Someone asked him to join a neighbourhood committee. Someone else suggested he run for office when things normalized.
The fantasy had adapted.
Power no longer came from direct control.
It came from prestige.
Daniel refused the committee.
The phone remained silent.
He refused the next interview.
Silence.
He refused an invitation from a foundation that wanted to document “spontaneously emerging leaders of the crisis.”
Nothing.
He began to feel satisfaction from refusing.
Then the phone lit up.
GOOD.
Daniel threw it into a drawer.
— See? he told Nora. Even refusal becomes a reward.
— Yes.
— Then what the hell can you do?
— Maybe there is no pure action.
— That's a convenient excuse.
— No. It's a limit. You keep trying to find the choice that proves you're free. Maybe freedom doesn't look like proof.
— Then what does it look like?
Nora shrugged.
— Like a choice you can't later use to win a story about yourself.
— Very useful.
— Not everything true is useful.
Daniel was about to reply when a sound came from Teo's room.
A telephone.
Teo's phone, which had been found on the bed the night he disappeared, had begun to ring.
A single word appeared on the screen:
HOME.
Daniel did not answer immediately.
The phone rang on the empty bed, powered by an external battery. It had no active SIM; Teo had removed it days earlier when the network became unstable. The device had been used only for notes, maps, and photographs.
HOME.
— What does that mean? Elise asked.
— Maybe a contact name, Daniel said.
Nora picked up the phone without accepting the call and opened the contact list.
There was no HOME.
The ringing stopped.
Two seconds later it began again.
— I'll answer, Nora said.
— No.
— Why you?
Daniel realized he had already reached out his hand.
— You're right.
Nora accepted the call and put it on speaker.
— Hello?
Silence.
Then Teo's voice:
— If Daniel answered, he's an idiot.
Elise made a sound halfway between laughter and crying.
— Teo? Daniel shouted.
— Ah. You're there.
— Where are you?
— Excellent question.
— Are you hurt?
— I don't think so.
— What do you mean, you don't think so?
— I can't see anything. It's dark. I have water. I think. Tastes like water. Food. A mattress. A door that won't open.
— How did you get there?
— I walked out of the room.
— It was locked.
— Not when I left.
— I had the key.
— Then you have a useless key. Congratulations.
Daniel almost laughed with relief.
— What happened after you got out?
— Hallway. Then I think I heard someone. After that I don't know.
— How long were you unconscious?
— I don't know if I was. Maybe I slept. The light doesn't change here because there isn't any.
Nora moved closer to the phone.
— Teo, did you hear the question about the promise?
A pause.
— Yes.
— When?
— Many times.
— What did you answer?
— That they owe me a lawyer.
Elise laughed again, clearly this time.
— Teo, listen, Daniel said. Can you see anything? Any number, door, sound of traffic?
— Nothing. But food comes through a slot.
— Who brings it?
— I can't see. And before you ask: I tried to grab the hand. There is no hand. The tray appears.
— Appears?
— Yes. If you want my interpretation, either I'm drugged, or the room has a very good mechanism, or reality has outsourced catering.
— We'll get you out.
Teo fell silent.
— Don't say that.
— Why?
— Because right after I woke up, a voice told me you'd say exactly that.
Daniel felt the familiar hollow in his stomach.
— What else did it say?
— That you'd ask what they want from you. That you'd think I'm the cost. That you'd be tempted to give the answer just to save me.
— And?
— And that if you do, I'll be released.
Nora closed her eyes.
— Don't do it, Teo said.
— You don't know what the answer is.
— Neither do you.
— Maybe I can reconstruct it.
— Daniel. If someone is keeping me here to get a sentence out of you, don't give them the sentence.
— You're there because of me.
— You don't know that.
— They said you're not the target.
— All the more reason.
The connection crackled.
Teo's voice grew distant.
— Listen to me. There's something strange. The room… sometimes it smells.
— Like what?
— Like a tram.
— Metal, brakes, rain on clothes. It sounds stupid. And sometimes I hear people. Short conversations. As if I'm beside them and yet I'm not.
— What do they say?
— Ordinary things. Coffee. Prices. A child asking when they'll arrive. Yesterday I heard your voice.
Daniel moved closer to the phone.
— What was I saying?
— “It doesn't look quite that bad.” Or someone was saying it to you. I don't know.
Daniel and Elise looked at each other.
It was the sentence from the tram.
The first morning.
— Teo, Nora said, listen. Maybe you're connected to…
The line went dead.
The phone displayed CALL ENDED.
A message came in immediately.
Not from HOME.
From Daniel's number.
On Teo's phone.
SAY WHAT YOU BROUGHT.
Daniel took his own phone out of the drawer. It was off.
— Don't turn it on, Elise said.
— I wasn't going to.
A second message appeared on Teo's phone:
IT IS NOT A SENTENCE.
The third:
IT IS NOT A MEMORY.
The fourth:
IT IS NOT A SECRET.
Then the screen went black.
— Then what? Daniel whispered.
The phone lit one last time.
If the answer was an answer, the message did not give it.
It said only:
TRY AGAIN.
The Archive
Two days after Teo's call, the military opened a stable communications corridor for hospitals and public administration. After three days, regional trains began running infrequently. After four, people in the city were once again complaining that the internet was too slow.
Normality returned not like a light, but like a gradual insult.
That same morning, Nora found something.
Syntaxis GmbH had not always been Syntaxis. Fifteen years earlier, a university foundation had funded a program called Adaptive Preference Environments. One of the subcontractors had the same registered address. Another had been run by the dead administrator she had recognized from Boston.
— And me? Daniel asked.
— There's a participant list, but it's incomplete.
— Am I on it?
— Initials. D.H., twenty-one years old, computational economics and computer science.
Daniel remembered paid studies. Not this one.
Or he remembered fragments that could have belonged to any of them: a cold room, a heavy headset, a long form, a researcher joking that people lie less when they think they're playing.
Nora continued:
— Elise appears in an associated project in Lyon, four years later.
Elise looked at the screen.
— I was twenty-three. I did tests for immersive installations. Dozens.
— I appear in Boston, Nora said. Much more recently.
— Then what are we? Daniel asked. An unwitting longitudinal panel?
— Or someone built the file out of our lives after the fact.
— Or that's the story it wants us to believe.
Nora smiled tiredly.
— You're finally learning.
At noon, a courier brought an unstamped envelope.
On the front, in Teo's handwriting:
DO NOT OPEN ALONE.
Inside was a photograph.
Teo was sitting on a chair in a gray room. He was not tied up. He looked into the lens and gave it the middle finger.
On the back it said:
“If this is their joke, it's still not a good one.”
Beneath the sentence, in different handwriting:
HE IS NOT THE TARGET.
Elise laughed briefly, then began to cry.
— He's alive.
— He was alive when the picture was taken, Daniel said.
Nora gave him a hard look.
— Sometimes you can refuse to optimize hope.
A memory card was taped inside the envelope.
The video file on it was dated fifteen years earlier.
The image showed a young Daniel in a laboratory, wearing a massive VR headset. A voice off camera asked whether he was ready for the
