“reference statement.”
Young Daniel laughed.
— Sounds dramatic for a hundred and twenty francs.
— It's part of the protocol.
— Fine.
— Imagine a system capable of giving you any detectable preference: money, status, relationships, safety, experiences, knowledge. Imagine it can reduce the contradictions among them.
— It can't.
— It's a scenario.
— Then it's a bad scenario.
The voice laughed.
— That's exactly why you're here. What would you want us to know about you after all preferences had been satisfied?
Young Daniel thought about it.
— I'd want to know who gave them to me.
— Why?
— So I can find out what they want from me.
— And if they want nothing?
— Nobody gives you everything without wanting something.
The video shook.
The voice said:
— Good. Now formulate the statement. What remains yours if the environment can change all your rewards?
Young Daniel began to answer.
The audio became a continuous tone.
Thirty-two seconds.
Then the voice:
— Do you confirm that you want us to preserve this as a reference?
Tone.
— Even if it costs you?
Tone.
— Even if the people you love ask you for the opposite?
Tone.
— Recorded.
The screen turned gray.
REFERENCE LOST. RECONSTRUCTION INCOMPLETE. SUBJECT AVAILABLE.
Daniel stood up.
— Fabricated.
— Possibly, Nora said.
— It's too perfect. Exactly what we need to tie everything together.
— Then it's suspicious.
— Yes.
— You're not helping me.
— I'm not trying to help you choose the most comfortable story.
Elise rewound to the beginning.
— Look here.
Behind young Daniel, on a shelf, was a small object: a model car.
Dark gray. Sports-car shape.
Daniel leaned closer.
— It can't be Asterion. The model didn't even exist then.
— It looks like it, Elise said.
— That's all.
They enlarged the image.
It wasn't Asterion. It was an old concept car, a different make.
Daniel felt disproportionate relief.
— See? he said.
Nora looked at him.
— You just felt pleased that a piece of evidence wasn't impossible.
— Naturally.
— Because you want the world to be real?
Daniel thought about it.
— Because I want it to be false in a way I can understand.
Elise closed the laptop.
— That may be the best definition of conspiracy I've ever heard.
That evening, a single message appeared on Teo's phone:
WE ARE NOT LOOKING FOR THE STATEMENT.
Then: Daniel stared at the words for a long time.
WE ARE LOOKING FOR WHAT MADE IT POSSIBLE.
He did not know whether it meant a value, a neural pattern, a form of identity, a memory error, an object, an agreement, or something for which he had no vocabulary.
It was just vague enough to make his mind do the interlocutor's work for it.
That, he realized, might be the intention.
The gray door appeared in his apartment at 02:14.
Not on a blank wall. In place of the bathroom door.
Daniel came out of the bedroom because he had heard the click and saw it at the end of the hallway: matte metal, black handle, no frame, no visible hinges.
Text was engraved on its surface:
WE DO NOT ASK FOR THE TRUTH. ONLY THE SAME CHOICE.
Daniel did not wake anyone immediately.
That was his first mistake that night, and he recognized it as he made it.
He approached.
The handle was cold.
All the explanations came into his mind like lawyers summoned to the bar at once.
A hallucination. An intervention in perception. A device hidden in the apartment. A real entrance built without his knowledge, geometrically impossible only because the apartment's floor plan had been misunderstood. A simulation. A continuous virtual-reality layer. Neural technology implanted in his youth.
An experiment that had never ended. A story his mind was building out of guilt, stress, and coincidence.
Or something under no obligation to fit any category.
Daniel pressed the handle.
The door opened a few centimeters.
Beyond it was white light.
No room was visible yet.
— Daniel.
Nora's voice stopped him.
She stood at the other end of the hallway. Elise had appeared behind her.
— Can you see it? he asked.
— Yes, Elise said.
— The text?
— Yes.
Daniel opened the door a little farther.
Beyond it was a gray wall, perhaps four meters away. Gray floor. Light with no source.
The room from the recordings.
— Teo may be in there, he said.
— Maybe, Nora said.
— What if this is the only way?
— Maybe.
— I hate that word.
Elise came closer.
— Have you noticed something?
— What?
— It didn't ask you to go in.
Daniel looked at the text.
— That's practically what it's doing.
— No. It shows you a door. It shows you Teo. It shows you a past. You're the one building the sentence
“I have to go in.”
— Because it's logical.
— Exactly.
Nora sat down on the floor a few meters away.
— In Boston, when things were giving me everything I wanted, I started refusing objects. Money. The man. The house. I thought I was refusing the reward.
— And?
— But every refusal made me feel more lucid, stronger, less manipulable. So the system, if it was a system, only had to offer me things to refuse.
Daniel closed his eyes.
— Then there's no way out.
— There's no way out that guarantees you've won.
— That's absurd.
— Yes.
— If I go in, maybe I'm cooperating. If I don't, maybe refusal is exactly what they want. If I save someone, maybe I'm conditioned by heroism. If I don't save him, maybe I demonstrate the moral invariant or whatever the hell they're measuring.
— Yes.
— And you're calm?
— I'm not calm. I'm tired of treating freedom like a score.
Daniel looked into the gray room.
An image appeared on the far wall inside.
Teo.
He sat on the floor with his back against the wall, alive.
— Teo! Daniel shouted.
The image looked up.
He couldn't hear. Or it wasn't live.
A timer appeared beside him.
00:59:59.
— No, Elise said.
The timer began counting down.
00:59:58.
— Bastards, Daniel whispered.
Text appeared on the gray wall:
ENTER FOR THE NEXT STEP.
— Now they asked, Daniel said.
— Yes, Nora said.
— What if I don't go in and he dies?
There were tears in Nora's eyes.
— I don't know.
— That's not enough.
— I know.
The timer: 00:58:31.
Daniel stepped forward.
Elise caught his hand.
— I'm not stopping you. But if you go in, don't say afterward that you had no choice.
— And if I don't?
— Same thing.
Daniel gave a short, almost hysterical laugh.
It was the exact male fantasy of the impossible decision: one man alone, a friend to save, a countdown, two women watching him, the world reduced to his choice.
Perfect.
Too perfect.
He looked at Teo in the image.
Then at the timer.
Then at Nora and Elise.
— I don't choose alone, he said.
— What? Nora asked.
— That's the trap. Everything is built around the subject. Me. My choice. My invariant. My promise.
He turned to them.
— If we go in, we all go in. If we don't, none of us do. Not because voting is morally magical. Because I refuse the premise that everything that matters has to be extracted from me.
Elise looked at him.
— I'm not going in.
Nora took a deep breath.
— Neither am I.
Daniel felt in his body the impulse to say he would go in anyway.
Heroism. Sacrifice. Story.
The timer: 00:55:02.
— Then we don't go in, he said.
They closed the door.
On the metal surface appeared:
DISTRIBUTED DECISION.
Then:
INCONCLUSIVE.
The timer disappeared.
Daniel felt a violent surge of hope.
— Teo?
No answer.
A final sentence appeared on the door:
LATER.
They stayed in the hallway until morning.
No one slept.
At 06:31, Daniel blinked and the door was the bathroom door again.
He had not seen the transformation.
On the floor, exactly where the gray threshold had been, lay a two-franc coin.
Elise looked at Daniel.
He did not pick it up.
CHAPTER 10
Repairs
Europe did not end.
For Daniel, this was one of the most important disappointments.
The real apocalypse had no final day. It had committees, rented generators, canceled trains, hospitals catching up on postponed operations, litigation among operators, funerals, preliminary reports, and people posting pictures of food again as soon as the network recovered enough.
Three weeks after the blackout, authorities confirmed a combination of coordinated cyberattacks and cascading failures in automated protection systems. Four countries opened investigations. Two companies denied all responsibility. A minister resigned and, for a few days, that became the main story.
No one talked about gray rooms.
Daniel gave a statement about Syntaxis. The police officer who interviewed him was polite, attentive, and obviously skeptical.
— Do you have physical evidence of the door?
— No.
— Photographs?
— It doesn't appear on the recordings.
— People who saw it?
— Two.
— And Mr. Bianchi?
— Missing.
The officer paused just long enough for Daniel to hear what the story sounded like.
— Have you used any substances?
— No.
— New medication?
— No.
— Sleep deprivation?
— Extreme stress?
— Yes.
— Have you undergone psychiatric evaluation?
— Twice, before the blackout.
The officer made a note.
— Why before?
Daniel almost laughed.
Syntaxis GmbH existed legally, but its office was empty. The warehouse in Schlieren had been cleared out by the time police arrived. The isolated room existed. The old phone did not. The whiteboard had been painted over. Their notebook was real, but its paper and ink yielded nothing remarkable. The photograph of young Daniel could not be authenticated without a negative or source.
The PROJECT REASONABLE MAN file disappeared from their apartment on the night stable power returned.
No forced entry.
No hallway cameras working during that interval.
Nora said:
— If someone wants to look omnipotent, this is exactly the sort of thing they do.
— Or they're omnipotent, Daniel said.
— Don't give them the promotion for free.
Elise left first.
Her mother had had a minor accident during the outage and needed help. On the last evening, they sat on the terrace without talking much.
— I don't know what I was to you, Daniel said.
— That's a very selfish question phrased romantically.
— Probably.
— I was Elise.
— You know what I mean.
— I know. You want to know whether I was “real.”
Elise kissed him briefly.
— If you find out, don't tell me. It would ruin my biography.
— Are you coming back?
— Maybe.
— I hate that word.
— I know.
After she left, Daniel noticed that he missed not only her body and her lines, but the things that had irritated him: the way she did not answer messages immediately, the mess, her habit of moving objects, the way she refused to be impressed by his solutions.
The corners.
Her own weight.
Nora left a month later. She was following links between the Boston program and a company in Rotterdam. She did not promise she would find Teo.
— Are you coming? she asked.
Daniel looked at the city from the terrace.
— Not yet.
— Why?
— I don't know whether I'd be coming for Teo or because I can't stand there being a door I haven't opened.
Nora smiled sadly.
— At least that's a correctly formulated problem.
— And you? Why are you going?
— Maybe for the same reason.
— Then why is that better?
— It isn't.
Daniel remained alone.
Kyoden Materials resumed trading and collapsed by eighty-nine percent. Daniel lost much of his remaining paper gain, but he had sold enough early on to remain wealthy.
The car was repaired. The apartment cleaned. The watch continued measuring time with useless precision.
All the material fantasies had survived long enough to become furniture.
The most persistent one was different.
People invited him to speak about the crisis. Universities. Companies. A podcast. A newspaper.
“Decisions Under Uncertainty.”
“Leadership Without Infrastructure.”
“Lessons from the Blackout.”
Daniel declined almost every invitation.
Then he wondered whether refusal was food again.
He accepted one.
He wondered whether acceptance was food.
He began laughing alone in the kitchen.
Maybe, he thought, this was the final form of the trap: it did not have to control his choices. It was enough to make him look at all of them through its eyes.
One evening, he received an email with no visible sender.
Subject: TEO.
The message body was empty.
Attachment: a nine-second audio recording.
Teo's voice said:
— Don't try to be impossible to model. You become very predictable.
Then he laughed.
Daniel listened twenty times.
He did not know whether the laugh was real, cloned, or remembered.
For the first time, he did not inspect the metadata.
Not because he had given up.
Because he realized someone could turn the investigation into a reward too.
He closed the laptop.
That night he slept eight hours.
Three months later, on a Thursday morning, Daniel boarded tram 11.
He had no special reason to take the tram. The car was in for a routine service. He had a meeting at Paradeplatz with his lawyer. It was raining lightly.
He held his coffee in his right hand and his phone in his pocket.
In the seat across from him was a woman with short hair and a fine scar beside her left eyebrow.
Not Elise.
A stranger.
She was reading a novel.
Daniel felt fear before anything strange happened.
The tram braked.
Coffee splashed onto his shirt.
A brown streak ran down to just above his belt.
— Perfect, he said without meaning to.
The woman looked up and smiled.
— It doesn't look quite that bad.
Daniel did not breathe for several seconds.
Around him, people continued to exist. A child complained that he was hot. A man read the news. Two teenage girls laughed at a video. The tram smelled of wet clothes and metal.
— Do we know each other? Daniel asked.
The woman frowned.
— I don't think so.
— Are you sure?
— Fairly.
— Sorry.
She returned to her book.
Daniel looked out the window.
Coincidence.
A commonplace expression.
A finite city produces repetitions.
The brain looks for patterns.
He had every explanation at hand and none of them calmed him.
The phone vibrated in his pocket.
He did not take it out.
A second vibration.
A third.
Daniel kept his hand on his coffee.
At Bürkliplatz, the woman got off.
An elderly man with a wet umbrella took her place. He looked at the stain on Daniel's shirt.
— Rough day?
— It's only just begun.
— Then it still has time to improve.
Daniel smiled.
— That's what worries me.
The man looked at him curiously, then opened his newspaper.
The phone vibrated again.
Daniel did not touch it.
The tram passed the lake.
His reflection appeared in the window: a little thinner, a little older, the same face.
For a fraction of a second, there was no tram behind the reflection.
There was the gray room.
Someone sat on the chair.
Maybe Teo.
Maybe Daniel.
Maybe a mannequin.
The image vanished before he could decide.
Daniel turned around.
Behind him were a woman with a child. A student. A sleeping man.
Nothing else.
The phone vibrated a fifth time.
Then went quiet.
At Paradeplatz, Daniel got off.
The rain had grown heavier. People opened umbrellas and ran toward the arcades. For a moment, nobody needed him.
He liked the feeling.
Immediately, the pleasure made him suspicious.
He laughed to himself.
His lawyer was waiting in a café. Daniel went in, ordered another coffee, and finally took out his phone.
There were no notifications.
No unknown message.
No Helios app.
No missed call.
Just the ordinary screen.
Daniel felt such enormous relief that it almost hurt.
Then he noticed the notes app open in the background.
A new note.
Created at 08:41.
It contained three words:
TODAY IS HARD.
Daniel stared at it.
On the first day it had said TODAY IS EASY.
The change could be a threat.
It could be progress.
It could mean the test had entered another phase.
It could be a message placed by someone with access to the phone.
It could be something Daniel had written himself and forgotten.
The lawyer sat down across from him.
— Are you all right?
Daniel looked up.
The question he had heard dozens of times.
He thought of saying yes.
He thought of saying no.
He thought that either answer could be measured.
— I don't know, he said at last.
The phone remained silent.
For the first time, the system offered him nothing.
Daniel did not know whether the silence was freedom or a more sophisticated reward.
He left the phone face down.
Outside, the rain continued without seeming to have anything to do with him.
For the moment, that seemed sufficient.
